OUR WORLD 

tower so far above them. Because of this lowering of their height, the Appalachians are called old, worn-down mountains, though they are still quite high.

The Appalachian highland extends from the 

Gulf Coastal plain past the northeastern border of the United States to the Gulf of St. 

Lawrence. It is a region of thin, rocky soil and many mountains which make transportation difficult. The Appalachian highland has fewer people and fewer industries than the 

Piedmont plateau.

PLAINS 

Beyond the Appalachian highland comes the region of the Central plains, sweeping far out to the west, far south towards 

CENTRAL the Mexican border, and far north into Canada. These great plains have rich soil. In the grain lands of this section people live far apart on great farms. 

However, the Central plains region boasts many large cities which have grown up as shipping and manufacturing centers.

North of the Central plains, lying mostly in Canada, is the Laurentian highland. Much of 

LAURENTIAN HIGHLAND 

it is made up of great stretches of uncut forests and desolate grasslands. Few people live in the northern part, though some large cities and good railroads are found in its southern section.

GREAT PLAINS 

West of the Central plains and the Laurentian highland is the Great plains region, extending lengthwise of our continent from the cold, lonely Arctic ocean, across Canada and the United States, to the warm Gulf Coastal plain. Here are broad grasslands, with but few inhabitants. Grazing is the great industry. Scanty rainfall makes the region a poor one for the raising of crops, but the soil is good and there are rivers which can be used for its irrigation. The Great plains are more level than the Central plains and lift gently to the foothills of the Western highlands. 

The Western highlands form the greatest natural region of all. They stretch north and 

WESTERN HIGHLANDS 

south from end to end of our continent, and even through the entire length of South America.

In this one enormous area we find rugged mountains, high plateaus, and broad valleys. 

Here are rivers and lakes and hot, sandy deserts. In some places rainfall is plentiful. In others, the streams are used to irrigate the land. Some of the mountains wear snow caps all the year round. Stately forests cover many of the mountain slopes. Parts of the region are hardly known to man, and parts of it have great cities and fertile farms.

The Western highlands are very different from those in the east. They are so much newer than the Appalachians that we call them young mountains. But even young mountains are very old as we think of time! Wind, rain, frost, and sun have been busy with them, too, for centuries upon centuries. But the western mountains are still very high and still have jagged peaks towering above deep valleys.

So beautiful and so imposing are the rugged mountains, the lakes, and the streams of the Western highlands that the United States government has claimed many places lying within our country and made them into national parks. 

Moisture-laden winds sweep in from the 

Pacific and strike the high coast ranges, losing their moisture as rain. This makes the western slopes well watered, while the eastern slopes have little rain. High, dry plateaus and great deserts lie between the ranges.

INTERIOR HIGHLANDS 

Within the great natural regions of North

America are other natural features not so vast but no less interesting. Many of these are in the United States. 

Set between the southern part of the Central plains and the Gulf Coastal plain is a small highland section called the Interior highlands. This strange little section is really just a part of the great Appalachian highland, cut off by itself. 

The Interior highlands lie in the wide basin of the Mississippi river system, one of the two greatest inland waterways not MISSISSIPPI only of the United States but of BASIN 

North America. With its wideflung branches, the Ohio and Missouri rivers, the Mississippi drains the whole central part of the United States and makes a water highway

THE GREAT LAKES REGION 

North of the Mississippi basin is the second great inland waterway - the five Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence river. The land about the Great Lakes has nearly an oceanic climate, just suited for the raising of many kinds of fruits and grains. Minerals are plentiful here. The lakes with their connecting canals and rivers form a splendid avenue for transportation during many months each year. 

Why should we study these regions? Because it is interesting to learn how much man's surroundings have to do with the work he does, the clothing he wears, the house he lives in, and the way in which they affect his character. 

And it is even more interesting to study about the wonderful things man has learned to do to overcome the natural disadvantages of a region and to turn its possibilities to his own benefit. 

1. In early times, glaciers covered the far northern and southern lands of the earth, rounding off mountains, breaking up rocks, hollowing out lake beds, and changing the courses of rivers. 

2. Soil is made by the action of sun, rain, frost, and wind, called weathering. There are many kinds of soil. Limestone, lava, and alluvial soils are all very rich. 

3. Three important types of climate are the oceanic, the 

Mediterranean, and the continental. 

4. Warm and cold currents moving through the great oceans affect the climates of nearby lands.

SOME FLORIDA INDUSTRIES

(1) (2) (5) (6) Florida East Coast Railway. (3) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. 

1. A grapefruit tree. Notice the clusters of fruit and the dense foliage. Florida leads all states in the production of grapefruit. 

2. A celery field in central Florida. The rows are walled in with paper so that the light is shut away from the stalks of the plants. This causes the stalks to bleach, or whiten. During the winter and early spring Florida ships trainloads of celery and other produce to northern markets. 

3. Gathering sap in a pine grove. Turpentine is obtained from the sap of certain kinds of pine trees. Slanting grooves are cut in the trees, and from these the sap flows into cups fastened below. (4) U. S. Bureau of Mines. 

4. Digging phosphate rock. In the west-central part of the Florida peninsula are the largest deposits of phosphate rock in the world. After the surface soil is removed, the phosphate is dug out by steam shovels and shipped to factories for use in making fertilizers. 

5. A scene in a Florida cigar factory. In some factories the cigar-makers each give a small sum to pay a reader who entertains them as they work by reading aloud from. newspapers or novels. 

6. Florida sponges. Sponges are gathered by divers or by means of hooked poles from the rocks of the sea bottom along the southern and western coasts of the peninsula.

VARIED SCENES IN THE FLORIDA PENINSULA 

10 (7) (8) (10) (11) Florida East Coast Railway. (9) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. 

7. A glimpse of the Everglades. This great swampy area of more than 5000 square miles lies south of Lake Okeechobee in southern Florida. In the Everglades are wooded islands, many waterways, and marshes covered with sawgrass and other tall grasses. Large areas are now being drained for farm lands. 

8. A scene in the oldest city of the United States, St. 

Augustine. Notice the beautifully shaded paths lined with palms and other tropical trees. Odd cactus plants line some of the walks, and beds of blooming flowers are everywhere in the city. 

9. The city of Tampa from the air. Tampa is the leading commercial center and seaport of Florida's west coast.

Its chief industries include cigar-making and preparing sponges for market. It leads the world in the shipment of phosphate. 

10. Ormond-Daytona beach at low tide. Here the sea is so warm that people bathe in it all the year round. Most of Florida's beaches are flat and wide, and so firm that automobiles can be driven over them. This beach is used for automobile speed trials. 

11. A railroad which runs over the sea. For more than one hundred miles, from the Florida mainland to Key West, this railroad runs above the water on a viaduct built from one to another of the Florida Keys. From 

Key West vessels reach Havana, Cuba, in a few hours.

THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA A SEMITROPICAL REGION-THE FLORIDA PENINSULA 

There are forty-eight states in the United 

States of America. One of the best known is

Florida. Florida stretches in a narrow strip along the northeastern shore of the Gulf of Mexico, and then extends southeast in a broad peninsula. From the southern end of the peninsula a fringe of islands, the Florida Keys, trails off to the west.

The Atlantic ocean rolls in on the sandy eastern shores of the peninsula, and the Gulf of Mexico washes the western shores. On the south is Florida strait which joins these two bodies of water. Florida has a longer coast line than any other state. 30 88° 86 Pensacola

BAMA 84°

GEORGIA L TALLAHASSEE 

829 Jacksonville 

Florida lies in the great Coastal plain region, but its climate is so unlike that of the rest of the Coastal plain that we study it as a separate region.

CLIMATE 

The climate of the Florida peninsula is its greatest attraction. The weather varies somewhat in different parts, but the temperature is warm during most of the year. There are reasons for the warmth and evenness of the climate. One is that the peninsula is not far from the Tropic of Cancer, so the sun is high in the heavens each day in the year, and its hot rays beat down on the land. The climate is called subtropical, or 80° 78° 76 74°

The Florida peninsula―a semitropical, lowland region where the Atlantic and Gulf Coastal plains meet. 74° 11

NORTH AMERICA 

semitropical, because of the nearness of the peninsula to the tropics. Another reason for the warm, even climate is the warmth of the Gulf stream which flows all around the Florida peninsula, down the west coast and up the east coast.

The prevailing winds, that is, the winds that blow most often, are from the southeast. They, too, bring warmth. Southern Florida is warm all through the winter months, with little chance of frost. The summers are very hot, but refreshing ocean breezes temper the heat, and the nights are cool. The central part of the peninsula is like the southern part, but with more possibility of frost, and not quite such warm weather. Sometimes in winter cold winds blow from the north, making northern

Florida quite cool, and bringing killing frosts. 

Florida has some rain all through the year, and a good deal from June to September. The rain usually comes in heavy, drenching showers. These do not last long at a time, and the sun quickly comes out again.

Occasionally in the late summer or early fall a hurricane sweeps across the peninsula. A hurricane is a windstorm of

HURRICANES 

great force and violence. Usually these hurricanes travel across the sea from the southeast. The government warns people when they are coming, so that they do less damage than they formerly did.

Much of the land of the Florida peninsula is level and sandy. Travelling along the coast, one sees miles and miles of this flat 

SURFACE 

land, much of it covered with pine forests. But a trip inland shows that not all the surface is flat and near sea level. Stretching from north to south through the center is a low elevation which here and there reaches more than four hundred feet in height.

LAKES 

There are many lakes scattered through the Florida peninsula. In the central part are thirty thousand of them, some large, some small, but most of them shallow. The way these lakes are formed is interesting. The heavy rains quickly pass through the light, sandy soil to the limestone rock beneath. The limestone is soft, and holes, or sinks, are hollowed out by the action of the water. In some of these hollows the water collects. Where the surface over a sink caves in, a lake is formed. Some of the water flows along the limestone layer in underground streams. The water of these streams comes to the surface as springs or finds its way underground to the ocean.

The largest of the Florida lakes is Lake Okeechobee, lying just north of the strange region known as the Everglades. Lake Okeechobee is forty miles long and about twentyfive miles wide. Except for the Great Lakes, which are much larger, this is the second largest lake in the United States. 

THE EVERGLADES 

Much of the southern section of Florida is covered by the swampy land of the Everglades. 

They are a great wilderness of waterways and wooded islands and boggy marshes. Cypress trees and many varieties of ferns are to be found in this semitropical jungle. Saw grass, so called because it has sharp, saw-like edges, grows along the waterways. Alligators live in the water and along the muddy shores. Deer, bears, wildcats, and panthers inhabit the dense underbrush. Herons, pelicans, and cranes search the waters for fish or other food, DRAINAGE CANALS 

Parts of the marshy wilderness of the Everglades are being made over now into productive farm land by means of drainage canals. These canals drain the water from the swamp land, leaving the ground dry enough for farming. 

The rich muck land, made of vegetation that long ago decayed, makes wonderful soil for growing crops. The peninsula is so low and flat that its heavy rainfall does not run off quickly, and drainage is a difficult problem.

Much has been done already, but there are still many miles of swamp land that may be drained and made into productive fields. 

VEGETATION 

Besides the cypress, Florida has many other beautiful trees. Live oaks, long-leaf pines, palms, and palmettoes grow in all parts of the state. Only in southern Florida, however, does one see the royal palm rising tall and stately, with its feathery top, and the coconut palm with its rich fruit.

THE FLORIDA PENINSULA 

13 One misses green grass in the peninsula. 

Grass grows there only under cultivation, and there are many wide stretches of nothing but sand and low bushes called scrub. But beautiful blossomy vines decorate the walls of even the poorest houses, and flowers can be grown all the year round. The winter visitor sees yellow jasmine and cherokee roses and dogwood, and if one goes into the damp forests, one finds magnolias, white and lavender orchids, water lilies, blue flag, and blue lupin. A most peculiar plant, found all through Florida, is the Spanish moss, which hangs in long, gray ribbons from the branches of the trees.

VEGETABLES 

The raising of citrus fruits-oranges, grapefruit, lemons, and limes—is a very important industry in Florida. They can

FRUITS AND be grown throughout the peninsula, but on account of the killing frosts which are likely to come in the northern section most of the citrus groves are farther south. In the region of the deep eastcoast inlet called the Indian river, some of the finest fruit is grown. All through the lake region there is grove after grove of citrus trees. 

They are planted in straight, even rows, very much like the orchards of the northern states, but instead of green grass beneath the trees there is sandy soil.

Grapefruit and orange trees look very much alike, except that the grapefruit is larger, and lighter in color. When the fruit trees are in blossom there is a wonderful fragrance in the air. Often blossoms and fruit are on the trees at the same time. A large part of the fruit is sold to northern fruit dealers and is shipped north by express trains. Besides this wholesale selling, many boxes of fruit are sold to tourists who like to send presents home. 

Like oranges, pineapples need much warmth. They grow in the southern end of the peninsula. They grow on the ground surrounded by their spiked leaves. Still other fruits that are grown are cantaloupes, watermelons, strawberries, and grapes.

Many vegetables are grown in this subtropical region-sweet and white potatoes, celery, lettuce, corn, and cabbages. All through the central part of the peninsula, and south, in the drained land of the Everglades, are fields and gardens planted with these vegetables. Florida has a great advantage over other states in having her products ripen early. Her wintergrown tomatoes are in the northern markets by the middle of March, and her lettuce and celery in the early spring. Strawberries from 

Florida appear on northern fruit stands months before the berries ripen in the north.

In the Florida forests of long-leaf pines we may see pails attached to the trees. These catch the sap that drips from

AND LUMBER cuts made in the bark. This NAVAL STORES 

sap is valuable, for turpentine, tar, and pitch are made from it. These substances are called naval stores. The name comes from the days when tar and pitch were used to make wooden ships water-tight. 

Savannah, a seaport in Georgia near the

Florida forests, is the great market for naval stores.

PRODUCTION OF LUMBER IN THE UNITED STATES WASHINGTON OREGON MISSISSIPPI LOUISIANA ALABAMA CALIFORNIA TEXAS ARKANSAS GEORGIA NORTH CAROLINA FLORIDA IDAHO SOUTH CAROLINA ALL OTHER STATES 

2 This graph shows the states which lead in the production of lumber. 

Each unit of the graph scale represents one billion board feet. Notice that the main centers of lumber production are in the Pacific coast states and in the Southern states.

Much pine is cut in the thick pine forests of central and of northern Florida. Sawmills are scattered all through these regions, cutting logs into lumber.

NORTH AMERICA PHOSPHATE ROCK 

Besides its fruits, vegetables, naval stores, and lumber, the Florida peninsula has an important mineral product as well. Underneath the soil west of the lake region, and especially around Tampa bay, are the largest beds of phosphate rock in the world. The sandy top soil is cleared away and the phosphate is dug out. It is then sent to factories in 

Tampa and other places to be made into fertilizer. Nearly all soils need something added to them to make them more fertile. Phosphate alone, or combined with nitrate which Florida imports from South America and potash which she buys from Europe, makes fine fertilizers which are very important wherever there is farming. The phosphate from the Florida peninsula is used not only in our country but in Europe and Asia, as well.

The manufacture of tobacco is another industry of the Florida peninsula. Tobacco is

TOBACCO 

grown in parts of Florida, but most of the raw leaf used in the tobacco factories is brought over from Cuba, where tobacco is grown in enormous quantities. 

Cuba is an island lying just south of Florida. 

Havana, its chief port, is in easy reach of Florida ports.

We are all used to seeing sponges, but perhaps we do not know that a very large number of them are gathered by spong- 

SPONGES 

ing fleets in the waters around the southern and western coasts of the Florida peninsula. Sponges are the soft skeletons of certain tiny animals which grow only in warm salt waters. These animals are found clinging tightly together in groups on the rocks of the sea bottom. Where the water is shallow, they can be pulled off the rocks by hooked poles. Where the water is deep, men put on diving suits and go to the bottom for them. 

Before they can be used, sponges must be treated. They are left awhile to soften and dry. Next they are washed and beaten to clean them. Then they are dried again, and are ready for use. 

Tobacco manufacturing and sponge gathering are also chief industries of the Florida 

THE FLORIDA KEYS 

Keys. These islands and parts of the Florida peninsula were formed by tiny animals, called corals, which lived in the warm waters of the sea. 

As they died, their skeletons formed a hard, rocky material. This was built up bit by bit as new corals lived and died, leaving their skeletons on top of the older ones. The coral islands are very beautiful with their tropical vegetation. Pine trees and tall coconut and royal palms grow on them. There are many of these islands, some inhabited, some not. 

VACATION LAND 

The Florida peninsula is an ideal vacation spot. It is reached quickly and easily from the north by fast express trains; by automobiles which can speed over straight, level, fine roads; by luxurious steamships which run on frequent schedule down the Atlantic coast; or even by airplanes. When the winter, with its bare trees and dark, cloudy days, makes the north cold and dreary, it is wonderful to go to this southern land where the sun is shining brightly, flowers are blooming, and there is fun outof-doors all day. The Gulf stream makes bathing delightful along the coast. The inland lakes are used for boating, and for bathing by those who like fresh water better than the salt water of the ocean. 

For those who enjoy fishing, there are many kinds of fish in the sea shad, mullet, mackerel, trout, and even the huge tarpon. There is also excellent fishing in the lakes. Fish are caught for pleasure, and they are also caught for profit and shipped north in refrigerator cars. 

POPULATION 

Many who go to Florida for vacation like it so well that they stay and make their homes there. This keeps the population constantly increasing. 

Florida is a large state but still has few people in proportion to its size. New York state, which covers less area, has about eight times as many people. Most of the people of Florida are found in the cities, and in the central and northern parts of the state where they can make a living by growing fruit and vegetables, by lumbering, or by digging phosphate. There are many negroes in Florida but there are many more white people.

THE FLORIDA PENINSULA 

15 

CITIES 

Tampa is the important commercial center of the west coast of the Florida peninsula. 

Its chief industries are the manufacture of cigars and cigar boxes, the exporting of phosphate, and the preparation of sponges for market. 

Though not on the peninsula, Jacksonville is the important commercial and trading center on the east coast. A great railroad center for trains running north and south, it is also an ocean port for commerce with other countries. 

Among its chief imports are nitrate and coffee from South America. It has a fine harbor on the lower St. Johns river. This river is very broad and is navigable for 250 miles. 

Perhaps the most interesting city in Florida is St. Augustine. It is the oldest city in the 

United States. It was settled by the Spaniards in 1565, and some of the old, narrow streets and old Spanish houses are still very much as they used to be.

Many of the houses throughout the peninsula are built in the style of these Spanish houses. They are low, and are coated with light-colored stucco. They have long windows, some of which open on little balconies, and many of them have small courtyards, bright with blooming flowers. They seem to fit in with the natural beauty of the region. 

Miami, St. Petersburg, and Palm Beach, on the coast, are among the most popular resort cities. They have huge hotels for the thousands of winter visitors, wonderful sandy beaches for bathing, and beautiful pleasure grounds for the enjoyment of all.

Key West is the most southern city of the United States. It is on the most westerly island of the Florida Keys. A railroad more than 100 miles long connects Key West with the Florida peninsula. It has been built over the Keys and over concrete bridges extending from island to island.

Florida and the north depend upon each other in several ways. Florida needs a market for her products and the north enjoys the fresh fruits and vegetables which come from the south during the winter months. Florida offers her northern visitors much in the way of climate, health, and pleasure, and the money which these visitors spend in Florida means much to that state in supporting her great hotels and running her many lines of business. 

1. The Florida peninsula extends southeast from the mainland of the United States, between the Atlantic ocean and the Gulf of Mexico. even. 

2. The climate of the Florida peninsula is warm and 

This is due to its nearness to the Tropic of 

Cancer and to the warm Gulf stream. There are occasional frosts caused by cold north winds. 

3. The surface of the peninsula is almost on a level with the sea except for a slight elevation running through the center. Parts of southern Florida are low swamplands, called the Everglades. These are being reclaimed by means of drainage canals. 

4. Cypress trees grow in the swamplands. Pine trees, live oaks, palms, and palmettoes grow throughout the state. 

5. The long-leaf pine trees are used for lumber. Turpentine, tar, and pitch are obtained from the sap of these trees. 

6. Pineapples are grown in southern Florida, and oranges, grapefruit, lemons, and vegetables in the central and southern sections. All these products are shipped to northern markets. 

7. The Florida peninsula has the largest phosphate mines in the world. 

8. The manufacture of cigars and tobacco and the gathering of sponges are important industries. Tampa is the commercial center for these products. It imports tobacco, nitrate, and potash. 

9. The Florida Keys are coral islands off the southern tip of the Florida peninsula. The city of Key West is a center for the tobacco and sponge industries. 

10. Florida is a winter resort because of its warm climate. Among the resort cities are Palm Beach, St. 

Petersburg, and Miami. 

11. Jacksonville is one of the chief commercial, railway, and shipping centers. St. Augustine is the oldest city in the United States.

THE LAND OF COTTON 

4 

YPRESS & BARGES 

3 CYPRESS 

(1) The Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Co. (2) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. (3) (4) Mobile Chamber of Commerce. 

1. Picking cotton in a Texas cotton field. Such scenes as this are familiar sights throughout the cotton belt during the harvest season. Picking usually begins in July and sometimes lasts until January. The pickers go through the fields again and again as the bolls burst, gathering the cotton by hand. 

2. A perfect cotton boll, fully open. When the bolls are ripe, the hard shells burst open and the white, fleecy fibers appear.

At this stage, the cotton is ready for picking. 

3. A cotton warehouse in Mobile, Alabama. Cotton is usually ginned-or the fibers separated from the seedsin ginning mills near the fields. Then it is baled and is stored in warehouses at railway centers and shipping ports. From these warehouses it is sent to cotton mills in our own country and abroad. 

4. Cotton arriving at the port of Mobile. Down all the navigable rivers of the cotton belt come flatboats pushed by river steamers, bringing cotton to the seaports. The steamers are flat-bottomed and draw so little water that they can navigate very shallow streams. Notice the paddle wheel on this steamer. It is placed at the back of the boat and almost out of the water to keep it from striking snags and sandbars. Tell what you see in the background of this picture.

5. A general view of the Burbank oil field in Oklahoma. 

This is a splendid example of a developed oil field. 

Notice the regular spacing of the wells, as shown by the derricks, or frameworks which support the drills used in boring the wells. To the left are houses for the employees. 

6. A scene in a Texas oil field. The tank in the foreground is an oil filter. Oil from wells beneath the derricks flows through pipes to the filter, where dirt and other impurities are strained out. The oil is then stored in large tanks until shipped to the refineries. 

7. Sulphur wells in southeastern Texas. Because of overlying quicksands, much of the sulphur of the South must be reached by wells instead of underground tunnels. In one process, pipes 10 inches, 6 inches, 3 inches, and 1 inch in diameter are lowered into the well, one within the other. Steam is forced down the 3-inch pipe to melt the sulphur. Compressed air is then forced down the 1-inch pipe to drive the liquid sulphur up through the 6 and 10-inch pipes. 

8. An oil-refining plant at Fort Worth, Texas. The crude oil is piped from the oil fields to refineries which are usually located at shipping ports or railway centers. 

Here the oil is refined, or separated into gasoline, kerosene, fine oils, and fuel oil. In the background you can see several storage tanks. The products of the refinery are shipped in tank cars or in the metal barrels stacked in the yard.

THE SOUTHERN, OR SOUTH ATLANTIC AND GULF, STATES 

"Way down south in the land of cotton!" 

What a picture this line from an old American song brings to us! Fields and fields of white cotton beneath sunny skies! Hundreds of negro men, women, and children working there! Their dark faces, their brightly-colored cotton clothing, and the colored handkerchiefs, or bandannas, which many wear about their heads to keep off the heat, make a striking contrast to the white fields. All are busy. They work steadily but happily, whistling and singing as they pull the soft fiber from the cotton plants to fill the great baskets placed at intervals among the rows. In many parts of our 

Southern states you may see a sight such as this at cotton harvest time. 

The Southern states include those which lie wholly or partly in the Gulf Coastal plain and the southern part of the Atlantic Coastal plain. 

They are sometimes called the South Atlantic and Gulf States. In this group are North and 

South Carolina, Georgia, Florida, Alabama, Mississippi, Louisiana, Arkansas, Texas, Okla. homa, and Tennessee. Most of this region is known as the cotton belt. 

CLIMATE 

NEEDED FOR 

GROWING COTTON 

Cotton requires a long growing season-at least 200 days of warmth and freedom from frosts. The cotton belt has a growing season of from 200 to over 240 days. Cotton needs, too, at least twenty-three inches of rainfall every year. Most of the cotton belt has from forty to sixty inches of rain, yearly. The rain comes every two or three days during the growing period, but the harvest season is dry and sunshiny. How difficult it would be to pick wet cotton!

The planting season for cotton extends through February, March, and April. The southern parts of the belt are planted earlier than the

THE GROWING AND HARVESTING SEASONS 

northern parts. Plants about four feet high grow from the cotton seeds. In the early spring the plants become covered with white blossoms, which turn pink and then drop off. In their places grow small, hard pods, called bolls. 

Within each boll are many seeds, to each of which are attached several long, white cotton fibers. When the bolls are ripe they burst open, and the white fibers puff out in fluffy balls.

Some of the bolls open as early as July, and the white fleecy fiber is gathered from them. 

Later, more bolls open, and the workers go through the fields again. The picking season sometimes lasts until January, but its chief months are October and November.

Here are three facts which will help you to remember how much the growing and harvesting of cotton depend upon climate. To the west of Texas lies a region which has too little rainfall for the growing of cotton. There, cotton plants, even though well started, will shrivel up and die. North of the cotton belt, frosts shorten the growing season and affect the cotton plants much as they would affect flowers planted too early in a northern garden. 

South of the cotton belt, very heavy rains cause the bolls to decay, or rot, and so prevent the fiber from developing. For a good cotton crop, then, a long growing season, without frosts, and with a moderate, not-too-heavy rainfall, is required.

SOIL There is another thing, besides climate, which makes it possible to raise cotton in the 

South Atlantic and Gulf states; that is good, rich soil. The Atlantic Coastal plain was formed long ago of soil washed down from the Appalachian highland by rivers on their way to the sea. The rivers left this soil in the ocean. Gradually the broad, flat, fertile plain was built up on the floor of the sea and finally lifted above sea level.

it drains most of the Gulf states on its way to the Gulf of Mexico. In much the same way as the Atlantic Coastal plain was formed, the Gulf Coastal plain was built up, by the deposits of the Mississippi and other streams flowing south into the Gulf of Mexico. The soil of both these coastal plains is mostly light and sandy. It is rich and easily cultivated. 

Nature has been particularly good to the farmers in certain parts of the cotton belt. In 

Alabama and Mississippi there is a stretch of land known as the "black belt" because of its rich, black clay loam. There is another stretch of rich loam in Texas, called the "black waxy prairie." When wet, the soil of the "black waxy prairie" resembles little balls of wax.

Nature has also been kind to those living in 

Louisiana on the flood plains of the Mississippi.

COTTON 

PRODUCTION 

Texas produces more than one-fourth of our total cotton crop, and ranks first among the states. Tennessee is farther inland and so does not receive the warm rains of the Gulf coast. It is also farther north than the chief cotton states. This state produces the smallest cotton crop of the cotton belt states. In a few sections of the southwestern part of our country is grown a kind of cotton very valuable for its long, strong fibers.

Cotton was grown in Asia and Africa for hundreds of years before it was raised in the United States. The Egyptian cotton was especially valuable on account of its long, strong fibers. It became known as "long staple cotton." Because of the demand for this excellent cotton, cotton growers in the United States tried to raise it. After much experimenting, they found that it grew best in the irrigated valleys of our western lands, and it has become one of the important crops of 

California. As a result, although California grows less cotton than any of the cotton belt states, what she does produce is of the finest grade.

In the early days in the south, cotton was raised on plantations, or farms of many acres. 

These plantations were owned by rich planters, who built beautiful homes on their lands. 

There was so much to be done in the fields that 

PLANTATIONS AND THEIR WORKERS 

many negroes were brought here from Africa to do the work. Little cabins were provided for them to live in, and they were given food and clothing, instead of money, for their services. Today there are still large plantations in the south, but the men and women who work on them receive a regular wage.

Much cotton is also raised on small farms, where the owner and his family do the work.

Let us see the processes through which cotton goes before it is manufactured. Some skill is required in picking the fiber from 

GINNING 

the bolls, because if it is soiled or broken it must be sold for less money. When it is picked, it is sent to the mills nearby to be ginned. Ginning is the separating of the seeds from the fibers. At first this was done by hand, but it was a long, tiresome process for one man

cotton a day. The invention of the cotton gin made possible the present great cotton industry of our country, which produces more than half the cotton of the whole world. In a recent year, the cotton crop of the United 

States amounted to nearly 18,000,000 bales.

COTTON MANUFACTURING 

When the cotton is ginned and baled it is ready for the factories. Until recent years most of the cotton was sent abroad or shipped north to the textile mills of New England to be made into thread and many kinds of cloth. But cotton factories are rapidly increasing in the South Atlantic and Gulf states, especially in North and South Carolina and Georgia. Today much cotton is manufactured in these states. Raw materials are abundant and near at hand, labor is cheap, and there is plenty of water power for running the mills. These things may in time cause the textile industry of the south to rival that of the 

New England states. 

How different are the swift machine methods today from the long, weary methods of the early colonists! Nearly all homes, then, had spinning wheels, on which the housewives spun their thread and yarn. And they had hand looms on which they wove cloth to make aprons, dresses, shirts, and suits for their families. The use of machinery has made the production of both cotton and woolen cloth much quicker and cheaper than ever before.

There are several reasons why the cotton crop pays well. It is produced by cheap labor. 

It will not spoil, like the fruit crops of Florida, if it does not reach market quickly. It is easily shipped, and it is widely used by people everywhere for clothing and many other necessary articles. 

COTTON SEED 

What becomes of the cotton seed? After ginning, the seeds are crushed and used as food for cattle, or as a fertilizer. In the crushing, a thin, sweet oil is obtained. This is used in packing sardines, in butter substitutes, and in a lardlike substance which is used for cooking. Fine soaps are made of cottonseed oil. Often in the south the oil is used on bread in place of butter.

MIXED 

FARMING 

Has the land of cotton any other products? 

Let us see. Nearly every farmer of the cotton belt raises some corn, usually for food for his family and his cattle. 

Mixed farming, or the raising of a variety of fruits and vegetables, is being carried on more and more by farmers of the cotton belt. This is because mixed farming has several advantages. Some crops which are not produced in such large quantities as cotton are easily grown with less labor, and often bring better prices than cotton.

When a farmer raises several kinds of crops, his entire harvest does not depend on a certain kind of weather, or on the absence of some particular insect pest or blight. Perhaps, if he is unsuccessful with one crop, another product of his farm will pay for his losses. This was learned by the cotton farmers years ago when the boll weevil came from Mexico and attacked the cotton crops. This little insect did so much harm that hundreds of thousands of dollars worth of cotton was lost. Farmers who raised only cotton were a long time recovering from this great tragedy. They had no other crop which they could sell to help them out of their difficulties. 

Today the boll weevil still threatens great damage to the cotton crop, but on many farms other crops are being grown which do not suffer from this pest.

Farmers of the South Atlantic states raise many peaches. There is always a great demand for this luscious fruit. It ripens early in the warm climate of the south. As we go north along the Atlantic Coastal plain we find the peach crop ripening later and later. Because of this fact, peaches can be had over a long period both in the north and the south. 

When the negroes came here from Africa they brought with them some nuts of which they were very fond. These were peanuts.

They planted the nuts, and today peanuts are raised in many sections of the Southern states. 

Peanuts grow on the roots of low, bushy plants. The roots reach far into the soil. 

When they are pulled from the earth they break up the soil so that the air can enter and help prepare it for later crops. Peanuts are a

NORTH AMERICA 

valuable crop. They are roasted in the shell, and some are shelled and salted. Many are crushed for their oil. Peanut butter is made from crushed peanuts.

The pecan, another nut which is good to eat, grows along the Gulf coast. As pecans grow on very tall trees, it is harder and more expensive work to harvest them, so they cost us more to buy than peanuts. In some places pecan trees are grown in orchards like fruit trees. 

Tobacco is raised in quantities around Durham, North Carolina, and near Clarksville,

Tennessee. Tobacco also grows along the northern Atlantic Coastal plain.

The south is not a great dairy region. Other parts of the United States, particularly New York state and the Great Lakes region, which have a cooler climate, clear, running streams, and naturally green pastures, are far more suitable for this occupation. Beef cattle, however, are raised in certain parts of the Southern states, especially in Texas.

Sugar cane and rice are two other products farmed in the south. Like cotton, they thrive only under certain conditions of 

SUGAR CANE climate and soil. Where the Mis sissippi river flows into the Gulf 

AND RICE 

of Mexico it has left great deposits of silt or sand. Some of these deposits are so large that the main stream has divided around them into several smaller streams. Each of these in turn has left soil along its banks and, since the work has been going on for many, many years, a large area of land has been gradually built up. 

This land, spreading out from the mouth of the main stream, is fan-shaped, and is called a delta. Do you remember a delta formed at the mouth of a river in hot, sunny Egypt? 

What is its name?

The climate of the Mississippi delta and of the land along the north coast of the Gulf of Mexico is subtropical, and the section is known as the subtropical belt. In this region sugar cane flourishes wherever the soil is not swampy.

In the swampy districts, rice is grown, for this crop needs heavy rainfall and land that may be easily flooded. Rice, in our country, was first raised in South Carolina and Georgia, but now it is not extensively grown beyond the Mississippi delta.

MINERALS 

Even now, we have not discovered all the wealth of the cotton belt states. In addition to farm crops, the South Atlantic and Gulf states are rich in minerals and forests. Foremost among the minerals of this region is petroleum, an oil which we could hardly do without. From it we obtain gasoline for our automobiles, kerosene for lighting homes, lubricating oil to make engines run smoothly, and other products. After all these are removed, there is left a cheaper fuel oil for heating homes and running machinery. 

Some of the world's largest deposits of petroleum are in Oklahoma and Texas.

There are natural reservoirs of oil under the earth's crust. To reach this oil, holes are drilled through the layers of rock which cover these reservoirs. Often, when the oil is reached, it gushes out, just as the water gushes out when a garden hose springs a leak. Sometimes a heavy, black stream, higher than your school building, rises into the air. 

When an oil well has been drilled, pumps are built above or near the well. The oil is

9. Hogs in a pasture in Texas. Once the southern farmer depended for his pork on the razorback hog, which ranged the woods, feeding on nuts and roots. 

Today many farmers raise high-grade hogs which provide much better meat than the razorback. 

10. Cutting sugar cane on a Louisiana plantation. Sugar cane requires a deep, rich soil and at least eight months free from frost. The Mississippi delta is especially suited to this crop.

The odd knives used in cutting the stalks are heavy and sharp. See if you can tell from the picture about how high the stalks grow. 

11. Rice fields in the Mississippi delta in Louisiana. The fields are plowed and planted by machinery. Then they are flooded from irrigation ditches, for rice grows best in water. When the rice begins to ripen, the water is drawn off. Later, when the rice is fully ripened, it is harvested by machinery. 

12. Spraying a peach orchard in Georgia. Good fruit is the result of constant care of the orchards. These trees are being sprayed with sulphur to protect them against brown rot, a disease that attacks the leaves. The motor on the cart drives a pump which forces out the sulphur spray. 

13. A sawmill and lumber yard at Natchez, Mississippi. 

Members of the same family have run this plant for over a century. Notice the stacks of seasoning lumber. 

The tops and ends of the stacks are slanted so that rainwater will run off and not soak into the wood.

14. Air view of the business section of Houston, Texas.

Houston is the largest city in the state and one of the greatest cotton markets in the world. It is connected by ship canal with the port of Galveston. Notice the grouping of tall office buildings, a sign of modern business development. 

15. Air view of the port of Galveston, Texas. The city is built on an island and is connected with the mainland by a raised road, or causeway, built across the shallow water. A great sea-wall protects Galveston from heavy seas. Notice the many piers along the harbor channel. 

Here one hundred ocean steamers can dock at one time.

Railway lines run out onto the piers. 

16. Pirates' Alley in the old quarter of New Orleans.

New Orleans was long under French and Spanish control. The old buildings have a foreign look. Notice the overhanging balconies, the narrow street, paved with wide slabs of stone, and the central drain. 

17. The Mission San Francisco de la Espada, in San 

Antonio, Texas. In this oldest of Texas cities, founded by the Spaniards, are a number of ancient mission buildings. See the heavy wooden doors, and the bells each set in its own arch. 

18. The Patio Royal in old New Orleans. Enclosed courtyards like this are common in old French and Spanish houses. Vines, flower beds, and tables and chairs make this courtyard a delightful place to rest.

pumped up and stored in great tanks, or is piped long distances to some port. From there it is shipped to many parts of the world. 

In Texas, Oklahoma, and Louisiana, oil has made property valuable and has made men wealthy.

Sulphur and salt are two other important and valuable minerals. All along the Gulf of 

Mexico are marshy lands. Under the marshes, especially in the states of Louisiana and Texas, there are great sulphur and salt beds. In its natural rock form raw sulphur is yellow. It melts at a low temperature, so heat is used in various ways to mine it. One way is to shoot a stream of hot water into the sulphur bed. 

This melts the sulphur, and when it is in liquid form it is pumped to the surface and drawn off into great vats. There it cools and becomes solid again, and in this form it is sent to factories. Sulphur has many uses. It is valuable in making gunpowder and in killing insect pests. Substances made from sulphur are used in fertilizers and medicines and in bleachingtaking the color from cloth and other materials. A sulphur substance was used to remove impurities from the wood fibers of which the paper of this book was made. 

Have you ever seen a picture of an Eskimo's snow hut? If you live in the northern part of our country, perhaps you have built a snow hut for yourself. How white and bright it was in the crisp winter air! The inside of a salt mine looks white and bright like that. Some of these mines are very large, and are so deep that they must be reached by underground elevators. The salt is taken out, leaving a series of rooms whose roofs are supported by columns of wood or by columns of the salt itself. 

The mined salt is broken up, shoveled into cars, and taken on the elevators to the surface of the earth. Some salt is mined by dissolving it in water. The brine is pumped to the surface and the water is evaporated.

Salt is one of the most important minerals we possess. Like sulphur, it is used in bleaching. It is also used in preserving many kinds of food, especially meats and fish. We use a very refined, or purified, salt to season our food. Salt has a very great use as a cleanser. 

Other valuable minerals are found in the Southern states in somewhat smaller quantities. Tennessee, where the Appalachian highland crosses it, has deposits of lead, zinc, iron, coal, and other minerals. Soft coal, zinc, and lead are also found in Arkansas. Alabama has deposits of iron, limestone, and soft coal. 

FORESTS 

When the early Spaniards explored the South Atlantic and Gulf coasts of our country they took back with them to Europe tales of deep, rich, beautiful forests. 

What a wilderness it was! Although during the past years many trees have been carelessly cut or burned, the oak, the cedar, the cypress, and the pine still flourish in the districts best suited for them. The oak, and certain varieties of the cedar, grow on the higher levels. 

Great cypress trees, many of them growing in swamps, provide a lumber which is valuable because it does not easily rot. 

Along the coast, from North Carolina to eastern Texas, grow thousands of stately pines. Pines are valuable, not only for lumber, but because they yield turpentine, pitch, and tar. Turpentine is used in making paint. Oils from pine and cypress trees are used in polishes. 

The question of restoring old forests, planting new ones, and preserving those now standing has lately become of great interest to the south, which uses much lumber for building and for making furniture.

POPULATION 

A region rich in so many resources as the Southern states naturally has a large and increasing population. The total number of people living in the United States is about 120,000,000. Over onefifth of this number live in the South Atlantic and Gulf states. Texas has the largest population of any of these states over 5,000,000. 

This is, however, less than one-half the population of New York, which has more people than any other state. In all the Southern states there are many negroes, but the total white population exceeds them in number. 

The chief ports of the cotton belt region are 

Galveston and New Orleans. Both these cities are gateways to the interior of the continent and have fine harbors. Galveston is connected

by ship canal with Houston, which is the largest city in Texas and sells enormous quantities of cotton. The imports of Galveston and Houston include petroleum from Mexico and sugar from Cuba. 

Great ocean steamers can enter the port of

New Orleans, although it is about 100 miles upstream from the mouth of the Mississippi.

Besides being in a most favorable location for foreign commerce, New Orleans is in the midst of a great farming region, and is connected by railroad, and canal and river, with inland cities. 

You would like to see this great, old city.

Parts of it are like sections transplanted from the Old World. The French established the little village of New Orleans in 1718. It was later given to Spain, then back to France, and finally, in 1803, it was bought by the United States.

The old part of the city is an attraction to tourists. It is called the French Quarter, though parts of it are more Spanish than French. Here are low, brick houses, with queer, overhanging balconies, and tiny courtyards where little gardens still flourish. Much of the district is now so neglected that wealthy citizens, who want to preserve the quaint beauty of various spots, are paying privately to restore them. Here one sees many descendants of the early French and Spanish people. One often hears French and Spanish spoken in New Orleans. However, English is the language generally used for trading.

The business sections of New Orleans are modern and up-to-date. The city has a very large trade, and its business districts have grown rapidly in recent years.

In New Orleans are many old churches and public buildings which are examples of fine Spanish and French architecture. The houses, even in the newer residential sections, for the most part follow the Spanish design, and are built low, with courtyards. 

New Orleans is the largest port of the southern states. As the city lies below the river level, a great wall has been built along its beautiful river front to protect it from the 

Mississippi. At this wall, the famous "levee," steamers from South and Central America and the West Indies unload bananas, sugar, coffee, cacao, and mahogany. From Asia come rubber and jute. Jute is a plant fiber which New 

Orleans factories make into burlap and gunny bags. The ships carry away cotton, cotton goods, wheat, oil, lumber, and flour, which other countries buy in return for their goods. OTHER 

CITIES 

Pensacola, Florida, and Mobile, Alabama, are smaller harbors on the Gulf of Mexico. To them come nitrate from South America and bananas from Central America.

Many of the southern manufacturing cities are in North and South Carolina, northern Georgia, Alabama, and Tennessee. 

Because the Appalachian highland crosses these states they have a greater variety of natural resources than Mississippi and Louisiana, which lie wholly in the Gulf Coastal plain. 

The highland supplies water power, and, in places, lumber, coal, and iron, all of which are needed in many kinds of manufacturing. The small Ouachita range, in the states of Arkansas and Oklahoma, supplies lumber for the manufacturing cities of these states and Texas. 

Savannah, Georgia; Charleston, South 

Carolina; and Wilmington, North Carolina, are ports of the South Atlantic states. Because of the highland beyond them, these cities have less direct routes to the interior than have New 

Orleans and Galveston. Chief among their exports are lumber, naval stores, cotton, and cotton goods. Petroleum and gasoline from the Gulf ports and materials for fertilizers from 

South America and Europe are imported. 

Dallas is a railroad and banking center of 

Texas. It is also a great manufacturing city. 

Fort Worth is another railroad and manufacturing center. Between these two cities are large flying fields where aviation experiments are made. 

San Antonio ranks with Dallas in size, and is the oldest city in Texas. The original mission church there dates from 1744. The modern growth and development of the city as the business and manufacturing center of southern Texas are due to its location between grazing

NORTH AMERICA 

lands and farm lands and to its excellent rail connections. The city is a center for the cattle trade and for meat packing.

Another inland city of importance is Birmingham, Alabama. It is the greatest steelmanufacturing city of the south. Montgomery, the capital of Alabama, is set in rich farming lands. Jackson, the capital of Mississippi, is a lumber manufacturing and railroad center. 

Tulsa and Muskogee, in Oklahoma, have grown up as a result of the rich oil deposits there. 

Oklahoma City has also grown rapidly be- I SHOULD 

1. Cotton requires a growing period of at least 200 days, and an average yearly rainfall of at least twentythree inches. 

2. The cotton belt lies in the South Atlantic and Gulf states. It extends from southeastern Virginia through 

North and South Carolina, Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi, Louisiana, Arkansas, Texas, Oklahoma, and a small part of Florida and Tennessee. 

3. The cotton belt has from 200 to over 240 days of growing weather. Most of it has from forty to sixty inches of rain, yearly. Most of the belt has light, sandy soil which is rich and easily cultivated. The finest soil is the clay loam found in the "black belt" in Alabama and Mississippi, and in the "black waxy prairie" of Texas. 

4. In the early days in the south most of the cotton was raised on plantations, or large farms, and the work was done mostly by negroes. Now much cotton is also raised on small farms. 

5. The cotton gin, a machine for separating cotton fiber from the seeds, was invented in 1793 by Eli Whitney. 

It made possible the present great cotton industry of our country. 

6. Cotton is used by people all over the world for clothing and for many other things. Cotton seeds are crushed and the oil obtained from the crushing is used in making butter and lard substitutes and in making soaps. The crushed seeds are used as food for cattle, or as a fertilizer. 

7. The United States raises over one-half the world's cotton. Most of it used to be shipped to the New 

England states, or abroad, to be manufactured. But cheap labor, nearness of raw materials, and water cause of the development of the oil fields. It is centrally located and so is a natural trading and manufacturing center, as well as the state capital.

All these things show us that the sunny southland is not only a land of cotton. It is also a land of many other growing crops, of lumber, minerals, and water power. All these resources have caused large seaports and inland trading cities to grow up and become markets for its farm products and homes for its many factories.

REMEMBER 

power for running mills have led to the growth of the textile industry in the south, and much cotton is now manufactured there. Raw and manufactured cotton are shipped to other countries from the Gulf ports of New Orleans and Galveston, and from Charleston, Wilmington, and Savannah, ports on the 

Atlantic ocean. 

8. Other crops raised in the cotton belt are corn, used as food for men and for livestock; peaches, which ripen early and are easily marketed; and pecans, peanuts, and tobacco. Sugar cane and rice are grown on the Mississippi delta and along the northern coast of the Gulf of Mexico where the land is low and level and the climate is subtropical. 

9. Oil is found in large quantities in Texas, Louisiana, and Oklahoma. Salt and sulphur are also found in 

Texas and Louisiana. The South Atlantic and Gulf states have deposits of lead, zinc, iron, and coal. 

10. The Southern states have forests of cedar, oak, pine, and cypress. Tar, pitch, and turpentine are products of the pine. Oils of pine and cypress are used in polishes. 

11. The population of the South Atlantic and Gulf states is over one-fifth of that of the United States. Texas has the largest population in this section. The number of negroes in the southern states is large, but is less than that of the whites. 

12. Pensacola and Mobile are other Gulf ports. San 

Antonio, Dallas, and Fort Worth are manufacturing and railroad centers of Texas. Tulsa, Muskogee, and Oklahoma City owe their growth to the oil industry. Jackson, Montgomery, and Birmingham are important interior cities.

FAMILIAR SCENES ON THE ATLANTIC COASTAL PLAIN 

(1) (2) Norfolk-Portsmouth Advertising Board. (3) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. (4) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. 

1. Dismal swamp. This great marsh lies partly in

Virginia and partly in North Carolina. The trees which you see growing on wide-spread roots in the water are cypresses, valuable for their tough and durable lumber. 

In the foreground are twisted roots left from cutting down other cypress trees. Parts of Dismal swamp have been reclaimed for farming by digging canals to drain off the water.

George Washington was partner in one of the first companies formed to drain this swamp. 

2. A Virginia cabbage. The grower is proud of the size and quality of this fine head. All through the 

Atlantic Coastal plain, market gardens provide fresh vegetables for the large industrial cities. 

3. Ducks on a duck farm. Raising ducks is a common industry on Long island, which is so close to New York City that the eggs and ducklings have a good sale near home. Duck farming is often profitable on land which is not rich enough to raise good crops. On this duck farm the ducks have plenty of room to swim. 

4. A grove of thriving young pine trees. Notice how tall and straight they are. Along the Atlantic coast there are many square miles of sandy soil covered with pine woods. The pine groves of the South are particularly valuable. From them are obtained turpentine and other naval stores.

ALONG THE ATLANTIC COAST 

7 

7. Ocean piers near Norfolk, Virginia. Much of the foreign trade of the state passes through these extensive docks and warehouses. The railroad tracks run directly onto the piers, and in the freight yards beyond cars are made up into trains. 

(5) (6) Keystone View Co., N. Y. (7) (8) Norfolk-Portsmouth Advertising Board. 

5. Oysters on board a New Jersey oyster boat. Oysters are an important product of the sea all along the Atlantic Coastal plain north from Chesapeake bay. This pile was dredged, or scooped up, from the beds of an oyster farm. The owners of this farm "plant" old shells in shallow water. Baby oysters attach themselves to the shells. In a few years they are fully grown, and a crop of large oysters is collected. 

6. The Boardwalk at Atlantic City, New Jersey, with the beach beyond it. On a summer's day, thousands of people may be seen walking or riding in wheel chairs on this famous promenade. This view shows several celebrated hotels and an amusement pier. 

8. Giant machine handling coal cars at Norfolk. Several railroads bring soft coal from the Appalachian highland to be exported from Norfolk. Notice that there are tracks at two levels. Cars full of coal from the mines are run onto the upper level. This machine seizes them firmly and turns them upside down, dumping the coal into the electric cars on the lower level, which carry it to the ships or storage bins.

THE ATLANTIC COASTAL PLAIN 

Along the Atlantic ocean from the northern edge of Long island through Florida is a long stretch of land called the Atlantic Coastal plain.

It was named from the ocean which it borders. 

It varies in width. From being just a narrow strip in the north, it broadens out towards the south and reaches its greatest breadth in the most southern states bordering the Atlantic. 

In Georgia, it is about 110 miles wide. From the ocean, the Coastal plain extends back to the Fall line, which is the eastern slope of the 

Piedmont plateau.

The states which lie either wholly or partly in the Atlantic Coastal plain are New York, New Jersey, Delaware, Pennsylvania, Maryland, Virginia, North and South Carolina, Georgia, and Florida.

SURFACE 

The Atlantic Coastal plain, you remember, was at one time under the ocean. Rivers flowing from the Appalachian highland to the sea kept bringing with them mud and sand which gathered on the floor of the ocean. In course of time this fertile plain was lifted above sea level and became coast land. 

The land of the Atlantic Coastal plain is generally flat and sandy. The valleys are shallow with no high divides between them. In many places along the coast are salt marshes or swamps. Dismal swamp on the border between North Carolina and Virginia is the largest of these. It is much like the Everglades, only it is smaller. It is a jungle of trees and bushes and thickets. Some of the mistletoe, holly, pine cones, and evergreens, for our 

Christmas decorations, come from this swamp.

As in the Everglades, a drainage canal has reclaimed part of the swamp for cultivation. The canal runs between Norfolk, Virginia, and 

Elizabeth City, North Carolina.

Along its northern half, the coast line of the 

Atlantic Coastal plain is broken by bays and sounds, which make it quite ir- 

COAST LINE 

regular. Part of the river-made land which was lifted above the sea sank again, and the sea water flowed back into the valleys of the rivers, forming deep bays and estuaries. 

An estuary is the wide mouth of a river in which the tide of the sea rises and falls as it does in the sea itself. Chesapeake bay is an estuary. It cuts deep into the land and with its many small bays and inlets forms excellent harbors for ships. New York bay and Delaware bay are two other estuaries of the Atlantic Coastal plain which provide good harbors for shipping.

But not all of the Atlantic coast is good for shipping and commerce. In many places offshore the water is too shallow for large ships to approach, and there is danger of their running aground on sand bars.

CLIMATE The climate of the Atlantic Coastal plain is oceanic, that is, it is mild, without the variation in temperature that is found farther inland. This mildness is due largely to the nearness of the Atlantic ocean. The water of the ocean heats and cools more slowly than the land. In winter the ocean is warmer than the land, so the breezes from the Atlantic help to warm the Coastal plain. In summer the ocean is cooler than the land, so the breezes help to lower the temperature of the nearby shores. The Gulf stream also helps in making the climate mild, as it does in Florida.

There is rainfall along the Coastal plain throughout the year.

MARKET GARDENING 

The soil of the Atlantic Coastal plain is light and sandy. Large areas of it, as we already know, will produce good cotton. 

Though wheat, corn, and grass grow better in a different kind of soil, fruits and vegetables grow well in the sandy soil of the Coastal plain when phosphate or other fertilizers are added to it. Because of this fact, market gardening has become one of the important industries of this region, and peas, beans, cabbages, melons, and strawberries are raised. As the warm weather advances 29

NORTH AMERICA 

up the coast, there is a succession of crops of these fruits and vegetables. Strawberries first ripen in Florida, and Florida berries are in the markets of New York by the last of February or the first of March. A few weeks later they are ripening in Georgia and South Carolina. 

As the weeks pass by, the crops ripen farther and farther north, until in late June the strawberries on Long island are ready for market.

During the harvesting seasons, you will find people of all ages busy gathering crops for the market. Fruits and fresh vegetables must be gathered as soon as they are ripe, and must be taken to distributing centers before they spoil. 

Auto trucks on the roads and boats on the streams carry the produce of the market gardens to nearby markets. Fast express trains carry the fresh fruits and vegetables in refrigerator cars to such buying and selling centers as New York, Philadelphia, and Baltimore.

All the fruits and vegetables are not sent to markets. Quantities of them are shipped to factories where they are canned. OYSTER 

FARMING 

Another important industry of the Atlantic 

Coastal plain is the gathering and marketing of oysters. Chesapeake bay produces more oysters than any other body of water in the world. Long 

Island sound and Delaware bay are other centers for the oyster trade. Oysters grow where the water is shallow. The fishermen go out in their boats all through the fall and winter to gather the oysters. Some are taken from the muddy bottoms by big shovels called dredging machines, which scoop up mud and oysters and bring them aboard the boats. Others are gathered from oyster farms. These are areas of shallow bay which men rent from the state to which they belong. Over the bottom of the bay the men scatter old oyster shells and tree branches. To these the baby oysters cling, and there they stay until they are gathered. Most of the oysters that are gathered are sent fresh to the markets of the east and west, but some of them are canned. 

The Atlantic Coastal plain has another profitable kind of industry—its sea shore pleasure resorts. From New

Jersey to Virginia there is a long PLEASURE 

RESORTS 

line of seacoast towns that depend upon the visitor for much of their income. One of the best known is Atlantic City. People from far and near come here the year round to enjoy the climate. It is bright and sunshiny, with fresh salt breezes from the sea. In the summer time, the beach is very gay with the striped shore umbrellas and the bright costumes of the bathers. During all seasons the board walk, which stretches like a wide road along the beach, is filled with gaily dressed people in holiday mood. Some of them walk briskly in the bracing air. Others are wheeled about in the comfortable wheel chairs. Attractive shops line the board walk, filled with tempting articles for the visitor. OTHER 

CITIES 

Since the cotton belt lies across the southern part of the Atlantic Coastal plain, we already know about several of the large cities of this region. Three cities of the central part of the Coastal plain are just south of Chesapeake bay, all clustered around the splendid harbor of Hampton 

Roads. They are Norfolk, Newport News, and 

Portsmouth. All three are connected with the interior of the country by railroad lines. Norfolk is the shipping port of Virginia. Cotton, oysters and fish from Chesapeake bay, and early fruits and vegetables are shipped from 

Norfolk by train and boat. Norfolk receives nitrate from South America and wood pulp and potash from Europe. Newport News has a large shipbuilding industry. Steamship lines run from both these cities to New York and other American ports as well as to those of Europe.

In New Jersey are some of the larger and more important cities of the Atlantic Coastal plain. Newark is a great industrial city, producing machinery, paints, and leather. Jersey

City, close by, packs meats and makes goods of many kinds. Her waterfront is part of the great port of New York. Paterson manufactures beautiful silk goods. Camden is near the western border of the Coastal plain.

Here large numbers of phonographs and radios are made. This city is also noted for its great shipyards.

Much of the eastern part of New Jersey is

THE ATLANTIC COASTAL PLAIN 

31 still forest land. One may go for miles without seeing a house or a settlement. Probably in time the pine trees will be cleared away, as they have been in so many places on the plain, and the sandy soil will be fertilized for more truck gardening.

1. The Public Library, Philadelphia, seen from one of the city's many parks. Philadelphia is the largest city of the Fall line, and one of the oldest. splendid public buildings, and the Public Library is one of the finest of these. The fountain adds to the beauty of the scene. In the background is one of Philadelphia's many factories.
