INTERESTING SIGHTS ALONG THE FALL LINE 

པའི (6) Ewing Galloway, N. Y. (7) Keystone View Co., N. Y. (8) Keystone-Underwood, N. Y. (9) J. A. Glenn, Albany, N. Y. 

6. The falls of the Passaic river at Paterson, New Jersey.

This picture shows us how the Fall line gets its name. 

The cataracts along the Fall line provide a great deal of water power for manufacturing. Before steam engines became important, factory towns were built near the falls. River boats which traveled upstream as far as the falls brought trade to these towns and helped many of them to grow into cities. 

7. Making money in the Philadelphia mint. Into this heavy machine round silver blanks, or disks, are fed from the left. The machine stamps each blank with a design which shows its value as money. The stamped coins drop into a box from the spout at the right. 

8. A scene in a Fall line peach cannery. The workers in the background are peeling the peaches and removing their stones. The others are packing the peaches into cans. Many canneries are very large and employ hundreds of workers. Notice how clean everything in this cannery is kept. 

9. A potter at work. Beside this skilful worker is a pile of clay. He places lumps of the clay on the wheel before him. The wheel spins rapidly, and by pressure of his hands and of the wooden paddle which he is holding up he shapes the clay into flower pots. Trenton,

New Jersey, is one of our country's chief centers for the manufacture of pottery.

THE FALL LINE West of the Atlantic Coastal plain stretches the Piedmont plateau, a region of low, rolling hills. The plateau rises from the Coastal plain, gently in some places, sharply in others. For thousands of years many rivers, on their way to the sea, have flowed eastward across the plateau and plunged in falls and rapids to the plain below. The escarpment, or slope, between the Piedmont plateau and the Coastal plain is known as the Fall line.

WHY THE CITIES HAVE 

GROWN The Fall line extends south from New York through New Jersey, Pennsylvania, Maryland, Virginia, North and South Carolina, and Georgia, into Ala- In each state through bama. which it passes there is at least one busy Fall line city. There are several reasons why this is so. The falls along the Fall line supply water power for manufacturing. 

Factories which have grown up near the falls are also near the railroads of the low, flat 

Coastal plain. Ships can travel up some of the rivers as far as the Fall line, but there the rapids stop them. Cities naturally grew up at these "heads of navigation."

West of the Fall line are lands rich in iron and coal. These and other raw materials can easily be shipped to the Fall line cities by canal, river, or railroad. So it is easy to understand why the Fall line cities are large and prosperous and carry on many different industries. 

In some places canals have been built around the falls or rapids in the rivers, to aid navigation. These canals have locks by means of which a boat can pass from one water level to another. Usually more than one lock is needed to raise or lower a boat over the Fall line, and where there are several of them they are built like a flight of steps.

When a boat enters a lock, great steel gates close tightly behind it, and it seems to be in a very large box. If the boat is going upstream, water is let into the lock until the vessel is raised to the level of the water in the next lock. 

Then the upper gates are opened, the boat passes into the second lock, the gates are closed again, and the boat is raised another step. 

Thus, gradually, it reaches the level of the river above the falls. When the upper gates of the last lock are opened the boat passes out of the lock and on up the river. If the boat is going downstream, the water in one lock after another is lowered gradually until the boat reaches the level of the river below the falls, and when it leaves the last lock the boat can continue its journey to the ocean. 

With your finger trace the Fall line on the map on page 32. The Fall line passes close to 

Trenton and Philadelphia, both on the Delaware river. Trenton is at the head of navigation on the Delaware. Philadelphia is located where the Delaware is joined by the Schuylkill, one of its western branches. These two cities are among the most interesting and best developed on the Fall line. Railroads connect them with all parts of the country. 

TRENTON 

Trenton is the capital of the state of New Jersey, and is famous in history as the scene of an important battle of the Revolutionary War. Clay is found in quantities near Trenton. From it, rough pottery, bricks, and a building material called terra cotta are made. Materials for making china, fine porcelain, and pottery come from nearby points. Trenton manufactures more pottery than any other United States city.

Philadelphia has grown up between the falls of the Schuylkill and the point where this river flows into the Delaware. Over 

2,000,000 people live and work

PHILADELPHIA 

in Philadelphia. It is the third city in population and manufacturing in the United States, and it is the largest city of the Fall line. Although over 100 miles from the Atlantic ocean, Philadelphia is a great seaport as well as a river port. This is because the estuary of the 

Delaware is large and deep enough to allow ocean steamers to sail up the river to the city. 33

NORTH AMERICA 

Because Philadelphia is both a railroad and shipping center, raw materials of all kinds are brought into the city to be made into useful articles. Here come sugar from Cuba; wool, hides and skins from South America and Asia; and wood pulp and paper from Europe. The sugar is refined, and the hides are tanned to make leather. Iron and steel goods, including tools, machine parts, and locomotives, are manufactured here, as well as clothing, cotton, and worsted goods. Worsted is the name given to yarn made by twisting long wool fibers together. The yarn is used in making rugs, for which Philadelphia is famous. Shipbuilding is another important industry. The city also has fine publishing houses and printing shops. Benjamin Franklin, an early and very famous American, had a printing business in Philadelphia. 

From 1790 to 1800 Philadelphia was the capital city of the United States. During this time, the first United States mint, or factory for making money, was founded here. The mint makes copper, nickel, gold, and silver coins. Visitors, accompanied by guides, may see how this very interesting work is done. In the mint are large vaults, rooms with strong, thick walls and doors. These vaults contain stacks of gold and silver bars, and quantities of finished money tied up in bags.

We have no coins made of pure gold, silver, copper or nickel. In the making of coins from these metals other metals are melted with them. The melted metal is run into a machine from which it comes out in thin bands. 

These bands are cut into round pieces by a steel punch, a tool with sharp, hard edges. The pieces differ in value according to the kind and amount of metal they contain. 

The money value of each coin is stamped on it. When the designs are stamped on the round pieces they become coins, or money. 

Philadelphia is one of the most famous of early American cities. In Independence Hall, a very old red brick building which still stands, the Declaration of Independence was adopted, July 4, 1776, and from the tower the Liberty bell rang out the glad news. From that day the United States of America has been a free and independent nation.

A little south of Philadelphia, on the Delaware river, you find Chester, Pennsylvania, and Wilmington, Delaware. 

These two cities, though very much smaller than Philadel- 

CHESTER WILMINGTON 

phia, have similar advantages and so their industries are much the same. Both are shipbuilding centers. They also make heavy machinery. Wilmington is the headquarters of a great paint-making industry, and here, also, two explosives, dynamite and gunpowder, are made.

BALTIMORE 

Baltimore is located on a tidewater river which empties into Chesapeake bay. It is a great shipbuilding and trading center, and manufactures clothing and tobacco. Fresh vegetables and fruits raised in the market gardens of the Atlantic

Coastal plain are sent to the canning factories in Baltimore. Canning is one of the city's chief industries.

One of the earliest railroads built in America was the Baltimore and Ohio, which stretched from Baltimore as far west as Wheeling, West Virginia. Later it was extended through Ohio to the west, and became one of the great railways connecting the east and west.

As Baltimore is on a tidewater river, it, like Philadelphia, is both an ocean and a river port. Baltimore is a shipping center for cities and districts near and far. Some of the goods brought here go north or south to Atlantic ports of our own country. Others go by direct steamship lines to the ports of other continents. 

The harbor at Baltimore is wide and deep enough to receive very large boats. Vessels bring to Baltimore petroleum and gasoline from the Gulf ports, copper from Europe and 

Mexico, coffee and nitrates from South America, wood pulp from Europe, sugar from Cuba, Porto Rico, and the Philippine islands, and rubber from Asia. They take away in return coal, wheat, tobacco, canned goods, iron and steel goods, cloth, and dyestuffs for coloring. 

Baltimore is the second largest city of the

Fall line. Its population is over 800,000. Johns 

Hopkins University, a splendid educational institution, is located here,

THE FALL LINE 

35 WASHINGTON 

Just an hour's ride by fast express train from 

Baltimore is the beautiful city of Washington, the capital of the United States. 

If you like traveling by water, you could sail down the Chesapeake bay to the Potomac river, then up the Potomac to Washington.

Washington is the only city in the country which is not in a state. It is in the District of 

Columbia, a small area of land lying between the states of Virginia and Maryland. The 

District of Columbia is owned and governed by the United States government. Because of this it is free from the laws of any one state. 

Representatives from all the states meet in Washington to make new laws for our country, and to change old ones.

About 500,000 people make their homes in 

Washington, and thousands of visitors go there every year to see its many places of interest. 

You could not be in Washington very long without catching sight of the white dome of the great Capitol building. How it shines in the sun! At night, it is lighted by searchlights and can be seen from nearly every part of the city and from many points outside. The great work of legislation, or law-making, is carried on in this building. Its white walls, majestic columns, wide steps, and heavy, bronze doors express the dignity, beauty, and strength of our great nation. The Capitol is surrounded by well-kept parks. Long avenues, or streets, extend from it in all directions like the spokes of a wheel. Washington is famous for the beautiful views along these avenues. 

Opposite the Capitol is the Library of Con gress. This, too, has a dome a golden one. 

In the building are many famous historical papers, among them the Declaration of Independence. These papers are framed or are in glass cases, and you may go in and look at them. Here are valuable and rare books on law, history, geography, and many other subjects. There is a whole room filled with unusual maps of every country. 

In Washington, the United States paper money, called paper currency, is made. The work is done in the United States Bureau of Engraving and Printing, another large government building. Here, also, our postage stamps are printed. 

If you ride through the city you will see many other fine buildings. There is Continental Memorial Hall, the headquarters of the 

Daughters of the American Revolution. Near it stands the Pan American building, where members of the Pan American Union hold their meetings. Pan American means All-American, and this Union was formed to bring about a better understanding between the countries of North and South America. At its headquarters one may obtain information about these countries. You will see also, on a trip through Washington, the splendid white marble Union station. A section of the station is reserved for the exclusive use of the President of the United States. Perhaps you will enjoy a visit to the United States Treasury building. Here are kept records of all government money received from taxes, or paid out for any government expense. Officers in this building oversee the running of all national banks in the country. From the Treasury building all United States paper money is issued, and here old, worn-out bills are sent to be exchanged and destroyed. You will see the

White House, our President's home, with its sloping lawns and lovely gardens. You will see the fine houses where representatives from other countries live while they are in Washington.

The Washington monument is over 500 feet high. Its name tells you that it was built in honor of our first President. Visitors may reach the top of it by elevator, or by a stairway. From it they can see far beyond the city in all directions.

An The Lincoln memorial is among the newest of the national shrines, or monuments. old negro guide who was showing visitors about the city said, "People will tell you that the Lincoln memorial is the most beautiful thing in Washington, but I am quite sure it is the most beautiful thing in the world." This expresses the feeling of many Americans for this building which honors one of our country's greatest men.

NORTH AMERICA 

At Arlington, across the Potomac from Washington, is our country's most beautiful National cemetery. It contains the graves of hundreds of American soldiers and sailors and of many great statesmen. Among its most famous monuments is the recently erected tomb of an unknown American soldier who was killed in France during the World War. The tomb is a memorial to all soldiers who served their country in this war. 

Some years ago, an Englishman, James

Smithson, left a large amount of money to the United States, to be used for educational purposes. With this money the Smithsonian institution was founded at Washington. Connected with it is the National museum containing hundreds of strange and interesting things. Among them are small models, built in detail, of real United States steamships.

Clay figures have been made to show how faraway peoples live and dress. Models show the life of the early American Indians. There are small models of machinery, such as the cotton manufacturing machines. From these we may learn how the real industry is carried 

Colonel Charles Lindbergh's famous plane, the "Spirit of St. Louis," in which he flew across the Atlantic, is now in the museum. on.

Besides its museums, art galleries, government buildings, and splendid homes, Washington has excellent schools and universities. 

Washington is on a tidewater river, and large vessels can sail up the Potomac to her docks. A short distance northwest of the city are the Great falls of the Potomac. Washington differs from other Fall line cities, however, in that she does not use the water power of her nearby falls for manufacturing. Her chief business is the governing of our great nation. For this work factories are not needed. Her fine, white buildings, lovely gardens, parks, streets, and monuments are kept free from much of the dust and smoke which would be caused by the running of great factories or mills. 

However, the city has a large business district and splendid stores to supply its needs. All about Washington lie fertile farm lands which send fresh fruits and vegetables to the city.

Just a few miles down the Potomac from Washington is the beautiful estate of Mount 

Vernon where George Washington used to live. Here his home still stands, furnished much as it was in his lifetime.

MOUNT VERNON RICHMOND 

South of Washington, in Virginia, is Richmond. This city is at the head of navigation on the James river, and is in the midst of a great tobacco-growing region. It has many tobacco factories. Its port is not large enough for large ships, but it is a trading center for small vessels and is connected by railroad with the coast and inland cities.

COLUMBIA, MACON, RALEIGH 

Raleigh, the capital city of North Carolina, is one of the smaller Fall line cities. Cotton, phosphate fertilizers, and tools are made there. Columbia in South Carolina, and Macon and Augusta, in Georgia, are other Fall line cities. As their states lie partly in the cotton belt, these cities manufacture the chief product of that region cotton.

 1. The Piedmont plateau rises from the Atlantic Coastal plain, gently in some places, sharply in others. The slope, or escarpment, between these two regions is called the Fall line. Here rivers which flow east across the plateau drop to the plain in rapids or waterfalls. Boats which sail up the rivers must stop at these falls, except where canals have been built around them. 

2. Cities have grown up along the Fall line at the "heads of navigation." Waterfalls along the Fall line supply these cities with water power. Several cities 

AND AUGUSTA 

of the Fall line are on tidewater rivers, which have estuaries deep enough to allow large vessels to reach their docks. 

3. Philadelphia and Baltimore are great seaports as well as river ports. They are connected by railroad with other parts of the country and by steamship lines with ports of all continents. 

4. Philadelphia is noted for its manufacture of locomotives, its shipbuilding, and its sugar refineries. Among its imports are sugar, wool, and hides.

37 5. Baltimore has large canning factories, due to its nearness to the market gardens of the Atlantic Coastal plain. Baltimore imports copper, coffee, sugar, and nitrate. 

6. Washington, the capital city of the United States, is the only city of the country which is not in a state. 

It is in the District of Columbia. Its chief occupations have to do with the government of our country. 

The Capitol building, the White House, the many fine government buildings, and the beautiful memorials are some of the attractions which draw many visitors to Washington each year. 

7. Trenton in New Jersey, Wilmington in Delaware, Chester in Pennsylvania, Richmond in Virginia, Raleigh in North Carolina, Columbia in South Carolina, and Macon and Augusta in Georgia are other cities of the Fall line. The more northerly cities manufacture machinery and other goods. Those of the 

South Atlantic states specialize in the manufacture of cotton.

COUNTRY SCENES ON THE PIEDMONT PLATEAU 

1. Where plateau and highland meet. Here we are looking across the Piedmont plateau in Virginia to the Blue Ridge mountains, which form the eastern boundary of the Appalachian highland. Much of the Piedmont region is rolling land, well suited to mixed farming. See the large orchard at the left of the picture. High grade apples and peaches are grown in this region. 

2. Husking corn in a North Carolina cornfield. In the great fields of the Central plains corn-husking is done by machinery. In the smaller fields of the east, however, husking is usually done by hand. Often the whole family helps in this work. 

3. A field of high grade tobacco on the Piedmont plateau. Tobacco grows well in most parts of the plateau and is an important market crop. The plants in this field are about ready to cut. After the leaves have wilted, they will be hung in buildings to dry and cure. 

4. An old mill beside a Georgia stream. Many mills like that in this picture are still found on the smaller streams in the Piedmont. They are used to grind grain or to saw timbers for the farmers who live nearby. The large waterwheel which runs the machinery in this mill is called an "overshot" wheel, because the water strikes the wheel at the top and flows over it.

ON THE PIEDMONT PLATEAU 

​6 (5) (6) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. (7) Keystone View Co., N. Y. 

5. An air view of the business section of Atlanta, Georgia. Atlanta is the capital of Georgia and the leading city of the Piedmont plateau. It is called the "Gate City" because railroads pass through it from every direction, connecting the south with the north, east, and west. 

These railroads bring raw materials to the city's factories and take away its many manufactured products. Because of its location and its railroads, Atlanta is a great commercial and manufacturing center and is growing rapidly. 

6. On a Piedmont farm. The southern part of the 

Piedmont plateau lies in the cotton belt, and much cotton is raised here. The man in this picture is dusting the cotton plants with a poisonous powder to kill the boll weevils and other insect pests which are a constant danger to the crop. 

7. Spinning machines in a cotton mill. These machines twist coarse cotton thread into finer finished thread ready for weaving into cloth. The number of cotton mills in the cotton-belt section of the Piedmont plateau is steadily increasing. The abundant supply of raw cotton from the fields nearby and the nearness of mountain streams which can be used to produce electric power help to make the manufacture of cotton profitable. The South is beginning to be important in turning its own cotton into cloth.

THE PIEDMONT PLATEAU 

Suppose you were to take a journey westward from the Fall line to the Blue Ridge mountains. In every direction you would see low, rolling hills and pleasant little valleys. 

These hills have been so worn down that their slopes are gently rounded, and in parts the land is nearly level. This region is called the Piedmont plateau. Piedmont means "foothills," and this plateau forms the eastern foothills of the 

Appalachian highland.

The Piedmont plateau extends through most of the New England states, New York, New 

Jersey, Pennsylvania, Maryland, Virginia, North and South Carolina, into Georgia and

Alabama. It grows wider toward the south. 

The highest parts of the plateau are not over 

1000 feet above sea level.

SOIL AND 

CLIMATE 

The soil of the Piedmont is rich, wellwatered, and suitable for raising many kinds of crops. Most of this soil is not so light, nor so sandy, as that of the 

Atlantic Coastal plain, but nevertheless it produces some of the same varieties of fruits and vegetables. Because it is higher, the plateau is a little cooler than the plain, and it has a little less rainfall, especially in the western part.

MIXED 

FARMING 

With the Piedmont's many natural advantages, its farmers manage to keep busy all year round. They engage chiefly in mixed farming. Among the products which they raise are fruits, tobacco, wheat, corn and rye. They grow many vegetables, too, for their own use. These various crops require different planting seasons, and different soil conditions demand different fertilizers. The harvest season for one crop may sometimes be the planting season for another. There is little time on a Piedmont farm when there is not some work to be done. 

While several crops are usually raised on a 

Piedmont farm, there is generally one main crop-the commercial, or market, crop. The smaller crops are largely for the farmer's own needs, or are such as can be sold nearby. As you would expect, cotton is the main crop of the southern section of the Piedmont. Farther north on the plateau very different crops are raised extensively.

In many parts of Maryland, and especially in northern Virginia, large, fine apples are grown. Apples raised on the Piedmont are shipped to many parts of our own country, as well as to other lands. APPLES In sections of southeastern Pennsylvania, and throughout Maryland, southern Virginia, and North and South Carolina, to 

TOBACCO 

bacco is an important market crop. 

If you ride through these sections, you will see many fields of tobacco, and you will smell its heavy fragrance. Tobacco leaves are wide and shiny, and grow on thick stalks. Tobacco takes a great deal of plant food from the soil, and plots of ground where it is planted must be thoroughly refertilized before tobacco or any other crop can again be grown there. In parts of the Piedmont you will see many acres of barren land which were once tobacco fields. 

The early colonists learned from the Indians how to raise and use tobacco. At first tobacco was shipped to England but later a tobacco trade with other countries sprang up. Today the tobacco crop in this country is over a billion pounds yearly, and has a tremendous commercial value.

CORN AND 

WHEAT 

Corn and wheat, too, are raised in Maryland and southeastern Pennsylvania, but neither is a really great commercial crop.

The corn is used largely as food for cattle and hogs. In some sections much sweet corn is grown and canned. 

During the planting and harvesting seasons, and after they are over, there are always the dairy cattle to be cared for. Dairying is a thriving industry of the northern part of the Piedmont. The rich grassy slopes provide fine pastures, and dairy products are needed in the busy cities of the Fall 

DAIRYING 

39

NORTH AMERICA 

line, as well as by the farmers themselves.

LIFE ON THE PLATEAU 

How pleasant is this country of the Piedmont farms! There are broad fields and meadows and sturdy orchards. Fields, planted with many different crops, vary in color and lie like a great patchwork quilt over the hills. There are many small farm houses, neat and white and cheery, nestled in the valleys. There are great barns back of them, often much larger than the houses themselves. Here hay is stored and the cattle are kept warm during the winter months. Some farm products, which do not need to be sent to market at once, are also stored in the barns.

Other homes of the plateau are large and stately, with sloping lawns and great, old trees. These houses, too, have barns and outbuildings, and all around them the fields are well cultivated. People who live in the Piedmont are surely thrifty and hardworking, and they have a right to be proud of their comfortable homes.

There are fewer cities on the Piedmont than on the Atlantic Coastal plain or along the Fall

THE GROWTH OF CITIES 

line. Let us see why. The Blue 

Ridge mountains, along the western boundary of the Piedmont, are a barrier to travel. You will remember, too, that where there are no canals, ships must stop at the Fall line, the eastern boundary of the Piedmont. When it is hard to get to and from a region, raw materials cannot be gotten quickly, or conveniently. So manufacturing and commerce, on which the growth of large cities depends, do not thrive in this section. 

ELECTRIC POWER 

But water power, everywhere plentiful along the Piedmont belt, is offsetting some of the disadvantages of location and poor transportation. Especially along the mountains of North and South

Carolina, the power from streams is now being made into electricity in great hydroelectric plants. The electricity is used to light homes and run electric car lines, as well as to operate ice plants, furniture factories, and cotton mills. 

With the increasing industries, population in the cities of the Piedmont is gradually growing. 

Electric power is sometimes obtained from steam-driven machines, in sections where coal can be had from nearby. Electric power lines connect many of the steam and hydroelectric plants along the Piedmont. This means that if the electricity from one plant fails, an electric current from another plant will keep the factories running. 

Have you seen articles made from a bright metal called aluminum? Aluminum has a bluish, silvery color. It is obtained ALUMINUM 

from bauxite, or aluminum ore.

Bauxite is a clay-like material which is found in parts of the Appalachian highland, and in the Interior highlands. Plants on the Piedmont plateau extract aluminum metal from the bauxite by electricity. The metal is then shipped to factories where it is used in the manufacture of many articles. Aluminum conducts heat so well that it is used for cooking utensils. As it is also very light and strong, it is used in airplane, automobile, and boat parts where lightness and strength are needed.

ATLANTA 

Far south on the Piedmont we find the big city of Atlanta, Georgia. It is called the "Gate 

City" of the south. Railroads stretch from it in all directions, much as the streets in Washington stretch from the Capitol building. Raw materials may easily reach the city from many places. Minerals are brought from the mountains on the west. From districts near the city, lumber is brought and made into fine furniture. Cotton products are manufactured and sugar is refined there. Many, many people work and have their homes in the Gate City.

During recent years part of the power for running machinery in Atlanta has been obtained from the Chattahoochee river, about fifteen miles outside the city. 

Atlanta is growing very fast. Smaller cities are growing up around it, and its commercial and business importance is increasing. 

Among other cities of the Piedmont is 

Greensboro, in North Carolina, a cotton manufacturing city. Here was built one of the first cotton factories in the south. A little

Charlotte and Winston-Salem, in North 

Carolina, are important cities. Charlotte manufactures much cotton, and Winston-Salem is a great tobacco center.

Charlottesville, in Virginia, is a trade center for the Piedmont. Among its manufactures are woolen goods, tobacco, and agricultural implements. 2. The soil of the Piedmont is well suited to various kinds of agriculture. The climate is cooler than that of the Atlantic Coastal plain, and there is less rainfall, especially in the west.

Dairying is carried on in the northern Piedmont because the pastures are fine, and the dairy products can easily be sold to the cities nearby.

EXERCISE

1. Harpers Ferry, Virginia, where the Potomac river cuts through the mountains. Highway, railroad and canal follow this river gap.

Monongahela rivers join to form the Ohio. Across the river is the main business section, on the Monongahela river. Set in the midst of a vast coal region, and within reach of rich iron mines, Pittsburgh is one of the world's great steel centers. 

3. Mining anthracite, or hard, coal in eastern Pennsylvania. These miners are working in a tunnel more than a thousand feet below the surface of the earth. 

4. Workers in a bituminous, or soft, coal mine in Pennsylvania. The machine used is a coal cutter. Metal teeth on the bar extending from the right-hand corner of the machine eat into the coal, cutting a deep gash. 

It is then easy to loosen the overhanging coal. 

5. The world's largest anthracite coal breaker, located in eastern Pennsylvania. Cars carry coal from the mine to the building at the left, where the coal is emptied into hoppers, or huge buckets, below the ground. The hoppers are attached to a belt which moves up the inclined plane to the breaker on the right. Here the coal is cleaned, broken up, and sorted according to size. 

The large pile beyond the breaker is waste material. NASA-3

THE APPALACHIAN HIGHLAND-A MINERAL STOREHOUSE

(6) (9) Birmingham Chamber of Commerce. (7) (8) Bethlehem Steel Co. 

6. Birmingham, Alabama. This city on the southern edge of the rich mineral-laden Appalachian highland, with the cotton belt near at hand, has greater advantages for manufacturing than have its southern neighbors of the Coastal plain. Though a comparatively young city, it has fast grown to be an important one because coal, iron, and limestone are found close by. 

7. One of the huge blast furnaces at Bethlehem, Pennsylvania. Iron ore, coke, and limestone are sent up the steep incline at the left of the building and dumped into the top of the furnace. After the fire is once lighted it is kept continually burning by forcing hot compressed air through it. When the iron is melted it is run off into ladles on cars in the openings below the furnace. 

The slag, or impurities, may be seen running out of the pipe at the right of the openings. The hot gases from the furnace are used in the pipe-like stoves at the right to heat the compressed air. 

8. A white hot steel ingot, or bar, being passed between rollers to press it into the form desired. When the ingot has passed, water is sprayed on the rollers to cool them. 

9. A limestone quarry at Birmingham, Alabama. Two blasts have just been shot off, loosening tons of rock. 

The cars in the foreground, loaded with rock, are on their way to the iron works.

THE APPALACHIAN HIGHLAND 

The rolling hills of the Piedmont plateau end abruptly in the steep slopes of the Appalachian highland to the west. This highland region is made up of several mountain ranges, the Great valley, and the Allegheny-Cumberland plateau.

The Appalachian mountains are named after a tribe of Indians, the Appalaches. The Ap- 

THE APPALACHIANS 

palachians are very old mountains, much older than the mountains in the west. They extend from north of the Canadian border of northeastern United States to the middle of Alabama, and include all or part of Maine, Vermont, New Hampshire, Massachusetts, New York, Pennsylvania, Maryland, West 

Virginia, Virginia, Ohio, Kentucky, Tennessee,

North and South Carolina, Georgia, and Alabama. 

RIVER 

GAPS The Appalachian mountains with their steep, wooded sides would be difficult to cross were it not for the gaps which the rivers have worn through them. Harpers Ferry, where the Potomac river cuts through the mountains on its way to Chesapeake bay, is such a gap. There are other gaps where the Susquehanna and the Roanoke rivers break through, making natural highways for trade and travel. In New York state is the gap made by the Mohawk river. The railroads take advantage of these openings and pass through them, connecting the cities of the east and west.

THE BLUE RIDGE MOUNTAINS 

The Blue Ridge mountains, an eastern range of the Appalachians, get their name from the fact that at a distance they seem a deep blue color. Nearer by, they are seen to be green with forests. The tops of these mountains do not rise in peaks but are rounded. Nowhere do they rise above the timber line. The timber line is the line above which the air is too cold for trees to grow. 

From Carlisle, Pennsylvania, through Maryland, the Blue Ridge is a single, low ridge called South mountain. It gradually becomes higher and wider as it extends south, until, in 

North Carolina, it broadens into several ranges.

Among these are the Unakas and Great Smokies of North Carolina and Tennessee and the ranges which enclose the Asheville basin. The city of Asheville in North Carolina is 2300 feet above the sea. Its high, dry climate and beautiful scenery make it one of the most healthful and popular resorts in the south. The highest point east of the Mississippi river is the summit of Mount Mitchell, in North Carolina, 6711 feet above sea level. VALLEYS

AND 

RIDGES 

Just west of the Blue Ridge mountains are long, low, nearly parallel mountain ridges extending northeast and southwest. 

There are valleys between these ridges. Some of the valleys are broad, with rich, fertile soil formed from limestone. Others are narrow, with no room for farms. The Lebanon-Cumberland valley in Pennsylvania, the Shenandoah valley in Virginia, and the Knoxville-Chattanooga valley in Tennessee are the greatest of these valleys. They are grouped under the name 

Great valley.

The Great valley, stretching so many miles, with highlands running lengthwise through it but none across it, has been a route of travel from early days. There are gaps through the ridges the Delaware Water gap, the Lehigh 

Valley gap, and the Cumberland gap. Roads and railroads cut through these gaps on their way east and west. Transportation in the north is across the valleys and through the gaps, while in the south it is chiefly lengthwise of the valleys.

The Shenandoah valley, drained by the Shenandoah river, is very beautiful and fertile. 

With its mild and pleasant climate, it is an attractive place to live. There are farms throughout its length which produce wheat and corn and hay. There are herds of cattle grazing in 43

NORTH AMERICA 

the pastures. Rows upon rows of apple trees in the valley and on the hillsides are pink with apple blossoms in the spring. This is a rich and prosperous country.

The Shenandoah valley has long been a popular route of travel. Winchester, Virginia, at the northern end of the valley, and Staunton, Virginia, at the southern end, are the main trading centers.

To the west of the Great valley the mountain ridges are high and steep, with narrow valleys between them. Here are few people and few farms. In Virginia and Maryland there are apple and peach orchards on some of the steep slopes. Some lumbering, too, is carried on, but as a good many forest fires have burned away the trees on the hillsides it is not an important industry. 

West of the last ridge, called the Allegheny front, is the low, hilly Allegheny-Cumberland plateau. It extends from the center of New York state southwest into Alabama. On the west, it slopes into the 

THE ALLEGHENY. 

CUMBERLAND

PLATEAU 

Central plains.

The Allegheny-Cumberland plateau was once a mass of fairly level sandstone, but the rock has been worn down where the streams have cut into it, making deep valleys and steep hillsides. In West Virginia and Kentucky, the valleys are especially deep and the hills are especially high. Here and to the south much of the land is heavily wooded, and travel and trade are very difficult except along the streams.

The few people who live on the mountain farms of this region find it very hard to make a living. Agricultural schools are teaching them good methods of raising goats and sheep. The steep mountain slopes are better suited to grazing than to raising crops.

LIMESTONE 

Limestone, which is found all through the Appalachian region, is useful for other things beside making fertile soil. It is used in making cement. We see cement all around us, in our roads and in our buildings. Much of this comes from eastern 

Pennsylvania. It is manufactured especially in Allentown and Bethlehem,

Limestone is used, also, in the manufacture of iron and steel. It is put into the furnaces with coke and iron ore to help separate the iron from the ore. This process is called smelting. Bethlehem has become a center for the iron and steel industry, as well as for making cement.

In the eastern part of Pennsylvania, on the highlands between the Delaware and the

Susquehanna rivers, are beds of anthracite, or hard, coal. Most of the HARD hard coal that is used in the United States comes from these beds. COAL

Perhaps you would be interested to know a little about how coal was made. Ages and ages ago, trees and plants died and became covered with water in the swampy places where they grew. Gradually earth covered them up, and new trees and plants grew over them. These in turn died and were covered by earth. This process went on for longer than we could possibly imagine. Finally a sinking of the land buried this vast amount of vegetable matter deep under clay and rock material. The weight above pressed it into solid form. After thousands of years it became bituminous, or soft, coal. Where the pressure was greatest or lasted longest, anthracite, or hard, coal was formed. Anthracite coal is used for heating. It does

THE APPALACHIAN HIGHLAND 

45 not catch fire quickly, but it burns with very little smoke and gives intense heat.

In mining hard coal, shafts, like deep wells, are dug, leading down into the coal beds. From these shafts, long, narrow passages

MINING 

are dug along the coal layers. There are always difficulties in mining the coal. The roofs of the passages may cave in, if they are not carefully built. Any water which flows in must be pumped out. The air must be kept fresh for the miners who are working so far underground.

When the coal is brought up out of the mines, it is taken to buildings, called breakers, where it is broken into pieces, sorted into sizes, and the rock which is mixed with the coal is taken out.

SOFT COAL 

Vast beds of bituminous, or soft, coal are mined in the plateau sections of western Pennsylvania, West Virginia, and Alabama, in easy reach of many cities. Soft coal is used largely in steam plants and smelters. It is easier to mine than hard coal because it lies nearer the surface of the ground and there are not so many difficulties to overcome in getting it out of the rock. 

Petroleum and natural gas are found together in the soft coal sections of the plateau. OIL The oil is carried in pipes from the wells to central points for storage or for delivery to the refineries. Tank steamers carry oil across the ocean to foreign countries. 

PITTSBURGH CHARLESTON 

The great industries and the large and increasing population of the northern and central parts of the Allegheny- 

Cumberland plateau are due to its mineral resources. Pittsburgh, in Pennsylvania, Charleston, the capital of West Virginia, and other large industrial centers are located in this region. 

Pittsburgh has a fine location at the point where the Monongahela and Allegheny rivers join to form the Ohio river. It has the double advantage of being able to ship products on the many railroads which center there, and on barges down the Ohio river. Pittsburgh is the greatest center for steel and iron manufacturing in the world. It is also a center for making glass. Coal and iron and its favorable location for shipping first made Pittsburgh grow.

Today, most of the iron ore comes from the states around Lake Superior — Minnesota,

Wisconsin, and Michigan. The ore is shipped by boat down the Great lakes to Lake Erie and then by train to Pittsburgh and other manufacturing cities.

OTHER 

The Pennsylvania cities of Scranton and 

Wilkes-Barre are the two largest coal-mining centers in the Appalachians. With coal at hand to furnish power for their CITIES factories, they have become important manufacturing cities as well. Scranton is a busy railroad center. It has a large trade in coal and mining supplies and in iron and steel products. It also manufactures silk and many other things. Reading and Bethlehem, too, have become important centers for manufacturing because of their nearness to the coal fields. 

Birmingham, Alabama, at the southern end of the Great valley, is a very important industrial center. It is situated in the midst of a rich iron, coal, and limestone region. It is the only place that has right at hand all these three raw materials needed to make steel. 

Naturally, the city has grown to be a leader in the manufacture of iron and steel. Another raw material, cotton, is also found close at hand, and cotton goods and cotton seed oil are manufactured in Birmingham. 

In Tennessee are Knoxville and Chattanooga. They are both manufacturing cities and trading centers. Knoxville manufactures iron goods and cotton and wood products. 

Nearby are marble quarries which make the city a shipping center for building stone. 

Chattanooga has cotton mills, sawmills, and iron works.

NATURAL WONDERS 

In the Appalachian highland are some of the natural wonders of our country. Caverns and natural bridges are found in the limestone regions. Natural bridge, over Cedar creek, in the western part of Virginia, is a natural rock formation ninety feet long and from fifty to a hundred feet wide. It is 215 feet above the stream. 

Over it runs a public highway. Cedar creek once flowed through a cavern with limestone

NORTH AMERICA 

walls. The bridge is all that remains of the roof of the cave. 

Luray cavern in Virginia attracts a great many visitors. It is a series of caves from the roofs and floors of which project icicle-like forms of limestone. Some of these are hollow and sound like bells when they are struck. 

Another series of wonderful underground passages that the visitor to Virginia enjoys seeing is called Endless caverns. All through this region are such caverns made by water which has worn away the soft limestone. 

1. The Appalachian highland is a great belt of land lying between the Piedmont plateau and the Central plains. 

2. The Appalachian mountains are old and worn down. 

They are a barrier to travel between the east and the west, except where the rivers have made gaps through the ridges. The eastern range of the Appalachians is known as the Blue Ridge. 

3. The Great valley is an area of valleys and ridges west of the Blue Ridge. The valleys form natural highways from north to south. One of the most important of these is the Shenandoah valley, a rich farming district. 

4. The Allegheny-Cumberland plateau extends from the center of New York state southwest into Alabama.

Petroleum, natural gas, and bituminous coal are its chief resources. Pittsburgh and Charleston are important cities of the plateau. Pittsburgh is the leading iron and steel manufacturing city of the world. 

5. The Appalachian highland is rich in deposits of coal, iron, and limestone. These resources have caused the growth of important industrial cities among which are Birmingham, a steel city in Alabama; Wilkes- 

Barre and Scranton, coal-mining centers, and Allentown and Bethlehem, cement centers, in Pennsylvania. 

Reading, in Pennsylvania, and Knoxville and Chattanooga, in Tennessee, are manufacturing cities. 

6. The Appalachian highland has natural wonders, including Natural bridge, in Virginia, and many beautiful limestone caverns. One is the famous Luray cavern.

THE HUDSON-MOHAWK PASS

(1) Ewing Galloway, N. Y. (2) J. A. Glenn, Albany, N. Y. (3) Bear Mountain Hudson River Bridge Co. 

1. The Mohawk valley, a part of the Hudson-Mohawk pass. We see here the only low, natural pass through the Appalachian highland. Before white men came to

America, the Indians made forest trails along the banks of this river. Early roads and railroads followed the same route, and the Erie canal made use of the channel of the Mohawk river. The picture shows the four types of passenger and freight traffic which pour through the pass today. The wagon roads of the past have been made into automobile highways. The slow, old-fashioned trains have been replaced by fast, luxurious express trains. 

The canal has been deepened and widened, and the small horse-drawn canal boats have given way to great barges with engines of their own. Air lines follow the pass to avoid the mountains. 

2. A lock in the New York State Barge canal. The canal boats are entering the lock, to be raised to the level of the canal beyond it. This part of the canal is in the 

Mohawk valley. The barges are loaded with goods received from ocean steamers at New York, to be reshipped to the west at Buffalo on lake boats. 

3. Bear Mountain bridge, which crosses the Hudson river south of Newburgh, New York. This high bridge is one of the few places where automobiles can cross the 

Hudson valley above New York City.

GREAT CITIES AT THE ENDS OF THE HUDSON-MOHAWK PASS 

4 5 (4) (5) W. M. Frange, from the Chamber of Commerce of the State of New York. (6) Buffalo Chamber of Commerce. 

4. New York City, from Governor's island in New York harbor. This is the banking district, and in these tall buildings are the offices of many banks and business firms. The men who have their offices here control much of the business of our country. The high tower with the pointed top is the Woolworth building, for many years the tallest building in the country. Along the shore are wharves where huge steamers and many ferry boats dock. 

5. The uptown office buildings of New York. In the foreground is a section of Central park. At the extreme right of the picture is the Empire State building, the tallest structure so far made by man. Way at the left is the Chrysler tower, second in height to the Empire State building. The buildings which face the park are apartment houses, in which thousands of families live. 

If it were not for such immense apartment houses nearly seven million people could not live in New York City. 

6. The waterfront at Buffalo. Tied at the wharf are a lake passenger steamer, to the left, and a freight steamer, to the right. On the deck of the freight steamer notice the wide covers which can be taken off for easy loading and unloading. Traffic through the Hudson- 

Mohawk pass reaches the Great lakes at Buffalo, and

Buffalo has become a large city through handling this traffic and traffic from the Great lakes, and by manufacturing the raw materials brought to its doors.

A LOW THE REGION OF THE HUDSON-MOHAWK PASS 

Thousands of years ago before the glaciers covered northern North America, the Appalachian highland was unbroken NATURAL PASS throughout its entire length. 

It stretched northeast and southwest like a great wall, cutting off the eastern coast of North America from the interior of the continent.

When the glacier began to melt back toward the north, the waters from it poured into the Great lakes. The lake outlet, through the 

St. Lawrence valley, was still blocked by ice, so the water could not reach the ocean that way. Deeper and deeper grew Lake Ontario, the lowest lake, and more and more of the surrounding land became flooded. At last the waters, dammed up between the ice sheet and the surrounding highlands, flooded out across the highlands to the east, then followed the slope of the land south to the Atlantic ocean. As they flowed they wore away rocks and soil and cut a deep valley. 

In time, the ice that blocked the St. Lawrence valley melted, and the waters of the Great lakes again followed that route to the sea. But they left traces of their work in the form of the deep valley they had cut. This valley-the Hudson-Mohawk pass-is the one low pass through the Appalachian highland.

At its highest point it is only 450 feet above sea level. Through it flow the great Hudson river and its tributary, the Mohawk.

Long, long before white men came to this country, the Indians used the Hudson and 

HUDSON AND MOHAWK PASS AS A TRADE ROUTE 

Mohawk rivers as travel routes for their birch-bark canoes. When they came to rapids or falls, they carried their canoes around the rough waters. 

These carrying places were called portages. 

With only a few portages, the Indians could go from the Hudson river up the Mohawk and one of its tributaries to Lake Ontario. 

A forest path which led along the Mohawk river was known as the Mohawk trail. When the white men came to our country, the whole of the Mohawk trail lay through a dense wilderness. The white men learned of the trail and the waterway from the Indians, and they too followed the Mohawk valley inland, building forts and small settlements here and there. 

As time went on, the Hudson and the

Mohawk rivers came to be used more and more as avenues for trading. More and more settlements sprang up, for much of the land along the pass was very fertile. 

The "Big Ditch." Gradually people moved into the west and raised crops on the plains around the upper Great lakes. Soon they wanted to send these crops and the other natural resources of the land to markets of the east, where they could buy eastern products in exchange. But there were the high and impassable Niagara falls between Lake Erie. and Lake Ontario blocking the way. Finally a few people began to ask: "Why not dig a canal from Lake Erie to the Hudson river and make trade easy between the west and the east?" To most people this seemed impossible. How they joked about the "Big Ditch"!

But the men who believed in it were very determined, and at last, in 1825, the Erie canal was completed. It started from Lake Erie, near a town called Buffalo, and went east through the Mohawk valley to the Hudson river. And what a change it made! The products of the west now came pouring into Buffalo in such quantities that when they were unloaded from the lake boats there were not enough canal boats to carry them farther east. 

Great grain elevators and ore docks had to be built to store the grains and iron ore until they could be taken away. This was a wonderful thing for Buffalo and the little town grew into an important city.

The loaded canal boats were drawn by mules which followed a towpath along the 47

THE REGION OF THE HUDSON-MOHAWK PASS 

49 canal. When the boats reached the Hudson river they were lashed together in groups and towed by steamer to New York City. The plentiful products of the west helped that city too. Here were many sorts of raw materials for her to manufacture or to export. As other 

Atlantic seaports were cut off by the mountains from the rich inland plains, they had fewer goods to load on ships bound for foreign lands. So with this stream of inland commerce, New York became the greatest seaport of our country. With so much in the way of raw materials to manufacture, she soon grew to be the greatest American manufacturing city, and to have the greatest population of any city of the Western hemisphere. 

New York State Barge Canal. Some years ago the Erie canal was deepened and widened, and in a few places its course was changed. 

This was to make it possible for lake barges to travel right on to New York City through the canal without their cargoes being reshipped at Buffalo. The canal was not only rebuilt but was renamed. It is now called the 

New York State Barge canal. Branches ex tend to Oswego, on Lake Ontario, and to some of the larger Finger lakes, in the central part of New York state. The northern part of the

Hudson river is also connected by canal with Lake Champlain, thus forming a commercial highway to New York for Canadian and 

Adirondack products. 

Railroads. Along the route of the Hudson- 

Mohawk pass, the earliest railroads found their way. The pass was low, and it was easy to lay railroads through it to connect the inland plains with Atlantic seaports. Today the main line of one of the greatest railroad systems in the country-the New York Central-follows this route through the highland.

Increasing Transportation. From its begin ning, then, as an Indian travel route, the

Hudson-Mohawk pass has grown into a commercial giant. As we have seen, first came the Erie canal with its mule-drawn canal boats. This was improved, and became the 

Barge canal, through which lake barges, some of which carry as much as 1000 tons, travel under their own power. The first rough road followed Indian footpaths along the river banks. Today these banks carry railroads with fast express trains and heavy freights, and their concrete highways are crowded with automobiles, buses, and trucks which transport passengers and goods between New York and Buffalo and the west.

The first railroad built through this pass was a queer little line with wooden rails and small, odd-looking engines. Today huge locomotives thunder along the steel rails, drawing luxurious passenger trains. Then there are freight and express trains loaded with manufactured goods and raw materials, and refrigerator cars which carry meats, fruits, vegetables, milk, and butter to market.

And now the very newest form of transportation is following the famous route. Air lines carry passengers and mail from New York City to Albany and from there to Buffalo and on into the west. It is not looking far ahead to believe that soon these planes will be carrying great quantities of goods and products of all sorts swiftly over the pass long ago discovered by the Indians.

Much of the Mohawk pass is bordered by highland regions. To the north of the 

Mohawk valley are the Adiron 

HIGHLANDS 

dack mountains, and to the south is the Allegheny plateau-both parts of the 

Appalachian system. To the west of the 

Hudson river are are the Catskill mountains, while the lower Hudson cuts through the 

Southeastern plateau. 

Lake Ontario and Lake Erie give the Lake plains which border them a mild, oceanic climate. The Atlantic ocean THE REGION does the same for Long Island PRODUCTS OF 

and the lands about the lower 

Hudson. So the growing season is long. As a result many kinds of fruits-apples, peaches, pears, and plums-are raised in these two sections. Many grapes are raised near the southeastern shores of Lake Erie, and around the long, narrow Finger lakes in the central part of New York state.

On the Allegheny plateau the climate is not so mild. Here hay and dairy cattle are raised, and dairy products are manufactured. Salt is

NORTH AMERICA 

mined west of the Finger lakes. Some lumbering is done in the Adirondacks, although less and less timber is cut each year. All these products find their way to market through the busy Hudson-Mohawk pass.

MANUFACTURING 

With so many raw materials at hand and others easy to get because of fine transportation, and with no lack of markets for finished products, manufacturing has sprung up along the entire length of the pass. Most of the cities here have fine water power from streams tumbling down from the mountains or over the bluff of the Allegheny plateau. Those cities without sufficient water power can easily get coal from the rich mines in Pennsylvania, not far to the south. It would be hard to find a place with more ideal conditions for manufacturing than the region about the Hudson- 

Mohawk pass.

POPULATION 

Cities and towns lie so close together along the Hudson river and the Mohawk pass that sometimes it is hard to tell where one ends and another begins. 

Most of the people of New York state live along this historic route and in the great cities at its ends. This is one of the most densely populated sections of our country. 

We have spoken of Buffalo in connection with the building of the Erie canal. First she stored goods that were waiting to 

BUFFALO 

be reshipped to market. Then she began to manufacture these goods into finished products. Great flour and cereal mills grew up. Slaughtering and packing houses were established, and meat, instead of livestock, was shipped out, usually by rail. Several factories were built to use animal fats in making soap. This product, too, went out by train. Iron and steel and copper works grew in size and number. In these the ores not only were crushed and smelted but the metals were made into many articles, from small tools to fine automobiles. So Buffalo became an important railroad and manufacturing center as well as the leading lake port of this region. She now has a population of over half a million.

Rochester makes flour and clothing, but OTHER 

CITIES 

perhaps her most interesting manufacture is cameras and photographic supplies, for which the city is an important center. Syracuse, like all the cities of this region, has a variety of manufactures. 

She makes fine automobiles and a number of iron and steel products. Most of the candles used in the United States are also made in Syracuse. Utica has for a long time been especially noted for her knit goods, although she has a large variety of other industries. 

Schenectady is known all over the world for the manufacture of electrical goods of all kinds. Albany is the capital of New York state and is an important center for the manufacture of pulp and paper. Troy, across the 

Hudson above Albany, is a leading city in the manufacture of shirts and collars. Yonkers, farther south on the Hudson, has large carpet mills and the greatest sugar-refining plants in the world. On the Allegheny plateau are a few small, busy manufacturing cities. 

Jamestown makes much furniture. Binghamton and two nearby towns are important producers of shoes.

NEW YORK CITY 

And now we come to the most interesting city of all-New York, the metropolis, or chief city, of the Western hemisphere. Covering 300 square miles of land, New York City has a population of nearly 7,000,000 people. 

What drew so many people to this one city? 

First, the fact that New York City, with her great harbor and many inland connections, is in touch with the whole world. At her docks we see ships unloading tea and spices from 

Asia; coffee, cocoa, and rubber from South 

America; fruits from Central America and the

West Indies; cotton from our own southern states and from Africa; and a thousand and one other interesting things from different parts of the world. The ships are reloaded with grain, meat, kerosene, textiles and clothing, steel rails, engines, and machinery of all kinds. These go to many far-away places of the world: part of the food products to 

Europe, many manufactured goods to South 

America, Africa, and Australia.

Manufactures of the Metropolis. Highways,

THE REGION OF THE HUDSON-MOHAWK PASS 

51 swift railroads, bus lines, air lines, and inland waterways connect New York City with all parts of our country. Since these many avenues of commerce are constantly supplying New York City with raw materials, and her foreign trade is calling for manufactured goods, is it hard to see why she has become the greatest manufacturing center of the world? This great city leads the country in the quantity and variety of her manufactures. 

A list of every manufacture of New York City would look very much like a list of the manufactures of all the other cities of the United States. Clothing, millinery, and fur goods are manufactured in enormous quantities. 

Books, magazines, and newspapers are published in vast numbers. Great sugar refineries face the waterfront of the East river and turn out thousands of barrels of sugar daily. Much of this sugar is used in making candy, an industry in which New York City leads the country. Cigars and cigarettes are manufactured on a large scale. Tin, copper, iron, and steel are worked into a great variety of finished products.

Steady Growth of the City. As the world's demand for these products increases, new workers are constantly needed, and more people are drawn to the city each year. They come from everywhere, but the greatest numbers are from various parts of our own country.

What is more natural than that those who come to America from different lands should settle down in New York City where work is plentiful, and where they can find others speaking their language and living together in some part of the city? So in this great city many foreign sections have grown up, and one visiting such a section might well think he was in another country.

A great many people who live outside of New York depend on the city for their living, and go back and forth to and from work each day. The nearby residential towns are called suburbs. If all the people living in the suburbs were counted in her population, New York would be the largest city in the world.

Transportation Problems of the Metropolis. It is a big problem to carry all these workers to and from their work. At first New York City built street car lines to run along the surface of the streets. When these were unable to handle all her traffic, she built elevated railroads that run on tracks high above some of the streets. Later still, she had great tunnels dug under the ground. Through these subways, as they are called, swift trains run day and night. Buses, taxis, and private cars jam the streets. Ferries, bridges, and great tunnels under the Hudson river, some for trains, others for automobiles and trucks, bring workers to the business districts daily. 

Sights of New York City. In the business section of New York City are the famous skyscrapers, towering so high above the streets that you feel very small indeed as you bend your head back and try to see to their tops. 

Some of these buildings are over eighty stories high. 

New York City has its playgrounds, too.

Beautiful parks are scattered throughout the city. One park has a zoo with strange animals from all parts of the world. In a park at the lower end of Manhattan island is the Aquarium, where one can see tanks filled with odd and beautiful fish. Another park has flowers of every kind, from every corner of the world. There are theaters and motion picture houses almost without number. There are a number of large museums with beautiful pictures and statues, relics of bygone ages, and many other interesting things. 

The city has a wonderful public school system. She also has many colleges and universities where students may continue their education. 

New York City owes much of her greatness to the Hudson-Mohawk pass that made it possible for the raw materials of the Central plains and the mining regions of the Great lakes to be manufactured in her factories and exported from her harbor.

NORTH AMERICA  

1. The Hudson-Mohawk pass is the only low pass through the Appalachian highland. It extends along the Mohawk river and down the Hudson to the Atlantic seacoast. 

2. The Mohawk pass was first known to the Indians, who traveled on the river in birch-bark canoes or followed its shore by forest trails. The white men learned of this inland route and along it built forts and settlements. As trade grew in importance, a canal was built to connect Lake Erie and the upper lakes with the Atlantic ocean. This was the Erie canal, which turned vast amounts of valuable western products to Buffalo and New York City. 

3. Transportation is constantly improving in this region. The canal has been improved to carry large barges and is now called the New York State 

Barge canal. Highways and railroads have been built, and air service is showing promise of great commercial importance. 

4. The highlands bordering the Hudson-Mohawk pass are the Adirondack mountains, the Allegheny plateau, the Catskill mountains, and the Southeastern plateau. 

5. On the Allegheny plateau are raised hay and dairy cattle. Lumber is cut in the Adirondacks. Apples, peaches, pears, and plums are raised on the Lake plains and along the lower Hudson. Grapes are raised near Lake Erie and the Finger lakes. Salt is mined in the neighborhood of the Finger lakes.

All these products are shipped through the Hudson- 

Mohawk pass. 

6. Raw materials, abundant water power, coal from 

Pennsylvania, fine transportation, and good markets have made the region of the Hudson-Mohawk pass an ideal manufacturing center. 

7. The greatest population of New York state lies along the Hudson-Mohawk pass. Here are many cities and towns. Buffalo, a great lake port, turns many of the raw products of the inland plains into finished goods. Rochester, Syracuse, Utica, Schenectady, Albany, Troy, and Yonkers are other large cities of this region. Each has important manufactures. 

8. New York City covers 300 square miles and has a population of nearly 7,000,000. With her fine harbor and excellent inland connections, she is in commercial touch with the whole world. Foodstuffs and raw materials pour into this city from our own country and from all parts of the earth. 

She sends out manufactured goods of many kinds, for she is the greatest manufacturing center of the world. Her growing industries constantly draw new people from our own and foreign countries. 

9. People can go from place to place in the metropolis by surface cars, elevated trains, subway trains, taxis, or buses. Ferries, bridges, subways, and tunnels under the Hudson bring workers to the city. 

10. New York City has fine parks and museums, many theaters, and a splendid educational system.

NEW ENGLAND'S VARIED SCENERY

1. The cliffs near the end of Cape Cod, Massachusetts. 

These high cliffs are of sand and gravel. Below them is a broad sandy beach. In heavy winter storms, great waves from the Atlantic cut into the cliffs and dash up their slopes, washing the sand out to sea. the ocean cuts away a few feet from the eastern edge of the cape. 

Each year 2. Echo lake, in the White mountains of New Hampshire. Though the White mountains include some of the highest peaks of the Appalachian highland, they are old, worn-down mountains. Few peaks in the Appalachians are so high or so rocky that trees do not grow all the way to their summits. New Hampshire is a vacation land with hundreds of hotels, camps, and cottages that are filled with visitors in summer. 

3. A winter scene in New England. Winter in New

England is a season of cold and snow. Such cold weather is bracing and the New England climate makes people vigorous. The New Englanders are active and industrious. 

4. Along the Maine coast. In many places the New 

England coast is rough and rocky. See how the pine trees crowd down almost to the water's edge. Because the cold Labrador current sweeping along its coast lessens summer heat, the Maine shore is a favorite summer resort for thousands of people.

5. Picking cranberries on Cape Cod. Cranberries grow thickly on little bushes in "bogs" which are kept flooded while the berries are growing. Cranberries grow best in moist soil. At harvest time the bogs are drained. 

Here the workers are gathering the cranberries. With wide scoops that have comb-like teeth they easily comb the berries from the plants. 

6. A potato field in the valley of the Aroostook river in northern Maine. This broad valley has a light, fertile soil well suited to potatoes, and a cool climate in which they grow and ripen well. More potatoes are grown in 

Maine than in any other state in our country. 

7. A marble quarry in Vermont. Marble is quarried in even blocks, as the walls of the quarry show. As the marble is removed, concrete braces are built to prevent the walls of the quarry from caving in. Some Vermont quarries are more than 300 feet deep. This picture shows one of the deepest. Compare this quarry with the limestone quarry shown opposite page 43. 

8. The water front of Gloucester, Massachusetts, with a fishing schooner starting on a trip to the Atlantic fishing grounds. Gloucester has been a fishing port for 300 years, and has a fishing fleet of over 150 schooners. 

9. The fish pier at Boston, Massachusetts. Fishing vessels like those in the picture bring here every year enough fish to supply two pounds to every man, woman, and child in the United States. Boston is our chief fishing port.

When colonists came from England and built their settlements in the northeastern part of what is now the United States, the lands they settled were called New England. Today the states of Maine, New Hampshire, Vermont, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and Connecticut lie in the New England section of our country. 

New England is in the Piedmont plateau and the Appalachian highland. The eastern hills are typical Piedmont country. Some of the highest Appalachian peaks are in the 

White mountains of New Hampshire. Mt. 

Washington reaches a height of 6,239 feet.

But New England is considered as a separate region because it is different in many ways from the rest of the country. It is off by itself, up in the northeastern corner of the United States. Its coast line is not so even as most of the Atlantic coast line, but is curving and jagged and has any number of harbors, both large and small. In many places the coast is so rough and rocky that lighthouses are needed to warn ships of dangerous places.

The New England climate is the opposite of the Florida climate. While Florida has a 

CLIMATE 

long growing season, New England has a short one. The New England climate is a variable climate. Changes in weather come very suddenly. Each season is entirely distinct from the others. In New 

England, winter is really winter. The air is cold and raw, deep snow covers the ground, and the people muffle themselves up well. 

Then a few months later it is warm enough so that these same people in the same places can go in swimming. It is not only warm in summer but at times extremely hot, and often people go to the seashore or up into the mountains to get away from the heat. 

Perhaps you wonder how it can get so cold after being so hot. The latitude, the winds, and an ocean current from the north make this region unusually cold in winter. New 

England lies near the middle of the North 

Temperate zone. from the west.

Its winds frequently come 

They cross great wintry stretches of land, and are very cold when they reach New England. From way up in the frozen Arctic regions where the glaciers start, comes the cold Labrador current. With a frosty breath and an icy touch, it sweeps down the coast of New England. It chills the land, the air, and the people. 

In northern New England snow is on the ground for four or five months of the year.

The little lakes and rivers are completely frozen over. Some of the ice is cut and saved for the summer. During very severe winters, the larger rivers and even some of the harbors are blocked with ice. In southern New England the winters are not so severe and the snow does not stay so long.

POPULATION 

Many people like a climate that has such complete changes as are found in New England. They say it is more enjoyable and makes them feel better. That might be one reason why so many people live in New England. This whole section is only a fourth as large as Texas, and yet it has more than 8,000,000 people-about onefourteenth of the population of the United 

States. When you know that the northern part of New England has only a small part of these people, you can imagine how they are crowded together in the southern part. 

Perhaps you will guess that there must be many towns and large cities in the southern part. This is quite right. Little Rhode Island is the most thickly populated state in the 

United States, and Massachusetts comes next.

New England has an interesting history. 

You have heard many times the story of the brave Pilgrims. You have seen pictures of them with their broadbrimmed hats and big white collars. In 1620 they landed at Plymouth, and decided to settle in that region, although it seemed barren and 

THE STORY OF 

NEW 

ENGLAND 

55

NORTH AMERICA 

dreary. They built their homes and planted their gardens. Later, other ships landed on this rocky coast, bringing more settlers with them. After a time there were a number of little settlements scattered along the coast and clustering around the mouths of the rivers. At first settlers came only in small groups and the growth of the settlements was slow. 

As the people became used to this new land, and less afraid of its dangers, they began to move away from the coast. They thought that farther inland the soil would be better and they could raise more crops. They followed the rivers because it was easier to travel along a river than through a forest. Along the Connecticut river is a narrow lowland. There the soil is rich, and the people who settled in this region prospered. Others were not so fortunate. In many places the soil was so thin and rocky that farming was a hardship. 

The people on the coast also planted crops, but they found other ways, as well, of making a living. Some built very sturdy and well-made sailing vessels. Men sailed in these vessels up and down the Atlantic coast, and even to Europe and the West Indies. But these were not pleasure trips; they were for business. Going to the West Indies, the ships were loaded with lumber. Coming back they brought sugar and molasses. This is how the commerce of New 

England started.

Life in New England was very different from that in early Virginia. In Virginia the soil was rich and fertile. The people lived on large plantations. They did not need to live together in little settlements as the people in New England did, so fewer towns grew up in Virginia. Neither did the people of Virginia have to work so hard for their living as did the New Englanders. They did not have such long, cold winters to endure. Not having the weather and the crops to worry over, they learned to live in a much more care-free manner than the New England people. 

In the early days whaling and fishing were important industries along the New 

England coast. The whaling expeditions were especially dangerous. 

Sometimes the men would be gone 

WHALING 

AND 

FISHING 

three years on a single trip, and no one would know whether they were safe until they came home.

Whaling is now carried on largely from Pacific ports, but fishing is still a very important industry of New England. Gloucester and Boston, in Massachusetts, are two of the greatest fishing centers of the world. Many fishing boats also sail from the harbor of Portland, Maine. Many boats go to the Grand 

Banks, great fishing grounds southeast of Newfoundland, where cod and halibut are found. Other sea food caught and shipped out from New England ports includes mackerel, lobsters, oysters, and herring. Much of this sea food is canned before it is shipped. 

The fishing trade depends on many other industries. Fishermen must have ships and nets and tackle. Someone must market the fish.

Because of this, many workmen in different trades make their living from the fishing industry.

AGRICULTURE 

In the Connecticut valley, which the early settlers found so fertile, there are still many beautiful and prosperous farms. Other good farming regions are the basins of Narragansett bay and Boston bay, and some of the pasture regions of Vermont. These lands need careful cultivation, but they produce varied and rich crops. 

If you were to ask a New England farmer what he raised, his answer would depend upon where he lives. In northern Maine are the broad potato fields which make Maine the leading state in this crop. In the Piedmont hills are orchards of fine apples of the best flavor. In the Connecticut valley you would see fine cigar tobacco. Cape Cod has extensive bogs in which grow more than half of our cranberries. If you asked a farmer in a forest section in the early spring, he would take you down into the woods and show you how he collects the sap of the maple tree to be made into maple syrup and maple sugar. A farmer usually has a little clearing in the woods where this is done, and this section of his farm is called the sugar bush. 

Farmers near the cities depend greatly

upon their market gardens. Fresh vegetables are grown in these market gardens and early each morning are taken to the city by truck.
