DAIRYING 

farmer can pasture cows on land where crops will not grow. If a railroad is near, he takes his milk to the station every morning. If he is not near a railroad or a city market, he makes the milk into butter and cheese. Cities and villages need these dairy products, and dairying gives the farmer a use for land that might otherwise be wasted. Even so, there is much waste land in New England. In some places the soil is so rocky that crops will not grow, cattle will not graze, and even trees have a hard time to live.

There is much forest land in New England, although in the past the forests have been wasted, as they have in LUMBERING many parts of the United States. 

When people first settled in our country, there were so many trees that it seemed as if the forests would last forever. But trees were chopped down so recklessly that many regions of the United States today have no forest growth left at all. In the spring the roots of forest trees hold back the water from the melting snow. If there are no trees to do this the water swells the streams and may flood the land. This is one reason why we are more careful of our forests than we used to be. 

The lumbering industry and the other industries dependent on the forests provide work for many people. There are many lumber camps in northern New England. Logs are floated down the streams in the spring to paper mills, many of which are along the Connecticut river. Much lumber is made into pulp, from which paper is made. Other lumber goes to furniture factories and for building. 

Pine wood is shipped in great quantities from Maine. Perhaps you have heard Maine called the Pine Tree state. There are also large forests of spruce in Maine. The cities of Bangor and Augusta, in Maine, are both lumbering centers. In parts of New England the slopes of high hills and mountains are still covered with trees, but the lumber industry is slowly declining.

There are large granite quarries at Milford and Quincy, in Massachusetts;

QUARRYING 

at Barre, Vermont; and at Concord, New Hampshire. Here, men are busy cutting out granite blocks from which huge buildings and monuments are made. From the Green mountains of Vermont and the 

Berkshire hills of western Massachusetts, we get marble. The slate for your blackboard may have been quarried in Vermont or Maine. 

We have mentioned various New England industries: agriculture, lumbering, fishing, quarrying. These are all important. But we shall now take up an industry so much more important that it makes these others seem very small indeed.

MANUFACTURING

Manufacturing is the biggest industry in

New England today. There are a number of reasons for its growth in this region. The people who first settled here had a hard time making a living from the stony soil. As their crops brought them little or no money, they learned to be saving, and to make the most of what they had. Even time was not wasted. 

During the long, cold winters, the women spun thread or yarn, which they wove into cloth. Each family had a spinning wheel, and the girls of the household all learned to use it. 

The cloth was finally made into clothing or household linens, Shoes were made by the village cobbler.

The animals were probably killed on nearby farms. Their hides were taken to the village to be tanned, or made into leather. Then the leather was taken to the cobbler. When a boy got a new pair of shoes he knew its whole history.

The rough furniture of the home was made by the men of the family from trees cut in the forests. All this work was done by hand. 

Because the New Englanders had to make so many of the things they used or wore, they became very skilful. 

Making things by hand, however, was a slow process. There were many waterfalls in the region, and in time, small mills and factories were built near them, and here little settlements grew up. In these mills and factories, the work was done by crude machines made by the few craftsmen of that day. But even these machines turned out goods far faster than could be done by hand.

As time went on, machinery was greatly improved and big factories took the place of the little ones. Many people left their farms and went to work in the factories. The money they earned there bought them food and clothing and much besides.

New England became known for her skilled workmen, and people from other sections who wanted to go into manufacturing and needed skilled labor naturally looked to New England for it. As New England also offered water power, where could they find a better place to build their factories?

As more factories were built, more workers were needed, and people from many parts came here to work. In place of the little manufacturing settlements there were now towns and even cities. Today, New England is a great manufacturing center, one of the most prosperous regions of the United States.

Where does New England get her raw materials for manufacturing? As she makes cotton and woolen cloths, she must have cotton and wool. In her great shoe factories she needs the leather from many hides. For making brass products she must have zinc and copper. For making machinery, she must have iron. Of course, cotton cannot be grown in a cold climate like that of New England. 

While some sheep and cattle are raised here, there are not nearly enough to furnish all the wool and hides the factories demand. It is plain to see that New England must buy her raw materials elsewhere. Some are brought from the south or other parts of our own country. Some even come from foreign lands. 

At first, practically everything was shipped in by boat. The building of railroads across our country gave an added means of transportation. Coal could now more easily be had, and many of the factories came to be run by steam instead of water power.

COTTON New England now manufactures half the cotton, woolen, and leather goods made in the United States. Great bales of cotton are rolled off the ships that come to Boston from Galveston, New Orleans, and other southern ports and even from Africa. Much cotton that was picked "Way down South in the land of cotton" is sent away as cloth in the big ships that leave New

England harbors so regularly. Many children in our southern states and in the countries of 

South and Central America wear clothes made of New England cloth..

The Merrimac river supplies much of the power that transforms this cotton into cloth. 

On this river are Nashua and Manchester, in 

New Hampshire, and Lawrence and Lowell, in Massachusetts.

New Bedford, Massachusetts, has over seventy cotton mills. Fall River, Massa-

NORTH AMERICA 

chusetts, and Pawtucket, Rhode Island, also manufacture much cotton cloth. 

You use much of it and many things in your home are made from it. It is on your tables and beds and is hung at your windows. You wear it, and sleep on it. No wonder New England makes miles of cotton cloth and then has to make still more.

The wool that New England makes into woolen cloth comes from the western part of our country, from Australia, and 

WOOLENS 

from South America. There are famous woolen mills in Lawrence, Massachusetts, and Providence and Woonsocket, Rhode 

Island. You use much of this wool, too. Your sweaters, your heavy blankets, and the rugs on which you walk are made of it.

LEATHER GOODS

At this very moment, all over the world, there are people walking in shoes that came from New England factories. You may be wearing a pair. Most of these shoe factories are in Boston and nearby towns. Lynn, Haverhill, and 

Brockton, Massachusetts, make thousands of pairs of shoes yearly. Manchester, New

Hampshire, has large shoe factories. 

Though some hides are still tanned in New England, most of them are shipped in already tanned. These come from Pennsylvania and Wisconsin and from Australia and 

South America. You not only walk on this. leather, but you use it in many other ways.

Your pocketbook and your traveling bag are made of it. New England manufactures both of these articles, and many others made from leather. 

If you were asked to make a list of the things about your home that are made from metal, you could easily name fifty at least the locks on the doors,

METAL PRODUCTS 

the needles in the sewing basket, the tools on the work bench, the clock whose alarm goes off much too early in the morning. 

The pictures hang from wire made of metal. 

Metal jewelry is worn by the girls. The pots and pans in the kitchen are metal products.

Busy New England manufactures half of the small metal products that are made in the 

United States. Beautiful little watches are made at Waltham, Massachusetts. Jewelry is a product of Providence, Rhode Island, and 

Attleboro, Massachusetts. Wire and hardware are made at Worcester, Massachusetts. 

Waterbury, Connecticut, makes cheap watches and brass wares. Hardware and firearms are made in Springfield, Massachusetts, and in 

New Haven, Connecticut. New Haven also makes many clocks. Silverware and cutlery come from Meriden and New Britain, Connecticut.

HEAVY MACHINERY 

Large machines used in manufacturing cotton goods, woolens, and shoes are made in Worcester and Springfield.

Many of these machines are sold and used in New England. Others, especially the shoe machinery, are shipped to other parts of the world.

PAPER New England also has paper mills, especially through the Connecticut valley where there is much water power. For this industry she supplies much of her own raw material, getting it from the forests of her northern states. Holyoke, Massachusetts, on the Connecticut river, makes very fine writing paper. In western Massachusetts, in the town of Dalton, all the paper that the government uses for printing bank notes is made. The process for making this paper is kept secret, and the mills are guarded. The New England has led the country in manufacturing for many years, but today she is finding rivals in other regions 

COMPETITION 

of the United States. south is now manufacturing cotton cloth, and is becoming a serious rival because, besides water power, she has cheap labor and raw materials close at hand. Other sections of the country are also starting to manufacture many of the products that New England turns out.

New England has the advantage over the other states of an early start. This means much, because once an industry is established in a place, men who want to build a similar business go there, as they can find people skilled in making that particular thing. One successful factory leads to others making the same product, and the locality becomes known

NEW ENGLAND—A LAND OF MANUFACTURES

(10) Massachusetts Industrial Commission. (11) Boston Chamber of Commerce. (12) (13) S. D. Warren & Co. 

10. A New England factory, seen from the air. Notice the homes for the workers which have been built close by.

This large factory makes machinery for shoe factories all over the world. Beyond the factory are market gardens where farmers raise vegetables to be sold in Boston. The large sign on the top of the factory is a helpful guide to air pilots. 

11. Interior of one of the largest cotton mills in the world at Salem, Massachusetts. The window far at the end of the rows of looms is a quarter of a mile away. 

These looms are run by machinery, and turn out a large quantity of cloth each day. New England mills produce cloth of fine quality, and cotton and woolen goods are leading New England products. 

12. The beater room in a New England paper mill. 

In this room the wood pulp or other material of which the paper is made is mixed with water in large tanks and beaten until it is smooth and even and just thick enough to make paper. This is called "paper stock."

From 

13. A paper machine in a New England mill. Paper stock from the beater room is poured out on fine wire screen to form a sheet. Part of the water drains off through the screen, and the sheet is then run through rollers, which press out the rest of the water. these rollers it passes between steam-heated rollers which dry it thoroughly and press it into finished paper. 

As the paper is finished, it is wound in rolls for shipment.

14. Boston from the air. This picture shows the business district and a part of the waterfront of Boston. 

Not all of the great seaport's many miles of wharves can be seen. The bridge in the lower right-hand corner leads to South Boston, where there are other great piers. 

Vessels of any size can dock in Boston harbor. 

15. The Old State House, Boston. This historic building was built several years before the Revolutionary War. 

The Boston massacre took place in front of the Old State House, and the Declaration of Independence was announced to the citizens of Massachusetts from the balcony which we see in the picture. This is only one of many colonial buildings still standing in the city of Boston. 

16. An old cobbler's shop. Before the invention of machinery for making shoes, footwear was made in little shops like this. Every New England village had a shoemaker, who was a skilful workman. When a customer needed shoes, he ordered them from the shoemaker, who cut the leather, sewed the seams, and made the shoes by hand. 

17. The interior of a modern shoe factory. These machines have taken the place of the old-fashioned cobbler. Each workman does only part of the work on a shoe instead of making the entire shoe. Thousands of pairs of shoes are turned out daily in this factory, which supplies stores all over our country.

for that product. People naturally go there for it. If they do not like the article one factory makes, there are other factories near at hand whose goods they may like better. 

One instance of the way a place becomes noted for an industry is found in Troy, New 

York. Years ago the collar industry was started there. Its early success attracted other people workers, and men with money who wanted to go into the collar business. 

Today Troy is a great center of the collar industry. 

Danbury, Connecticut, is a New England town which has her own special industry. Felt hat factories have grown up here, and the raw material for this industry comes from Australian rabbit fur.

Just as a city becomes noted for an industry, so do certain sections of a city. In most cities, the large department stores are in one section, the automobile salesrooms in another, and the wholesale houses in another. 

COMMERCE 

You probably do not need to be told why commerce is so important to New England. 

Not only must she have raw materials shipped to her factories. 

She must buy corn, wheat, and other foodstuffs. Since she cannot use all the products she makes, it is equally important that these be shipped away. All along the New England coast you will find good harbors where every day many ships come and go. They bring needed goods to New England from all over the world and carry away the things made there. To Boston come bananas, sugar, and molasses from the West Indies, and coffee from South America. Portland, Maine, imports oil from Mexico, and china clay and wood pulp from Europe. Fall River, too, imports crude oil from Mexico.

Boston is a railroad center as well as a seaport. The little inland towns and cities send

BOSTON 

their products to Boston, and, from the far west, railroads bring grains and meat to be shipped from Boston harbor. 

In order that Boston might have a better railroad connection, the Hoosac tunnel was dug through the mountains of western Massachusetts. This tunnel is five miles long, and at the time it was made was considered a wonderful achievement. 

HISTORIC LANDMARKS 

Boston is a most interesting city. If you visit its historic landmarks, you will probably start, as so many others do, at

Bunker Hill, across the river from Boston. Then you will see, the old North Church where the lanterns were hung that signalled Paul Revere. Paul 

Revere's quaint little house is still standing. 

You will surely want to see Faneuil Hall, a famous meeting place of colonial days. The famous Boston Common has been a meeting place, a parade ground and a playground from early times. From it you can see the State 

House with its shining golden dome.

Not far from Boston are Lexington and Concord, famous in Revolutionary history. 

And every year many people make the trip to 

Plymouth especially to see the famous Plymouth Rock, where the Pilgrims landed.

In Cambridge, just across the Charles river from Boston, is Harvard University, the oldest college in the United States. New England has many other well-known colleges. 

A NATURAL 

Other interesting places to visit are the summer resorts. The entertaining and housing of summer visitors makes an other profitable industry for PLAYGROUND 

New England. The hotels and inns and cottages employ hundreds of people and use the products of the market gardens and dairies.

New England has many beauty spots. When you ask your friends where they are going on their vacation they may answer, "We're going to the Maine woods," or "We're going to Cape Cod," or "We're going to motor through the White mountains." New England has them all, woods, seashore, mountains and lakes.

NORTH AMERICA  

1. The early New England settlements were along the coast, but many of the people soon moved inland. 

Early industries were farming, shipbuilding, fishing, and whaling. The trade which the settlers carried on with their small ships was the beginning of New 

England commerce. 

2. The climate of New England is variable. The winters are cold, and the summers have much hot weather. Latitude, winds, and the Labrador current are responsible for the unusual coldness of New 

England winters. 

3. The land is hilly in many parts and the soil is thin and rocky. This makes farming difficult. The good farming regions are: the Connecticut valley, the 

Narragansett basin, the Boston basin, the potato district of northern Maine, and the pasture lands of Vermont. Many farmers also carry on dairying, and supply the cities and towns with milk, butter, and cheese, which are needed in large quantities. 

4. Lumbering, quarrying, and fishing are three other

New England industries which are quite important. 

The lumber is sent to paper mills, furniture factories, and builders. From the quarries come granite, marble, and slate. Many different kinds of fish are shipped out. 

5. The chief industry of New England is manufacturing. The early New England people became skilful at hand work in their homes. Today the waterfalls of the region furnish water power for many large factories. The industry of her early settlers, her waterfalls, her skilled labor, and her good harbors all helped to develop manufacturing in New England. 

6. New England is finding rivals in manufacturing, as other regions are beginning to make the articles she produces. 

7. Cotton, woolen, and leather goods, paper, and metal products such as watches, jewelry, firearms, machinery, silverware and cutlery, are among the products turned out by New England factories. 

8. New England depends upon her railroads and harbors to carry away her manufactured articles and to import what she needs. Boston, Fall River, and

Portland all have fine harbors and do much exporting and importing. Among the chief imports are cotton, wool, hides, sugar, molasses, coffee, oil, china clay, and wood pulp. 

9. New England is a natural playground. Her mountains, lakes, forests, streams, and ocean beaches, and her many historic spots draw many visitors every year.

TWO LEADING CROPS OF THE CENTRAL FARMING REGION 

1 

(1) (2) Kansas State Board of Agriculture. (3) (5) Caterpillar Tractor Company. (4) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. 

1. Plowing fields for wheat or corn. The tractor is drawing a "gang" of three plows. With this outfit one man does more work in a shorter time than could be done by three horse-drawn plows and their drivers. 

2. A field of winter wheat on the Kansas plains. The heads of wheat have formed and the grain is beginning to ripen. From the figure of the man you can judge the height of the grain. The farms in the Central farming region are large and far apart, so there are few near neighbors. 

3. A fleet of harvesters at work on a wheat farm in 

Kansas. These harvesters gather the wheat from one hundred acres in an hour. One million bushels of wheat were produced in one year on this enormous prairie farm. 

4. Missouri corn fields. Corn is our most valuable crop. 

Although corn is raised in many other parts of the country, most of our crop is raised in the Central farming region. Modern concrete highways have replaced the earth roads, which were almost impassable in wet weather. 

5. Picking and husking corn in an Illinois cornfield. 

Where fields are small, the ears are picked and husked by hand. In the immense fields of the corn belt, however, machines like this pull the ears from the stalks, husk them, and dump them in wagons. The entire outfit is drawn by the tractor and is operated by one man.

GLIMPSES OF THE CENTRAL FARMING REGION 

(6) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. (7) Omaha Chamber of Commerce. (8) Kansas State Board of Agriculture. (9) St. Louis News Service. 

6. Hampshire pigs in an alfalfa pasture in Iowa. The white band around the body is a distinguishing mark of this breed. Most of the corn raised in the Central farming region is used to fatten pigs and beef cattle. The farmer on the wagon is throwing feed to the pigs. 

7. View of the stockyards in Omaha, Nebraska. Here cattle are received from the Great plains and sold to farmers of the Central plains for fattening, and here hogs and fattened cattle are received from the farms and sold to the packing houses. The animals are driven to the pens through narrow lanes. Overhead walks enable men to reach any of the pens. Notice the feed racks and the bales of hay. 

8. A grinding floor of a flour mill in Atchison, Kansas. 

The grain is fed into the tops of the machines from the floor above. The grain passes through a series of these grinders, each containing a pair of steel rollers with slightly ridged surfaces. Dust and chaff are drawn off by suction through the round pipes near the tops of the grinders. 

9. A view of the Municipal Plaza of St. Louis, Missouri. 

In the center of this picture we see the Civil Courthouse. 

The statue is of Pierre Laclede-Liguest, a French merchant who founded the city as a post for trade with the Indians. Today, St. Louis is the leading commercial center of the Central farming region.

THE FARMING REGIONS OF THE CENTRAL PLAINS THE CORN BELT 

Do you remember the story of how Australia was settled? First, prisoners were sent to its shores from England. Then a few brave men and women went there who were willing to work hard to make homes for themselves in a strange, new country. They took with them their families, their domestic animals, and a few household goods. They did not intend to come back, for it is the spirit of the pioneer to go ahead. They settled on the fertile eastern coast. Later they crossed the Great 

Dividing range and moved on into the grasslands to the west and into other parts of this smallest continent. The Australian settlers accomplished much and their work is still going on.

The settlement of lands in North America began in much the same way as in Australia, but the earliest white men to come here were not prisoners.

EARLY SETTLERS 

First the east coast was settled. 

Then pioneers, on horseback, on foot, or in canoes or flatboats, followed the streams, and the Indian trails through the Cumberland gap, into the valley of the Ohio river. They even made a few settlements in this land west of the 

Appalachian highland.

After the Revolutionary War was over, more and more people crossed the mountains to make homes in the western lands. Little groups of them set out with their families, their household goods and necessary provisions loaded on flatboats or in great wagons. 

The travel was slow and the difficulties were many.

In what is now Ohio and Indiana the settlers found rich forest-covered lowlands. Here was much work to be done. All the men helped in cutting the trees and clearing the land for crops. Rude, but comfortable, little homes were built of the rough timber, and shelters were made for the farm animals. 

Gradually roads were opened from one settlement to another. In wet, muddy places logs were put down crosswise and very close together. How the wagons joggled over these rough roads! As roadbuilding progressed slowly, much of the travel was by flatboats and rafts on the streams.

Because of the difficulties of building roads, and because the villages were usually far apart, each group of early settlers was, for a time, shut off almost entirely by itself. The settlers were very lonely, as we would be if we lived far from our friends and had no rapid means of going to see them or of communicating with them.

Indians in the region were not friendly. 

Like the black men of Australia, they had been pushed back by the advancing frontier of the white men, and they did not like it. Frequent battles occurred between the Indians and the settlers. For protection against enemies, each settlement had a fort where all might gather in time of danger. Sometimes a whole settlement, fort as well as homes, was burned by the Indians and many lives were lost. But still the great work of developing the region went on. 

The pioneers found deep, rich soil in the lands they cleared. They found, too, many swamps that caused sickness because diseasecarrying insects bred in them. In time these swamps were drained and added to the farm lands. 

Stretching west from these early settlements there were miles and miles of flat land, almost treeless except along the streams. The pioneers considered this treeless land quite worthless at first. They were used to having trees right at hand to furnish them with lumber for their homes and buildings. Since trees did not grow here, they thought the soil was not good for crops. THE CORN BELT 

65 ever, who came to feel that this flat prairie land might be made to yield good crops, and they decided to try and make it do so. 

As they found little timber to use in building houses, the settlers lived in huts built with blocks of sod. It was not an easy life. There was practically no fuel, and little of anything else they could use. But when the settlers came to work the soil they soon discovered that it was even richer than the land they had left. The thrifty, hardworking pioneers quickly saw that it was far too valuable to be wasted, and they planted as much of the ground as their scanty numbers could look after. Soon stories traveled eastward that the prairies had much to give settlers, and new workers came to the land.

FARMING METHODS 

This vast region west of the Appalachian highland is known today as the Central plains. 

From the small fields of the early pioneers have grown the great farms of the the central United 

States. The early farmers used rude wooden plows drawn by horses and oxen. But even so they reaped rich harvests. Now tractors and other modern machines are used to furrow and cultivate the soil, and to plant and cut and gather the crops. Improved roads and railroads have helped to carry this machinery to the farms and so to bring about better farming methods. Though the machines are very expensive, the time and labor they save make their purchase well worth while.

Most of the men who settled on the Central plains had been engaged in mixed farming in 

THE CORN 

BELT their eastern homes. So when they moved west, they started right in to raise many different crops. They found that some of these did not grow very well, some grew better than others, and corn grew best of all. Before long, corn came to be the principal crop, and today a great section of the Central plains is known as the corn belt. 

If you look at the map, page 32, you will see that the corn belt is farther north than the cotton belt. This means that its growing season is shorter. But in the summer the days are long and even the nights are warm. Rainfall is moderate and comes in the form of heavy showers, followed by sunshine. Droughts do not often occur. Here, then, are the very conditions necessary for growing corn. 

Look at the map again and study the boundaries of the corn belt. You will see that the corn belt extends into the Great plains of 

South Dakota and Nebraska. Beyond this western boundary dry spells occur more often, so less corn can be grown there. Temperature limits the belt on the north, for there the climate is cooler and the summers are too short for growing corn.

67 tween states followed these original dividing lines, and were also straight. Where state borders in this region are irregular, you will see that they are formed, for the most part, by the Mississippi river and its main tributaries or by other bodies of water. Trace these on the map, page 64.

The Central plains are level and flat. This is convenient for travel, and highways have been built across them in straight lines. Many of these roads follow the property lines laid out by the government and cross each other at right angles.

The corn belt is worthy of its name. Nearly 

3,000,000,000 bushels of corn are produced in 

THE VALUE OF THE CORN CROP 

the United States yearly, and most of it is raised in this region. Corn is our most valuable crop and is grown successfully in many smaller sections throughout the country.

LIVESTOCK 

It may surprise you to know that most of the corn raised in this country is fed to pigs and cattle. But think how much meat is needed by our rapidly growing population. Good meat is so important, especially to those who do heavy physical labor, that thousands of animals must be raised yearly to feed our workers. And corn fattens livestock rapidly and cheaply. The corn belt raises more hogs and choice lambs for market than any other part of North America. Cattle are sent there from the Great plains to be fattened on corn and alfalfa during the fall and winter for market.

DAIRYING 

Dairying is carried on in all the corn belt states, but mainly in Ohio, Illinois, Indiana, and Iowa. Cities of these states are distributing centers for milk, butter, and cheese. They are in excellent communication by railroad with other cities of the plains. Wisconsin, north of the corn belt, is the leading dairy state of our country, surpassing even New York state in the value of its dairy products.

Most of the mixed farming carried on in the corn belt is the raising of grains and forage crops. These include oats, wheat, and hay, much of it alfalfa. MIXED 

FARMING 

The greatest advantage of mixed farming is that it allows crops to be rotated, or changed, from year to year. Crops are planted to put back plant food used by earlier crops, and in this way the soil is kept fertile and a farm keeps its value. Another advantage of mixed farming is that

This allows the farmer to use his time to the best advantage. The raising of several crops also protects the farmer from heavy loss. If one crop fails he still has the others to market.

WINTER 

WHEAT Wheat that is planted in the autumn and harvested the next summer is known as winter wheat. Kansas raises more winter wheat than any other state in the country. As winter wheat is harvested a little while after the corn is planted, a farmer in northern Kansas can keep busy nearly all the growing season raising and harvesting these two crops. Nebraska and Illi-

NORTH AMERICA 

nois, which lie largely in the corn belt, also produce winter wheat. The winter wheat area extends into Oklahoma and Texas. COAL OIL The corn belt is like the cotton belt in having more products than its name suggests. Not all these products are agricultural. Do you remember some minerals found in the Southern states? Some states of the corn belt have very valuable coal deposits. Soft coal, which is found largely in Illinois, Iowa, Missouri, and Indiana is an aid to the industrial development of these states. Oil is found beneath some of the rich soil in Kansas and Illinois.

GROWTH 

OF CITIES 

Agriculture and stock raising were the first occupations of the people of the Central plains. 

As these industries grew, the need arose for trade centers where the farmers could sell their products and buy the things they required. So trading villages grew up. When railroads were built into the region they helped farming methods by bringing in new kinds of machinery. They also helped industry by bringing in raw materials quickly, and by carrying products to market. All this meant greater trade and the little trading villages grew into cities.

Even when boats were run by crude paddle wheels, the Mississippi was a great trade route, and along this river, and along the Missouri, small trading posts were established. These later became the largest trading, manufacturing, and commercial cities of the Central plains. 

The most important of these river cities is St. Louis, in Missouri. Steamboats were re- 

ST. LOUIS 

sponsible for the early development of St. Louis. The city is located near the point where the Missouri joins the Mississippi and is not far from the mouth of the Illinois river. So it was a natural center for river traffic to the north, south, and west. 

After 1850 a network of railroads was built across the level Central plains. As many of these roads met in St. Louis, the city became still more important in trade and commerce. 

Review the products of the corn belt and you will understand the industries carried on in St. Louis. Cattle are brought to its stockyards and slaughter houses to be sold or killed.

Hides are tanned and boots and shoes are made of the leather.

Fur was sold in St. Louis by early hunters and trappers, and the city is still a great fur market. Here are prepared the skins of the fur seals killed in Alaska. Clothing is manufactured. Nearby mines supply soft coal for factories which make tools, cars, machinery, and tobacco. Other goods are brought in to be sold, so that St. Louis has grown from a little trading post to the business center for many states to the south and west of it. 

Kansas City, Missouri, and Kansas City,

Kansas, are practically a single city on the Missouri river, in a rich agricultural district. Meat packing, flour milling, and oil refining are the leading industries. KANSAS 

CITY 

Topeka, in Kansas, and Omaha, in Nebraska, both manufacture agricultural implements and both are railroad centers and distributing centers for livestock and TOPEKA grain.

OMAHA 

COLUMBUS DES MOINES INDIANAPOLIS 

Columbus, the capital of Ohio, Des Moines, the capital of Iowa, and Indianapolis, the capital of Indiana, are located near the centers of their states. 

They are connected with each other and with large cities in every direction by many railroads and highways. They are all three distributing centers, and manufacture much the same products as the other cities of the corn belt. Farmers from the country nearby can get nearly everything required in the way of clothing, tools and household supplies from these cities. Each has a fine university, and here you will see many of the fine office buildings, homes, schools and churches which are a part of every prosperous city of our great country. 

There are many other flourishing cities in the Central plains. Most of them are trading centers for farming regions. 

Springfield, Elgin, and Rockford, in

Illinois, have factories which make fine watches. Springfield and Akron, cities in

Ohio, are centers of the rubber industry. OTHER 

CITIES

THE CORN BELT 

69 There, automobile tires and other rubber goods are made. Dayton is famous for cash registers. Springfield, Illinois, is a manufacturing center for farming tools. This city is also of interest to all Americans as the burial place of Abraham Lincoln.

1. The corn belt of the United States lies largely in the 

Central plains. The states which lie partly in the corn belt are Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, Nebraska, South Dakota, Missouri, Kansas, and Minnesota. 

2. The land of the corn belt is rich and fertile and generally level. Roads and highways are built straight across the Central plains, and in this region state boundaries are generally more regular than in the east. 

3. The corn belt is so named because the necessary conditions for raising corn are found in this large area. The growing season is shorter than that of the cotton belt, but there are warm summer days and nights. Rainfall comes in the form of heavy showers, and is followed by sunshine. 

4. Corn is the most valuable crop of the United States. 

It is raised in many other smaller regions outside the corn belt. Much of the corn crop is fed to hogs and cattle, which are raised for meat. 

5. Dairying and mixed farming are carried on in the states of the corn belt. Besides corn, the crops in this region are mostly grain and forage crops. Among them are wheat, oats, and hay. 

6. More winter wheat is raised in Kansas than in any other state. Winter wheat is planted in the autumn and harvested in the early summer, a little while after the corn is planted. The winter wheat region extends

7. Deposits of soft coal are found in the corn belt, especially in Illinois, Missouri, Indiana and Iowa. These aid manufacturing and the growth of cities in these states. 

8. Cities grew up in the Central plains because farmers there needed centers for marketing crops and for securing supplies. 

9. The Mississippi was an important trade route before the development of railroads. Trading and manufacturing cities grew up along this river and the Missouri. 

10. St. Louis is the most important city of the corn belt. It grew up as a fur-trading center. It has large stockyards and slaughter houses, and manufactures boots and shoes, clothing, and tools. Kansas 

City in Missouri is in a rich agricultural district and carries on meat packing and the manufacture of flour. Topeka and Omaha are distributing centers for livestock and grain.

Other cities of the Central plains are Kansas City in Kansas, Springfield, Akron and Dayton in Ohio, and Springfield in Illinois.

THE FARMING REGIONS OF THE CENTRAL PLAINS THE SPRING WHEAT REGION 

In the central part of our continent there was once an immense lake. It was formed by the melting of the great glacier. As the glacier melted back to the north the waters of the lake found their way into Hudson bay.

The valley of the Red River of the North, in the Central plains, is now the richest section of this old lake bottom. It includes parts of Minnesota and North Dakota in the United 

States, and Manitoba in Canada. From this valley, miles and miles of deep, fertile soil stretch west into the Great plains of North and 

South Dakota and Nebraska. To the north this rich land extends into the provinces of 

Manitoba, Alberta, and Saskatchewan in

Canada. This whole great, fertile area is the spring wheat region of North America. The whole region is so level, and so free from stones, that agricultural machines can be used there to the best possible advantage.

THE SPRING WHEAT REGION 

71 The spring wheat belt, like the corn and cotton belts, is limited by climate. On the west the lack of sufficient rain marks the limit. As we go north, farther and farther into LIMITS OF Canada, the climate grows colder 

THE BELT 

and colder and the summers shorter so that the grain does not have time to ripen. 

The part of the spring wheat belt lying in the United States has a growing season of three to five months, followed by a long, dry, very cold winter. Experiments with different varieties of wheat are beginning to produce more hardy kinds that will grow in cooler sections, well beyond the present limits of the spring wheat belt.

SETTLEMENT 

The pioneers in the corn belt did not push their way into the spring wheat belt for two reasons. Farther north from where they were happily settled they knew the climate was much cooler and was unsuited to corn, their principal crop. 

Brave as they were, they did not care to face the hardships of a severe winter in a country where easy communication with other settlements would be impossible. 

But with the coming of the railroads the development of the wheat country began in earnest. Wheat is needed by people almost everywhere, and the demand for it has steadily increased. Year by year, the spring wheat belt has become a busier and more important region, and its population, though still sparse, is growing.

We have learned that Kansas raises more wheat than any other state, and that the wheat 

PLANTING 

AND

HARVESTING 

grown in Kansas is winter wheat, planted in autumn and harvested in early summer. 

The Kansas winters are not cold enough to keep the wheat from growing. 

If you traveled north, into North Dakota, in April, you would find the farmer there just beginning to plant his wheat crop, which is called spring wheat. The wheat in this region is planted in the spring because the winter weather is often so cold that the young plants would die.

April is the best month for planting spring wheat. At that time the ground is soft from light rains, and the grain sprouts quickly.

Heavy rains come early in the summer and make the wheat grow. Dry weather, in late summer and fall, ripens the grain for harvest. 

It also lessens the risk of damage to the crop, for heavy rains would beat the grains from the

Because the fall season in the northern wheat belt is dry, threshing can be done out of doors and large barns are not needed to protect the grain. 

Threshing is separating the wheat grain from the stalks and from the hulls.

In the spring wheat belt, wheat is grown in such quantities that the broad, golden fields of ripened grain seem almost endless. But wonderful machines that do a tremendous amount of work in a short time have been invented for planting, cutting, gathering, and threshing the grain. As a result these wheat regions are able to supply not only the wheat needed for Canada and our own country but also great quantities for export to other lands.

NORTH AMERICA 

Mills near the wheat belt are many and large, and barrels and barrels of flour are 

MILLING AND SHIPPING 

WHEAT turned out by them every day. 

Unmilled wheat is stored in great grain elevators near the wharves and railroads of the larger nearby cities. Grain from these elevators is constantly being loaded on boats and trains to be sent abroad or carried to mills and cities in different parts of our own land. Much of the grain is sent by train to the Great lakes ports, from which large barges carry it to the east.

Mixed farming is carried on in the spring wheat region. Oats, barley, rye, flax, and po- MIXED 

FARMING 

tatoes are raised. The flax is raised for its seed which, when crushed, yields linseed oil. This oil is used for making varnishes.

TEXAS ALL OTHER STATES 

100 150 200 This graph shows the states which lead in the production of oats. Each unit of the graph scale represents fifty million bushels. How does 

Iowa's oat crop compare with that of Minnesota? 250 Although the soil of the spring wheat belt is exceptionally well suited to growing potatoes, this crop is not raised to the extent it might be. Potatoes are heavy and so are expensive to ship, and they do not keep well when shipped in cold weather. Besides, potatoes are grown in so many sections of the country that there is not a large demand for wheat belt potatoes. So the farmers of the spring wheat belt, as a rule, raise only the potatoes required for their own use.

DAIRY PRODUCTS 

Many farmers in the spring wheat region keep dairy cows. The cows, grazing on the land, help keep up the fertility of the soil. The care of the cows. and the making of milk into dairy products provide employment when the wheat and other crops do not demand attention. 

CITIES 

One of the last regions of our country to be settled, the wheat belt has so far developed only a few large cities. Some started as lumbering settlements, because near them there was plenty of growing timber, but their present importance depends mainly on the wheat industry. 

Cities within the spring wheat belt are usually distributing centers for the rich farms around them, and do not manufacture extensively. Among them are Fargo and Grand 

Forks, in North Dakota. Both are railroad centers and gateway cities to the west. 

The largest city which serves the belt is 

Minneapolis, in Minnesota. Minneapolis began as a lumbering settlement. It is located on the 

Mississippi river, near the Falls of St. Anthony.

These falls originally supplied the water power for lumber mills. Now the power is used mainly to grind the grain from the wheat lands. In fact, in Minneapolis more wheat is ground into flour than in any other city of the United States. 

Just below the Falls of St. Anthony, only a little way from Minneapolis, is St. Paul. Being at the head of navigation on the Mississippi, the city began as a river port. 

Minneapolis and St. Paul are so much alike and so close together that they have been called the Twin Cities. Trade comes to them up the 

Mississippi river from the south. They are connected by railroad with the Great lakes ports. They are on a trans-continental railroad, which extends east and west across North America. There are sawmills in the Twin 

Cities, but their great industry centers around the flour mills. The Twin Cities also manufacture agricultural implements. Minneapolis makes great quantities of linseed oil. Winnipeg, in Canada, is like Minneapolis, in being the center of the flour industry of its country.

THE SPRING WHEAT REGION 

73 

1. The spring wheat region of North America includes parts of the states of North and South Dakota, Minnesota, and Nebraska, and stretches north into

Canada. The richest part of the area is the valley of the Red river of the North. The land of the spring wheat belt is so flat and so free from stones that agricultural machines can be used there to the best possible advantage. 

2. Wheat requires light rains to soften the soil at planting time, heavy rainfall during the growing season, and dry weather for the ripening and harvesting periods. These conditions prevail in the spring wheat belt. 

3. The wheat is planted, cut, gathered, and threshed by great machines. Much of it is sent to mills to be ground into flour. Unmilled grain is stored in great grain elevators near the wharves and railroads of the principal nearby cities until it can be shipped to other cities in our land and abroad. 

4. Other crops of the spring wheat belt are oats, rye, barley, potatoes, and flax. Flax is raised for its seed which produces an oil used in making varnishes. 

Although the land is excellent for potatoes, few are grown, because they are expensive to ship and the demand is not large enough. Dairying is carried on in the spring wheat belt. 

5. The cities within the spring wheat belt are largely distributing centers for the rich farms around them. 

Two of these cities are Fargo and Grand Forks in 

North Dakota. They are gateway cities to the west. 

6. Minneapolis and St. Paul are large flour-milling cities. Minneapolis leads the cities of the country in the making of flour. Winnipeg, in Canada, is the wheat center of that country.

The Mississippi basin includes the area drained by the Mississippi river and its tributaries. It is one of the great river basins of the world. The Mississippi basin lies almost entirely in the United States and covers two-fifths of the area of the country. Parts of several natural regions lie within its boundaries. 

The Mississippi river has many tributaries. A number of these are great streams which are navigable by large river steamers. Find the Ohio, the Cumberland, the Tennessee, the Missouri, the Arkansas, and the Red rivers. Of these streams, the Missouri is the largest and longest, but the Ohio has the greatest river traffic. Notice the many lesser streams which are also parts of the great Mississippi river system. Now name the states which lie wholly or in part in the Mississippi basin. 74

OPEN SPACES IN THE GREAT PLAINS

(1) Northern Pacific Railway. (2) North Dakota Agricultural College. (3) Union Pacific System. (4) Great Northern Railway. 

1. A roundup on a Montana cattle ranch. The cattle are driven together by cowboys, whom we see on their horses. At the roundup the young calves are branded with hot irons having the owner's mark, to show to whom they belong. These white-faced cattle are Herefords, which are very popular with cattlemen of the 

Great plains. Herefords grow fast and make beef of fine quality. 

2. A scene in the Bad lands of North Dakota. The 

Bad lands are a part of the Great plains. The occasional very heavy rains have cut deep gullies with steep banks through the clay soil. Travel in this district is very difficult. 

3. A buffalo herd in Yellowstone National park, Wyoming. When explorers first crossed the Great plains, millions of these shaggy beasts roamed over the broad, flat lands. But Indians and hunters killed most of them. 

Today there are only a few left, and these are carefully protected by our government. 

4. Bringing hay for the sheep. Although sheep need less food than cattle, there are many parts of the Great plains where forage is so scanty that it is necessary to feed the sheep from time to time in order to keep them in good condition. These sheep seem to know that dinner is arriving.

CITIES AND INDUSTRIES OF THE GREAT PLAINS 

(5) United States Beet Sugar Ass'n. (6) Caterpillar Tractor Co.

Western R. R. 

(7) Cheyenne Chamber of Commerce. 

(8) Denver and Rio Grande 

5. A beet-sugar factory in Colorado. Much of our sugar is made from sugar beets, which are raised in the cool climate of this part of the Great plains. The tremendous piles outside the factory are beets waiting to be made into sugar. They were piled by the machines shown in the picture. 

6. Harvesting sugar beets in Colorado. A gasoline tractor is used to draw the beet puller which takes the beets from the ground much faster than they could be pulled by hand. The flat surface of the Great plains makes it possible to use agricultural machinery in planting and harvesting practically all crops. The water needed for these beets is supplied by irrigation. 

7. Cheyenne, the capital of Wyoming. The large building near the center of the picture is the state Capitol. 

Cheyenne is a distributing center and shipping point in the western part of the Great plains. It is not a very large city. Though few of the plains cities are as large as those of eastern United States, they are very important to the regions they serve. 

8. Denver, the largest city of the Great plains. Denver is at the very edge of the plains, and behind the city we can see the high, rugged peaks of the Rocky mountains.

The Great plains slope upward from the Mississippi valley toward the west, and the city is a mile above sea level. 

The pure, dry air makes Denver a health resort.

THE GREAT PLAINS 

The map on page 32 will show you that the Great plains of the United States lie largely in the Mississippi basin. They stretch from 

Alberta, Canada, through western Texas. The

Rocky mountains form their western boundary, and on the east they join the Central plains, where they have their lowest altitude. 

The states which lie partly within the Great plains are North and South Dakota, Nebraska, Kansas, Montana, Wyoming, Colorado, Oklahoma, Texas, and New Mexico.

These widespread plains, a land of ranches, are the scene of many exciting western stories.

In these stories there are cowboys and roundups and fierce struggles between ranchers over the ownership of cattle. There are outlaws who hide in the canyons and gullies of the Bad lands and in the Black hills. Only to perform their desperate deeds do they ride out on to the plains. There they do not feel safe, because the land is so nearly flat and has so little high vegetation that a man on horseback can be seen from far off.

The Great plains were once called "buffalo lands" because great herds of buffaloes roamed over them. When the western railroads were first built, the buffaloes would sometimes get on the tracks and stop the trains. These big animals are seen no more on the Great plains, for the Indians and the buffalo hunters killed most of them. There are still a few in Yellowstone National park, Wyoming, and one herd, which escaped into Canada, has grown quite large.

SURFACE 

The Great plains are now called "grasslands," because their chief vegetation is clumps of grass, known as bunch grass. 

This and sagebrush, a low shrub which grows in dry areas, are often the only kinds of vegetation you will see for miles and miles. 

From east to west the Great plains slope gently upward. When they reach the mountains they are between 3000 and 4000 feet above sea level.

Their slope is so gentle that you would hardly notice it, and the Great plains actually appear in many places to be as flat as a table top, with little in sight but the horizon.

The Bad lands, which are found in South 

Dakota, Nebraska, and Wyoming, certainly deserve their name. Here there are long dry periods. The occasional very heavy rains have cut deep gulleys through the clay soil, molding the land into the oddest of shapes. Traveling is very rough in the Bad lands and the region has little to tempt visitors.

There are a few forest spots in the Great plains region. One of these is the Black hills in 

South Dakota and Wyoming. The Black hills get their name from the dark forests of evergreen trees which cover their slopes. These hills are really very valuable because of their lumber and their large deposits of gold and other minerals.

Trees are also found along the streams which cross the plains. These streams start from the snowy summits of the Rocky mountains. The Missouri river is the largest of them. Because of the scant rainfall, many of the smaller streams dry up and never reach the plains. In many places on the plains, there are no rivers at all, not even little streams, and the country is nearly a desert. 

CLIMATE 

In crossing the Great plains, one becomes rather tired of the seemingly endless stretches of level land, and welcomes the sight of the towering Rocky mountains when they first appear in the west. They look so cool and beautiful. They are cool and beautiful. But it is these mountains that are responsible for the dustiness and bareness of the Great plains. As the winds from the 

Pacific ocean blow up the western mountain slopes the air becomes cooler and most of the moisture falls as rain before the mountain tops are crossed. When the winds descend to the plains on the eastern side of the mountains the 75

NORTH AMERICA 

air becomes warmer, and not only holds what little moisture it has, but takes up more from the streams. If it were not for the moisture that occasional winds bring from the Gulf of Mexico, the plains would be even drier than they are. 

Shut away from the great oceans by high mountains on the west and other mountains and wide plains on the east, the Great plains have a true continental climate. In summer the heat is intense. Occasionally a tornado, or very severe windstorm traveling in a narrow path and at a high rate of speed, crosses a part of this section and does much damage. 

The winters are generally mild in the southern part, although Texas has many windstorms which cause the temperature to drop very rapidly, and which at times even bring snow. In the northern part of the Great plains the winters are very cold and there are often blizzards. Blizzards are wind storms with stinging, blinding snow and sleet. Many people have been lost in these blizzards, and large herds of cattle have frozen to death.

SETTLEMENT 

This must be a hard country, you will think, in which to make a living. Perhaps that is the reason it was not settled to any extent until about 1869, though the first permanent settlement in our country was as early as 1565. Many hardy pioneers crossing these plains pushed on rapidly, as they thought this no country in which to live. A few attempted to settle on the plains, but grew discouraged and moved away. Others settled and stayed. All through the history of our country there have been brave men and women like those who settled on these plains. 

They were willing to face the hardships and dangers of life in a new section, and by doing so opened the way for others to follow. 

With the completion of a transcontinental railroad in 1869, more people came to the Great plains. The population, however, has never become very large, and it is said that in all this vast region there are no more people than in the single city of Chicago, Illinois. 

The first people to settle on the plains built 

RANCHING 

their homes along the rivers, where the soil had some moisture and there was a little shade. They built their houses of sod and lived a poor and lonely sort of life. They tried to raise a few crops, and kept beef cattle and horses. There was not enough grass for dairy cattle. Later the settlers started raising sheep. Each flock of sheep had a shepherd. He lived entirely alone out on the plains, with his sheep and a dog. Often he saw no human beings for months. 

As the railroads opened up this section to the markets of the east, the settlers found their cattle raising and sheep raising very profitable. The little farms with their sod houses

Each unit of the graph scale represents ten million cattle. 80 grew into great ranches with comfortable ranch houses. The small herds of cattle and sheep became enormous herds, so large that much pasture land was needed. At first the animals were free to wander as they pleased,

THE GREAT PLAINS 

77 as there was hardly a fence in the whole territory. This sometimes led to bitter quarrels. 

One man might keep cattle and another sheep. 

Sheep spoil the grazing for cattle, as their feet leave an oily substance on the grass which the cattle do not like.

As more people came and settled on the land the days of unfenced ranges reached an end. A rancher now fences his many acres of land. 

Often one man is kept busy riding around these fences to keep them in order. Cattle and sheep are given different grazing pastures. The sheep are given the poorer land, as they will eat much that cattle will not touch, and they eat the vegetation very closely. 

As the ranchers have prospered, living conditions have greatly improved. The ranchers have better homes and send their children away to school. They run their affairs in a very businesslike manner and some of them make a living by housing eastern guests who go west to see what ranch life is really like.

INDUSTRIES AND PRODUCTS 

The raising of sheep and cattle has become a very important industry in the United States. 

This country now ranks third among sheep-raising nations. Both Montana and 

Wyoming are famous for their sheep. Billings,

Montana, is a sheep center and a wool market. 

Texas is another sheep state. Western Texas raises goats, whose wool is made into plush for covering upholstered seats. 

Very little of a sheep is wasted. The wool is sent to the woolen mills of New England. 

The meat appears as mutton on many a Sunday dinner table. The hide is tanned into leather and the bones are sold as fertilizer.

Cattle, too, are put to many uses, some of which would surprise you. Their meat, of course, is their most valuable product. But various parts of them are also made into leather, bristles for hair-brushes, soap, violin strings, gelatin, and even medicine. When we speak of cattle on the Great plains, we are not, of course, thinking of dairy cows, but of beef cattle. You remember that dairy cows are kept for their milk. Beef cattle are raised for their meat and for their hides. 

To feed their animals during the winter, the ranchers must raise forage crops. The animals in the south need less of these, as they graze outdoors all year round. The forage crops are alfalfa, sorghum, Kafir corn and some kinds of wheat. All of these crops need very little moisture to make them grow. 

Not all of the settlers in the Great plains own great ranches. You will see many little homes scattered far apart. The owner of one of these may

SMALL FARMS 

raise a few cattle or sheep, or do a little farming, and if he is near the hills perhaps he tries his hand at mining.

IRRIGATION 

Some places in the Great plains look as if a bit of the fertile coastal plain had been transplanted. There are farmhouses quite close together. There are gardens, orchards, and fields of rich crops. 

These are made possible by irrigation. You have heard of the wonders of irrigation in studying about Egypt and the Egyptians, who use the Nile to water their dry lands. 

When you send the waters of a river through the land in little ditches to moisten the fields, it is called irrigation. In the Great plains, the 

North Platte river, the Arkansas river, and other rivers, are used for irrigating the land. 

To do this our government has built great dams across the rivers where their valleys are narrow. 

Of course, you know that when you build a dam across a little creek, the water forms a pool just above the dam. In the case of a river the water forms a lake or reservoir. Reservoir means storage place, and such a lake is a storage place for the water until it is needed. 

Canals carry the water from the reservoir to the surrounding country, and ditches carry the water from the canals into the fields. Smaller ditches carry the water from the bigger ones between the rows of plants so that each plant receives moisture. In the larger ditches are water gates, which can be opened when the fields need water and closed when they have had enough. In this way the water is conserved, or saved. Some farmers say irrigation is better than depending upon rainfall because they have the water when they need it and can let in only as much as they need. NASA-5

NORTH AMERICA 

Of course, the farmers must pay the government for this water, and perhaps help pay for building the dams and the canals, but they are glad to do so. Their land would be of little value without irrigation, but with it even a small piece can raise rich crops, for the soil is very fertile.

CROPS Alfalfa is an important crop because it can be gathered several times during a season. It is also a good forage crop. Much wheat, corn, and other grains are grown in irrigated land. Sugar beets are raised. Western Texas is famous for its onions and Colorado for its cantaloupes. Hardy fruits and vegetables are grown, and in the south even cotton and dates are raised. As rule the farmers choose those crops that need the least moisture.

DRY 

FARMING 

Another way of raising crops in the dry regions of the plains is by dry farming. The land is carefully plowed, and is gone over many times to keep the soil loose. Loose soil holds the rain and prevents evaporation better than closepacked soil. Very often, in dry farming, the farmer will let a field lie idle one year, so that when he plants his crop the next year it will have the benefit of two years' rain. 

Wheat is the most important crop of the dry farming areas, and the wheat of these sections is especially valuable. It is hard and dry, and makes better flour than the softer and more starchy wheat which is grown in irrigated regions or where there is plentiful rain. 

Kafir corn and millet, two plants much like corn, are grown for fodder. The hard work of dry farming is done by machinery, so that a few men can cultivate a large area and produce heavy crops.

Except in the Black hills, mining in the Great plains has not been very extensive. There are 

MINING 

coal beds and oil fields which when properly worked should be a means of income to the region. Sheridan, Wyoming, is a coal and oil center.

You would not expect to find so many large 

CITIES 

cities in the Great plains as you did on the Atlantic coast. Cities grow up where people come together to carry on manufacturing and commerce, but not where they live far apart on farms and ranches. Of course, there are some towns and cities in the Great plains, especially along the river valleys. 

Some of these were once trading and outfitting centers for the early settlers. Some of them are now centers for shipping meat and wool to the eastern markets.

In the borderland between the Great plains and the Rocky mountains you will find the rich and beautiful city of Denver, Colorado. It is beautiful because it is on a high plateau, with many mountain peaks and ranges in the distance. The city is very clean and looks very bright because so many buildings are built of white stone. The streets are broad and carefully laid out. The air is dry and bracing but newcomers have to be careful not to overexercise until they become accustomed to it. This is because of the city's high altitude. The higher we get above sea level the lighter we find the air. Some people who have lived in the air of low-lying regions have trouble at first in breathing the air of high places. 

Denver is a rich city because the mountains on the one hand and the plains on the other have helped to make it so. The ranchers from the plains and the miners from the mountains come here to buy their supplies. The plains send their products to Denver's meat-packing plants and flour mills. The neighboring mountains send their ores to its smelters. Denver is a railroad center and is visited by people from all parts of the country. Some of them come for their health. Many come up from the plains in the summer to escape the heat. 

Colorado Springs is also a health resort, and

Pueblo, another borderland city of Colorado, has large ore smelters for the minerals of the nearby mountains. While these three cities are in the borderland between the plains and the mountains, the mountains have done more than the plains to aid their growth.

1. The Great plains reach from Alberta, Canada, through western Texas. The Rocky mountains are their western boundary, and the Central plains their eastern. The states which lie partly within this region are North and South Dakota, Nebraska, Kansas, Montana, Wyoming, Colorado, Oklahoma, Texas and 

New Mexico. 

2. The Great plains slope gently upward to the Rocky mountains, although they look almost level. 

3. The Great plains have a continental climate. The summers are very hot, and the winters in the north are very cold, with icy blizzards. There is little rainfall, as the westerly winds drop their moisture on the west side of the mountains. The climate limits the crops, and discourages people from settling. The natural vegetation is chiefly bunch grass and sagebrush. 

4. Sheep, cattle, and horses are raised on the Great plains. In western Texas there are herds of goats.

THE INTERIOR HIGHLANDS 

Between the Central plains and the Gulf Coastal plain you will find a region of low mountains which rise like islands from the sea of land about them. They are known as the Interior highlands and are really a westward extension of the Appalachian highland.

The Interior highlands are divided into two sections by the valley of the Arkansas river. 

The highlands to the south are the Ouachita mountains. Those to the north form the 

Ozark plateau. In the Ozark plateau is a beautiful artificial lake, more than 120 miles long. 

This lake was made by building a dam across the Osage river. The water power at this dam makes electric current which is used in St. 

Louis and other cities.

OZARK 

PLATEAU 

The Ozark plateau lies partly in Missouri, partly in Arkansas, and partly in Oklahoma. It is really a plateau only in its western part. The rest of the uplands, although called mountains, are nothing more than a mass of overgrown hills formed by streams cutting through the soft limestone rock. Mixed with the soft limestone of the plateau is a hard rock called flint. As the limestone crumbles, much of it is carried down the mountains by the streams. Pieces of the flint which wears away very slowly are left on the slopes. This accounts for the fact that the Ozark mountains have a stony soil. 

Fruit Raising. At one time there was very little agriculture in the Ozark plateau, because the stony soil made it impossible to raise good crops. The people were poor. In many places about all that a family could hope to do was to raise enough to eat, and sometimes they could not even do that.

Such conditions are now practically ended. 

It was noticed that there is one thing that will grow in the Ozarks in spite of the stony soil, and that is a tree. Now many of the people of the Ozark mountains are tending orchards. 

They are proud of their fine peach trees and apple trees.

The mountains are a great help in the raising of fruit because they protect the orchards from the cold northern breezes that might blight the fruit before it is ready to pick. In other fruit-growing areas of our country where there is no such protection, the frost sometimes nips the blossoms or spoils the fruit before it can be picked and shipped away. The people of this region, who were once so poor, are becoming busy and prosperous. They are sending their children to school and are building comfortable homes. 

Other Industries. Some of the land is too rough and hilly for raising fruit, and in these sections other industries are carried on. Riding along a road in the Ozark mountains one fine morning, you might hear the tinkle of sheep bells or you might see men cutting down trees. Then, if you had studied your geography carefully, you would say to yourself, "Sheep raising and lumbering." And you would be quite right. These are two more in dustries of the Ozark region. The Ozark trees are oaks and other hard woods. 

The streams are responsible for another Ozark industry. While they were busily wearing away the soil and carving hills and valleys they also uncovered mineral deposits that had long been stored away. Coal is mined, especially near the Arkansas river valley. Lead and zinc, however, are the chief ores. They are mined all through this section. Where there are mines huge furnaces are usually built nearby, in which the metals are prepared for market. These furnaces are called ore smelters. Many people come to work in the mines and in the smelters, and soon towns and cities grow up. Joplin, Missouri, is the largest zinc and lead mining center in the country, and the state of Missouri leads all the states in the production of lead. 

Another city in the Ozarks is Springfield,

Missouri. It is a railroad repair center. There are not many railroads in this section of the 80

INDUSTRIES OF THE INTERIOR HIGHLANDS 

(1) Union Electric Light and Power Co. (2) St. Louis-San Francisco Railway. (3) U. S. Bureau of Mines. 

1. An air view of the Lake of the Ozarks, Missouri.

This beautiful lake, formed by a great dam 148 feet high, lies amid thick woodlands and rugged cliffs in the Ozark mountains. It is over 120 miles long and has 

1300 miles of shoreline. Notice how very irregular the shoreline is. The water power is changed to electricity in a hydroelectric plant at the foot of the dam. The electricity is sent by wires to St. Louis and to other cities and towns. Find the booms, or lines of timbers, which stretch from the dam into the lake to catch and stop any driftwood that might otherwise be drawn into the inlets of the power plant and damage the machinery. 

2. An apple orchard in the Ozarks. The soil of this region is well suited to fruit growing and the mountains give protection against cold winds. Apples and peaches of fine quality are grown in the sheltered valleys and on the lower mountain slopes. 

3. Digging bauxite in Arkansas. Bauxite is a mineral clay from which aluminum is made. Although bauxite is found in other parts of the country, our main supply of this clay comes from the southeastern border of the 

Interior highlands. As much electricity is needed in making aluminum, the clay is shipped to places where there are large hydroelectric power plants.

81 country, because a railway usually follows a natural route, and there are few here. The hills and valleys run in every direction, so that most rail routes avoid the region and swing around it to the north or south. 

ARKANSAS 

VALLEY

The Arkansas river valley is narrow, but it is warm and fertile. There is a drop of 1500 feet from the Ozark mountains to this valley, so you can see it must be quite a bit warmer down there than it is up in the highlands. As this warm valley lies in the cotton belt the people living there grow cotton. 

OUACHITA MOUNTAINS 

South of this lowland region are the Ouachita mountains. They stretch from Arkansas over into Oklahoma but cover less area than the Ozark plateau. 

The Ouachita mountains are not high and sharp, with jagged peaks, but are formed in ridges, just as if folds of rock had risen from the earth. 

The mountains are made of sandstone instead of limestone. But, like their neighbors, the Ozarks, they are covered with forests, and are criss-crossed with valleys. You can travel for many miles in this part of the Interior highlands without meeting more than a few people, for not many live here. 

Along the southeastern border of the Interior highlands, in Arkansas, are immense beds of the mineral clay called bauxite, from which aluminum is obtained by the use of electricity. 

Three other states Alabama, Georgia, and Tennessee also yield some of this clay, but the main supply for the entire country comes from Arkansas. The bauxite mines help to make 

Little Rock, the capital of Arkansas, the most important city in the state. It is also an important manufacturing and distributing center. 

In the southern Ouachita mountains is Hot 

Springs, Arkansas. This is a health resort and many visitors come here every year in search of health and rest.

1. The Interior highlands are a westward extension of the Appalachian highland. 

2. There are two groups of highlands here. The 

Ozark plateau, to the north, is a hilly limestone section. The Ouachita mountains, to the south, are ridges of folded sandstone. The Arkansas river valley divides these two highland regions. 

3. The Interior highlands remind one of islands of upland country surrounded by plains. Many hills and valleys were formed on the Ozark plateau by streams cutting their way through the limestone rock. 

4. The people in the Ozarks have apple and peach orchards, carry on lumbering, and raise sheep. Zinc and lead are found in large quantities. 

5. Joplin, Missouri, is a zinc and lead center. Springfield, Missouri, is a railroad repair center. 

6. Great beds of bauxite, a mineral clay from which aluminum is obtained, are found along the southeastern border of the Ouachita mountains. 

7. Little Rock is an important trading and manufacturing city and the capital of Arkansas. Hot Springs, Arkansas, is a well-known health resort.
