THE 

OHIO 

RIVER THE OHIO BASIN 

The Ohio river is the largest eastern tributary of the mighty Mississippi. It is formed in western Pennsylvania by the joining of two rivers, the Allegheny from the north and the Monongahela from the south. Where the two come together stands the busy, thriving, manufacturing city of Pittsburgh. Find this city on the map, page 28. From Pennsylvania the Ohio flows southwest and forms the border line between different states until it enters the Mississippi. Its basin includes parts of the states of 

Pennsylvania, Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, West 

Virginia, Kentucky, and Tennessee.

The southeastern half of Ohio and parts of 

Pennsylvania, West Virginia, Kentucky, and 

Tennessee are in the Cumberland-Allegheny plateau where the land is rough and the soil unfitted for many crops. The western part of the Ohio basin is one of the most fertile and prosperous sections of our country. It is rolling, hilly land, not so steep as the plateau country to the east nor so level as the plains to the west.

EARLY TRANSPORTATION DOWN THE 

OHIO When the Ohio region became part of the United States, people began to move westward to make their homes in this new country. Many of them came by way of the 

Ohio river. They brought their belongings with them on flatboats and rafts. Some of them made their homes in the 

Ohio valley. Others pushed on farther west.

In those days, some of the boats were floating stores. Their counters were filled with dry goods and other things from the east that people needed. Until the Erie canal connected the Great lakes with the Hudson river, the

Ohio was the chief means of transportation between the east and the west. At first rafts and crude boats were used to carry products down the Ohio and down the Mississippi river to New Orleans. Later came the steamboats

TRANSPORTATION 

TODAY and barges, which traveled much faster.

Today the Ohio river is an even more important and busier waterway than it was in earlier times. The river bed has been deepened and a series of canal locks built to regulate the level of the water so that large boats can travel up and down its length. When the work was finished a celebration was held at which President Hoover said: "We are reopening the great trade routes upon which our continent developed." Fleets of barges can now carry cotton, grains, sugar, tobacco, and iron ore, to towns along the river.

Two cities along the Ohio river that are important ports on this improved waterway are 

Cincinnati, Ohio, and Louisville, Kentucky. Cincinnati was one of the early 

CITIES 

river ports. When the railroads came and took away a good deal of the river trade, Cincinnati became a railroad center. It now receives goods by river or rail from all parts of the country, and ships its products in all directions. 

Clothing, machinery, boots and shoes, lumber, and tobacco are manufactured in Cincinnati.

The city is also noted for its beautiful pottery and glassware. It has stockyards where cattle, sheep, and hogs are sold, and packing plants where they are slaughtered. It has grain elevators, which store wheat and other grains until they are needed. 

Near Cincinnati is Dayton. This Ohio city is best known for its manufacture of cash registers and as the place where the Wright brothers first made airplanes. 

Louisville, the largest city in Kentucky, is also a river port and a commercial center. It owes its early growth to what is known as the Falls of the Ohio. These falls are really rapids formed by the Ohio flowing over rocky ledges. 

In the old days the falls made a break in river traffic. Here goods had to be unloaded, carried past the falls, and then reshipped. What 82

THE OHIO BASIN 

83 could be more natural than that a town should grow up beside the falls, where travelers brought trade, and goods were changed from one boat to another? Today steamboats go around the Falls of the Ohio through the Portland canal, but Louisville continues to be a trade center.

The city of Louisville is built along the river, in the midst of a rich agricultural country. It is the greatest market for tobacco in the world. 

It manufactures cigars, cigarettes, furniture, and machinery. It has repair shops for locomotives and steam railroad cars and also has meat-packing plants and tanneries. Then, too, Louisville is known for petroleum refining and lumber manufacturing. The many coal mines near the Ohio help to supply fuel for the factories.

AND 

PRODUCTS 

The climate of the Ohio basin is good for agriculture. The summers are warm, with frequent rains. The winter weather 

CLIMATE is changeable, but is seldom very cold. Along the greater part of the Ohio river are prosperous farms where mixed farming is carried on, though corn is the chief crop. On the hills, in some parts of southern Ohio, apples are grown. Tobacco is grown all through the region. In the upper Ohio valley, in Ohio, Pennsylvania, and West Virginia, sheep raising is an important industry. Merino sheep, which have very long, fine wool, are raised in Ohio. 

THE BLUE GRASS BASIN 

There are two smaller river basins which are parts of the Ohio basin. They are the Blue 

Grass basin, drained by the Kentucky river, and the Nashville basin, drained by the Cumberland and Tennessee rivers. All of these rivers flow into the Ohio. The soil of both regions is made rich by the crumbling of the limestone which underlies them. 

The Blue Grass basin lies in a half circle south of the Ohio river, in the north-central part of Kentucky. It extends east to the 

Appalachian highland. Kentucky is named the Blue Grass state because of the bluishgreen grass that grows luxuriantly in the Blue Grass basin. This grass enriches the soil and makes fine food for horses. This good grass and the mild winter climate make it possible for the horses to pasture out-of-doors all winter. 

So this region has long been a place for the breeding of fine horses. Lexington is a market for high grade saddle and racing horses.

Since the automobile has so largely replaced the horse, western beef cattle are often brought to these pastures and fattened for market. 

The soil is so fertile and the rainfall is so plentiful in the Blue Grass region that it is a fine agricultural section. Mixed farming is carried on, but tobacco is the chief crop. The 

Blue Grass region is the leading center in the country for the growing of tobacco, and Louisville is its great market. Buyers come from all parts of the United States and other countries to purchase its crops. Hemp is another important product of the Blue Grass basin. Hemp is a plant like a nettle, with tough fibers that are used for making rope.

MAMMOTH 

CAVE In many parts of Kentucky and Tennessee one finds great caverns made by the action of water on the limestone which lies below the soil. Some of the caverns are filled with beautiful crystals and strangely-carved rock. The best known is Mammoth cave, in Kentucky. It is one of the largest natural caves in the world. Though the cave itself extends back only about ten miles from its mouth, if one followed all its passages, avenues, grottoes, and streams, one would cover about 150 miles. 

NASHVILLE 

The Nashville basin lies in Tennessee between the Appalachian highland on the east and the Tennessee river on the west. Except on the north, this THE large basin is surrounded by a rim BASIN of hilly land. The rich limestone soil makes agriculture the leading industry. 

Tobacco, wheat, oats, white potatoes, and sweet potatoes are grown, but corn is the chief crop. Many hogs are raised and are fed on the corn. Hogs are sent here from other parts of the country to be fattened. You remember this is also true in the corn belt, In the forests of the surrounding hills there are oaks and other hardwood trees. These are cut and turned into lumber.

NORTH AMERICA NASHVILLE 

Nashville, the capital of Tennessee, is the center of the Nashville basin. It is on the 

Cumberland river and is an important river port and a railroad and trading center for the region. It is also a manufacturing city, whose factories are busy preparing coffee and spices, packing meats, and making stoves and other goods. Nashville is a lumbering center as well. 

The rich and fertile region of the Ohio basin has great possibilities of future development. With the great waterway of the Ohio giving it cheap connections with home and foreign markets, and with its natural resources, trade and manufacturing should steadily in- crease.

1. The Ohio basin, drained by the Ohio river, includes parts of Pennsylvania, Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, West 

Virginia, Kentucky, and Tennessee. 

2. The Ohio river was an early route of travel and trade between the east and the west. The deepening of the river bed and the building of locks have made it today an even more important trade route. 

3. Cincinnati is one of the chief commercial centers. 

Louisville, the world's greatest tobacco market, is also an important river port. 

4. Mixed farming is carried on in this region, though corn is the principal crop. The raising of sheep is an important industry in the upper Ohio valley. 

5. The Blue Grass basin in Kentucky is drained by the 

Kentucky river, and has a rich, limestone soil. Its chief crop is tobacco, a very large part of which is marketed in Louisville. Fine horses are raised on the grasslands. Lexington is the chief market for the horse trade. 

6. The Nashville basin in Tennessee is drained by the 

Cumberland and Tennessee rivers. It is a region of mixed farming with corn as its chief crop. The raising of hogs is an important industry. Nashville is the trading center of this region and the capital of Tennessee.

THE FATHER OF WATERS 

1 2 3 

(1) Great Northern Railway. (2) Frisco Lines. (3) Keystone View Co., N. Y. 

1. The northern Mississippi from the Wisconsin shore.

This part of the Mississippi is not very wide or very deep, for the river has not yet received the waters of its tributaries, the Missouri and the Ohio. Its banks are high and in many places covered with woods. Notice the rowboats drawn up on the shore. Even small boats are made with flat bottoms, as the river is so shallow here. 

2. "The Rock of the Cross," in the Mississippi just north of Cairo, Illinois. The rock was named by a French missionary in 1699. The river has formed this high island by eating away the soft shale rock around its edges. This is almost the southernmost point at which the Mississippi has high banks. To the south it flows through a wide flood plain which the river itself has built up. 

3. A part of the Mississippi delta, from a height of many thousand feet. All the flat land in this view is made up of silt deposited by the river. The left branch of the river extending toward the top of the picture is the old 

Main pass.

Because this pass led more directly toward the Atlantic ocean and Europe, it was the most used mouth of the river in the early days of New Orleans. 

It is not deep enough for present-day ocean steamships, which use deeper passes in the delta.

NORTH AND SOUTH ON THE MISSISSIPPI

4. At the head of navigation on the Mississippi. Here the Twin Cities, Minneapolis and St. Paul, lie close together, about 2000 miles from the mouth of the river. 

These cities of the north are near the spring wheat region, and their chief industries depend on the wheat farms. 

5. Along the Mississippi water front at New Orleans. 

Nearly 2000 miles south of the Twin Cities, New Orleans handles cotton, rice, and sugar from southern plantations. 

It also exports wheat and flour brought down the river. 

A number of steamships are at their docks, and others are lying at anchor awaiting their turns to be loaded or unloaded. New Orleans is one of the busiest of our southern. ports. 

6. The Keokuk dam, built across the Mississippi at 

Keokuk, Iowa. This long dam is one of the most important sources of water power in the United States. The electric current made here is used in St. Louis and many other cities. 

7. The rear view of a Mississippi river steamboat. Notice how strongly and heavily made is the wide wheel at the stern. Steamers on the Mississippi are built to travel safely in very shallow water. 

8. Building a levee to protect a bank of the lower Mississippi. This floating raft is called a mattress. It is made of concrete, and will be towed into position at a point where the river is likely to break its banks and flood the country beyond. At this point the mattress will be sunk to protect the bank.

THE FATHER OF WATERS THE MISSISSIPPI BASIN 

The Father of Waters, as the Mississippi is often called, is a fitting name for a river so old, so large, and so mighty. This river was old even before the glacier came, and no one knows exactly how long ago that was.

The Mississippi, including its chief tributary, the Missouri, is 4200 miles long, longer than any other river in the world. The Mississippi basin, drained by the Mississippi river and its many tributaries, includes a little more than two-fifths of all the land in the United States.

On the map, page 74, find and name all the states of the basin. This basin extends from the wheat fields of Minnesota through the cotton fields of Louisiana to the Gulf of Mexico, and from the Appalachians on the east to the 

Rocky mountains on the west.

THE COURSE OF THE MISSISSIPPI 

The Mississippi river rises in Lake Itasca, in northern Minnesota, and flows generally south into the Gulf of Mexico. As it journeys south it visits regions that vary greatly from one another. It flows through rocky mining sections, and past fields of golden wheat. At Minneapolis it tumbles madly over the Falls of St. Anthony. Then it flows along, joined by tributary after tributary. The Illinois and Ohio are the largest tributaries which come from the east. From the west come the 

Missouri, the Arkansas, the Red, and many other rivers. 

Then, through fertile countrysides, winds the Father of Waters passing fields of dark rich soil with fine crops and orchards where the trees are bowed with the weight of their fruit. At St. Louis, at Memphis, and at New

Orleans the river tugs at long lines of vessels moored along the shores, loading and unloading goods. Boys fish from the banks. Laborers straighten up from their work in the fields to gaze at the river's traffic. On its broad surface the Mississippi carries tugs and steamboats, barges laden with coal, and the pleasure craft of people who live along its course. Finally this great river glides into the waters of the 

Gulf of Mexico. 

At its source the Mississippi river is 1570 feet above sea level. By the time it has reached Minneapolis, Minnesota, it has fallen to 800 feet. At Cairo, Illinois, the river is only 270 feet above sea level.

EXTENT 

OF FALL 

You might compare the drop of the Mississippi with that of the Hudson river, which rises at an elevation of over 4300 feet, falls at the rate of over sixty feet per mile for a distance of some fifty miles, and has dropped to 

240 feet above tidewater before it reaches

Troy. In its course the Hudson has many falls and rapids.

Some of the tributaries of the Mississippi take greater drops than the Mississippi itself and are not even navigable, due to the falls and rapids in their courses.

One of the greatest services of the busy Mississippi is the supplying of water power to run mills and to make electricity. At the Falls of St. Anthony the river POWER furnishes Minneapolis with power to 

WATER 

run its flour mills. This city, you remember, serves a vast wheat region, and owes much of its importance and growth to its flour mills. 

Farther to the south, at Keokuk, Iowa, the river is again put to work. At this point a huge dam has been built. Here the water flows over great wheels which turn machines that make electricity. This electricity is carried by wire over 150 miles to St. Louis. There, people merely press a button or turn a switch to light their homes, run their radios, and drive the machinery in their factories. All this is done by the electricity made from the water power of the Mississippi river. 

Besides furnishing power, the Mississippi is engaged in other work. Some of this work is useful and some is not. In the thousands of years the river has SILT 85

NORTH AMERICA 

traveled along its course, it has picked up many millions of tons of sand and silt, a fine mud. 

Where it flows slowly it has dropped some of this material, which has made many square miles of new land, built up the river bed, and formed troublesome bars. 

LEVEES 

Building up the river bed, of course, brings the waters to a higher level. The land which lines the river's banks is very low, and to keep the waters of the river from flooding it, 1500 miles of high embankments have been built along the river. It is important that these embankments, called levees or dikes, be kept in good repair. 

Thousands of years ago the Gulf of Mexico extended far into central United States, probably as far as Illinois. Can you picture what a difference this would FLOOD 

PLAIN 

make in the appearance of the country? All this land where the old gulf used to be has been built up from the silt carried down by the river. It is a very level stretch of land, many miles of it being lower than the levees or built-up banks of the river. This low, level land, stretching from Illinois to the Gulf of Mexico, is called the flood plain, because it is here that the Mississippi floods occur when the river waters rise too high. 

The floods come when the spring season brings heavy rains and the snow in the highlands starts to melt. The melting snow and heavy rains swell the streams which pour into the Mississippi. Then, if the levees do not hold the water in its channel, the angry river becomes a terribly dangerous neighbor. Once over its banks, the water rushes across the land covering miles of the low-lying country, ruining crops, sweeping away houses, and making people homeless. Silt dumped near the river's mouth forms sandy islands, and where this happens the waters of the river split and go either side of the obstruction, each time making two mouths instead of one. In this way the Mississippi has acquired several mouths and has built up much land along its lower course.

The land which the river has built up along its lower part is called the Mississippi delta. DELTA The delta forms the southern end of the flood plain. New Orleans, at the head of the delta, is 100 miles from the river's mouth. This gives you some idea of the extent of the delta. The Mississippi delta is a splendid agricultural region, as the soil that is formed from silt is very rich and fertile. 

The silt that the river brings down is useful on the flood plain and the delta, but it is a nuisance when it blocks the chan- 

SAND BARS 

nel. Sand bars formed in the river by shifting sands make shallow places which are very dangerous to ships. If the captains do not know the channel they may easily run their boats aground on the bars. 

JETTIES 

Near its mouth, the river flows more slowly and drops much mud and sand. At one time the river passage to the gulf was choked with silt, and the southern commerce of New Orleans was almost cut off. As New Orleans is a great ocean as well as river port, this was very serious. Engineers were asked to solve the problem. Along the banks they built a series of great jetties, or walls, slanting downstream. These narrowed the channel and deepened it. The same amount of water flows much faster in a narrow channel than in a wide one, and the river then carried the silt farther out into the gulf waters. 

You can easily realize that because of sand bars and shallow waters, and the need of levees and jetties, it costs great sums of money to keep the Mississippi open for navigation and commerce. Very often silt must be dredged out and in places the channel must be narrowed and the water deepened. 

RIVER 

TRAFFIC Flowing through the center of our country from north to south, the Mississippi is a natural lane for travel and transportation. 

Even before the coming of the white man the Indians traveled over it from one Indian village to another. When white men came and established trading posts here and there along the banks, the birch bark canoes of the Indians glided along the broad river as the red men journeyed to the posts to do business with the white men. Then the early settlers built flatboats and rafts to take

THE MISSISSIPPI BASIN 

87 their wheat and salt pork and furs down the river, sometimes even as far as New Orleans. 

These boats and rafts floated down the river, but they could not, of course, float back against the current and the men had to return home as best they could.

In 1811, which was very soon after the steamboat came into use, steamboat traffic opened on the river. Now boats could go up the river as well as down, and trade grew very rapidly. These first steamboats burned wood and threw off showers of sparks. They made a pretty sight at night, but passing by wooded shores in dry weather they often started forest fires. At first the steamboats made only short journeys, but gradually they made longer and longer ones. Soon they were going up the tributaries and into the interior of the country. 

They brought back grain and hides and furs. 

Our country, which was growing so fast, needed all of these things.

Travel and commerce on the river were increasing splendidly, when in 1869 the railroad came. 

People laughed at first at its funny little chugging engines and said it was just a crazy scheme and would never last.

However, the railroad not only lasted, but it improved rapidly, and little by little took away the passengers and freight from the Mississippi. It was so much faster to send things by rail. Fruits and produce that would spoil going by boat could be shipped by train and reach the markets while they were still fresh. For a time the Mississippi lost much of its life, but as the west developed, river traffic again became popular on the Mississippi. Today steamboats are plying up and down its length. Rafts of lumber, and barges loaded with cotton and coal, make the river a busy place as these products can be shipped more cheaply by water than by rail.

The tributaries which are best for navigation are the Missouri, the Ohio, the Illinois, the Red, and the Arkansas rivers. It is estimated that the Mississippi system has 15,000 miles of navigable waterways, which are expected, as time passes and improvements are made, to become more and more important in linking together different sections of the country. 

Travel and transportation along a river, of course, builds up cities. St. Louis started as a trading post. Later it was a stopping

CITIES 

place for steamboats. Now trains slip into it from all directions on shining rails. 

Farther down the river is Memphis, Tennessee, a cotton and lumber market. Two other lumber cities are Dubuque, Iowa, and La

Crosse, Wisconsin. The river also has manufacturing cities. Davenport, Iowa, manufactures silos. Rock Island and Moline, Illinois, both manufacture agricultural implements. 

Then way to the south is New Orleans, dependent upon the river for its prosperity and its very life.

1. The Mississippi basin includes over two-fifths of all the land in the United States. It extends from northern Minnesota to the Gulf of Mexico, and from the 

Appalachians to the Rocky mountains. 

2. The Mississippi river has many tributaries, some of which are navigable. The Mississippi system has 

15,000 miles of navigable waterways. 

3. At the Falls of St. Anthony the Mississippi furnishes power to run the flour mills of Minneapolis. The river's power is used to make electricity at Keokuk, Iowa. St. Louis, Missouri, gets its electricity from this source. 

4. Through thousands of years the Mississippi has been carrying downstream vast amounts of silt. With this silt it has built up its own bed, formed sand bars and made many miles of new land. High embankments, called levees, have been built to hold the river in its channel and prevent floods. 

5. The land along the river from Illinois to the Gulf of 

Mexico was built up by the river with silt. It is very low and is called the flood plain. The lower part of this plain is called the Mississippi delta. This delta is a rich agricultural region. 

6. Silt that has been carried past the delta and dropped at the mouth of the river has at times almost blocked the stream to gulf commerce. For this reason jetties have been built to narrow and deepen the channel so that the stream will flow faster and carry the silt farther out into the gulf. It is a constant expense to keep the Mississippi in shape for navigation.

NORTH AMERICA 

7. The Mississippi is a natural lane for travel and transportation. First the Indians and then the white men used it in their trading. The steamboat made transportation possible up the river as well as down, and opened up the tributaries. The railroads took away much business from the river. Of late river transportation has been increasing because bulky products, such as cotton, coal, and lumber, can be sent much more cheaply by water. 

8. Among the important cities on the Mississippi are 

Minneapolis, St. Louis, Memphis, and New Orleans.

THE GREAT LAKES AS A WATERWAY 

(1) (2) National Development Bureau, Dept. of the Interior, Canada. (3) Duluth Chamber of Commerce. (4) J. C. Allen, West Lafayette, Ind. 

The 

1. Niagara falls, seen from above the Canadian shore.

These beautiful falls are one of Nature's wonders. water power they furnish provides electric current for thousands of homes and hundreds of factories. Lying between Lake Erie and Lake Ontario, the falls are an obstacle to water travel through the Great lakes. Ships passing between Lake Erie and Lake Ontario must pass around the falls through the Welland canal in Canada. 

2. A passenger steamship in the Canadian lock of the 

Soo canals which connect Lake Superior and Lake Huron. 

The Soo canals are short but have more traffic than any other canal. Two of their five locks are the largest ever built. This vessel is one of many fast steamers which make travel on the Great lakes as comfortable as ocean travel. as to 

3. A Great lakes freight steamer loading at a grain elevator in Duluth. Freighters on the Great lakes are very long.

Their engines are in the sterns, so leave almost all of their hulls, or bodies, open to receive grain, coal, or iron ore. Wheat is pouring into this freighter through long pipes. Wheat is one of the most important cargoes of the Great lakes. 

4. A large Great lakes freighter entering one of the four American locks of the Soo canals.

5. Ore docks at Duluth, Minnesota. These docks are so well equipped with loading machinery that thousands of tons of ore can be poured into a vessel in an hour. 

Freight trains run out on the broad tops of the high docks and dump ore into bins below. From these bins it is poured through tubes into the holds of freighters which are tied closely alongside. The tall steel frames are unloading machinery. At the right, a cargo of coal is being unloaded from a freighter. The broad bands across the deck of the freighter are the covers of its hold. When these are removed, the ship is practically an open box. 

6. An iron mine in Minnesota. The iron ore lies close to the surface of the ground, and steam shovels dig it out and dump it directly into freight cars like those in the foreground. These will carry it to the docks at 

Duluth. Because they are simply open pits, mines of this kind are called open-cut mines. 

7. Unloading machinery at Cleveland, Ohio. These huge machines are scooping iron ore from the hold of a freighter. We see in the foreground a part of the freighter's deck, with the covers removed. 

8. The water front of Cleveland, seen from the air. 

Notice the extensive docks. Cleveland has no natural harbor, so long breakwaters have been built to protect the wharves. We see them across the top of the picture. 

The great airship is a Navy dirigible.

The Great lakes are like an immense stairway of water leading down step by step from the interior of our continent to the St. Lawrence river. Lake Superior, the largest body of fresh water in the world, is at the head of the stairway and has the highest surface level. 

Going east from lake to lake the level of each step is lower than the one before it. This is because the lakes lie in an area of land which, in general, slopes eastward.

LEVEL AND SIZE OF THE LAKES 

But the steps in the stairway are not all the same size, and the drops in level between the steps are not the same. Lake

Superior is the largest of the lakes. Then come Lake Huron, Lake Michigan, Lake Erie, and

Lake Ontario which is the smallest. In order of height above sea level they are: Lake Superior, 603 feet; Lake Michigan and Lake Huron, 580 feet; Lake Erie, 573 feet; and Lake 

Ontario, 246 feet.

We know that where rivers drop from one level of land to another in rapids or water- 

HOW THE LAKES ARE 

JOINED falls, canals with locks must be built or boats cannot pass the falls. Lake Superior and Lake 

Huron are joined by St. Marys river in which are St. Marys falls. So if we should sail from one of these lakes to the other our boat would have to pass around the falls through locks. There are two canals around the falls, one on the American side of the river, one on the Canadian side. They are known as the Sault Ste. Marie, or Soo canals. In the 

American canal there are four locks, side by side, so that several boats can pass through at the same time. More freight passes through the Soo canals than through either the Suez canal or the Panama.

There is no difference in level between Lake Huron and Lake Michigan, and so no canal is needed here. These two lakes are connected by the Strait of Mackinac. 

Lake Erie is about seven feet below Lake 

Huron. Through the St. Clair river, St. Clair lake, and the Detroit river, which connect these lakes, a deep channel has been dug. This channel is divided, as are the Soo canals, for upbound boats and downbound boats. 

Lake Erie and Lake Ontario have the greatest difference in level of any two of the lakes -over 300 feet. They are joined by the Niagara river whose waters rush over a high ledge and drop 160 feet, forming the gorgeous and tremendous Niagara falls. Below the falls the drop is in cascades and rapids.

Neither Niagara falls nor the river for several miles below them can possibly be navigated. So, to make a passage for boats from 

Lake Erie to Lake Ontario, Canada has built the Welland canal on her side of the falls. Instead of a canal around the falls on the American side, New York state, way back in 1825, built the Erie canal across the state. This canal connected Lake Erie with the Hudson river and diverted much Great lakes traffic from the St. Lawrence route down the Hudson to the port of New York City. In recent years the Erie canal has been widened and deepened and parts of its course have been changed. It is now known as the New York 

State Barge canal.

What a wonderful work of Nature is this series of great lakes and rivers and waterfalls!

What a splendid work man has done in planning and constructing locks and channels and canals to link the lakes in a chain which reaches from almost the center of our vast continent to the 

Atlantic ocean! Together, Nature and man have made the greatest system of inland waterways in the world. However, it is a system that has one real disadvantage. It is closed to navigation several months in each year because of ice.

The surface area of the Great lakes is nearly one and one-half times that of the whole of New England. The lakes, and the streams flow- 89

SURFACE AND DRAINAGE AREA

North America. Study the map on page 90 and see how many and which states border the Great lakes.

The Great lakes section has a most healthy and bracing climate. This climate is nearly oceanic because the lakes are so

CLIMATE large that breezes from them keep the temperature of the surrounding land fairly even during many months of the year. When the lake waters freeze in winter, however, the winds that blow across them bring intense cold.

After an extremely cold winter, the weather is cool a long time and spring is delayed. But when the growing season once begins, the temperature does not suddenly shift back again to cold and frosts, so that crops once started are not destroyed. Such a climate gives fine agricultural advantages. 

Northwest of the Great lakes, in Canada, and to the west of them, in the United States, is the great spring wheat re 

AGRICULTURE 

gion. Tons and tons of wheat are shipped from the lake ports every year, especially from Duluth and Superior at the head of Lake Superior. 

Acres and acres of apple, peach, and cherry orchards are found along the shores of the 

Great lakes. Cherries are especially abundant about Lake Michigan. There are many apple orchards in western New York. Along Lake 

Erie and throughout the Lake plains and

Finger lakes region in New York state there are many vineyards. More grapes are grown here than anywhere east of California.

Minnesota is the leading lake state in the raising of white potatoes. Michigan produces immense crops of sugar beets, from which sugar is made.

In the Great lakes region, from western 

Wisconsin to eastern New York, you will see

DAIRYING 

many herds of dairy cattle. Fine and healthy and clean they look. 

Much of the land which is too rough for good crops provides rich pasture for them. 

You will see great barns, and clean farmyards, and tall silos where winter feed is stored. You will see many creameries and factories where milk is bottled or canned, and butter, cheese, and chocolate are made. More dairy cattle are raised in Wisconsin, Minnesota and New York than in any other states. To feed their cattle these states raise great crops of hay.

MINING 

Parts of Minnesota, Wisconsin, and Michigan are in the Laurentian highland which noses in from Canada. In this upland region around western Lake Superior are actual mountains of iron ore. 

More iron ore is produced here than in any other region in the world. The ore lies so near the surface and is so easily broken up that all the miners have to do is to dig it out with steam shovels and load it into cars which carry it to nearby lake ports. There it is shipped down the lakes on ore boats to lower lake ports. Some of the ore is used right at these ports but much is shipped inland to points nearer the coal districts, especially to those in

NORTH AMERICA 

Ohio and Pennsylvania. There the ore is smelted. 

Copper as well as iron is found in northern

Michigan. Copper is used to make wire and electric cables, boilers, and delicate parts of machinery. This metal is more rare and costs more to produce than iron. How many things can you think of that are made of iron? Of copper?

LUMBERING 

Much of the country around the upper Great lakes was once forest-covered, and the section was a leading lumber region. Because of careless, wasteful cutting and forest fires, the lumber business has lost its importance in the southern part of the lake states, and much of the old forest land is now used for farms. In the northern part of the region there are still forests, and lumbering is still a leading industry. 

The United States government is now reforesting the land here and is introducing modern methods of forestry. The states are also helping in this work. 

The Great lakes make rich fishing grounds. 

Many salmon trout live in Lake Huron and

FISHING 

Lake Superior. They have the highest commercial value of any fish found in the Great lakes, because they are large and heavy, and are very good to eat. 

Some of these fish are packed in refrigerator cars as soon as they are caught and are sent to many nearby places. Some, after being frozen to keep them from spoiling, are sent longer distances. Speckled trout are found in streams near the upper lakes. Georgian bay, a Canadian arm of Lake Huron, has many whitefish. A smaller kind of whitefish is plentiful in Lake Erie. What kind of fish are found near the shores of New England?

We have spoken of the Great lakes as a most important inland waterway. There are two main reasons for this import 

COMMERCE 

AND TRAVEL ance. One is that the wheat and iron and other products of the Central states are needed in the east. In turn, the coal, textiles, and machinery from 

Pennsylvania, New England, and the cities of the Fall line must be sent to the busy industrial cities which have grown up throughout the 

Central states. 

The fact that the countries of Canada and the United States, the two largest countries of North America, have many products to exchange is the second reason for the commercial importance of the Great lakes. Trade and travel are carried on between these two countries through lake ports, where goods are received and visitors from across the border are admitted. Such ports are called ports of entry. Among the chief American ports of entry on the Great lakes are Detroit, Chicago, Buffalo, and Erie.

People, small freight, and heavy or bulk freight are carried on the Great lakes, and each has a special kind of vessel. The best passenger ships are much like great ocean liners. They are equipped with fine dining rooms, bedrooms, and recreation rooms for the comfort of their many passengers. East and west, the lake routes connect with the best railroad lines, and many people journey across the continent part way by water and part way by rail.

Much small freight, which includes articles wrapped, boxed, or crated, is carried on small vessels. These do not usually make long trips but go between neighboring points and carry on what is known as local trade.

Most important of all the lake traffic is the shipping of bulk, or heavy, freight, consisting of tons and tons of ore, grain, coal, and oil. Vessels called freighters, built for this purpose, are often 600 feet long. They frequently carry about 6000 tons of material, which is more than a long freight train carries. 

Quick and marvelous means have been invented for loading these boats at the wharves right from the cars which bring the materials from the mines or mills. A boat can be loaded in about an hour, and with much less labor than is needed to load a freight train. This is one of the reasons why it is cheaper to ship a ton of material by water than by rail. Another reason is that it costs great sums of money to keep railroads in condition. So the railroad companies have to charge more than the boat companies for carrying goods. 

It will be easier to understand the enormous

9. Chicago as it appears from Lake Michigan. Chicago has almost as many skyscrapers as New York. Notice that the tall buildings are set well back from the lake shore. The land between, a beautiful city park, was built up by dumping earth into the lake. The part of the lake seen in this picture is kept calm by breakwaters like those which protect Cleveland's waterfront. 

10. Lincoln Park, Chicago, on the shore of Lake Michi 

Notice the fine boulevards and the protected gan. anchorage for pleasure boats. Chicago has many fine parks, which add to the health and pleasure of her people. 

11. Dressing lamb in one of the great Chicago meatpacking plants. Chicago is the world's greatest center of meat packing, and its packing plants have many devices for doing work quickly and easily. In this picture we see carcasses of lambs which move along an overhead rail past several workmen, each of whom has one special duty to perform. The nearest workman is a government inspector, who makes sure that each lamb is fit for food. 

12. The water front of Milwaukee, the chief city of Wisconsin. Milwaukee is a large and busy center of many industries. To the city are brought grain, lumber, and other products from all parts of the state to be reshipped to the east by boat and train.

13. Dairy cattle on a Wisconsin farm. These cows are purebred Holsteins, large black-and-white cows which give more milk than cows of other breeds. Wisconsin leads in the United States in dairy products, and to feed its cows it raises more hay than any other state. Wisconsin has plenty of fertile pasture lands. 

14. Making automobile tires in a factory at Akron,

Ohio. The workman is standing before the form on which he builds the tires. Around him are the different materials he uses. When the tire has been built, it is put in a mold and vulcanized, or heated, to make the rubber tough. 

15. Assembling automobiles in a large Detroit factory. 

Here we see nearly finished cars following each other in a line. As each chassis reaches a certain point a body is lowered onto it from the floor above and is fastened in place by men waiting to do that work. In this automobile factory the most efficient means is used in doing each kind of work. 

16. Lumbered lands in the Great Lakes section. Lumbering is an important industry in northern Michigan. This field has been ruined by wasteful cutting. When the large trees were cut, the lumbermen did not take care to provide for the growth of young trees to take their places. Wasteful lumbering has destroyed much forest land in the United States.

DULUTH SUPERIOR 

amount of traffic which passes over the Great lakes if we study a few of the cities on or near them and learn what these cities ship over this trade route. All of these cities have grown and prospered because each of them has, near at hand, materials to manufacture into finished products, and a convenient means of sending goods to market. 

The cities of Duluth, Minnesota, and Superior, Wisconsin, lie near one another at the western end of Lake Superior. The bay on which they are located makes a fine harbor. 

At the docks of these two cities great freighters are loaded with wheat. Still others carry lumber, much of which goes to Grand Rapids, Michigan, where there are fine furniture factories. Coal comes to Duluth and Superior in freighters from the lower lakes. From nearby mines, cars bring iron ore, much of which is shipped from Duluth. Some of the ore is smelted in Duluth, where agricultural implements are made from it. 

Though Duluth and Superior have flour mills and lumber mills neither of them is a great manufacturing city. They are principally ports through which raw materials pass to the east and manufactured products pass to the west.

MILWAUKEE 

Milwaukee is the largest city of Wisconsin.

Like Duluth and Superior, it is a distributing center for grain and lumber, much grain being shipped from 

Milwaukee by train to cities in the east. Milwaukee is the chief manufacturing center of the state. It manufactures motor vehicles, machinery, steel and iron, knit goods, and boots and shoes, and packs meats.

Probably no other city gives us a better picture of the busy industrial life of the people of the Great lakes section than the 

CHICAGO largest of them all-Chicago, Illinois.

Starting as a little fort on the shore of Lake Michigan, Chicago has grown into the second city in size in the United States, and fourth largest in the world. Its growth began when the invention of steamboats led to the Great lakes becoming an important trade route. 

From being a leading lake port the city became a railroad center as well, and today is the largest railroad center in the United States.

There are now more than 3,000,000 people living in the city of Chicago. Many of them are immigrants who have left their own countries and come here because they felt that in this great city they could surely get work to do. As in New York City, the people from a foreign country often live together in one section of the city. Some of these foreigners publish newspapers in their own languages. Chicago also has a large negro population. 

Chicago is the greatest trading center of the northern Central plains and the Great lakes region. Like all large cities of the Central plains, it is a grain distributing center. Chicago, the second largest manufacturing center in the United States, manufactures heavy machinery and railroad equipment. The city has the largest stockyards and slaughter houses in the world, and leads in the manufacture of agricultural implements. 

Pullman, a suburb of Chicago, is a model factory town where railroad cars are made. 

The dining cars, sleeping cars, pullman coaches, and recreation cars which are made here are used on most of the railroads of the country.

About fifty years ago the people of Chicago laid out over 1800 acres of land in and about the city to be used for parks. The neighborhood parks in the crowded school districts are particular blessings to the city's children. 

There are sand gardens and swimming pools and athletic fields to enjoy during the warm summer days.

Chicago's streets are broad and meet each other at right angles, like the corners of a square. Her people have every modern means of transportation from one busy, crowded section of the city to another. There are surface cars and elevated trains. There are buses and taxies, and a subway system that carries freight instead of passengers. This rids the streets of many heavy trucks and freight trolleys.

Chicago gets its water supply from Lake Michigan. To keep the waters of the lake pure, drainage canals have been built which

NORTH AMERICA 

carry waste from the city's sewers into the Illinois river. This river serves the city in another way. Canals connected with it make it a part of a continuous passage for small boats from Chicago to the Mississippi river.

DETROIT 

Detroit, Michigan, is the second city in size of the Great lakes section. It is located where 

Lake St. Clair joins the Detroit river.

The distance from Chicago to Detroit by railroad is over 250 miles. Is it much longer by way of the lakes?

Detroit is a distributing center for wheat, iron ore, and lumber from the upper lakes, and for coal from the lower lakes. Railroads from east, west, and south bring in many other products which are likewise shipped from the city. Many more automobiles are made in 

Detroit than in any other city in the country. 

This industry is mainly responsible for the great growth of the city. Iron and steel goods, including airplane parts, are also manufactured here. Like Chicago, Detroit has stockyards and slaughter houses. Hides are made into boots and shoes and other leather goods.

TOLEDO 

Toledo, Ohio, is on Lake Erie and has a harbor where the largest of the lake vessels can enter. Among the city's chief industries are shipbuilding and the manufacture of iron and steel products, including car parts. There are also large flour mills here. Toledo has excellent railroad connections with other cities of the country. 

CLEVELAND 

Cleveland, Ohio, is the largest ore market in the world and the largest fresh-water fish market. Here coal and oil from 

Pennsylvania and Ohio are continually being exchanged for iron from west of Lake Superior. Cleveland and the cities nearby have many machine shops and foundries which use all these raw materials. Hundreds of machines and instruments are made here. These include sewing machines, car wheels, automobiles, and telescopes. 

Cleveland is built on a plateau and has a commanding position on Lake Erie. It is well known for its broad streets, excellent traffic conditions, and many lovely trees, parks, and drives.

Erie, Pennsylvania, is a center for the steel 

ERIE 

industry and also makes paper, wood pulp, and rubber goods. Tobacco is manufactured at Erie in large quantities. Erie is well located in the grape-raising district. 

BUFFALO 

Buffalo, at the eastern end of Lake Erie, is the second city of New York state in size and in the number of its manufactures. 

Its industrial growth is due to its excellent connection both by water and rail with cities east and west. Buffalo is at the western end of the Barge canal which connects Lake Erie with the Hudson river.

OTHER CITIES OF THE GREAT LAKES REGION 

Besides all these thriving cities there are many others whose growth has been much helped by the Great lakes trade route. From the lake ports ore is taken in cars to Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, to Youngstown, Ohio, and to Joliet, Illinois. All of these cities have become centers of the steel industry because they can get iron ore so easily. Being in coal districts they have, right at hand, the coke needed in smelting the ore. 

Gary, Indiana, is another steel city. Gary has the largest steel plant in the world. It is near Chicago, and was originally founded as a steel town to provide that city with the steel needed for its factories. 

Southeast of Gary, in the center of Indiana, is Indianapolis, an important railroad center, and the capital of the state. It is also an important trading center, due to its location in a rich agricultural district.

Not far inland from Cleveland is the city of Akron. This city leads the entire world in the production of rubber goods. Its leading rubber products are tires and inner tubes for automobiles. 

Canton and Columbus, Ohio, Flint, Michigan, and Fort Wayne, Indiana, carry on many of the same industries as Cleveland. Columbus is the capital of Ohio.

The most famous beauty spot of the Great lakes is Niagara falls. The city of Niagara

Falls is about twenty-two NIAGARA FALLS 

miles northwest from Buffalo.

So much water rushes over the falls that their power has been put to work to supply elec-

tricity for Buffalo, Niagara Falls, and other cities in New York state and in Canada. The

United States and Canada have an agreement, however, not to spoil the beauty of the falls by using too much water for power. 

Over 9,000,000 people live in the Great lakes cities which we have mentioned. There are many smaller cities besides. This region shows, as well as any in the world, that where there is work to be done and advantages for all, both industry and home life flourish. 

1. Named in order of size the Great lakes are: Lake 

Superior, Lake Huron, Lake Michigan, Lake Erie, and Lake Ontario. In order of height above sea level they are: Lake Superior, 603 feet; Lake Michigan and Lake Huron, 580 feet; Lake Erie, 573 feet; 

Lake Ontario, 246 feet.

The 2. The lakes are joined by a series of smaller waterways and have been made continuously navigable by canals, locks and channels to the St. Lawrence river and to the Barge canal, and so to the Atlantic ocean.

Great lakes system is the greatest inland waterway in the world. The surface area of the Great lakes is nearly one and one-half times that of New England. 

The lakes, and the streams flowing into them, drain about one-thirtieth of the area of North America. 

3. The climate of the Great lakes section is favorable to agriculture because it is oceanic and is not subject to sudden changes which would destroy crops. 

4. Along the shores of the Great lakes quantities of apples, peaches, cherries, grapes, and potatoes are raised. Sugar beets are grown in Michigan. Northwest and west of Lake Superior is part of the great wheat belt. Wisconsin, Minnesota, and New York raise many dairy cattle and manufacture dairy products. Much hay is raised in the Great lakes section. 

5. Copper is mined in Michigan. Iron ore is mined in 

Minnesota, Wisconsin, and Michigan. The ore is shipped from the upper lake ports to the lower lake ports. Much is carried by rail to points nearer the coal fields, especially in Ohio and Pennsylvania, where it is smelted. 

6. The most valuable fish found in the Great lakes are salmon trout, plentiful in Lake Superior and Lake 

Huron. Whitefish are found in Georgian bay and in Lake Erie. Cleveland, Ohio, is the largest freshwater fish market in the world. 

7. Commerce on the Great lakes is extensive for two reasons. First, because the products of the Central states are needed in the east, and the manufactured goods of the east are needed in the west, and it is cheaper to ship materials by water than by rail. 

Second, because Canada and the United States exchange many products and visitors through the ports of entry on the Great lakes. 

8. Chicago, the second largest city of the United States, is a great trading center. It is also the greatest railroad center in the world. It has the largest slaughter houses in the world. Agricultural and railroad equipment are manufactured there. 

9. Among the important cities on the Great lakes are: 

Duluth, Superior, Milwaukee, Detroit, Toledo, Cleveland, Erie, and Buffalo. Duluth and Superior are grain and lumber distributing centers. Milwaukee excels other cities of the state in its manufactures. Detroit manufactures more automobiles than any other city in the country. Toledo is a shipbuilding center. 

Cleveland is the largest ore market in the world. Erie is a steel city. Buffalo, on Lake Erie, is at the western end of the Barge canal and so is connected by water, as well as by railroad, with cities east and west. 

10. Other cities of the Great lakes section are: Gary, in 

Indiana, a steel city; Indianapolis, a railroad center and the capital of Indiana; Youngstown, Canton, and 

Columbus, in Ohio; Flint, in Michigan; Fort Wayne, in Indiana; and Joliet, in Illinois. Akron, Ohio, is the greatest rubber manufacturing city in the world. 

11. The most famous beauty spot of the Great lakes section is Niagara Falls. Part of the water power of these falls is used to make electricity for some cities of New York state and Canada.

WESTERN MOUNTAINS AND NATIONAL PARKS 

4 2 5 (1) The National Park Service. (2) (5) Californians, Inc., San Francisco. (3) Chicago, Milwaukee, St. Paul and Pacific R. R. (4) Denver and Rio Grande Western R. R. 

Here, 1. A scene in Glacier National park, Montana. high in the Rocky mountains, many visitors enjoy the beauties of Nature unspoiled by the hand of man. 

2. El Capitan, a mighty rock in Yosemite National park, California. The steep cliff rises 3600 feet from the river valley at its foot. 

3. Old Faithful geyser, Yellowstone National park,

Wyoming. Each hour, this hot spring shoots a great column of steam and hot water high into the air. The park has many geysers, some of which hurl out more steam than Old Faithful, and spout to a greater height, but not with such dependable regularity. 

4. The Royal gorge of the Arkansas river, Colorado.

For many thousands of years the river has been at work, cutting this pass through the mountains. Notice how man has taken advantage of Nature's work by laying a railroad through the pass. The highway bridge which spans the top of the gorge is more than 1050 feet above the stream. 

5. Lassen peak, in California. This is the most recently active volcano in the United States. In May, 1915, Lassen peak erupted so violently that steam and ashes were hurled almost seven miles into the air. Most volcanoes are cone-shaped, and are more regular in form than other mountains.

6. Business section of Los Angeles, from the air. 

Angeles has grown amazingly in recent years, and is now the largest city on the Pacific coast. She carries on heavy ocean commerce through her port of San Pedro. 

7. An orange grove and oil wells near Los Angeles.

Petroleum and oranges are among the important products of southern California. 

8. Steamships loading at the wharves of San Diego, California. The ship to the left is a freight steamer; the ship to the right is a passenger liner. The long poles attached to the masts of the ships are derricks. They are used for loading and unloading cargo. 

9. Mount Wilson Observatory, near Pasadena, California.

In this building is a giant telescope which scientists use to study the stars, planets, and other heavenly bodies. 

10. An old Spanish mission near Los Angeles, California. 

Between 1769 and 1823 Catholic missionaries from Spain established twenty-one missions in California for the purpose of teaching the Indians to be Christians. Some of these historic old buildings are being carefully preserved, and large numbers of people. visit them each year.

The pioneers who crossed the Great plains saw ahead of them to the west an almost unbroken line of mountains stretching north and south. These were the Rocky mountains, the eastern outposts of the great Western highland region of North America.

The pioneers now faced the hardest part of their journey to the Pacific coast. No longer would their covered wagons roll across the almost level plains. The Rocky mountains must be passed, and beyond them lay high plateaus, deep valleys, burning deserts, and, near the coast, other towering mountain ranges all running north and south and making serious barriers to east and west travel. Many early travelers failed in crossing this last great region of our continent, and their bones were left to bleach along the way.

This mountainous region in the west is part of a great mountain system extending through both North and South America from the Arctic ocean on the north to the Antarctic on the south. It includes more than one-third of our entire country, and the states of Washington, Idaho, Montana, Oregon, Wyoming, California, Nevada, Utah, Colorado, Arizona, New 

Mexico and Texas lie wholly or partly within its borders.

WESTERN MOUNTAINS 

The mountains of this mighty system are the highest in the United States. The ranges follow a general north and south direction. The Rocky mountains, just west of the Great plains, extend from the far northern lands of 

Canada and Alaska nearly to the southern border of the United States. The Coast ranges, several hundred miles farther west, run close along the coast of the Pacific ocean. There is no wide coastal plain as there is on the Atlantic coast. East of the Coast ranges, with valleys between, are the highest ranges of all, the Sierra Nevadas and the Cascades. Between these high mountain chains and the Rocky mountains there are great plateaus, with shorter chains of mountains running through them. The Wasatch mountains in northeastern Utah are such a chain. 

How does the great Western highland region compare with the Appalachian highland in the east? From east to west it is about three times as wide. The western mountains are younger than the old, worn-down mountains of the Appalachians, and so are much higher and more rugged. The highest peak in the Appalachians is Mt. Mitchell, 6,711 feet above sea level, and the highest Adirondack peak, Mt. Marcy, is only 5,344 feet. In the western mountains there are many peaks rising over 14,000 feet above the sea. There are over twenty of them in Colorado alone. 

Mt. Whitney, in the Sierra Nevada range, is 

14,500 feet high and is the highest peak in the 

United States. 

Pikes peak in the Rocky mountains, near 

Colorado Springs, is a high mountain peak that can be reached by automobile and by train. A little cog train climbs up and up, at a very sharp angle, to the top. Many people get lightheaded and dizzy in the thin air at the mountain summit, more than two and a half miles above sea level. From such a height even the highest buildings would look like specks, and big rivers like silver threads. 

Volcanoes. Some of these western mountains were once volcanoes, with smoke and lava pouring out of their tops. Now they are inactive, and the snow lies on many of them all the year. Mt. Shasta, in the Sierra Nevada range in northern California, is one of these. 

It is 14,400 feet high. Glaciers move slowly down its sides. Mt. Hood and Mt. Rainier in the Cascade range are two other dead volcanoes. One volcano, Lassen peak, southeast of Mt. Shasta, was active not many years ago.

In 1915 it had a violent eruption. 

Years ago in the state of Oregon there was a volcano which became so active that it blew its own head off. During its last eruption so 97

great an explosion took place in the depths of the mountain that the entire summit was blown off, and a great hole left in the mountain top, many hundreds of feet across. Water from rain and melting snow ran into this hole, and finally formed a deep, clear, blue lake. Forests grew up around the lake, and the spot became so very beautiful that people travel from all parts of the country to see it. The 

United States government has claimed the region as a national park, known as Crater 

Lake park. 

Mountain Passes. 

The great western mountain ranges would be nearly impossible to cross, if it were not for the passes through them and for the canyons the rivers have cut. There are several high passes in the Rocky mountains, and through some of these railroads have been built. But even these passes are so high that powerful extra engines are

Marshall pass, where the 

Denver and Rio Grande railroad crosses the 

Rockies, is about 11,000 feet above sea level. 

The Royal gorge, where the Arkansas river cuts through the mountains, has wonderful scenery to delight the passengers on the trains running through it.

The snow-covered tops of the Rocky mountains are the source of many rivers. The size 

A ridge of high land which separates two or more river basins is called a watershed. The 

Rocky mountains are a watershed. 

For much of their length they separate the rivers which flow into the Atlantic from those which flow into the Pacific. 

The whole watershed which separates the Atlantic and Pacific drainage systems of North America is called the Continental Divide. 

The swift-rushing mountain streams are a source of much water power. Where they leap over cliffs they form waterfalls. Hydroelectric plants have been built near many of the falls, and the electricity they make is used to furnish light and power for mines and cities. Only a small part of the available power is now used,

NORTH AMERICA 

but more and more of this valuable resource is being put to work.

CLIMATE 

The towering mountain chains of the west have an enormous effect upon the climate of that part of our country. The prevailing westerly winds blow east across the Pacific ocean. Warmed by the 

Japan current, the air takes up much moisture from the sea. Along the western coast rise the 

Coast ranges, a great barrier over which the winds must pass. As the winds rise along the western slopes of the Coast ranges, they are cooled, and can no longer hold all of their moisture. So heavy rain falls on the western mountain slopes.

Over the mountains and down into the valleys beyond blow the westerly winds. Becoming warm again they pick up moisture from the streams and rivers which they cross, giving the region east of the Coast ranges a dry climate. 

Next the winds come to the highest ranges of all, the Sierra Nevadas and the Cascades.

Here, again, they drop their moisture, and the western slopes of these mountains, too, have heavy rainfall. Once past the Sierra Nevadas and the Cascades, the winds find few streams from which to gain more moisture. On across the plateau and desert lands they blow, drying up the country and seldom bringing rain. On the western slopes of the Rockies the winds drop what little moisture they have left, and then pass on to dry the western section of the Great plains. In this way the mountains and the prevailing winds cause heavy rainfall and great fertility in some parts of the Western highlands, and make nearly rainless deserts of other parts.

Moisture is necessary to the growth of plants. Great stretches of land have been

IRRIGATION 

called deserts because they lacked, each year, the twenty inches of rainfall needed for the growing of crops in the temperate zone. Some of these desert lands have rich, fertile soil and much sunshine.

They need only water to make them productive. Many of them have been made so by irrigation, or artificial watering of the land. A dam is built across a river, usually by the government. The river water forms a high lake behind the dam. From this reservoir the water is carried to the dry land by large ditches called irrigation canals. Little ditches carry it from the canals to the plants in the fields.

Most of these dams also supply water power. 

The great Boulder dam, at the Boulder Canyon site on the Colorado, supplies tremendous electric power to cities even as far away as Los Angeles on the Pacific coast. It is the largest dam in the world.

To repay the government for building the dams and keeping them in repair, farmers whose lands are watered by government irrigation systems usually have to pay for the water they use. So farming in an irrigated area is more expensive than in a region with ample rainfall. It is also harder work, for the irrigation ditches must be dug and kept open. Sometimes, too, the crops must be carried greater distances to market, because there are few cities and railroad lines in these naturally dry regions. These are disadvantages of the regions. One big advantage of living in an irrigation section is that the farmer who irrigates his land can be sure that his plants receive just the right amount of moisture, and he never has to fear droughts. Thanks to irrigation, parts of the old desert lands of the west have been turned into fertile farm lands. 

FORESTS 

The western region is noted for its forest lands, though on the eastern slopes, where there is little or no rainfall, the mountains are often bare rock with little vegetation. On the western mountain slopes, where there is much rain, are forests of spruce, live oak, pine, fir, and cedar. Pine and fir trees grow right up to the timber line, which is the line above which it is too cold for trees to grow. The timber line varies with latitude, being lowest in the cold climate of the north.

The forests are very useful. Besides furnishing lumber and fuel they help keep the streams supplied with water and prevent floods. The forest floor holds much of the rain water and

soaks up the water from the melting snow on the mountain tops. The shade of the trees keeps the soil from drying up in the warmth of the sun, and so the water drains off gradually into the streams.

Since forests are so valuable, it is most important that they should not be destroyed.

Forest fires, often caused by carelessness in the use of campfires and cigarettes, are a constant danger to the valuable forest lands. Wasteful cutting of trees also does great damage to the forests. Our national government has set aside great forest preserves in various parts of the country to protect the trees. Two of the largest lie in the Rocky mountain region, one near the source of the Missouri river, and one near the source of the Arkansas. The government hires men, called forest rangers, to patrol the forests and watch carefully for any sign of smoke that may mean fire. The rangers also see that only a limited amount of lumbering is done, so that too many old trees will not be cut before new trees can grow up to take their place.

NATIONAL PARKS 

Besides its forest preserves, the government has very wisely set aside many beautiful sections of the western land as national parks, or playgrounds for the people. A nation needs its beauty spots where people may travel and camp. Of course the national parks are far bigger than the small plots of ground that cities set aside as parks. 

One of the most beautiful parks is the 

Yosemite National park. It is in California, in the Sierra Nevadas, and covers many, many miles. Within it lies the beautiful Yosemite valley, famous for its jagged cliffs, its graceful waterfalls, and its immense trees. One of the waterfalls is over 1600 feet high, higher than nine Niagaras, one above another. 

Yellowstone National park in Wyoming is the largest of all the national parks. Here are survivors of the herds of buffaloes that once roamed the Great plains. And here are bears, deer, coyotes, and many other wild animals, living safe from hunters under the government's protection. This park is a large plateau, and contains a number of amazing sights. 

It has a canyon of colored rocks, waterfalls higher than Niagara, and rugged mountain peaks.

But most of the people who visit Yellowstone park wish to see the geysers first. They are fascinated by these springs of hot water that bubble and boil and sometimes shoot high into the air in a cloud of steam. There are many geysers in this park, but you cannot always tell when they will spout. There is one that is called "Old Faithful," because it can be counted on to spout every hour. The rocks and cliffs around each of the geysers seem to have beautiful patterns all their own. Some look like glass, they are so white and clear, and some are colored. 

Glacier National park is in Montana. Here great rivers of ice, moving slowly down the mountain valleys, make an unusual sight that is never forgotten.

These are but a few of the many national parks in the western region. Another park surrounds the Grand Canyon of the Colorado river. Crater Lake park, in Oregon, with its beautiful blue waters, is another. Each contains natural wonders that attract people from far and wide.

MINERALS 

The mountains of the Western highland region are rich in valuable minerals. Much gold, silver, copper, lead, zinc, coal, and phosphate rock are mined there.

It was the discovery of gold near Pikes peak that first brought settlers to the Rocky mountains, much as the earlier discovery of gold in California had attracted pioneers there. 

The first mining done in the west was placer mining. In the beds of the streams were pieces of gold which had been washed down from the mountains along with the soil and gravel. Mud and gravel were dumped into a long, slanting trough with strips of wood nailed across its floor. A flow of water washed the mud and gravel over these strips, but the heavy grains of gold were caught and held behind them, to be collected by the miner.

There is still some old-fashioned placer mining done in the west, but modern methods

NORTH AMERICA 

have largely taken its place. Today great dredges scoop up the mud, water, and sand from the stream bottoms, and sift out the gold much more quickly than the old-fashioned pans could do it. 

Sometimes a streak, or vein, of gold or some other valuable metal is located under the ground. Then great shafts are dug and men go down into them and dig out the precious metal ore and bring it to the surface. Where rich veins of ore have been discovered, mining towns have grown up. Some lasted but a few short months or years until the ore was gone.

Some have developed other industries and have become good-sized cities. Many of the western cities got their start as mining towns.

The coastal mountain ranges run close along the western shore for almost the entire 

SOUTHERN length of the Pacific coast. But 

CALIFORNIA along the coast of southern California there is a narrow coastal plain between the San Bernardino mountains and the sea.

Extending inland from the coastal plain is the fertile San Bernardino valley. 

Climate. Southern California has a Mediterranean climate-a long, dry season in the summer, and a wet season in the winter. Occasionally in the winter months there will be rain for three or four days at a time, but usually showers are short and heavy. The rain sometimes comes down so fast that the city streets run deep with water. Then the sun comes out and soon evaporates the water that has not already disappeared into the sand of the soil. 

Once in a while there are frosts in southern 

California, though these are rare. When the

Weather Bureau warns that a frost is likely, the fruit growers go through their groves lighting little oil-burning stoves which they call smudge pots. The fires burn all through the night to raise the temperature above the freezing point. The fruit is so valuable that a single frosty night might do thousands of dollars of damage. 

Planting fruit on the hillsides is another way in which fruit growers of California and other parts of the country protect fruit from frost. Cold air is heavier than warm air, so on cold nights when there is no wind the cold, frosty air settles in the valleys, and the warmer air rises to the hillsides. In this way trees which are above the frosty air are kept from damage by cold. This sinking of the cold air is called frost drainage. 

Southern California summer days are very hot but the nights are cool. For those who can go to the shore on hot days and enjoy the cool sea breezes the climate is delightful. 

There are advantages in having no rain at this season. It insures a dry harvest time for crops and an uninterrupted season for outdoor work. There are disadvantages because everything gets dried up and dusty except where there is irrigation. An easterner sometimes tires of always seeing blue sky and longs for a cloudy day. 

Agriculture. Throughout both the coastal plain and the San Bernardino valley are many fruit ranches with their even rows of trees. Oranges, lemons, grapefruit, olives, figs, and grapes are grown. Alfalfa is one of the chief products of southern California and there are several crops of it each year. Wheat and barley and many vegetables are raised. 

There are large egg and dairy farms and cattle ranches. 

Irrigation is extensive in the San Bernardino valley and in parts of the coastal plain. 

The water comes from the San Bernardino mountains and even from the distant Sierra 

Nevadas. Farmers are glad to see heavy snow caps in winter, for they know that during the long, dry summer months to come the melting snow will provide them with water. 

Cooperative Marketing. The fruit growers of southern California have banded together to help each other. In different sections great packing houses and canning factories have been built. These belong to no one grower, but to many, all of whom send their fruits there to be packed, preserved, or canned, and sold. Each such association has its own name, which is printed on all of its crates and cans. 

Such working together is called cooperative marketing. It is a great benefit to those who take part in it because the cost of running one large factory is much less than that of running several small ones.

CALIFORNIA'S CENTRAL VALLEY

11. San Francisco, California. Located at the western entrance to the Great valley, San Francisco is in a fine position for trade and manufacturing. The city is growing rapidly and is second only to Los Angeles in size among our Pacific coast cities. Notice the many tall buildings. Ferry boats connect the city with Oakland, Berkeley, and Alameda, across San Francisco bay. 

12. Giant sequoia trees in the Great valley of California. 

They are believed to be the largest and oldest living things in the world. Some sequoia trees reach over 300 feet in height and 30 feet in diameter. Some are more than 3000 years old. The government has established 

Sequoia National park, where about 12,000 of these trees are protected. 

13. Seedless grapes drying in the sun near Fresno,

California, in the southern part of the Great valley. 

When dried, the grapes are called raisins. So productive are these grape vines that they sometimes yield as much as four tons of raisins to the acre. The drying of fruits is a leading industry of the Great valley. 

14. The Golden Gate, San Francisco, California, the entrance to San Francisco bay, one of the finest natural harbors in the world. Except for this narrow passage, San Francisco bay is entirely surrounded by land, which gives protection against the storms and winds of the ocean.
