19. The highest apple orchard in the Hood River valley,

Oregon. This valley is known the world over for its fine fruit. The farmer is cultivating the ground between the trees to hold the moisture in the soil. The mountain in the background is Mount Hood.

Scenery. Many of us think of Southern California as the home of the moving picture industry, but few of us realize why most of the leading studios are built in this far western section. Of course the pleasant climate is partly responsible, but even more important is the beautiful and varied scenery. California has great, jagged, snow-capped mountains, as beautiful as any in the world. These serve as settings for stories laid in the Alps, or in any other high mountain ranges of the world. 

Likewise the great, sandy deserts which lie east of the California mountains make wonderful scenes for the filming of stories laid in the 

Sahara desert or the Arabian desert. There are forests of tall, stately trees, and many small, crystal-clear lakes are set in the woodlands. There are large cities and small towns, and along the Pacific coast one finds almost every kind of shore line, from level, sandy beaches to rugged, rocky headlands. So, without leaving their state, the California moving picture people can duplicate almost any scenery found in the world.

Of course the scenery has attractions for other people than those connected with moving pictures. All who visit the state are impressed with the variety and beauty of nature's handiwork. The scenery and climate combine to tempt thousands of tourists from all parts of the country to Southern California every winter. We have seen that Florida's warm, sunny climate also attracts an army of tourists. Southern California and Florida have become great rivals for the tourist trade. 

Each has much to offer the winter visitor. 

Los Angeles. The chief city of Southern California is Los Angeles. It lies on the coastal plain, near the mouth of the San Bernardino valley, about twenty-five miles from the sea. 

Los Angeles has about 1,500,000 people and is one of the busiest and most thriving cities in our country. On its streets may be seen people of every nation.

The city has grown very rapidly in the last few years. 

There are many reasons for this.

It is the main market for the fruits and vegetables that are raised on the coastal plain and in the San Bernardino valley. Over the many railroads centered here, carloads of fruit go east every day. From its shipping port at 

San Pedro harbor go dried fruits and machinery to the countries bordering on the Pacific, cotton to Asia and Europe, fresh fruits to Europe, and petroleum to all parts of the world.

Exports to Europe and the east are sent through the Panama canal. To San Pedro come nitrate from South America, sugar from the Hawaiian islands, coffee from Central and 

South America, rubber from Asia, and paper from Canada and Europe.

Petroleum, which is found near Los Angeles, has made fuel cheap for the factories that can fruits and vegetables, and in addition has brought much wealth and many people to the city. There are varied manufactures, too, including the making of automobile tires in which Los Angeles stands second in the world. 

The climate brings many people from the east who like the advantages of a big city where one can live out-of-doors in any season. But perhaps the biggest factor of all in the growth of Los Angeles has been the moving picture industry. From a small business, with a very little crude equipment, it has grown to a big industry with elaborate "sets," employing thousands of people.

So large a city as Los Angeles needs a great deal of water, much more than can be had near at hand. The needed water comes to the city from Owens lake in the Sierra Nevada mountains. It flows to the city through an enormous steel tube 240 miles long, the Los 

Angeles aqueduct. The aqueduct from the 

Catskills to New York City is 92 miles long.

When this was built, a few years ago, it was considered a great feat to carry water so far over the hills, through mountains, and under the Hudson river. But the Los Angeles aqueduct carries water over two and a half times as far, over the desert as well as through the mountains.

Other Cities. Pasadena and Long Beach are suburbs of Los Angeles. Many people who live in these cities. go to Los Angeles for their daily work. Both cities are very beautiful, and both are popular resorts for tourists.

fornia, has such a pleasant climate that many people from the east have made their homes here. San Diego is a center for California's important fishing industry. Sardines, tuna, salmon, and other fish are caught in large numbers off the coast. 

All through the towns and cities of California are reminders of the fact that this region 

SPANISH MISSIONS 

was first settled by the Spaniards. 

Most of the houses and public buildings are built in Spanish style, with walls of cream-colored adobe, a kind of baked clay. They have tiled roofs and graceful wrought-iron balconies. They seem well suited to a country so warm and sunny. Many of the old Spanish missions are still standing. Some of them are partly in ruin. Others have been restored to look as they did more than 150 years ago, when the priests from Spain came to this new land to teach the Indians to be Christians. The missions are very picturesque and a visitor to California should always make an effort to see some of them. 

North of the California coastal plain lies the Great valley-a beautiful and fertile section 

THE GREAT 

VALLEY between two mountain ranges. To the west, cutting off the Great valley from the coast, are the coastal mountain ranges, and to the east tower the lofty Sierra Nevadas. This great, mountain-enclosed lowland area is about 400 miles long and varies in width from forty to sixty miles.

When you come to study the Great valley, you discover that it is not a single valley at all, but is two river basins, end to end. From the south flows the San Joaquin river which drains the entire southern end of the Great valley. 

This southern end is called the San Joaquin valley. From the north comes the Sacramento river, draining the northern section, the Sacramento valley. These two rivers meet and join about midway up the Great valley, and from that point flow west through a break in the coastal ranges into San Francisco bay and the Pacific ocean.

Climate. The valleys of these two rivers have little rainfall. The coastal ranges to the west shut off the moisture-bearing winds from the Pacific ocean. It is from the western slopes of the high Sierras, farther inland, that the rivers get their water. Most of the rivers' largest tributaries come from the east where rainfall is abundant and there are great snow fields covering the mountain tops. During the winter rainy season, the rains on the mountain slopes feed the streams. During the dry. summer the melting snow fields furnish water. 

Agriculture. In spite of its dry climate, the 

Great valley is a rich agricultural region. The rivers and streams have been dammed up, and irrigation canals dug. The soil is fertile and the land is level and easily cultivated. The farms are mostly small, for it is expensive to irrigate great tracts of land, and the farmer finds it more profitable to water a small area and cultivate it thoroughly.

Wheat was the first food product planted in the Great valley and for many years was the main crop. Barley, oats, and alfalfa were also raised. At first only enough fruits and vegetables were grown to supply the settlers who had come to California in search for gold along the Sacramento river. Some sheep and cattle were pastured on the lower slopes of the mountains, where the land was too dry for agriculture.

When the Western Pacific railway was built into California, there came the possibility of commerce with the east. The eastern markets were getting all the wheat they needed from the Central plains region, but there was a great demand for fruits and early vegetables. 

So most of the large California ranches were cut up into small fruit and vegetable farms, watered by irrigation. As the demand has grown, so has the industry. The Great valley is the center of California's wide vineyards, from which grape juice and raisins are shipped to all parts of the country. Raisins are sun-dried grapes, and the climate of the Great valley is ideal for producing them. 

Fresno is the center of the grape district and the raisin industry. Other fruits grown in the Great valley are figs, peaches, apricots, and some oranges and lemons. Prunes, a kind of plum, are raised here and dried for our breakfast tables. Many other fruits, and

NORTH AMERICA 

vegetables, too, are canned in large quantities and sent east. In almost any part of the country, you will find canned peaches, pears, plums, cherries, and asparagus bearing the labels of the Great valley's cooperative marketing associations. 

In the lower Sacramento valley, rice is grown. The two things that rice especially needs warmth in the air and moisture in the soil can both be had here. The warm air is natural. The soil is wet by irrigation. In the southern part of the Great valley, in the 

San Joaquin basin, the growing season is long and cotton is raised. 

Oil. Petroleum, too, is found in the southern end of the San Joaquin basin. California has become one of the great oil-producing states. The oil fields do not add to the beauty of California, but they add much to her prosperity. The railway engines here use oil instead of coal, which makes traveling clean and pleasant. Oil also furnishes power for manufacturing, and it is used as fuel for steamships. 

San Francisco. There is only one natural entrance to the Great valley, and that is the pass which the Sacramento and San Joaquin rivers have cut through the coastal ranges. At the western end of the pass lies San Francisco bay, one of the best natural harbors in the world. Two peninsulas protect the harbor waters from the storm waves of the sea. Between them a narrow channel, the Golden Gate, connects the bay with the broad waters of the Pacific.

Perched on the steep hills of one of the peninsulas which enclose the bay is the city of San Francisco. With her fine harbor and her position at the front door of the productive Great valley, she has naturally established a fine trade. San Francisco is a live city with varied interests. The big harbor is busy with ships from all countries, loading and unloading their cargoes. Ferryboats move back and forth across the bay between San Francisco and the neighboring cities. These ferry boats carry passengers and freight. They also carry trains from the San Francisco railway station to Oakland, which is the meeting place for the railways from east and north and south. 

San Francisco is a manufacturing city and a trading center for the products of the Great valley. She carries on shipbuilding. She has machine shops, canning factories, tanneries, and lumber mills. She manufactures clothing and shoes. She has large sugar refineries. 

From her many resources she ships barley and flour to Europe and Asia, cotton to Asia, and petroleum and fruits to all the world. Through her harbor come raw silk and rubber from 

Asia, coffee from Central and South America, sugar and copra, or dried coconut, from the 

Philippine islands. 

In November, 1936, San Francisco was connected with Oakland, across the bay, when the world's longest bridge over navigable water was opened. The bridge is 84 miles long and has six lanes for passenger automobiles and other lanes for trucks and interurban railway lines. 

For many years San Francisco was the greatest city on the Pacific coast. Now Los 

Angeles is larger and rivals her northern neighbor as an industrial and trading center. 

Other Cities. On the mainland across the bay from San Francisco, Oakland, Berkeley, and Alameda lie so close together that they seem like one great city. It is hard to tell where one ends and the other begins. Berkeley and Alameda are mostly residential cities with beautiful homes. Berkeley is the seat of the 

University of California. Oakland shares with

San Francisco in being a trade and manufacturing center. She manufactures much the same things that are manufactured in San 

Francisco. Richmond is connected by pipe lines with the oil fields, and makes gasoline and kerosene from petroleum. 

Sacramento and Stockton, two other important California cities, lie in the Great valley. Sacramento, the capital of California, is a manufacturing center with many canning factories. It is located on the Sacramento river. The city of Stockton has grown up in the midst of a rich agricultural and fruit-raising district. It is an important trading center, and a shipping port for grain.

Far north of the Great valley is another simla

THE WILLAMETTE- PUGET SOUND VALLEY 

ilar lowland, the Willamette-Puget Sound valley. Here, too, coastal mountain ranges shut the region off from the sea, while the lofty Cascade range forms a barrier on the eastern side. Here, also, two great rivers join their waters and flow together into the Pacific. This region has a fine natural harbor, Puget sound, which extends far inland, and is protected from the fury of the ocean storms.

Puget sound is a drowned valley, and is considered one of the most beautiful bodies of water in the world. It is framed by mountains and the snow-capped peaks of old volcanoes. In its many safe harbors large steamers may anchor. It offers splendid natural sites for cities. Four of them are Seattle, Tacoma, Everett, and Bellingham, all in Washington. Seattle, with a population of more than 300,000, is the largest city of the entire valley. These cities are seaports, and are also business and manufacturing centers. Behind them are rich agricultural and fruit-growing districts, dairy farms, and market gardens in the valley, and valuable forests on the mountain slopes.

The two rivers which flow through the Willamette-Puget Sound valley are the Columbia and the Willamette. The Columbia rises in 

Canada, and flows in a southerly direction through the state of Washington. Turning west, it forms part of the boundary between 

Washington and Oregon, and passes through a break in the Cascade mountains into the Willamette valley. Here it is joined by the 

Willamette river which flows north through the Willamette valley in the state of Oregon. 

Where the two rivers meet, about 100 miles from the sea, the thriving city of Portland has grown up. Portland is a great shipping and manufacturing center. Ocean steamers can sail up the river to its docks. Its sawmills are run by water power from the falls of the 

Willamette. Portland is the second largest city in the Willamette-Puget Sound valley.

Climate. The Willamette-Puget Sound val ley is so far north that you would naturally expect to find the climate very cold, but such is not the case. The warm Japan current keeps the weather mild, never very hot in summer, and seldom very cold in winter, so the growing season is very long. 

The effects of the warm Japan current can be best appreciated by comparing the climate of this region with that of an inland region of the same latitude. Portland, Oregon, and

Plattsburg, New York, have about the same latitude. In the summer their temperatures average about the same, but in the winter months the temperature of Portland averages nearly ten degrees higher than that of Plattsburg. The growing season in Portland is about a month longer, too, and so more crops can be grown here.

Unlike the mountains west of the Great valley, the coastal ranges that shut off the 

Willamette-Puget Sound valley from the sea are low and broken, and do not keep out the rain-bearing winds. Except in the months of 

July and August there is plenty of rainfall. 

As the Willamette-Puget Sound valley has more rainfall than the California valleys to the south, it has much less need of irrigation. It has more frost in winter, and cooler summers. 

In many parts snow is most unusual, although one may see it in the distance capping onceactive volcanic peaks.

Agriculture. The Willamette-Puget Sound valley is an agricultural center. Puget sound cities make flour of wheat from eastern Oregon and Washington, and ship wheat and flour to Europe and Asia. The climate of the valley is too cold for delicate fruits, but on its fertile, well-watered soil such hardy crops as oats, barley, and potatoes, grow well. Plums, apples, pears, and cherries are grown. Very fine strawberries come from this section of the country as well as loganberries, raspberries, and blackberries. Quantities of these berries are shipped fresh, but most of them are canned or made into syrups. The canning industry is very important in this valley region. 

With its many cities, you can understand why market gardens and dairy farms do so well. Poultry farms, too, are a steady source of income for many a farmer. As more land

NORTH AMERICA 

is cleared and put to use, the returns from this valley will be even greater. 

Cooperative marketing, which brings such benefits to the fruit growers of the Great valley and the California coastal plain, is also practiced in the Willamette-Puget Sound valley. Some of the large canneries are owned by the growers themselves, who prepare, sell, and ship their goods in bulk, reaching more markets and getting better prices than individual growers could possibly do.

Lumbering. Lumbering is one of the important industries of the valley. The nearby mountains are covered with luxuriant forests. 

The Douglas fir, the Sitka spruce, and the

Oregon pine are much in demand. The light wood of the Sitka spruce is used for airplanes. 

These trees grow to immense sizes. The logs are hauled out of the woods by wire ropes pulled by engines and are carried down the mountains by railroads to sawmills at Tacoma, Seattle, Portland, and other cities. Paper is made from small logs in many mills near the coast. Seattle has a special lumber industry in the making of red cedar shingles.

The Willamette-Puget Sound valley is one of the greatest lumber regions in the world, and the United States government is helping to preserve the forest by caring for the trees. 

Lumber and many wood products are shipped by rail to the east, or are sent by steamer to Alaska, or down the Pacific coast and through the Panama canal to the eastern Atlantic ports and to Europe, Asia, and Australia. 

Salmon. Except for Alaska, the Puget sound region leads the salmon industry of the world.

These fish are hatched in the fresh waters of the Columbia and other rivers which flow into the sound or the Pacific ocean. When they are big enough they travel down the rivers and into the salt waters of the ocean. Here they live until they are fully grown. Then each year when the egg-laying season arrives the salmon swim back up the rivers, far above the reach of the tide, to lay their eggs. It is when the salmon are swimming upstream, away from the ocean, that most of them are caught, in nets set in the streams.

Great salmon-canning plants are built near the rivers. Some of the fish are canned, and others are shipped out packed in ice. The city of Bellingham, Washington, is one of the great canning centers. From it and from the other seaports of Puget sound thousands of tons of salmon are shipped every year to countries all over the world.

A few years ago it was realized that the salmon industry was in danger of dying out because the salmon were gradually being killed off. The United States government now forbids fishermen to spread their nets entirely across a stream. This gives the salmon a chance to get back upstream and lay their eggs. The government has also established hatcheries for salmon in Alaska, where the fish are protected until they are big enough to take care of themselves. Then they are put in the rivers. 

Manufacturing. Besides the sawmills and the canning factories the Puget sound region has other industries. There are shipyards and woodworking plants, and many of the cities have machine shops. The conditions are favorable to manufacturing because there is water power, good transportation, and plenty of lumber. 

Commerce. Transcontinental railroads con nect the cities of the valley with the east. Products are brought from states far inland to be exported from Portland and the Puget sound harbors. The wheat and fruits from the Columbia plateau, just beyond the Cascades, reach their markets through these shipping centers. Then there are the products of the valley region itself to be shipped. The lumber trade alone would make shipping an important industry.

But all of the harbor trade is not exporting. 

Many manufactured articles, cotton, and a number of raw materials must be imported. 

Among these are coffee from Central and 

South America, silk and rubber from Asia, and copper from Alaska, Canada, and South America. 

One way to leave the Willamette-Puget

Sound valley is to go by steamer up the Columbia river which has cut the only river pass in this region. THE

Mines of this 'type are called open-cut mines because the mining is done above ground instead of underground. 

This is the largest open-cut mine in North America. 

The miners live in the town at the bottom of the deep valley, or canyon. Notice how terraces, or steps, have been cut in the slopes as the copper-bearing ore has been removed. Railroad lines along the terraces transport the ore to buildings nearby, where some of the impurities are removed.

The tank cars in the foreground carry these products to many distant markets.

24. An irrigation canal in the Imperial valley, California. Water from such canals irrigates the rich soil and makes the valley one of the great fruit-growing sections of the country. Before irrigation was introduced, the Imperial valley consisted almost entirely of unproductive desert land. 

25. The city of El Paso, Texas. In El Paso, copper and lead are smelted, or separated from their ores. The ores are brought from the desert mines of Mexico, New 

Mexico, and Arizona. El Paso's dry climate attracts thousands of visitors in search of health. On irrigated lands nearby, cattle and cotton are raised. In these ways man is making use of the once desert land. 

26. Typical desert country near Phoenix, Arizona. The plant growths are different kinds of cactus plants and dry sagebrush. Such an area supports little life. Within a few miles is a rich agricultural section where fine oranges, olives, and cotton are grown and prosperous farmers live. 

Irrigation makes the difference.

As there are no inside stairways, ladders are used to reach the flat roofs, which serve as outdoor rooms.

foaming torrent. But these rapids do not stop boats, for a canal with locks has been built around them.

On the eastern side of the Cascades you will find a region of rolling plains and rounded hills, covered at harvest time with miles and miles of golden wheat. You are now on the

Columbia plateau. It includes the whole southeastern quarter of Washington, much of eastern Oregon, and part of western Idaho. This great region is drained by the Columbia and 

Snake rivers. These two rivers, during thousands of years, have cut deep canyons through the soil and rock, and if you look down into these canyons you will see the rivers flowing along far below the surface of the plateau. 

Transportation. Like the Great valley and the Willamette-Puget Sound valley, the Columbia plateau lies between two mountain ranges, the Cascade range and the Rocky mountains. In the early days the mountains were barriers to transportation, but men have largely overcome these difficulties. The Columbia river made a natural gap through the mountains and only the short canal around the rapids was needed to connect the plateau by boat with the Willamette-Puget Sound valley and the Pacific ocean. Through passes in the Rocky mountains railroad lines have been built leading to the east. So, in spite of the mountains, the people of the Columbia plateau carry on a considerable trade, shipping out the wheat they grow, and receiving in exchange manufactured goods and other needed articles. 

Climate. But the mountains are still a barrier to rain-bringing winds. There is some rain during the mild winters but the summer months are hot and dry. The small rivers and streams have long been dammed, and many irrigation canals carry their waters to the crops. The Columbia and Snake rivers flow through deep canyons far below nearby fields, but it is now planned to use their waters to irrigate crops in lower parts of the region. 

The plateau was at one time covered with lava, which has gradually broken up and formed a deep, rich soil.

Products. What crops are grown in this region? Winter wheat is the most important, because the mild winters and the hot, dry summers are ideal for this grain. Huge fields of wheat, of alfalfa, and of potatoes, stretch for miles. Oats, sugar beets, and various northern fruits are raised. The apples of the Hood river and the Yakima river valleys are famous all over the United States. In the poorer sections of the plateau where there is less water for irrigation, forage crops are grown, and cattle and sheep are pastured. 

Cities. Spokane, in eastern Washington, is the most important city of the Columbia plateau. It is built on both sides of the Spokane river, which rushes through the city in a series of rapids and waterfalls. The river is harnessed and made to furnish water power and electricity. Spokane is also an important railroad center, and is known as the gateway to the plateau. Other cities are Yakima and 

Walla Walla, in Washington, and Boise, in

Idaho. All these cities are distributing centers for the plateau products and for the manufactured goods brought from the east by rail. 

THE GREAT BASIN 

Traveling south over the Columbia plateau, one enters the Great basin. From southern 

Oregon the Great basin extends south through most of Nevada and the western part of Utah.

New York state could be set down in either of these great regions with plenty of space to spare. 

Like the Columbia plateau, the Great basin is shut in on either side by mountains. The 

Sierra Nevadas are on the west. On the east are the Wasatch mountains which are part of the Rocky mountain system. But no river has cut a pass to help travel to and from this section. 

In spite of its name the Great basin is really a plateau, from 4,000 to 5,000 feet high, built up by soils washed down the mountain sides. Small, separate mountain ranges running north and south are found here and there throughout the region. Rain falls on the western slopes of the Wasatch range and on a little of the land near its base. Otherwise this region has almost no rainfall. Some parts have none at all for a year or more at a time. The

NORTH AMERICA 

mountains greedily take the rain and let the basin parch.

Although the mountain slopes are forested, the plateau has few trees. For hundreds of miles, it is just a smooth, hot expanse of desert land. In some places the ground is covered with a whitish salt substance, called alkali. In other places there are sad-looking stretches of baked, cracked mud where once there were shallow lakes. Where there is any vegetation at all, cactus, sagebrush, and greasewood grow.

Sheep and a few small herds of cattle graze on what little forage they can find. There are a few ranches in this section, but one can travel fifty or one hundred miles without seeing a soul. The heat is terrific at times. Even people going through on trains become very dusty and thirsty and sick of the heat.

The Wasatch Mountain Section. The country at the base of the Wasatch mountains is the pleasantest part of the Great basin. It is here that cities and towns have sprung up. Many mountain streams pour down the slopes to furnish water power and to freshen the nearby land, before they disappear into the dry sections. Some of them really do disappearjust dry up from the intense heat out in the desert. Others flow into shallow lakes, from which the water evaporates as fast as it is brought by the streams. These streams gather salt or soda on their way down the mountain sides and the waters in the lakes they feed taste of salt and soda. Great Salt lake in Utah is the largest lake in the country except the Great lakes. It is so heavy with salt that a person cannot possibly sink in it. There is also a lake called Soda lake, and another from which potash is obtained.

Even before the white men came, Indians lived in the Wasatch section and raised crops. 

They dug crude irrigation ditches, and so rich was the soil that they were able to raise all the crops they needed.

It was back in 1847 that a group of settlers moved into the Great basin. They were from the east, and came seeking a place where they could follow the Mormon religion. The Mormons settled near Great Salt lake, and built irrigation dams, canals, and ditches. The desert blossomed as if by magic. Orchards of apple, pear, and peach trees flourished. Sugar beets, grain, and potatoes were grown. These products of the soil are still raised in large quantities in the Mormon land. Salt Lake

City, the capital of Utah, is the center of the region which the Mormons took from the desert.

In the western part of the basin is another fertile irrigated area. Here the Carson and

Truckee rivers have been dammed up and made to irrigate the fields. Carson City, Nevada, lies in this rich agricultural region. 

The Mormons also discovered that the Great basin has rich deposits of petroleum, gold, silver, and copper. Today these products bring riches to the region. Bingham, Utah, is the greatest copper city in the Great basin.

The mineral wealth of the region has led to the establishment of many small mining towns and settlements. All of these mining towns are in a dry, desert land, and all suffer from lack of water.

COLORADO PLATEAU 

The eastern neighbor of the Great basin, beyond the Wasatch mountains, is the Colorado plateau. About a mile and a half above sea level, it stretches, rocky and arid, through southern 

Utah, northern Arizona and western Colorado and New Mexico. In parts it is a fairyland region of colored rocks, of giant trees turned to stone, of gashes cut so deep in the surface of the earth that it makes one dizzy to look down into them.

In other parts this plateau is a desert region, with bare earth showing between its bunches of grass, with few trees, and with no cool little streams and rivers. The rainfall is scanty. 

The days are hot but at times the nights become extremely cold. Scattered about this region are many flat-topped hills. These hills look so much like tables that the Indians call them by the Spanish word for table, which is mesa. 

Few people live on the Colorado plateau.

There are some ranches where sheep and cattle are raised. Scattered groups of Indians have little villages perched on the flat-topped mesas. A few places have been irrigated, and

produce crops. 

Extensive irrigation is not practical because the waters of the only great river in the region, the Colorado, flow at the bottom of a deep canyon, and cannot easily be raised to water the fields.

Grand Canyon. The Grand canyon of the 

Colorado river draws thousands of visitors every year. From all over the earth people come to see this marvelous piece of carving that the muddy Colorado has performed. In all the world there is no other canyon so deep and so wide, and many people feel that the world has no more beautiful sight.

The Grand canyon of the Colorado river is over 200 miles long, and at places it is more than ten miles wide. Standing on the edge one can look down for a sheer distance of a mile. 

The walls look like the work of some strange painter, for red, and rich brown, and golden yellow, and grey, and many other colors are all blended in a wild and beautiful design.

These colorful rocks, which change their hue and shimmer in the sunlight and the rain, are carved in fascinating shapes. Some look like cathedrals and temples, jutting out from the sides of the canyon, and others look like trees or human forms turned to colored stones and set carelessly on the edge of space. 

It seems astonishing that a single river could do such a great and wonderful piece of carving, but the Colorado has been at its work for millions of years. Way back, long before the days of the glaciers, the area that is now the Colorado plateau was an inland sea with a bottom composed of layers of mud. Year after year, and age after age, more mud sifted down through the water, and layer after layer was formed. Little by little this mud hardened into rock. All the time, the sea bottom and the surrounding lands were slowly rising. When man first laid eyes on this region the former sea bottom was a plateau whose surface was well over a mile above the level of the sea. 

Across this high plateau flowed the Colorado river. As it flowed it broke little pieces from the rocks, and carried them along. Gradually a valley was formed which deepened and widened as age after age went by. It has taken a longer time than any of us can imagine for the river to carve this mighty canyon. And it is still slowly working to make the canyon even deeper, and to carve out more of the strange and beautiful rock formations which so delight all who see them.

The Colorado river has another piece of work under way where it empties into the Gulf of California. It is busily building 

LOWER COLORADO REGION 

up a delta at its mouth with the dirt and sand and rock which it carries away from its carving work up in the Grand canyon. The Gulf of 

California once extended much farther inland than it does now, but the river has filled in all this inland space with its rich and fertile delta. 

The land of this great delta is called the Lower Colorado region. It takes in part of southeastern California and western Arizona, and extends to the south into Mexico. It lies along the Colorado and Gila rivers, and depends upon these rivers for its existence. 

Irrigation. When white men first came to the Lower Colorado region, it was one of the driest places in all North America. The coastal mountain ranges shut out the rain-bearing winds from the Pacific, and the hot sun baked the dried-up ground. But the soil was rich, and men were not content to let the area go to waste. Several great irrigation dams have been built across the Colorado river, and across the Gila and its tributary, the Salt river. One of the most famous of these is the Roosevelt dam, east of Phoenix, Arizona, in the valley of the Salt river. This great, curved, stone dam holds back the river waters, and has formed a lake twenty-five miles long, two miles wide and more than 200 feet deep. Irrigation canals carry this water far and wide, and over 200,000 acres have been made productive by this one irrigation dam alone. Another important irrigation project is the Boulder dam across the 

Colorado river.

Products. Phoenix, Arizona's capital, is in the center of the rich agricultural region watered by the Roosevelt dam. The lands nearby produce the long-fibered Egyptian cotton, which is considered the best grade that can be grown. Oranges, grapefruit, and olives are grown and are shipped to all parts NASA-7

NORTH AMERICA 

of the country. Ostriches, too, are raised in this region, and their beautiful white feathers are used on hats, in fans, and as trimming for garments. 

Farther to the east, in New Mexico, the waters of the Rio Grande are also used for irrigation. Here wheat, alfalfa, corn, and cotton are grown. Livestock raising is an important industry of the district, and much of the alfalfa and grain is fed to the sheep and cattle. Where there is some rainfall, wheat and Kafir corn are raised by dry farming.

Each unit of the graph scale represents ten million sheep. 50 Another important product of the Lower Colorado region is copper. Arizona produces more copper than any other state and some is also mined in New Mexico.

The Imperial Valley. Perhaps the most interesting section of the Lower Colorado region is the Imperial valley in southeastern California. You may remember how the Egyptians used the waters of the river Nile to irrigate their desert land and make Egypt a great crop-producing country. The people of the Imperial valley have done much the same thing. A great dam has been built in the valley, across the Colorado river, and the waters are spread through irrigation canals and ditches to the fields. 

The Imperial valley's splendid irrigation system, and the hot, sub-tropical climate of the valley combine to give the section valuable crops. Grapefruit, olives, melons, apricots, figs, peaches, alfalfa, and grains are grown. 

Cantaloupes from here are usually the first of the season to reach many breakfast tables throughout the country. Even date trees, which are natives of the Sahara desert, find the climate hot enough, and the irrigated soil moist enough to make them grow well. The long-fibered Egyptian cotton is another valuable product of the valley. The Imperial valley, in fact, has about the same latitude, the same rainfall, the same climate, and the same products as Egypt, and it is therefore known as the American Egypt.

DESERTS 

Many parts of the Lower Colorado and nearby regions have not yet been irrigated. 

These places are terrible indeed. 

Death valley, in California, is lower than the level of the sea, and is walled in by mountains which keep out the rainfall and even the winds themselves. There are few streams in this valley, and no lakes at all. Almost never is there any rain. The sun beats down upon the sand, and the sand reflects the glare and heat. Many of the early pioneers who tried to cross Death valley in search of gold lost their lives in the attempt. 

Between Death valley and the San Bernardino mountains to the south lie the great 

Mohave and Colorado deserts, extending nearly across California. These are also regions of heat and drought. They, too, have taken the lives of many of the people who tried to cross them.

Men are still working to reclaim the desert regions. Perhaps some day they will succeed in irrigating these deserts and turning them into productive areas, as they have done to so many sections of the west.

1. The Western highland region is made up of plateaus, deserts, valleys, and mountains running generally north and south. This region is part of the great western mountain system that extends the length of both North and South America. 

2. The mountains of the Western highland region are the highest in North America. The main ranges are the Rockies, the Coast ranges, the Sierra Nevadas, and the Cascades. Some of the mountains were once volcanoes. 

3. Through the mountain ranges are several high passes, and some canyons cut by rivers. Through these lie the main railroad routes. 

4. The Rocky mountains include a large part of the 

Continental Divide, which separates the rivers which run into the Pacific ocean from those that run into the Gulf of Mexico and the Atlantic ocean. The rivers of the western mountain region are a source of much water power. 

5. Except for the western slopes of the mountain ranges, the climate of most of the Western highland region is dry. In some places irrigation has made the dry lands productive. 

6. The Western highlands are noted for their forest lands. Besides furnishing lumber and fuel, the forests regulate the flow of rivers and prevent floods. 

The government has set aside forest preserves to protect its trees. 

7. The government has established many national parks as playgrounds for its people. Among these are 

Yosemite National park, Yellowstone National park, Glacier National park, Grand Canyon National park, and Crater Lake National park. 

8. The mountains of the Western highland region are rich in valuable minerals. Much gold, silver, copper, lead, zinc, coal, and phosphate rock are mined there.

Old-fashioned placer mining has largely given way to modern methods. 

9. In Southern California are a narrow coastal plain and the San Bernardino valley, lying between the San Bernardino mountains and the sea. These have a 

Mediterranean climate, with a wet season and a dry season. In these sections, oranges, lemons, grapefruit, olives, figs, grapes, alfalfa, wheat, other grains, and many kinds of vegetables are raised. Irrigation is carried on extensively. 

10. Many of the fruit growers of the Western highland region have banded together and have their own canneries and shipping centers. Such working together is called cooperative marketing. 

11. The beautiful and varied scenery of Southern California is valuable to the motion picture industry. It also brings many tourists from all parts of the country. Southern California and Florida have become rivals for the winter tourist trade. 

12. Los Angeles is the chief city of Southern California.

It is a railroad and shipping center, and a manufacturing city. Petroleum is found nearby. The moving picture industry is important in Los Angeles. Los 

Angeles gets its water supply from Owens lake, through an aqueduct 240 miles long. 

13. Pasadena, Long Beach, and San Diego are important cities of southern California. Pasadena and 

Long Beach are beautiful suburbs of Los Angeles.

San Diego is a center for California's important fishing industry. 

14. Throughout California one sees many buildings built in the Spanish style. These and the remains of old 

Spanish missions remind one that California was originally settled by the Spaniards. 

15. The Great valley lies between the coastal mountain ranges and the Sierra Nevadas. The valley is really two river basins laid end to end. The Sacramento and San Joaquin rivers flow through it. 

16. The Great valley has a dry climate, but irrigation allows many crops to grow. Grapes and raisins are the leading products. Peaches, prunes, figs, apricots, and vegetables are canned or dried. Rice and cotton are also raised, and there are some valuable oil wells. 

17. San Francisco bay lies at the outer end of the pass made by the waters of the Sacramento and San 

Joaquin rivers. Around this natural harbor are clustered the cities of San Francisco, Oakland,

Berkeley, and Alameda. The largest of these is 

San Francisco, an important trading and manufacturing center, and a great rival of Los Angeles. 

Sacramento and Stockton are important cities within the Great valley. 

18. North of the Great valley lies the Willamette-Puget

Sound valley. Hardy grains, potatoes and various fruits, and poultry are raised here in abundance. 

Much lumber is exported. This is the world's second most important salmon region. Among the important cities of this region are Seattle, Portland, Tacoma, Everett, and Bellingham. 

19. The climate of the Willamette-Puget Sound valley is cooler than that of California, but milder than that of eastern sections of the same latitude. This is due to the warm Japan current which sweeps the coast. 

20. East of the Willamette-Puget sound region is the 

Columbia plateau. This is a region of rolling hills enclosed by mountains. Some irrigation is carried on, and wheat, alfalfa, potatoes, oats, sugar beets, and northern fruits are raised. In some of the poorer sections of the plateau forage crops are grown and cattle and sheep are pastured. 

21. Spokane, the most important city of the Columbia plateau, is a railroad center, and a distributing center for the plateau products, and for manufactured goods brought from the east. Yakima and Walla Walla, Washington, and Boise, Idaho, are other cities of this region. 

22. South of the Columbia plateau is the Great basin, which occupies parts of Oregon, Nevada, and Utah. 

This is a high plateau with little rainfall. 

23. The country at the base of the Wasatch mountains is the most pleasant part of the Great basin. Here the mountain streams are used for water power and for irrigation, and apples, pears, peaches, sugar beets,

NORTH AMERICA 

grain, and potatoes are raised. Petroleum, gold, silver, and copper are also found in this region. The section was originally settled by the Mormons. Its main city is Salt Lake City. 

24. The Colorado plateau lies east of the Great basin.

This is a high, rocky plateau about a mile and a half above sea level. It extends through southern Utah, northern Arizona, and western Colorado and New

Mexico. This plateau is barren and few people live on it. The great canyon which the Colorado river has carved attracts many thousands of tourists every year. 

25. Near its mouth the Colorado river has built up a great delta of rich soil. The mountains to the west make the climate of this region dry, but great irrigation dams have been built, and many sections of the 

1. The life of a forest ranger. 

Lower Colorado region are now very productive. 

Oranges, grapefruit, olives, alfalfa, Kafir corn, grain, and cotton are grown in large quantities. Ostriches are raised in this section. 

26. The Imperial valley of southeastern California has irrigation and is one of the most productive parts of the Lower Colorado region. Grapefruit, olives, melons, apricots, figs, peaches, alfalfa, grains and dates are grown. Because this country is so much like Egypt, it is called the American Egypt. 

27. Many deserts in the Western highland have not yet been irrigated. Death valley in California is lower than the sea, and has very little moisture. The 

Mohave and Colorado deserts to the south are other dry and barren regions. Perhaps some day these will be irrigated, and will become productive. 

2. A comparison between Southern California and the

Great valley as to climate, products, and industries. 

3. Salmon fishing on the Columbia river.

The water in the canal behind this plant comes from the river farther upstream. It falls with great force through the pipes on the face of the cliff to the power house below where it turns machinery which makes electric current. Canada has many hydroelectric plants. 

Beyond the plant we see flat country which is typical of 

Canada's lake plains.

Fruit is most safely raised near large bodies of water. Nova Scotia has many such fine orchards.

SIZE, POSITION AND POPULATION CANADA 

North of the United States is the country of Canada, larger in area than the United States and Alaska together. From its southernmost point, about 

42° north latitude, Canada reaches north to within a few hundred miles of the North Pole. From east to west it extends over three thousand miles. 

It is a dominion, or colony, of the British empire. Great Britain sends a governor to be at the head of the government, but the people make their own laws.

The Dominion of Canada, as it is called, is divided into the provinces of British Columbia, Alberta, Saskatchewan, Manitoba, Ontario, Quebec, New Brunswick, Nova Scotia, and 

Prince Edward island. These provinces are somewhat like our states. The northern part of the country is divided into the territories of 

Yukon, Franklin, Mackenzie, and Keewatin.

The entire population of Canada is less than ten million, two million less than that of New York state alone. There are few large centers of population and there are vast areas with little or no human life.

Many of the great natural regions of our continent lie partly in the United States and partly in Canada, so in studying about Canada you will find regions you already know. Canada, however, has no Atlantic coastal plain. 

MARITIME PROVINCES 

Along the rugged Atlantic coast of Canada are the three Maritime provinces, New Brunswick, Nova Scotia, and Prince. 

Edward island. Maritime means "bordering the sea." What a good name this is for these provinces! They are open to ocean transportation the entire year.

In soil, in climate, and in natural resources, the Maritime provinces are much like our New

England states. Like New England, they lie within the Appalachian highland and the Piedmont plateau. Some of the valleys produce grains, potatoes, and apples. Livestock are raised and dairying is carried on. In Nova 

Scotia there is a fine fruit belt bordering the Bay of Fundy. Pine forests cover the hills. 

Unlike New England, Nova Scotia has de posits of coal.

The Maritime provinces were settled long before any of the other regions of Canada.

But due to rocky soil, and the cold winds from the Labrador current, they have not developed as rapidly as other regions. 

Along both sides of the St. Lawrence river is a broad valley, lifting in wide terraces to the Laurentian highland to the north and to the Appalachian VALLEY highland to the south. Fertile

ST. LAWRENCE 

soil and mild climate make this valley a rich farming region. The St. Lawrence drains a large area south of Hudson bay. Most of the streams which flow into it have falls that may be used for water power. The St. Lawrence river was the highway for the first explorers of Canada, and the fertile lands bordering the river soon became known, and drew early settlers. This area with its fertile soil is now a rich farming section.

In the southeastern part of the province of 

Ontario there are lake plains with rich soil. 

They form a peninsula between Lake Huron and Lake Erie, and extend north of Lake Ontario. The climate is made mild by the surrounding bodies of water.

The lake plains and the St. Lawrence valley are the most productive farming areas of 

Canada. Apples, grapes, other fruits, and vegetables are raised in great quantities. These two regions are more easily reached by rail, highway, and water than any of the interior provinces. Almost unlimited water power can be supplied by Niagara falls, the rapids of the

St. Lawrence, and the many tributaries of this great river. Their varied products, fine transportation, and water power all help to make the lake plains and the river valley the richest and the most densely populated regions of 115

LAURENTIAN HIGHLAND AND THE FOREST BELT 

North of the lake plains and the St. Lawrence valley stretches a great highland area, shaped like a rough new moon.

This is the Laurentian highland. 

On the west it curves down from the Arctic ocean, crosses parts of Saskatchewan, Manitoba, and Ontario, and reaches up to take in nearly all of Quebec. It includes all the land bordering Hudson bay, except a small area south of the bay, known as the Hudson Bay lowland. The southern parts of Manitoba,

Ontario, and Quebec are the only sections of the Laurentian highland which are thickly settled. Few people live north of the 60° parallel, which forms the northern boundary of British Columbia, Alberta, Saskatchewan, and Manitoba.

The Laurentian highland has wonderfully rich deposits of copper, iron, gold, silver, and nickel. Some mines are being worked, but many of these resources await future development. The highland soil is thin. Here we find a great forest belt. This belt extends beyond the Laurentian highland to the Pacific coast. 

Because of good transportation in the southern part of the Laurentian highland, the nearby forests are a source of great profit to Canada. Much of the forest is soft wood, and a great deal of wood pulp for paper is made from the timber. Vast forests to the north are as yet uncut.

North of the forest belt, bordering the icy 

Arctic ocean, are great, treeless plains called tundras. How bleak they are, TUNDRAS covered with only moss and grass, and swept by icy winds! A short distance below the surface, the ground is always frozen. 

The slow melting of the frost near the surface and of the snow which covers the ground so much of the year provides moisture for the roots of the grass and moss during the region's short summer. Even if there is no rain the grass and moss do not wither.

The Eskimos, the only inhabitants of this region, have been slowly dying out because of scarcity of game for food.

The Canadian government is now teaching the Eskimos to raise reindeer herds as they can provide the 

Eskimos with many of their needs. Reindeer thrive on the tundra moss. 

Off the northern coast of Canada are many islands, large and small. Such a group of islands is called an archipelago. 

Because of its location, this group is called the Arctic archipelago. Many of the islands are covered the year round with ice and snow.

ARCTIC ARCHIPELAGO 

Where the rolling Central plains of North

America extend into Canada they include parts of Manitoba and Saskatchewan. 

The spring wheat belt continues from our country and covers nearly all of the Canadian Central plains.

CENTRAL 

PLAINS 

West of the Laurentian highland and the 

Central plains are the Great plains of North

America. They include a cor- GREAT PLAINS 

ner of British Columbia, most of Alberta, and a strip of Saskatchewan, and extend north along the Mackenzie river to the Arctic ocean. In surface and in altitude the 

Great plains in Canada are like those of our country. The winters are often very cold. But even in the coldest weather, warm winds from the Pacific find their way over the mountains. 

They lose their moisture on the western slopes. 

Being warm and dry as they drop down the eastern slopes, they melt the snow rapidly and take up moisture. These winds are called chinooks. 

The northern part of Canada's Great plains lies in the treeless tundras. South of this is the forest belt, stretching west from the Laurentian highland. And still farther south is the spring wheat belt, continuing from the Central plains.

In Canada, the Great plains and the Central plains are often spoken of as the interior plains, or prairies. Alberta, Saskatchewan, and Manitoba are called the prairie provinces. The prairies were first used as pasture for sheep and cattle. The best land is now laid off in large farms. These are used almost entirely for the raising of grains. The growing season in the Canadian wheat belt is short, but in this

NORTH AMERICA 

far north country summer days are so long that wheat ripens rapidly. So much of this land is still unsettled that no one knows just how far north wheat can be grown, but the belt is large. In one recent year, Canada produced over 500,000,000 bushels of wheat.

Barley, rye, potatoes, and flax are also raised in the prairie provinces. Because it is possible to raise such fine crops in these provinces their population has increased rapidly. Many farmers from the United States have been drawn to them by the cheap wheat lands. As the world demand for wheat becomes greater, it is likely that more and more farmers will settle on the interior plains of Canada.

The prairie provinces are crossed by good railroads connecting them with different Canadian cities and also with the United States.

So it is easy for the farmers of this region to market their crops.

WESTERN HIGHLANDS 

North America's Western highland region stretches along the entire western part of 

Canada. It rises from the very shores of the Pacific and extends inland from 400 to 500 miles. It is made up of mountain ranges with broad, high valleys and plateaus between. These mountains reach northward through British 

Columbia, Alberta, Yukon, and the western part of Mackenzie. Many of the peaks are over 10,000 feet above sea level. A few in the north reach a height of over 17,000 feet. Glaciers are frequent. Mountain lakes and streams add to the beauty of the region. Rainfall sometimes reaches ninety inches a year on the western slopes of the mountains. This causes a dense growth of stately forests. There are fewer trees on the drier eastern slopes. The climate of Canada's Pacific coast is much warmer than that of its Atlantic coast because of the warm Japan current.

The valleys of British Columbia are in the same fruit belt as the Willamette-Puget Sound region in our states of Washington and Oregon. Vegetables and grains are raised in the main river valleys. Many high, grassy mountain slopes and valleys provide fine pasture for cattle. Because of its mild climate the east central part of Alberta produces the same vegetables that are raised around the city of Toronto, over 700 miles farther south. 

The Canadian Western highlands holds a wealth of minerals. In the southern part of 

British Columbia, where transportation is good, there is much mining. As mines are opened farther north, railroads will follow the path of development. Much of this mountainous province, however, is still untouched by man, though it holds splendid promise for the future.

Long, long ago the mountainous northwestern coast of North America settled to a lower level. The sea entered many of the FIORD 

valleys along the coast, forming deep COAST bays and fiords, or inlets. It even made mountain tops into islands! As a result, the western coast line of Canada is very irregular. It is also rather short, as Alaska extends south in a narrow coastal strip shutting about half of British Columbia away from the sea.

AND 

RIVERS 

Rivers are numerous in Canada. Over half the country slopes gently toward Hudson bay, into which many streams drain. An- LAKES other vast and important drainage system is the St. Lawrence, which includes the Great lakes. This system extends from the central part of the country to the Atlantic ocean. The northwestern part of the country is drained by the Mackenzie river system. 

WATER 

Canada's many rivers and lakes offer vast water power though much of it has not as yet been developed. The lakes act as reservoirs for the rivers, keeping POWER their flow more even, winter and summer. Heavy rains, also, keep the streams from growing smaller in summer. 

Many of the streams have falls and rapids that can be used for water power as manufacturing increases. Many great hydroelectric plants have already been built, especially in 

Quebec, Ontario, Manitoba, and British Columbia. Their power is used to run machinery, to light the streets of cities, to operate trolley cars, and for many uses in the homes. 

The largest share of Canada's wealth comes from the land under cultivation, although this

AGRICULTURAL RESOURCES 

is only a small part of her total area. The farming regions extend through the southern and central parts of the country, and their products are varied. Experiment stations are supported by the government at different places. Here men study conditions of soil and climate, and do all they can to help the farmer with his problems. 

Grains are a most important crop. With her broad farms and the use of modern machinery in cultivating and harvesting, Canada is one of the leading grain producers of the world.

Wheat of an unusually fine quality is grown in ever-increasing amounts. Oats, buckwheat, barley, rye, potatoes, and hay are also grown and harvested with the aid of machinery. 

Horses, beef cattle, and sheep are raised in the prairie provinces and in many mountain valleys. Through its dairy cattle, the St. Lawrence valley has become famous for butter, cheese, and condensed milk.

Fruits are grown in the valley around Annapolis, in Nova Scotia, all through the St. 

Lawrence valley, on the peninsula of southeastern Ontario, and along the valley of the 

Fraser river in British Columbia. Peas, beans, asparagus, tomatoes, cabbage, cauliflower, onions, lettuce, and celery are widely raised in these regions.

The forest belt stretches from the Pacific slope of the Western highlands entirely across the Laurentian highland. It in- 

FORESTS 

cludes many kinds of trees, from the giant Douglas fir and the valuable cedar of 

British Columbia to the spruce and scrub oak of Quebec. By discouraging the shipment of logs to the United States, Canada is trying to encourage the building of her own sawmills, pulp mills, and paper mills. She is developing a rich wood pulp industry in the east-especially in Quebec and Ontario. Vast tracts of forest land, however, are still too far from the railroads to pay for cutting the lumber. It would cost more than the logs are worth to get them to a manufacturing center. 

There are many valuable mineral deposits in Canada. No one can tell how great her

MINERALS 

mineral resources really are, as so much of her area is little known.

Gold has long been an important product. So valuable is even a small deposit of this metal that as soon as it becomes known that gold has been discovered in some section thousands of people flock to the district, hoping to make a fortune. This has often led to untold misery. 

Men have entered the gold fields with too scanty supplies of food, and too little money to buy more. Many have found starvation instead of wealth. Others have wasted their lives in a vain search. But others have become fabulously rich almost overnight.

One rich gold field, famous in history, was the Klondike, near Dawson, in the Yukon region, not far from the Alaskan border. It is no longer so productive as in years past. 

Most Canadian gold now comes from mines in the dense Ontario woods. These mines were found only a few years ago. One reason why they have become important so rapidly is that airplanes make it possible to reach them quickly and easily. 

Copper, lead, zinc, and silver are mined in 

British Columbia. Cobalt, in Ontario, has famous silver mines. Nickel is a metal found in very few places in the world. Rich deposits near Sudbury, Ontario, make this the world's leading section in nickel production. 

A strange mineral, called asbestos, is quarried in large quantities in southern Quebec, south of the St. Lawrence river. As it will not burn, but holds heat well, it has many uses after it is crushed and purified. You may find sheets of it around the pipes of your furnace, to keep the heat from escaping. Your mother may have little asbestos mats to keep hot dishes from marking the table.

Coal is found in Canada, but because of the distance of some of the coal fields from the railroads, much of it cannot profitably be mined as yet. There are three main areas where coal is now mined. Mines in the south of British 

Columbia supply much hard coal. In Alberta and Saskatchewan there are great soft coal fields, but, as they are far from the thickly populated parts of Canada, they have not been very much opened up. There are many rich

NORTH AMERICA 

mines near the sea, in Nova Scotia. This coal is shipped to seaports of the United States or taken by rail or boat to other Canadian cities. 

The recent discovery of petroleum fields near the source of the Mackenzie river promises added wealth for Canada. As transportation lines extend farther, opening up new regions, the value of Canada's mineral products will rapidly increase.

FISHERIES 

Canada is most fortunate in her fisheries. 

Not only her seacoasts, but her unnumbered lakes and streams abound in fish. 

From the time of the first settlers, fishermen have visited the great shallows, or banks, in the Atlantic ocean off the Canadian and Newfoundland shores. Cod, herring, haddock, and mackerel are the fish caught in greatest numbers in the salt waters along the eastern coast. The broad estuary of the St. 

Lawrence river is also rich in fish.

Nova Scotia is well fitted for the fishing industry. Her nearness to the fishing banks gives her a big advantage over New England. 

Her many protected harbors, lined with little fishing villages, offer refuge to small fishing boats as well as to ships that carry the dried, salted, or canned fish to foreign markets. 

Off the Pacific coast are salmon, herring, halibut, and many other kinds of fish. Herring are caught in the greatest quantity, but salmon are considered more of a delicacy and bring a much better price than the bony herring. Much of the herring is dried for market. The salmon is canned. The choicer, better flavored cuts bring a high price. The poorer cuts, too, sell well, although the price is less. Pacific coast salmon is sold in all the markets of the world. 

Whitefish, salmon trout, and several other kinds of fresh water fish make the inland waters of Canada ideal for sportsmen. Men who love hunting and fishing come hundreds of miles to pass their vacations along the Canadian lakes and streams.

The vast wilderness of Canada has long been the center of another industry-trapping. The forests shelter marten, foxes, mink, skunk, beaver, otter, and other fur-bearing animals. Lakes and streams 

TRAPPING 

supply the fish which many of them catch for food. The cold makes the fur of these animals thick and soft, and of a much finer quality than the fur of animals found farther south. 

Trappers set lines of traps in the winter time, and visit them on snowshoes every few days to get what has been caught. The skin with the fur on is called a pelt. The pelts of all of these animals are valuable. Some rare pelts bring as much as a thousand dollars apiece. 

HUDSON'S BAY COMPANY 

For over 250 years, an English company, known as the Hudson's Bay Company, has had the privilege of trading for furs with the Indian and white trappers. The men first sent out by this great company went into the Canadian wilderness, where they built and fortified lonely trading posts, called factories. The man in charge was called the factor. Provisions, blankets, guns, knives, traps, and clothing were kept at the factory and were traded for furs with the trappers.

The pelts of different kinds of animals were worth different amounts in trade. The trapper and the trader kept a close account of the pelts turned in and the supplies given out. Year after year, this famous company pushed farther into the wilderness and opened new posts. In a poor season, food and supplies would be given to the trappers, for which they were not made to pay until the trapping of the following year. So, little by little, good will was built up, and the trading posts continue to this day.

The Hudson's Bay Company sells its furs in London, but Montreal is an important center for the sale of other furs. Many of these now come from "fur farms," where foxes, raccoons, muskrats, and other animals are raised for their pelts.

Canada is a land of beautiful scenery and varied opportunities for recreation. The Maritime provinces with their quaint old fishing villages and bracing RECREATION sea air have many inviting spots 

SCENERY AND 

for those who love the sea. French sections of some of the older cities attract people who love to study the old-world customs, dress, and houses.

NEWFOUNDLAND AND LABRADOR 

(1) (3) Keystone View Co. (2) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. 

1. A fishing village in Newfoundland. The soil in Newfoundland is stony and only a few crops can be raised. 

The people get their living mainly from the sea. They catch several kinds of deep-sea fish, of which cod is the most important. When the fish have been cleaned they are spread on platforms to dry. The houses are made of wood. They have steep roofs to shed the heavy winter snows. 

2. A settlement on the Labrador coast. The cold Labrador current makes Labrador a chilly and uncomfortable place in which to live. This little settlement is on sheltered bay. The few people in Labrador build their little houses close together for company and protection during the heavy winter storms. These people, like those of Newfoundland, make their living principally by fishing. 

3. Fishermen of St. John's, Newfoundland. All about the island of Newfoundland are rich fishing grounds, where cod, haddock, and other fish are caught. These men, like many of the islanders, put out in their small boats to catch fish near the shores. Many others fish in larger vessels on the deep sea. Notice the steep and stony cliffs about the harbor. Newfoundland has a very rocky coast.

CANADA 

121 The Canadian Rockies, with their wilderness of forests, clear lakes, beautiful waterfalls, stately, snow-capped peaks, and broad, still plateau valleys, offer some of the finest mountain scenery in the world. 

The endless lakes and streams that spread like a net over the country invite sportsmen to set off in canoes for fishing, hunting, and camping trips. The Canadian government charges a small fee for hunting, camping, and fishing privileges. So many visitors take advantage of these privileges that the profit for the government is large. Canadian camps, hotels, resorts, and railroads also gain from the tourist trade.

Canada's railroad system is opening up more and more rich territory each year. Two rail- 

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMERCE 

road systems cross Canada from east to west. Because they cross the entire continent they are called transcontinental railroads. Of the two transcontinental lines, the 

Canadian Pacific is privately owned, and the other line, the Canadian National railroad, is operated by the Canadian government. Many branch lines connect the wheat-growing, mining, and lumbering regions with the main transportation lines. They also connect the transcontinental lines with markets in the 

United States.

Lakes and rivers form a network of waterways all through Canada. They offer easy transportation for light canoes through much of the interior country. 

Besides these smaller lakes and rivers, Canada has a great inland waterway that connects the prairie provinces with the sea. This great highway of commerce runs through the Great lakes and the Welland canal, down the St. 

Lawrence to the Atlantic. It offers cheap transportation for minerals and grain from far inland to the coast. But it has one great disadvantage. For nearly four and a half months every year the great waterway is closed by ice. This means a long idle period, then very heavy transportation in the late spring, summer, and early fall.

The Mackenzie river system is also farreaching. Since it empties into the ice-locked waters of the Arctic ocean, it is of no value for foreign commerce, but is used for commerce between settlements along its course. 

A great deal of trade is carried on with 

Canada's near neighbor, the United States.

Over one-third the exports of Canada go to the United States while about one-sixth of our exports go to Canada.

SEAPORTS 

Seven important seaports keep Canada in touch with the whole world. Two of these, Montreal and Quebec, seem so far inland, as we look at them on the map, that we might call them river ports. 

However, great ocean steamers dock both at

Quebec and at Montreal, so they are really seaports. They ship paper and wood pulp to the United States, and wheat, flour, dairy products and meats to Great Britain. From the 

United States they receive coal and oil, and from the United States and Great Britain, textiles, iron and steel goods, and machinery. 

Montreal, a little smaller than Pittsburgh, is Canada's chief city. It is at the head of ocean navigation on the St. Lawrence, and is a port from which Great lakes products are shipped in ocean vessels. As in the case of Buffalo, this has led to the growth of many manufacturing plants. Montreal is on both transcontinental railroads, and is connected with New York 

City by rail and water. 

Two other seaports, Halifax, Nova Scotia, and St. John, New Brunswick, are on the eastern coast. They have fine harbors, open the year round. To these harbors come petroleum from the United States, sugar from the West Indies, and manufactured goods from Europe. 

St. John ships forest products to the United 

States and wheat and flour to Great Britain. 

Halifax sends apples and lumber to Europe, and fish to the West Indies, South America, and the United States. Coal mines near Halifax have made it a coaling station, or a port where ships can come to get fuel. 

Vancouver and Prince Rupert are Pacific coast ports in British Columbia. Vancouver is the largest city on Canada's western coast. 

It has a fine harbor, which brings it trade with the far east and Alaska, and with the Pacific coast cities of the United States. Its rail- NASA-8

NORTH AMERICA 

roads bring wheat to the huge Vancouver elevators and rush trainloads of Japanese silk to 

New York. Flour, lumber, and canned salmon are shipped out, and petroleum, sugar, textiles, and machinery are brought in by sea. Prince

Rupert trades principally in fish. It is a new city, and is growing fast.

Fort Churchill was built on Hudson bay to provide a short route for wheat from western 

Canada to England. A railroad connects it with the western wheat fields, and during the warm months, when there is no ice in the bay, steamers load there with grain.

In addition to its seaports, Canada has many prosperous and growing interior cities. Ottawa, Ontario, is the capital of Canada.

INLAND It is a busy city, the industrial center of a great lumbering region. Toron- 

CITIES 

to, on the northern shore of Lake Ontario, is second in size to Montreal. It is both a railroad center and a lake port. It has many beautiful buildings and a large university. 

Kingston, on the northeast shore of Lake Ontario, is an attractive residential and educational center. There are numerous small manufacturing plants located there. Winnipeg, at the junction of the Assiniboine and the Red river of the North, is a great wheat distributing center. The two transcontinental railroads pass through Winnipeg, and the city also has many rail connections with the United States.

We have seen that the population of Canada is not great. Vast areas to the north are occupied only by trappers, In- 

POPULATION 

dians, and Eskimos. On the great western farms, people are few and live far apart. Yet, thinly as these sections are settled, so great is the area of the country that the population outside of the cities is greater than the population living in the cities. 

The frontier settlements, often called "outposts of civilization," lie far north in the lower Mackenzie valley. Many of these settlements are visited by only a few strangers in an entire year.

Canada is encouraging industrious, thrifty people to settle on her lands. Farms cannot be run, mines opened up, or new railroads built, without men to do the work. Great manufacturing plants, even with the most modern labor-saving machinery, cannot be run without many men and women to operate the machines. With all her natural advantages,

Canada is still poor in men. As the population increases, the natural resources will be developed, and the country and its workers will grow wealthy together.

The population of Canada is largely English, Scotch, and French. The province of Quebec was settled largely by the French, and much French is still spoken there. The law courts of Quebec recognize both French and English as official languages. Think how odd it must be to have some schools teaching in French, and some in English, and to hear the two languages spoken everywhere! The French section of the city of Quebec has narrow, winding streets, and its houses are built right to the edges of the walks.

The friendliest relations have existed between the United States and Canada for over a hundred years. As a memorial to this fact, the two countries have built a beautiful Peace bridge across the Niagara river boundary, near Buffalo. The people of Canada respect the people of the United States, and we, in turn, have every reason to respect the honest, thrifty, industrious people of Canada.

1. Canada, a dominion of Great Britain, occupies most of the northern part of North America. It is greater in area than the United States and Alaska. Its population is less than ten million. 

2. The great natural regions of Canada are the same as those of the United States, except that Canada has no Atlantic Coastal plain. Canada has a broken western coast line. A great crop belt overlying the 

Central plains and the Great plains of Canada is the spring wheat belt. The forest belt and the tundras cross northern Canada. The lake plains and the St.

Lawrence valley are the richest and most thickly populated regions of Canada. The Arctic archipelago and the tundras are mostly waste lands. 

3. Settlement in Canada spread from east to west. The 

Maritime provinces, first settled, have not grown so rapidly as regions more favored by soil and climate.

CANADA 123 

4. Canada's greatest wealth is her farm land. Grains, fruits and vegetables are important products of the country. 

5. Wood pulp and paper are important manufactures and exports of Canada. Much of her forest is still uncut because of distance from railroads and manufacturing centers. 

6. Many rich mineral deposits have already been discovered in Canada. Probably more will be found as the land is opened up. Gold, copper, lead, zinc, silver, nickel, asbestos, and coal are now mined in quantity. Sparse population and lack of transportation for ores hinder the working of many mines, but production is increasing. 

7. Canada has almost unlimited sources of water power. 

Most of the available power has not yet been developed. 

8. Canada has fine fisheries off the east coast, off the west coast, and in her inland lakes and streams. 

Trapping of fur-bearing animals is another source of wealth. Some of these animals are raised for their pelts on fur farms. Montreal is a famous fur market. 

9. The forests, lakes, and streams, and the beautiful and varied scenery make Canada an ideal vacation land. 

10. Canada has two transcontinental railroads, with many branches. These branch lines go north to mines and forests, and south to markets in the 

United States. The Great lakes and the St. Lawrence river furnish water transportation during about seven and one-half months of the year. The Mackenzie river system is navigable, but of no use for foreign trade, as it flows into the ice-locked Arctic ocean. 

11. Seven seaports of Canada are Montreal and Quebec on the St. Lawrence, Halifax and St. John on the

Atlantic coast, Vancouver and Prince Rupert on the western coast, and Fort Churchill on Hudson bay. Important inland cities are Ottawa, Toronto, Kingston, and Winnipeg. Most of Canada's people live in the country rather than in the cities. 

12. Outposts of civilization are far to the north in the 

Mackenzie valley. 

13. Canada's population is largely English, Scotch, and 

French. In the province of Quebec, much French is spoken.
