NEWFOUNDLAND AND LABRADOR 

Besides Canada, Great Britain has another dominion, or colony, in North America. It is made up of the island of Newfoundland and of Labrador, on the mainland. Labrador is called a dependency of Newfoundland. The English governor of these lands lives in the city of 

St. John's, Newfoundland, and governs both 

Newfoundland and Labrador from there.

There is a body of men chosen by the people to help the governor rule.

Both of these lands are so bleak and bare that their people have been forced to turn to the sea for a living. Their most important industry is the catching of fish and the preparing of them for sale. 

Newfoundland lies across the mouth of the

Gulf of St. Lawrence. On the north it is separated from the Canadian mainland by the narrow Strait of Belle Isle and on the south by the wider Cabot strait.

CLIMATE 

Labrador extends from the 60° parallel to the Strait of Belle Isle. Much of Newfound land lies south of the 50° parallel, in about the same latitude as the warm Willamette-Puget Sound valley. It is farther south than the mother country, England. Yet both the Willamette-Puget Sound valley and England are much warmer than Labrador or 

Newfoundland. What accounts for these differences in climate?

In studying the climate of the Willamette- Puget Sound region, we found much of its warmth due to the warm Japan current and the westerly winds. Another warm current, the Gulf stream, warms the shores of the British Isles. The shores of Labrador and Newfoundland, on the other hand, are bathed by the icy Labrador current. In winter, the sea water freezes along the coast and forms huge packs of ice. Even in summer the weather is not really hot, and the growing season is too short for many crops. But both Newfoundland and Labrador have dense evergreen forests which extend right down to the sea. SOIL 

Oddly enough, Newfoundland is really a part of the Appalachian highland. With the sinking of the coast, it was cut off from the rest of the highland by an arm of the sea.

Like parts of the Appalachians, it has rocky and unproductive soil. Labrador is a part of the Laurentian highland, and its soil, too, is poor. Because of poor soil and cold climate, only a few crops are raised and the people of Newfoundland and Labrador must depend for food upon fish and upon what they can buy after selling the fish they catch. Besides food, they import coal and manufactured goods from Great Britain, the United States and Canada.

GRAND 

All around Newfoundland are excellent fishing grounds. The sinking of the Atlantic coast not only cut the mountains of Newfoundland off from those of the main- BANKS land, but left broad shelves reaching out under the sea for several hundred miles to the southeast of Newfoundland. These have been named the Grand Banks. They have long been known to the fishermen of other lands, and used by them as a fishing ground. 

The cool waters over the Grand Banks are only a few hundred feet deep. Here numberless small fish come to feed on the tiny sea life of the shallow water.

And large fish come to feed on the small ones! 

FISHING 

With their great lines and nets the fishermen draw in cod, herring, mackerel, halibut, and haddock. Of these, the cod are the most important.

The 

Along the shores of Newfoundland many lobsters are caught. These are canned and shipped away. Seals are found on the ice packs along the shores of Labrador. skins of the Labrador seals are used in making leather, and their fat is used in making oil. The seal industry is less important than it once was.

The fishing industry centers in St. John's, the capital of Newfoundland. This is a great 124

NEWFOUNDLAND AND LABRADOR 

125 market for dried, salted, and fresh cod, which are shipped away in large quantities. Here, too, the fishermen buy their supplies. 

Two small islands south of Newfoundland, St. Pierre and Miquelon, are owned by France.

She uses them only as fishing bases. 

POPULATION 

The people of Newfoundland are mostly of 

French and English descent. Most of the people of Labrador are of English descent. As the sea provides them with their living they naturally live along the coast in small fishing villages. 

In the north of Labrador are a few Eskimos, and inland there are small bands of Indians who live by trapping animals and selling their furs.

THE PACIFIC REGION OF ALASKA

(1) The Alaska Railroad. (2) White Pass and Yukon Route. (3) U. S. Forest Service. (4) Chicago, Milwaukee, St. Paul and Pacific

1. The Inside Passage. Southern Alaska has a fiord coast like that of western Canada. Among its hundreds of islands winds the Inside Passage, a belt of calm deep water that is protected from ocean storms. Many ships follow this safe route. Forests of evergreen trees line the shore. 

2. A flower garden in Skagway. Warmed by the Japan current, the Pacific region of Alaska has many summer gardens of flowers and vegetables, even when nearby mountains are covered with snow. 

3. A Sitka spruce tree of southern Alaska. Most of the valuable timber of Alaska is preserved by the government for future needs. This great spruce tree is nearly 200 feet tall, and contains a great deal of useful lumber. 

4. A steamship sailing along the face of Taku glacier, near Juneau. This great river of ice creeps slowly down from the mountains. As it reaches the sea, huge pieces break off and form icebergs which float away in the current. 

5. Fur seals on one of the Pribilof islands. For hundreds of years seals have come to these rocky coasts to breed. Once they were killed in large numbers by fur hunters. Now they are protected by the United 

States and other nations.

CENTRAL AND POLAR ALASKA 

(6) (9) Alaska Railroad. (7) White Pass and Yukon Route. (8) National Development Bureau, Department of the Interior, Canada. 

6. Celery from near the Arctic circle! These long, straight stalks of crisp celery are growing in the Tanana valley in Central Alaska, near the city of Fairbanks and little more than a hundred miles from the Arctic circle. 

The Central region of Alaska is cold and dark in winter, but the long days and warm sun of summer make it possible to grow crops of delicious vegetables and grains in the Tanana valley. This celery farmer is pleased with his crop. 

7. Placer mining in the Yukon valley. These men are working in a sluice, the long trough in which earth and gravel are washed from the heavier bits of gold. This method of mining was used by the first miners in the 

California and Alaska gold fields. 

8. Eskimo children of the Polar region. Eskimo children are more used to playing in the snow than on the grass, and they are warmly dressed in heavy clothing made by their mothers from the skins of seals and reindeer. The Eskimos of Alaska live on the coast of the 

Arctic ocean and along the shores of Bering sea. 

9. A herd of reindeer on the tundras of Polar Alaska.

These animals are almost as tame as our cows. The 

Eskimos use the reindeer to draw sleds and to provide food and clothing as well. Some reindeer meat is even shipped to the United States. Reindeer were introduced into Alaska by our government because they can live on the moss and lichens that grow on the tundras.

ALASKA 

Alaska is in the northwestern corner of 

North America, separated from the United

States by the western provinces of 

Canada. Alaska is not one of the

OF ALASKA forty-eight states which make up the United States. It is a territory which belongs to the United States. 

THE PURCHASE 

The United States bought Alaska from Russia in 1867 for the sum of $7,200,000. Before that time, it was known as Russian America. William H. Seward, then Secretary of

State, arranged for its purchase, and for many years it was spoken of as "Seward's folly," because people thought it was such a poor bargain. Time has proved it quite otherwise. 

Alaska is rich in natural resources. It has brought a great deal of money to the United States through its gold, copper, silver, furs, and fish. It has paid a good deal of money to the United States by buying from us the things it has needed-food, clothing, oil, machinery, and manufactured goods.

LOCATION 

You will see from your map on page 116, that most of Alaska is a peninsula. The water that bounds it on the north is the Arctic ocean. On the south is the 

Pacific ocean. Bering sea and strait wash its western coast. To the east lies Canada, separated from Alaska by a boundary line which follows the 141° meridian from the Arctic ocean nearly to the Pacific. 

Southeastern Alaska, called the Panhandle, is a narrow strip of coast with the islands which border it. From the southwestern corner of the mainland, the Aleutian islands stretch out westward twelve hundred miles toward Asia.

The western coast of Alaska lies very near the great continent of Asia. At its narrowest part the Bering strait, which separates Asia from Alaska, is only thirty-six miles wide. The 

Arctic circle cuts across the northern part of 

Alaska, three hundred miles south of Point 

Barrow, Alaska's most northern point. This means that about one-third of Alaska lies in the North Frigid zone.

SIZE 

In size Alaska is twelve times as large as New York state and one-fifth as large as the United States. The distance from 

Point Barrow, the point farthest north, to Dixon Entrance, the point farthest south, is just about as far as the distance across the United States from the Canadian border to the Mexican border. From the western end of the Aleutian islands to the southeastern corner of the Panhandle is nearly as great a distance as from San Francisco to New York.

CLIMATE 

We think of Alaska as a very cold place but it has a surprising variety of climate. In the northern region, north of the Alaska mountains, it is really very cold, the thermometer sometimes showing sixty degrees below zero in winter. This is due to the latitude and the lack of sun in midwinter. There are forty days when the sun never rises over the horizon. During this time Point Barrow has no daylight. The summers in this part of Alaska are short but quite warm. For weeks at a time the sun never goes below the horizon. There is very little rain or snow in this region. The Central part, too, has long, cold winters, and short, warm summers with many hours of daylight. 

Rainfall is moderate.

Southern Alaska, on the other hand, has quite a different climate. The temperature is mild. The thermometer never goes above eighty degrees in summer and very seldom goes below zero in winter. The westerly winds, blowing over the Pacific ocean, warmed by the Japan current, make the climate mild. 

There is heavy rainfall in this region. 

Alaska has mountains, plateaus, and lowlands. The mountainous section along the 

Pacific coast is called the Pacific 

THE REGIONS region. It includes southern OF ALASKA 

Alaska and the Panhandle. The

Alaska mountain range separates the Pacific region from the Central region and forms 127

NORTH AMERICA 

a barrier to transportation between the two. 

The Central region is a broad plateau drained by the great Yukon river and the smaller Kuskokwim river. The Brooks range of mountains separates the Central region from the Polar region. Polar Alaska is the treeless coastal plain, or tundras, along the Arctic ocean.

The western mountain system that we studied in the United States and Canada 

THE 

PACIFIC 

REGION 

stretches 1200 miles along the coast of Alaska's Pacific region, and extends inland as much as 200 miles. 

There are many high ridges and deep valleys. In the coast range along the Panhandle is Mt. St. Elias, with its peak rising 18,024 feet.

In clear weather it is seen by passing steamers far out at sea, and it is one of the most imposing sights of Alaska. The Alaska range of mountains extends in a curve from the Yukon boundary to west of Cook inlet. It contains the highest peak in North America, Mt. McKinley, 20,464 feet above sea level. The region about 

Mt. McKinley has been made a national park. 

The Aleutian range extends along the point of land called the Alaska peninsula. It goes on as a mountain chain mostly under water, forming the Aleutian islands. 

Volcanoes. In the Alaska and Aleutian ranges, there are live volcanoes. Mt. Katmai, one of these live volcanoes, with the "Valley of Ten Thousand Smokes," has been reserved as a public park.

Glaciers. In contrast to the volcanoes of the 

Pacific region are its wonderful glaciers, for which Alaska is noted. The winds from the ocean, filled with moisture, pass up over the mountains. At a high altitude, they lose their moisture as snow. As this snow piles up it forms heavy ice masses, called glaciers. These move slowly down the sides of the mountains to the sea. In the sea, parts of these glaciers break off and form icebergs. The icebergs sometimes are deep blue and sometimes shine with many colors when the rays of the sun strike them.

The Inside Passage. The Panhandle country is very beautiful. It is the region best known to the tourist. The islands along this coast of southeastern Alaska are tops of mountains rising abruptly from the sea. In and out among them winds a channel called the Inside Passage. The islands shelter the passage from the roughness of the Pacific ocean, so that it is nearly as calm as a lake. The coast is lined with some of the most beautiful forest-covered mountains in North America. Waterfalls and cascades add to the beauty of the scenery. All along the Inside Passage are fiords, or inlets, set deep between high walls. They end in gorges, filled with glaciers. The Taku glacier is one of the most beautiful of this region. It extends from a distance of ninety miles back in the mountains to the edge of the ocean. 

Resources. The Pacific region has copper, gold and silver in its mountains, lumber in its forests, and fish in its rivers and surrounding waters.

The discovery of gold first brought many people to Alaska. Gold is now mined in many parts of the territory, but copper mining has lately become more important. Copper is found on the mainland and in the Aleutian mountains on the Alaska peninsula. It is also mined on Prince of Wales island off the coast of southeastern Alaska. Silver is mined to some extent. There are coal fields, but because of the few railroads and no ready market they have been little mined.

The forests along the coast are mostly spruce and hemlock. They are used for lumber. The lumber is not exported, but is used for building and for making the boxes in which canned and dried fish are exported. The forests are suitable for furnishing wood pulp, which is used for paper making. This is an industry which will probably be developed. 

The catching and canning and exporting of salmon is the most important industry of the Pacific region, and Alaska leads the world in this business. These fish are caught from May to September. They come in from the sea and go many miles up the rivers to spawn, or lay their eggs. The salmon vary in size. The red salmon usually weighs about seven pounds. The king salmon, while usually weighing about twenty pounds, may weigh as much as seventy or a hundred pounds. Herring, which are found in the

ALASKA 

129 waters near southeastern Alaska, are second in importance. Cod and halibut are caught to some extent. There are canneries in all the coast towns, where the fish are made ready to export.

The Pacific region is too mountainous for much farming and a large quantity of food is imported from the United States. Some hardy vegetables are grown, especially potatoes. 

There are also delicious berries. In the summertime, there are beautiful flowers of brilliant colors all along the coast. 

Harbors. There are many deep harbors in southern and southeastern Alaska. The climate is so mild on the coast that they are open the year round. 

Towns. Juneau in the Panhandle is the capital of Alaska and the chief mining town in the Pacific region. It is built on a steep mountain side. The streets lie in terraces, one above another. Juneau has electric lights, telephones, good schools, shops, and churches. It is the source of supply for the mining camps. Yet it has only about three thousand people. Sitka, the old capital, is a picturesque city but it has lost its commercial importance.

Ketchikan, the most southerly port of Alaska, is a center for mining and for the salmon industry. 

Skagway was important in the early days of the gold rush to Alaska. It was the gateway to the rich gold field called the Klondike. 

Cordova is a busy mining center. Seward is a little town with less than a thousand people but with an excellent harbor.

The mild climate, together with the good harbors and nearness to United States and

Canadian ports are reasons for the large number of towns in the Pacific region. 

Railroads. In 1923 the United States built a railroad connecting the interior with the coast. It is about 500 miles long and runs from 

Seward to the city of Fairbanks. There are several short branches running from the main line to mining centers. 

Across the Alaska mountains from the Pacific ocean lies the Central region. Its broad plateau extends through the center of the 

THE CENTRAL REGION 

country and includes the Seward peninsula and the land along Bering sea. The plateau has many wide lowland stretches.

Rivers. The Yukon river, rising in Canada, flows 1200 miles across Alaska from east to west. It is one of the finest drainage systems in the world. With its tributaries, it drains nearly one-half of Alaska. The Koyukuk river rises in the Brooks mountains and flows southwest into the Yukon. The Tanana rises in the Alaska range and flows northwest into the Yukon. The Yukon river flows into 

Norton sound and has a delta at its mouth.

There are many deep canyons in the rivers. 

The Kuskokwim river forms a smaller drainage basin. 

Transportation. The rivers, especially the

Yukon, are the chief means of travel and transportation in the Central region. In winter, dog sleds are used over their frozen surfaces.

In summer, during the four or five months of open weather, boats travel up and down the streams. Automobiles are used where roads have been built. Airplanes are taking the places of dog sleds in transportation between the most important places. They make regular trips in all seasons.

Forests and Crops. The Central region of 

Alaska is a rough, hilly country. Much of the land along the streams is covered with trees -poplar, spruce, and hemlock. During the short, warm summers the sun gives enough heat to thaw the top layers of soil, frozen deep by the severe winters. This makes some agriculture possible.

The principal farming region is in the valley of the Tanana. Here, potatoes, barley, wheat, and rye are grown. Here, too, are grasses which make good grazing for cattle and reindeer. Small fruits and berries are grown along the Yukon river. Agriculture is limited, partly because there are but few markets. 

Fairbanks and Nome. Fairbanks is the chief commercial city of the Central region. It was a rough mining town in the early days of the gold rush. Today, it has all the comforts and conveniences of a modern city. Fairbanks is connected with a shipping port by railroad and is at the head of navigation on the Tanana

1:

NORTH AMERICA 

river. In summer steamers make regular trips from the city down the Yukon. Fairbanks is called "the golden heart of Alaska" because it is the center for the gold mining along the Yukon and its tributaries. 

The Richardson highway connects Fair banks with the Copper river. The highway follows the old Fairbanks trail through the

Alaska range of mountains to Chitina. The traveler can motor over this new government road past farm lands, mines, Indian camps, lakes, mountain peaks, and glaciers. It passes through a great game country where one may see bear, moose, caribou, and mountain sheep. 

Mining is the chief industry of Central 

Alaska. Nome, on the Seward peninsula, is an important mining town. It has produced more gold than any other place in Alaska. 

Tundras. Along the Bering sca are coastal tundras. The soil, always deeply frozen, thaws on the top in summer and is covered with moss and lichens. As the moss of the tundras makes good food for reindeer, the United States government imported some deer from 

Siberia. The reindeer herds graze on the tundras along Bering sea and north on the tundras near Point Barrow,

POLAR ALASKA 

Polar Alaska lies in the cold, frozen north beyond the Arctic circle, where crops cannot be raised. The Brooks mountains form its southern boundary. Little is known of these mountains. They are an extension of the Rocky mountain system of Canada. Between the mountains and the sea are the treeless coastal tundras. It is too cold for most people to wish to live in this part of Alaska. The only inhabitants are 

Eskimos who live near Point Barrow.

Reindeer are used by the Eskimos for food, I SHOULD 1. The territory of Alaska belongs to the United States.

3. The climate of Alaska varies with the latitude. It is very cold in the north, cold in the central part, and clothing and transportation. The sale of reindeer meat to the United States is gradually growing. The raising of reindeer is sure to be an increasingly important occupation of Alaska, although this is somewhat limited by the slow growth of grasses and by lack of markets.

For such a large territory, there are very few people in Alaska and these are pretty well scattered. The total population 

POPULATION 

is estimated at about 60,000.

There are more people in Binghamton, New 

York, Hammond, Indiana, or Pasadena, California, than in all the territory of Alaska. 

About half of the population of Alaska are white people and the other half are Indians and Eskimos. Four-fifths of the white people live in the towns along the Pacific coast where the climate is mild. The Indians, mostly hunters or fishermen, live in the valley of the Yukon river. The Eskimos live on the coast of the Arctic ocean and the shores of Bering sea.

Fur from land and sea animals is a source of FURS profit to Alaska. Of the land fur-bearing animals, foxes are the most important. 

There are also mink, otter, beaver, and muskrat. Red fox are found all through Alaska.

These animals are trapped by the Indians. 

There are fox farms on Kodiak island and on the Aleutian islands. 

Seal skins come from the Pribilof islands, where fur seals come every year to breed. 

They were once killed so recklessly that it was feared none would be left, and other nations agreed with the United States to protect them. Now only a certain number are killed annually, under government supervision, and the herds of seals are increasing in size.

MEXICO, OUR SOUTHERN NEIGHBOR 

COMPANY 

1. The peak of Mt. Orizaba, seen above the clouds.

This is the highest mountain of Mexico. It lies in the eastern Sierra Madre mountains, a range which separates the interior Mexican plateau from the seacoast to the east. Mt. Orizaba is well inside the Torrid zone, but it is nearly always capped with snow. Although much of 

Mexico is in the Torrid zone, the climate of its high southern part is delightful because of its altitude. 

2. A silver mine in the Mexican mountains. This mine is reached over a narrow bridge across a deep canyon. 

The mine workers live in the little huts on the top of the hill. Mexico has great mineral wealth. As yet the methods of mining are slow and old-fashioned, though the mines have been worked for many years. 

3. An oil refinery near the port of Tampico. The district near this port is rich in petroleum, and several large companies have oil wells there. Many of the wells are owned by Americans. Some of the oil is refined in plants like this one, and some is shipped to the United 

States or Europe to be refined. 

4. A herd of beef cattle on the Mexican plateau. These long-horned cattle do not produce as much or as good beef as the white-faced steers of our western states. 

Mexican cowboys are excellent riders. 

5. Plowing with a pointed stick. The poorer people of Mexico do not have farm machinery. Even our simple modern plows are strange to them.

MEXICO'S PEOPLE AND CITIES

6. Mexico City, the capital of Mexico. This view shows us the city, spread out on the broad, flat surface of the 

Mexican plateau, with high mountains in the distance.

There are many broad avenues and beautiful homes in 

Mexico City. 

7. Mexican peons, the workmen of Mexico. The broad hats which protect them from the hot sun are worn in all parts of the country. Two of the men are wearing another national article of dress, the serape, a shawl. 

The serapes are as necessary as the hats, for, when the sun has set, the air on the Mexican plateau is cool. 

8. A wharf at Tampico, the great oil port of northern 

Mexico. A freight steamer is taking on a cargo. The round disks on the ropes that tie the vessel to the wharf prevent rats from crawling up the ropes and boarding the ship. On the wharf we see loading machinery and freight cars. In the barrels and casks are petroleum products made from oil which has been piped to Tampico from the oil wells. 

9. A hillside street in the mining city of Guanajuato.

Balconies like these, with iron railings, are common on 

Spanish and Mexican houses. 

10. A typical mining village of the Mexican plateau.

These small square houses are made of adobe, or sundried clay. Very few of the peons have better houses than these. The few plants are those of the dry plains. 

There are many parts of the plateau where crops cannot be raised without irrigation.

SURFACE MEXICO 

Just as North America's Western highland region reaches away north into Alaska, so it stretches south through the land of our neighbor country, Mexico.

Right through central Mexico runs a high, broad plateau, with rolling hills and mountain ranges in its central and northern parts. In the north this plateau is about 4000 feet above sea level, and at Mexico City, which is in its southern part, the elevation is over 7400 feet. 

All along the eastern and western sides of the plateau are mountain ranges, which meet just below the plateau's southern point in a number of high, jagged peaks, so high that they wear caps of snow nearly all the year. Mt. 

Orizaba is over 18,000 feet high. Popocatepetl is 17,888 feet high.

Many of these high peaks were once active volcanoes and even now one may see smoke rising from some of them. Volcanoes must have been very active in Mexico at one time, as the soil in some regions is made of the black lava given off by active volcanoes. 

Lower California, which is a peninsula off the western coast of Mexico, has many black lava plateaus. From time to time there are earthquakes in Mexico, which is one reason why very few high buildings have been built. Earthquakes are usually common in regions where there are many volcanoes. 

The mountain ranges enclosing the plateau slope sharply toward the coast on either side. 

At the foot of the east slope is a wide coastal plain, an extension of the Gulf Coastal plain of the United States. It follows the coast of the Gulf of Mexico to the peninsula of Yucatan, all of which is a part of the coastal plain. 

The Pacific coastal plain is much narrower than that of the eastern coast. 

The rainfall of Mexico varies greatly in different sections. The trade winds from the 

CLIMATE 

northeast bring moisture. They blow across Yucatan, dropping very little rain because there are no high mountains. As the winds rise to cross the eastern Sierra Madre mountains they grow cool and drop their moisture upon the eastern mountain slopes and the coastal plain nearby. 

Where this coastal plain is narrow it receives abundant moisture. After crossing the mountains these winds have little rain left for the plateau beyond.

The narrow eastern coastal plain and mountainous southern Mexico have the heaviest rainfall. The rainy season in southern Mexico lasts from May to October. But it does not rain continuously during that time. During the remainder of the year the climate is rather dry.

The Sonoran desert, on the western coast, and Lower California have little rainfall and have an extremely dry climate.

In general, you might say that in the south of Mexico, except in Yucatan, the rainfall is heavy, but that it grows less as one goes north. 

About midway across Mexico runs the Tropic of Cancer, so the southern part of this country is in the Torrid zone. This would make one think that the climate must be unbearably hot. But differences in altitude bring about very great differences in temperature. 

The high southern part of the plateau has delightful weather, with warm days and cool nights. Because of this, two-thirds of all the people in Mexico live in this section. Mexico 

City, the capital and chief city of Mexico, is located here, and here are many other of the country's leading cities.

In this section the sun becomes very hot at midday, and most of the people take a rest period between one and three o'clock.

Places of business are shut up, the streets are almost deserted, and the houses are closed against the heat. Late in the afternoon it grows cooler and everyone comes out again, to work or shop or ride about the streets. The nights are almost cold. 133

MEXICO 

135 As one travels north on the plateau the climate becomes warmer and drier. There are thousands of acres where only cactus and sagebrush grow and the country looks rather like the Great plains in our own country. Parts of Lower California are very much like this region in climate and vegetation. If all these waste acres could only be irrigated and put to work, they would yield vast returns. 

Yucatan's climate is hot as well as dry. Cool sea breezes sweeping across the land make it livable.

The rainy east coast lowlands, and the eastern mountain slopes up to a height of about 3000 feet, have a truly tropical climate. The 

Mexican name for them means hot lands.

They are hot and moist, and very unhealthful, especially for white people. Yellow fever is the most dreaded disease. Many snakes and poisonous plants and insects are found in the swamps, the lagoons, and the lakes.

REGIONS 

You have probably noticed that there are several distinct regions in Mexico. There is the hot, dry Yucatan peninsula; the eastern lowlands and mountain slopes, hot and moist; Lower California and the 

Sonoran desert; and the great mountain-enclosed plateau where the climate is mild. You can easily understand why the regions of Mexico, which differ so widely, yield different products.

Yucatan. Yucatan has an important industry which greatly increases the amount of Mexico's exports to the United States. On this peninsula are large plantations which are entirely devoted to growing henequen. From the fiber of this plant comes sisal hemp, from which cord and twine are made. The fiber is removed from the leaves and dried. Much of this dried hemp is shipped to our country. 

Stop for a minute and think how very many packages come into your own home with strong cord or string around them. You can imagine the immense amount of cord and twine that is used in stores and factories and mills. 

And then think of all the cord used in the fields to tie up the bundles, or shocks, of wheat stalks, when the grain has been cut! All this will perhaps give you some idea of the immense amount of sisal hemp that we call upon Yucatan to deliver to us.

The people of Yucatan also make baskets, rugs, and carpets. They make and export some really beautiful hammocks.

Yucatan is a thriving little peninsula, with its sisal plantations and its factories. It has its own seaport of Progreso, which ships out the sisal hemp.

East Coast. Farther north on the east coast of Mexico, around the city of Tampico, are rich fields of oil, or petroleum. Many people have gone to Mexico from the United States to develop these oil fields, and some bitter quarrels have arisen over them. Petroleum is a very valuable product, and the Mexicans feel that Mexico should own and develop these fields herself. So the Mexican government is trying to limit foreign ownership of oil wells.

The oil, as it comes from the wells, is sent through pipe lines to Tampico where it is refined or shipped to the United States refineries. 

Tampico specializes in exporting oil, just as 

Progreso specializes in exporting sisal hemp.

Most of the products of the eastern coastal plain and the lower slopes of the mountains are the products of a hot, wet climate. Much of this country is covered with dense jungle forest through which men must cut their way with knives. Here grow mahogany and ebony trees, whose lumber is so very valuable. Much of this lumber is exported, and more would be if there were more railroads to carry it to shipping ports.

Some of the trees in these forests yield valuable dyes. Others produce gums and juices.

The juice from one kind of tree is called chicle and is exported in large quantities to the United States to be used in making chewing gum.

This tropical region is very beautiful, even if it is unhealthful. There are coconut palms, and lemon and orange and banana trees. You can see pineapples growing, as well as the vanilla bean from which vanilla extract is made.

Parts of these hot lands have been cleared and turned into plantations, or haciendas, as the Mexicans call them. 

Here are grown

NORTH AMERICA 

sugar cane, rice, tobacco, coffee, and many tropical fruits. One plant which grows in this section is called guayule. Guayule was considered useless until someone discovered that rubber could be made from it. Now it is a very valuable product.

Lower California and the Sonoran Desert. Two regions of Mexico which so far have not been very productive are Lower California and the 

Sonoran desert on the northwestern coast.

Their natural industries are grazing and mining but the land is now being irrigated and divided into small farming sections. Cotton, fruits, and early vegetables are grown and some of these are shipped into the United States.

Central Plateau. The great Central plateau of Mexico is the real heart of the country. 

Here are centered many important industries. 

Cattle, sheep, horses, and goats are raised in the northern part of the plateau. The hides are exported but the meat is used in Mexico. 

The central part has much irrigated land and produces cotton, wheat, alfalfa, and beans. 

This cotton is now being sent to Mexico's own cotton mills, at Puebla and Orizaba. Wheat, beans, barley, vegetables, and corn grow in the southern plateau lands. As these products are needed as food for the Mexicans they are not exported.

In all of these sections the soil would produce much more if it were thoroughly worked. 

But the Mexican laborers, with their crude and old-fashioned methods, raise only a small part of what the land might produce.

The mines of Mexico are among the richest in the world. They lie mainly in the plateau and the surrounding mountains. Mexico leads the world in the production of silver. Gold is also found, as well as copper, quicksilver, lead, zinc, and sulphur. Many of these minerals are shipped to the United States and Europe.

The city of Guanajuato owes its growth to silver mines. There are mines all around it and even under it.

A large part of the work in the Mexican mines is done in a slow, old-fashioned way. 

The natives do almost everything by hand and there is little machinery used. Lack of transportation hinders the developing of this industry, as the ores in many cases must be carried by truck or pack horse to the railroad. 

San Luis Potosi has many ore smelters.

Mexico has coal fields which will become more valuable as the country turns to manufacturing. 

Manufacturing is slowly gaining in Mexico. 

Mexico City and Guadalajara have textile mills, flour mills, and meatpacking plants. Most of the

MANUFACTURING 

rivers of Mexico rise in the interior and flow east and west down from the plateau. Although they are too short and rapid for navigation they are ideal for furnishing the water power which is so valuable for manufacturing. 

TRANSPORTATION 

The growth of Mexico's manufacturing and other industries will depend a great deal upon the improvement in transportation. It is expensive and hard work to cover a mountainous country with railway systems. In Mexico the railroads have to cross mountains to reach the plateau where the business centers are. The oldest railroad runs between the seaport of

Vera Cruz and Mexico City. Several lines extend north and connect with trunk lines in the 

United States. Others go to Mexico's Pacific ports and to Central America. The city of 

Monterrey is a railroad center.

COMMERCE 

Mexico has over 6000 miles of coast line, but few good harbors. Its chief ports are 

Progreso, Tampico, and Vera

Cruz. The chief export of Pro greso is sisal hemp. Tampico's chief export is petroleum. From Vera Cruz go bananas, lumber, coffee, hides, silver, and gold. Few agricultural and meat products are exported, as Mexico needs all these for her people and must import much more besides.

More than half of the trade of Mexico is with the United States. We buy heavily of 

Mexican raw materials, and Mexico buys our manufactured goods. Our imports from Mexico include petroleum and petroleum products, silver, lead, copper, and sisal hemp. Mexico buys from us machinery, iron and steel, automobiles, flour and meats, and textiles.

MEXICO 

137 These things she also receives from Europe. 

Many business men have gone into Mexico from the United States and have invested millions of dollars in mines, plantations, railroads, factories, oil wells, and banks. With her great resources Mexico has a fine future. She and the United States should be very friendly and prosperous neighbors, doing much business with each other.

MEXICO'S PEOPLE 

In long ago times many different nations of Indians lived in Mexico. They spoke different languages and had different customs. Perhaps the best known of these nations was the Aztec Indians. There are in Mexico many remains of their buildings and their works of art. 

The Aztecs did not live in wigwams and roam about, hunting and fishing, as did the Indians of the forests and plains of the rest of 

North America. The Aztec Indians lived in towns and cities, some of which grew to be very large. They had houses and temples and schools. Many of the houses were made of stone, and some of the large public buildings were very imposing. They had huge columns and terraces, and were ornamented with carved woodwork and with precious stones.

The mines owned by the Aztec Indians were rich in gold and silver, and such precious stones as opals, turquoise, and emeralds. Some of the metal work that these people did and some of the pottery they decorated have been preserved in museums. They are very lovely.

The Aztec Indians were proud and industrious and religious. They were very warlike as well. They had an emperor to rule over them and had courts where criminals were tried. They kept a record of their history in picture writing. 

In 1519, a bold Spanish adventurer by the name of Cortez landed with a band of men on the coast of Mexico. Making his way to the high plateau which lies in the center of 

Mexico, he discovered the Aztecs with their fine buildings, their gold and silver, and their precious stones. Wishing to gain all these things for themselves, Cortez and his men fought the Aztecs and finally conquered them. 

They made slaves of the Indians and destroyed much of the work that the Aztecs had accomplished. 

For many years after that, Spain ruled the country, but finally Mexico threw off Spanish rule and became a republic. It is a republic today, with twenty-eight states, two territories, and one federal district.

The people who consider themselves the real Mexicans are a mixture of Spanish and Indian. 

They are called mestizos, and make up over two-fifths of the whole population of Mexico. 

Of the remainder about one-tenth are white and the rest are Indians, many of them descendants of the original Aztecs. It is said that the number of Indians is growing less all the time.

Most of the people in our country work for their living, and are neither very rich nor very poor. But in Mexico, with some exceptions, there are two groups of people-the rich and the very poor. The very poor people are Indians and mestizos. Most of them can neither read nor write. They live in little villages in huts made of bamboo or of adobe, which is sun-dried clay brick. The adobe huts are whitewashed or painted brilliant colors. 

The principal food of the poor people of 

Mexico is a little, thin, flat cake, called tortilla, made from corn. Another dish of which they are very fond is frijoles, which is their name for beans. If they can raise enough corn and beans to eat, they are satisfied. On the trees in the lowlands hangs all the fruit they could possibly want to eat. They do not have to worry about buying coal and many clothes, for they do not need them in their warm climate. On the cooler highlands a favorite article of clothing is the gaily colored blanket, slit so that it may be slipped over the head. 

Most white people who stay in the hot lands live on the plantations. Only natives live in the little towns, where the streets are lined with palms. The natives wander around wearing as little as possible because of the heat. There is no activity and bustle such as you would expect to see in a town in the United States.

In the northern part of the plateau, life is much like that on the Great plains of our own

NORTH AMERICA 

country. Cattle, horses, sheep, and goats roam the huge pasture lands. The ranches, also called haciendas, are very large, and the owners live very comfortably. They irrigate a little of the land and raise the crops needed for themselves and for their animals. 

The work is done by Mexican workmen, called peons, who receive very low wages for their labor. The workmen live on the lands of the great haciendas, in their own little villages, and grow small crops of corn, beans, and onions for their own use. They never expect to own any land of their own, so they do not work any harder than they have to. 

Many peons are skilful at ornamenting leather, and there is much demand for Mexican saddles, harnesses, and vests. 

Of course, there are many educated and intelligent people in Mexico. A look at the 

MEXICO CITY 

capital, Mexico City, will convince you of that. This city has schools, libraries, fine homes, and broad, welllighted streets.

Mexico City is situated in a slight depression of the plateau, and is entirely surrounded by a rim of mountains. This depression is drained by pipes, canals, and a tunnel which carry off surplus water and the city sewage. 

Looking over Mexico City you see an array of low, flat buildings, with very few chimneys.

Many of these buildings are white but some are gaily colored. High above the low-roofed buildings tower the spires of the cathedral, one of the most beautiful churches in North America. The houses of Mexico City are built close to the streets and have iron grills on their street windows. The rooms are built around a central garden. This garden, or patio, is the favorite gathering place for the family. 

The wealthy Mexicans have tutors for their children or send them to private schools. These people of wealth live in great comfort, own automobiles, wear beautiful clothes, and spend much time in travel.

But a small group of wealthy people does not make Mexico a strong country. The common people are not hard working and ambitious by nature, and in a warm climate with plenty of food they can live with little effort. 

They do not want to exert themselves. So most of the resources of Mexico have been developed by foreigners where they have been developed at all, and the country has not become important in the affairs of the world. 

1. Mexico has a high central plateau, enclosed on the east and west by mountain ranges that slope sharply toward the sea. These ranges meet south of the plateau in a series of high volcanic peaks. Volcanoes were once active throughout Mexico, as is shown by the black lava soil in many sections. The coastal plains vary in width, the eastern plain being much wider than that along the western coast. Yucatan is a low, hot, dry peninsula. Lower California and the Sonoran desert are arid lands. 

2. Rainfall is heavy in the south, growing less towards the north. It is light on the plateau and heavy on the slopes and lowlands, except in Yucatan and the desert regions. The northeast winds bring the rain. 

A rainy season from May to October affects chiefly the southern part of Mexico. 

3. Mexico is partly in the Torrid zone. The lowlands and lower mountain slopes are hot and moist. Yucatan has cool sea breezes. The altitude of the plateau gives it a temperate climate. Around Mexico

City, which has an altitude of over 7400 feet, the days are warm and the nights cool. 

4. The products of the country vary with the different regions. Yucatan has a very important industry in its henequen plantations, from which sisal hemp is produced. Much of this sisal hemp is shipped to the 

United States. Progreso, one of the leading harbors of Mexico, is the port from which sisal is shipped. 

5. A product of the coastal lowlands is petroleum, found in the region around Tampico. These oil wells are extremely valuable and Mexico produces and exports much petroleum. Tampico is the petroleum port, and also one of the leading harbors of Mexico. 

Other coastal products are from the forests, and include timber, dyestuffs, and gums and juices. 

6. The haciendas, or plantations, in the hot lands produce fruits, sugar cane, rice, tobacco, and coffee. Here also, is raised a plant called guayule which is used to make rubber and is very valuable. 

7. On the Central plateau, cotton, fruits, and vegetables are grown. Irrigation is carried on in the central part of the plateau and in the Sonoran desert and Lower California. Few of the agricultural products of 

Mexico are exported. On the ranches in the northern part of the plateau cattle, horses, sheep, and goats are raised. Hides are an important article of export for 

Mexico, but the meat is used in the country. 

8. Mining would be much more important in Mexico if the mines were developed by modern machinery. 

Mexico's mines are rich in gold and silver. Mexico

MEXICO 

139 leads the other nations of the world in the production of silver. There are also coal fields in Mexico which have been little developed. 

9. The rivers of Mexico are short and rapid. They are not suitable for navigation, but afford water power. 

This water power has been little used, but will be very valuable as Mexico turns to manufacturing. Puebla and Orizaba have cotton mills. Mexico City and 

Guadalajara have textile and flour mills and meatpacking plants. San Luis Potosi has ore smelters. 

10. Lack of good transportation has hindered the development of the country. There are railroad lines to the

United States, and between Mexico City and Vera Cruz and other ports. Tampico, Progreso, and Vera 

Cruz are Mexico's leading seaports. Vera Cruz exports lumber, bananas, coffee, hides, silver, and gold. 

11. The people of Mexico are largely of Indian or Spanish and Indian descent. There are not many white people. 

The Mexicans do much work by hand in the mines and on the haciendas, and work very slowly. 

12. Many Americans and other foreigners have invested money in Mexican plantations, factories, mines, banks, and other developments. The trade between Mexico and the United States is large.

A TRIP TO CENTRAL 

AMERICA 

CENTRAL AMERICA 

Let us take a trip to Central America. It is April when we sail from New York. We wear our spring coats as the spring months in the north are never very warm. As we sail south through the Atlantic, where the waters are warmed by the Gulf stream, we do not need our wraps. Just north of Cuba we cross the Tropic of Cancer, and pass from the Temperate into the Torrid zone. We feel less like moving around, and are content to sit in our steamer chairs and lazily watch the sunny blue skies, and the shiny leaves of the palm trees along the shores of the islands we pass. We think what nice fans these leaves would make. Our boat stops at Havana, and we buy some of the cool, white cotton clothing that is the usual dress of white people in the tropics. From Havana we sail to Puerto 

Barrios, a seaport of Guatemala, and we are in

Central America. 

Compared with our United States, Central

America is a tiny land. But it is rich in tropical fruits and flowers, and filled with sights strange to us. It is a land of high mountains and low, swampy coasts. It has temperate, tropical, and cool climates. There is something to interest everyone in this little group of countries which lies between Mexico and South America.

In Central America we find six little republics, or states. The government of each 

THE COUNTRIES 

OF is carried on by its people. 

The republics are Guate- 

CENTRAL AMERICA mala, Honduras, Salvador, 

Nicaragua, Costa Rica, and

Panama. Besides the six republics there is also the small country of British Honduras, which belongs to Great Britain. The countries range in size from Nicaragua, which is a little larger than New York state, to British Honduras, which is about one-sixth as large. Guatemala has the largest population. Over 2,000,000 people live there. The total population of all of the Central American countries is about that of the city of New York.

CITIES 

The few cities in the Central American countries, we find quite unlike our cities. They have no very high buildings and no great factories. Of course, each country has a capital city. The capital cities of 

Central America are Guatemala City, Teguci galpa, San Salvador, Managua, San Jose, the city of Panama, and Belize. Find each of them on the map on page 132, and notice in which country it lies.

Let us visit these capital cities. Picture each of them against the mountain slopes. Their houses are mostly low and white. Some of them are built around patios, or courtyards, open to the sky. In the patios and around the houses are gardens and fountains, and sometimes pieces of statuary. Most of the white people live in towns like these. 

Near the cities we see ruins of quaint old

Spanish buildings and ancient Indian towns. 

Most of the cities are old, much older than the cities of the United States. This is because explorers from Spain came to Mexico, Central 

America, and South America many years before the Atlantic coast of North America was settled by colonies of English, French, and 

Dutch people.

BANANAS 

A kind of fruit that most of us like very much grows in Central America in great quantities. 

This fruit is the banana. So one of our excursions must be to a banana plantation. It seems strange to see this fruit growing as plentifully as apples in northern 

United States, or peaches in our southern orchards. And the trees, with their enormous leaves and great bunches of fruit, look very, very different from apple or peach trees.

On the plantation we learn that most of the bananas are grown along the low, tropical coast of the Caribbean sea, in the region of the northeast trade winds. Here forests have been cleared away to make the largest banana plantations in the world. The soil is rich and deep, 140

1. The principal business street of Managua, the capital of Nicaragua. Compare this street with the business streets you have seen in our country. In Nicaragua most of the wagons are drawn by oxen and have wheels of solid wood with iron rims nailed on. Automobiles and motor trucks would have hard going because the pavement in the city is rough and uneven and the unpaved country roads are deep with mud. The stores have high, narrow doors, thick walls, and no windows. The thick walls keep out the heat, so that though the shops are dark they are cool. No great pains are taken to keep the streets clean, as is done in progressive cities. 

2. A mission church in Salvador. Spanish missionaries built this church in the early days of Spanish conquest and colonization in America. Compare it with the Spanish missions shown opposite pages 23 and 97. All of them are made with the same thick walls of adobe or sun-dried clay. This material is cheap and durable. 

3. Picking coffee in Costa Rica. The girls and women have turned out to help harvest the coffee berries on a large plantation. Coffee is one of the most valuable crops of Central America. The coffee of Costa Rica is of the best quality and brings high prices.

4. Homes of the workers on a Costa Rica banana plantation. Many of the workers are of Spanish and Indian blood, but there are also many Negroes. The workers live in small huts with walls of very light boards and roofs that are thatched, or covered, with palm leaves. 

Because the weather is warm the year round, the principal purpose of the hut is to keep out the heavy rains of the tropics. 

5. Hauling fertilizer on a banana plantation in Honduras. The company which owns this plantation uses farm machinery made in the United States to haul supplies to its fields and to bring its bananas to the railroad. 

The tractor and truck in this picture are especially made for use in heavy mud. The plants beyond the tractor are banana trees. 

6. Bananas piled alongside a railroad to be loaded on freight cars. This railway is owned by a plantation, and carries the fruit directly to the plantation's private wharf on the seacoast to be shipped to the United States. These bananas are green. They will ripen on the stems and be ready for eating by the time they reach our markets. 

7. A long steel pier on the Caribbean coast of Central 

America. There are not many good harbors along the coast, and piers like this one are built out to deep water so that the coffee and bananas can be loaded directly onto the ocean steamers.

CENTRAL AMERICA 

141 and there is, of course, much rainfall. 

A banana plantation will give us some surprises. Have you seen a grocer in one of your neighborhood stores cut several bananas from a great bunch to sell to a customer? The bananas in a store are hung downward, so that the man can cut them easily, one by one. On the plantation we see that the fruit grows pointing upward. Only one great bunch grows on a tree, but sometimes a single bunch has over a hundred bananas. The fruit is so heavy that one tree could not carry any more.

So many men work on the banana plantation that we are reminded of the cotton pickers in the southern United States. The workers are largely Indians and mestizos. They cut down the fruit with long knives, a whole bunch at a time.

When the bunch is cut the bananas are green, or unripe, and not yellow as they will be later. The fruit travels such a long distance to market that it partly ripens on the way.

Cars loaded with bananas run directly from the plantations to the wharves. There the fruit is reloaded on great boats and started on its long journey to Europe or the United 

States. Most of the bananas go to the United

States, for the banana plantations are largely owned by fruit companies of our country. 

Many of the railroads, the wharves, and the modern villages have been built by the money of these companies. So you see that while

Central America is an aid to us in furnishing fruit which does not grow in our country, she is also dependent on the people of the United States and other countries for help in developing her plantations. 

Bananas are shipped from all the Central American ports on the Caribbean coast, and from San Jose on the Pacific coast of Guatemala. The largest shipments go to New York and New Orleans. Bananas are Central America's most important export.

The cheap labor employed by white men on their coffee plantations are Indians and mestizos. Coffee is by far the most important crop of Central America and is shipped from her ports to OTHER 

PRODUCTS 

the United States and Europe. When we visit a coffee plantation we see the small coffee trees which bear the coffee berry growing on the mountain slopes. The finest coffee plantations are above the coastal plain in Costa Rica. 

The little country of Costa Rica is rightly named "Rich Coast." 

Central America also exports valuable woods, tobacco, tropical fruits, sisal, and chicle. 

The land is rich in gold and silver. But because of frequent government troubles and old-fashioned mining methods these resources have not as yet been developed to any great extent.

As we travel from place to place we discover many strange and interesting things about 

Central America. We soon

AND VOLCANOES learn that except for the LAND SURFACE 

plantation lands the country is largely mountainous. There is no one great central plateau, or table land, as in Mexico, but there are high areas that are comparatively flat. Most of the people live in the upland regions where the climate is more temperate and the rainfall plentiful. There is practically no lowland on the Pacific coast, as the mountains rise directly from the ocean.

There are many volcanoes in Central America, some of them very high. In the past they have thrown out large quantities of lava which have made the soil around the mountains fertile. Most people live at some distance from the active volcanoes, as cities built near them have often been destroyed by eruptions and earthquakes. Guatemala City, in Guatemala, was destroyed so many times that the people finally rebuilt it thirty miles from its original site. Because earthquakes are common in the volcanic region, the houses are built low, often with only one story, and are spread out over a great deal of ground. 

Aside from the white plantation owners and business men, the people we see in Central 

America look very different from those in the United HOMES AND 

OCCUPATIONS 

States. They are mostly mes- OF THE NATIVES tizos though there are also many Indians, and some negroes. Many of these people are so poor that they cannot even

NORTH AMERICA 

buy shoes and the right kind of clothing. They live in what we think very queer homes. Some of them have mud huts. Others have houses made of straw and thin sticks of wood, with roofs of straw or leaves. The huts and houses are usually grouped in villages in the midst of rich tropical forest growth, vines and shrubs. 

Besides building their own homes, the natives work with crude hand tools in cultivating and planting crops of corn, yams, and cassava. 

Cassava and yams take the place of our bread and potatoes. Yams are something like sweet potatoes, and cassava is a plant from which we get tapioca. In addition to gardening, the people raise chickens, cows, goats, and pigs.

The tool most commonly used by the mestizos is a long knife called a machete. With this they clear the underbrush to make space for their gardens, cut down bananas, and chop paths through the wild jungle lands. It is fun to watch them.

We find few of the natives of Central America well educated, and we soon discover that the year-round warm climate makes it hard for them to work long or energetically as do the people of temperate countries. They do make Panama hats and cigars by hand, and the United States buys these things from them. 

They manufacture few other things, but import iron and steel goods as well as cotton goods and flour from the United States and Europe. 

Railroads are few and the roads are poor except where the plantation owners have built them. In the Indian villages we see oxen draw 

TRANSPORTATION 

ing goods in heavy, rough wooden carts with canvas covers to keep off the hot sun. In other sections mules help with the work, or are used as pack animals. The men themselves often carry great loads on their backs.

THE FUTURE 

OF Since the building of the Panama canal, sanitary conditions in Central America have improved. There is much less sickness than there used to be, and more and more white people are tempted to live there. The CENTRAL AMERICA 

natural wealth of Central 

America is attracting them. They are eager to benefit from the many resources, and they are willing to invest the necessary money so that they may do this. The next time we visit Central America we may find that good schools have been built and that more railroads and better roads make it far easier for us to travel from one little country to another.

1. Central America lies in the Torrid zone, between

Mexico and South America. Its eastern coast is low and hot. The soil is rich and deep. The western coast is high, with mountains rising directly from the sea.

Much of the soil of this region is of volcanic formation. 

2. Bananas and coffee are the chief products of Central

America. They are grown on plantations, most of which are owned by white men from the United States and other countries. Indians and mestizos supply the cheap labor employed on these plantations. Coffee and bananas are shipped from Central American ports to the United States and to Europe. From these lands come iron and steel goods, cotton goods and flour. 

3. Other products of Central America are valuable woods, tropical fruits, sisal, and chicle. Panama hats

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

If you look on your map on page 132, you will see that a narrow neck of land connects 

North America with South America. This neck of land is called the Isthmus of Panama. The republic of Panama includes most of this region except the narrow Panama Canal zone. 

The Panama Canal zone consists of the Panama canal, fifty miles long, and a strip of land five miles wide on each side of the canal, along its whole length. The canal zone extends in a northwesterly-southeasterly direction from the Caribbean sea to the Pacific ocean. This seems odd until you realize that the isthmus runs almost directly east and west.

LEASING OF THE CANAL ZONE 

The United States leases the Canal zone from Panama. Our reasons for leasing it are interesting. Early in the sixteenth century, an expedition from Spain conquered the rich South American country of 

Peru. The conquerors wished to ship the

Peruvian treasures of gold and silver back to Spain. But to do this they had to send ships far down the western coast of South America and through the dangerous Strait of Magellan, or else ship the treasure by boat to the Pacific coast of the Isthmus of Panama, carry it overland across mountains and through unhealthful swamps and reship it on the Caribbean sea. The Spaniards talked of cutting a canal through the isthmus as this would make it easier to send their riches home to Spain, but they never carried out their plan. 

Years later the French tried to do what the Spaniards had talked of doing. After spending more than $250,000,000 and losing the lives of hundreds and hundreds of men because of fever, they gave up their efforts. 

The next country to attempt the work was the United States. Though her Atlantic and

Pacific ports were connected by rail it would be far cheaper to send large shipments by water if only the long journey around South

America could be avoided. It was easy to see how great would be the advantages of a shorter water route between the Atlantic and the Pacific. Then during the Spanish-American War an American warship off the west coast was needed in Cuban waters. Because there was no way through the narrow isthmus the ship had to travel thousands of miles around South America. This meant a long delay and might have meant the losing of a war. 

For these reasons the United States wished to build a canal across the isthmus, and in 1904 our country leased the Panama Canal zone. 

The French company was paid $40,000,000 for its partly finished canal and its right of way. 

The republic of Panama was paid $10,000,000 outright and was promised $250,000 a year for all rights to the canal zone. After ten years of hard work and the spending of great sums of money, the Panama canal was opened on 

August 3, 1914.

DIFFICULTIES IN BUILDING THE CANAL 

The difficulties in building the canal were many. First, something had to be done to make conditions healthful for the workmen. It was discovered that the mosquitoes carried the germs of malaria and yellow fever which had killed so many of the men who had worked for the French. Under the direction of Surgeon-General W. C. Gorgas of the United States Army, the swamps were drained and pools were covered with oil to kill the mosquitoes.

The next thing to consider was the best way to build a canal. DeLesseps, a French engineer who had already built the Suez canal, had planned a sea level canal. That means a waterway for ships to pass from ocean to ocean without the use of locks. This was not practical at Panama because parts of the surface of Panama are several hundred feet above the level of the sea, and it would be almost impossible to cut a sea level canal through them. 

Finally, the United States engineers, headed by General George W. Goethals, decided to 144

The people of Panama did not know how to take care of their health. started the Panama canal was forced to give up its attempt. 

7. How the Americans improved Panama. This picture shows the same street as that shown to the left, two months later. It has been paved and cleaned, and is no longer a breeding place for disease. American engineers made Panama an attractive and healthful country, in which thousands of workmen could live safely while they dug the canal. The Canal zone has been kept healthful ever since.

8. A modern hotel at Cristobal, at the Atlantic end of the Panama canal. This fine hotel is one of the many improvements made by the United States.

THE PANAMA CANAL ZONE 

145 build a canal with a series of locks. Even with locks, much digging and blasting had to be done through the mountain ranges, although they are quite low where the canal cuts through. During the digging there was always danger of landslides, which again and again hindered the work of building. Even now great care is taken to prevent such slides. 

The cut through the mountains, a distance of eight or nine miles, is called the Gaillard cut. 

Another difficult feat was the building of

Gatun dam which holds back the waters of the Chagres river and forms Gatun lake. Gatun lake is twenty-four miles long, and its waters fill the locks and raise ships to the next level. 

The overflow waters from the dam are used to run a hydroelectric plant which supplies the electricity for general use in the canal zone, and the power which opens and closes the lock gates. 80°

A ship going from the Atlantic ocean to the 

Pacific ocean through the Panama canal passes from the Caribbean sea into Limon bay, then travels for seven miles along the sea-level channel of the canal to the Gatun locks. Here it is lifted by a series of three locks to Gatun lake, eighty-five feet above the level 79°30' of the sea. The ship is towed by electric locomotives through the locks. It then steams twenty-four miles across the lake and through the Gaillard cut to the locks of Pedro Miguel. 

Here the ship is lowered thirty feet to Lake Miraflores. After traveling one and one-half miles across the lake it reaches the locks of Miraflores and is lowered fifty-five feet to the broad channel leading to the Gulf of Panama and the Pacific ocean.

The Ancon was the first steamer to pass through the Panama canal. This was on August 3, 1914. The passage took nine hours. 

Now the usual time for passing through is seven or eight hours. Forty-eight ships can pass through in a day. The toll, or charge, varies with the size of the ship. The average charge is about $4200. In 1915, the year after the Panama canal was completed, 1075 ships passed through the canal, paying tolls amounting to $4,367,550. In a recent year, 6456 ships passed through the canal, with tolls amounting to $26,944,500. Ships in the service of the

United States government do not pay toll. 

The United States has built forts to protect the canal in time of war, but it is open to the ships of all nations in time of peace. 

The Panama canal has greatly shortened the distances ships have to travel between many of the world's most important ports, and has brought about a vast improvement over the old days. Steamships now travel from

BENEFITS FROM THE CANAL 

the Pacific ports of both North and South America through the Panama canal to the 

Atlantic ports of America and Europe. And ships from all these Atlantic ports journey through the canal to the western coasts of the American continents, and to the countries of the far east.

Study these interesting figures and see what the Panama canal saves ships sailing from

New York to San Francisco: 

From New York to San Francisco by the Strait of Magellan 15,126 miles by the Panama canal 

Miles of travel saved 

6,059 miles 9,067 By studying a map of the world, see if you NASA-9

NORTH AMERICA 

can find about how many miles are saved by using the Panama canal in going by water from New York to China, Australia, and

Alaska. 

By use of the Suez and Panama canals, ships can sail around the entire world without passing south of either Africa or South America.

Heavy, bulky goods may be shipped between ports on our east and west coasts through the Panama canal much more cheaply than across the continent by rail. For the defense of our country, battleships can pass quickly from one coast to the other.

The Panama railroad, which runs along the east side of the canal from the city of Colon to the cities of Balboa and Pan 

THE PANAMA RAILROAD 

ama, was built in 1855 with money loaned by American bankers. The road was opened after the gold rush to California had started. It was bought by the French when they tried to dig the canal, and sold by them to the United States. 

It is now run and operated by our government. 

The city of Colon, at the Atlantic end of the Panama canal, belongs to the republic of Panama, although it is in the canal zone. 

It is a supply station for shipping. The 

CITIES 

American port, Cristobal, is separated from 

Colon by only a wire fence. Ocean steamers load and unload passengers and freight at her wharfs. Cristobal is also a coaling station. 

Balboa, which belongs to the United States, is at the Pacific end of the canal. It has railroad repair stations, machine shops, dry docks, and fuel oil tanks.

The city of Panama, adjoining Balboa, is the capital of the republic of Panama. It is an attractive city on the Gulf of Panama and is within the canal zone. It is quite like our modern cities, with electric lights, telephones, trolleys, and well-paved streets.

The sea breezes from the gulf cool the city. As in the other cities in the canal zone, the business of Panama is mostly dependent upon the ships which pass through. Many tourists make winter visits to Panama.

The people of the canal zone are largely natives and employees of our government. The natives raise fruit and vegetables and help in the shipping work. The Panama Canal zone is ruled by a commission and a governor appointed by the President of the United States. 

The principal government buildings are in Balboa.

 1. The Panama Canal zone consists of the Panama canal, stretching fifty miles from the Atlantic to the Pacific ocean, and a strip of land five miles wide on each side of it. 

2. The United States leased the Panama Canal zone in 1904. It took ten years to build the canal. 

3. A series of locks, the Gatun, Pedro Miguel, and Miraflores, raise and lower ships passing through the canal. The Gaillard cut, through the mountains, and the Gatun dam were difficult engineering feats necessary in building the canal. 

4. It takes about eight hours for a ship to pass through the Panama canal. Forty-eight ships can pass through in a day. The amount of toll varies with the size of the ship. 

5. The chief benefit of the Panama canal is that it shortens the route for ships between Atlantic and 

Pacific ports. 

6. The principal cities of the Panama Canal zone are 

Colon and Cristobal at the Atlantic end of the canal, and Balboa and Panama at the Pacific end.

THE WEST INDIES AND BERMUDA ISLANDS THE WEST INDIES 

The first land that Columbus saw after his long trip across the Atlantic proved to be one of the islands of the West Indies. In time a number of these islands were settled by Spaniards. And Spaniards, too, went to South

America and conquered rich lands there. The blue Caribbean came to be called the Spanish Main, from the many Spanish galleons, or ships, which sailed its waters, carrying away treasures of gold from the conquered lands. 

Those exciting days of adventure are over, but the story of these islands is still most interesting.

The West Indies are a chain of islands off the southeastern tip of the United States. 

They curve some 2000 miles around the Caribbean sea from the entrance to the Gulf of 

Mexico to the northern coast of South America.

ISLAND GROUPS 

The largest group of islands is called the Greater Antilles. This group includes Cuba, Haiti, Jamaica, and Porto Rico.

The Lesser Antilles are made up of many small islands which lie between Porto Rico and South America.

Among them are the Virgin islands, the Leeward islands, and the Windward islands. 

North of the Greater Antilles is the Bahama island group.

The Greater and Lesser Antilles lie between the Tropic of Cancer and the Equator. 

Havana, Cuba, which is about the northern point of the West Indies, lies just south of the Tropic of Cancer. It is about one and a third degrees, or nearly 100 miles, south of Florida. 

All the islands of the Greater and Lesser Antilles are in the Torrid zone. Florida, so 

CLIMATE 

short a distance to the north, is called a semitropical land but these islands are wholly tropical. They never have any snow or frost. The days are very hot in summer and very warm even in winter. The 148 northeast trade winds blow over these islands, bringing moisture from the Atlantic ocean. 

Because of the mountainous surface of the islands much more rain falls on the northeastern slopes than on the southwestern. 

There are only two seasons in the Greater and Lesser Antilles, a wet and a dry, instead of the four that we have. With our four seasons we have gradual changes in temperature, from spring to summer, to autumn, to winter. 

In a climate with only wet and dry seasons, there are no gradual changes and the seasons depend not upon temperature but upon the amount of rainfall. The rains come between 

May and October. During this period hurricanes sometimes strike the islands and violent winds do much damage to crops, buildings, and shipping. But, usually, the weather is bright with sunshine and so hot in the middle of the day that most people have to rest. 

SURFACE 

The Greater and Lesser Antilles are really the great tops of mountains whose sides and bases are under water. Most of the islands are mountainous, with fertile valleys and coastal plains. The Greater Antilles have broad lowland coasts. The Lesser 

Antilles are mostly high and are made of volcanic rock. There are a number of volcanic peaks in this group, and it is not uncommon for an eruption to wipe out whole towns, or for an earthquake to do great harm. The 

Bahama islands are low-lying islands of coral formation. They have a shallow soil.

PLANT LIFE 

All of the islands are covered with beautiful trees and plants and flowers, many of which are not found in the Temperate zones. 

The royal palm, which grows in southern Florida, is very common in all the West Indies. Some are as tall as a hundred feet. Then there is the bay tree, the bamboo tree and the coconut palm. There are ferns as high as trees. There are Easter lilies,

IN CARIBBEAN LANDS OF SUNSHINE 

3 2 4 

(1) (4) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. (2) Hamburg-American Line. 

1. Harbor scene at Havana, Cuba. Less than two hundred miles south of Florida, Havana is in close touch with our Gulf and south Atlantic ports, with which she carries on a large trade. 

2. Historic Morro Castle, at the mouth of Havana harbor. Built by the Spaniards in the 16th century, this castle served for more than 300 years as a fort for the protection of the harbor, and as a prison. At the time of the Spanish-American War, when Cuba was trying to win independence from Spain, captured American soldiers and seamen were imprisoned here. 

3. The new National Capitol in Havana, Cuba. Soon after the Spanish-American War, the United States gave 5 

(3) (5) Cuban National Tourist Commission. 

Cuba her independence. The Cubans made their government as nearly as possible like ours, and built this Capitol to look like the Capitol in Washington. 

4. Tobacco growing under cheesecloth, in Porto Rico.

The cloth keeps out many insects which might damage the crop and, by shading the plants from the sun, helps produce a light-colored tobacco used in fine cigars. 

5. Drying tobacco leaves on racks in Cuba. When dried, the tobacco is "cured," that is, exposed to a high temperature for several days. This brings out the best color and fragrance. The tobacco is then packed in bales or hogsheads and stored in warehouses for a long time to improve its quality before being manufactured.

Delightful climate and beautiful scenery make these islands a haven for winter tourists.

It is especially valuable on the islands because of the hilly surface of the country. Most of this raw sugar is refined, or prepared for use, in the 

United States.
