THE WEST INDIES AND BERMUDA ISLANDS 

149 and orchids of delicate hue. Nature is lavish in these tropical lands and everything grows in abundance.

The crops which grow on these islands are those found in warm climates. The tall, green stalks of the sugar cane cover huge 

PRODUCTS 

plantations. Some sugar cane is raised in the southern states of our country, but there frost may come and destroy it. In the West Indies, there is never any frost and is sugar grown on all the islands. Then there are acres and acres of the tobacco plant with its huge green leaves. Here and there are coffee trees with scarlet fruit containing the seeds called the coffee bean. Everywhere one finds tropical fruits pineapples, bananas, grapefruit, oranges, lemons, coconuts, mangoes, and guava. And in the forests grow beautiful trees mahogany, rosewood, and cedar. 

The West Indies send us sugar, tobacco, coffee, fruits, cacao, and fine woods, and also fresh vegetables which they can grow in the winter when much of our ground is frozen. 

But the trading is not all one-sided. The West 

Indies need things from our country that they cannot produce. We send them cotton goods and other manufactured goods, machinery, cereals, salt pork, and codfish. It is easy for our country to trade with the West Indies because they are so near.

OWNERSHIP OF WEST INDIES 

In the early days Spain owned most of these islands, but gradually she has lost them all. In 

1898 the United States helped 

Cuba win her independence from Spain. For a time after that our country governed Cuba, but later, when it was found she could govern herself, Cuba was made an independent republic. There are two other independent republics, the Republic of Haiti and the Dominican Republic. 

They are both on the island of Haiti. 

The United States won Porto Rico from 

Spain and now governs it. Three of the Virgin islands, St. Thomas, St. John, and St. Croix, the United States bought from Denmark.

They, too, are now governed by our country. 

Great Britain owns the largest number of islands, including the Bahamas, Jamaica, Trinidad, and many of the Leeward and Windward islands. The French and the Dutch own most of the remaining islands. Notice that all these mother countries lie in the Temperate zone. Their tropical possessions are, therefore, very valuable to them in furnishing products which cannot be successfully raised at home. 

But most of the West Indies' trade is with the United States.

With the opening of the Panama canal the West Indies became of even greater importance to the nations which own them. A large part of the world trade goes through the canal, so the West Indies are important as coaling stations for ships. If our country were at war, our islands in the West Indies would be necessary as naval bases for the coaling and repair of our warships.

POPULATION 

The population of the West Indies is large considering their size. The greater part of the people are negroes. Cuba and 

Porto Rico have more white people than any of the other islands. white people own most of the plantations and the labor is done by the negroes. The

The natives live a very simple life. Besides their work on the plantations some of them have their own gardens. Their houses are small, with roofs of coconut palm leaves laid across rods of bamboo. There are wooden shutters in place of glass windows. Every little home has its shade trees to protect it from the heat of the sun. The people wash their clothes in the rivers or streams and try to get them clean by pounding them on the rocks. 

On market days, the women come to town with their baskets filled with sugar, coconuts, fruits, vegetables, nuts, coffee, or cocoa to sell or to trade for the things they need. They walk barefooted and carry their baskets on their heads. Even the school children carry their books to school on their heads. 

There are some disadvantages in living on the islands. The inhabitants of many of the 

West Indies are cut off from the outside world with no contact except occasional passing steamships. To some of the islands, steamers do not bring even mail oftener than once a month. It is a very important event when

NORTH AMERICA 

they do come. Radio stations on some of the islands tell the people of world events. 

Then, too, these people depend upon agriculture for their living. When the amount of land is small in comparison with the number of people to be fed, a great deal must be produced from each acre of land. This means hard work, and these natives are not energetic. 

The largest island of the West Indies is Cuba, about 100 miles south of Florida. It is 

730 miles long and varies in width from twenty to ninety miles. The scenery is very beautiful in Cuba, for there are the mountains and the valleys and the sea, and everywhere the brilliant vegetation of the tropics. There are fine roads and many miles of railroad.

CUBA Cuba's chief product is sugar. The island produces more sugar than any other one country in the world. The sugar cane is grown on lowland plantations all along the coast. During cutting season, thousands of negroes live with their families in little houses on the plantations and work in the fields gathering the sugar cane. Some of the cane is hauled to nearby sugar mills on the island. Much raw sugar is sent to the refineries of the United States. 

Tobacco is the second important crop of Cuba. It is mostly raised on the lower slopes of the mountains in the western part of the island. Much of the crop is made into cigars and cigarettes in the factories of Havana. The rest is exported.

In eastern Cuba are forests of mahogany, ebony, rosewood, and cedar. In the mountains are iron and copper, but mining is not done to any great extent. The hills of the interior are used for raising cattle, horses, and mules.

Cuba has been called "The Island of a Hundred Harbors." On one of the finest of these harbors is Havana, the chief city of the West Indies and the capital of the Cuban republic. 

Havana's harbor is called "land-locked" because it is almost surrounded by land with only a narrow opening to the sea. Havana's bay is always filled with ships from the United States and Europe loading and unloading their cargoes. The famous Havana cigars manufactured here are sent all over the world.

Havana is a favorite resort for winter tourists. It can be reached from Florida by steamboat and by ferries which carry whole railway trains over from the Florida Keys. Or if one prefers to come more quickly, one can fly by seaplane from Miami in a little more than an hour. Havana is a fascinating city with much to interest the visitor. 

The chief port of Cuba's southeastern coast is Santiago. It, too, is situated on a fine harbor. It is a trading center for the minerals that are exported.

HAITI AND DOMINICAN REPUBLIC 

The island of Haiti is the second largest of the Greater Antilles. The republic of Haiti is on the western third of the island. Port au Prince is its capital and its chief port. France once owned this land and the French language is still spoken. Santo Domingo is the capital of the Dominican Republic, which occupies the rest of the island. Spanish is the language spoken here, as the land used to belong to Spain. The inhabitants are mostly negroes. They have not made much progress because of their poor government. 

Coffee and sugar are the most important products of the island of Haiti. Cacao, cotton, tobacco, and sisal are also grown. Most of the trade is with the United States.

In Porto Rico the American flag waves over the public buildings, showing that the island belongs to the United States. 

PORTO RICO 

It is the smallest island of the Greater Antilles, but it has a large population. 

A range of mountains runs through the middle of the island from east to west. Agriculture is the chief industry. Sugar furnishes the largest crop, and tobacco is second. Coffee is grown in the interior of the island. Citrus fruits, bananas, coconuts, and pineapples grow in different parts of the island. On the lowland pastures, there are herds of cattle and horses. 

Porto Rico buys most of what she needs from the United States and we are the chief customer for her products. The capital and largest port of Porto Rico is San Juan. Ponce is also a port of importance. 

Jamaica, "the beautiful," is ninety miles

THE WEST INDIES AND BERMUDA ISLANDS 

151 

JAMAICA 

south of Cuba. It is the largest and most valuable of the British West Indies.

Banana plantations are everywhere in Jamaica and furnish many of the bananas that the world eats. The island produces many spices, as well as sugar, coffee, fruits and other products of the West Indies. Kingston is the capital and is a popular resort for tourists on account of its beauty and climate. Port 

Antonio is the second largest port. Steamers go to New York and Boston from these cities. 

The Virgin islands are east of Porto Rico. 

Those owned by the United States are St. 

THE VIRGIN ISLANDS 

Thomas, St. Croix, and St. John.

The people are mostly negroes. 

St. Thomas has an excellent harbor. It is a coaling station for steamships passing through the Caribbean sea. 

The Leeward and the Windward islands stretch in a half circle from the eastern end of 

LEEWARD 

AND 

WINDWARD ISLANDS 

the Greater Antilles to the mainland of South America. Most of them belong to England, but some are French, some Dutch, and a few belong to the South American country of Venezuela. Some of these islands are used as coaling stations and others produce sugar and other tropical plant products. 

From the island of Trinidad, which is the most southerly of the West Indies, we get 

TRINIDAD 

much of the material for our asphalt roads. The tourist goes out to Pitch lake to see the digging of asphalt, and he is surprised to find he is driving on the lake itself before he realizes that he has reached there. A curious thing about this lake is the fact that as soon as pitch has been dug the hole fills up again and a new crust forms, strong enough to support teams and wagons.

The supply never gives out. Mineral oil, or petroleum, is another important product of Trinidad. Most of it is exported to Great Britain, the mother country. Port of Spain, the capital of Trinidad, is one of the finest towns in the 

West Indies. 

THE BAHAMAS 

The Bahamas are in the Atlantic ocean off the southeast coast of Florida. Like the Florida 

Keys, these islands are made of coral. They produce sisal, sugar, cotton and citrus fruits. Sponges are gathered around their shores and are their chief product. The capital, Nassau, is a popular winter resort.

A MUTUAL HELP 

The West Indies and the United States are very useful to each other. The fact that they lie so near to each other is of great advantage in trade.

The West Indies have in abundance the tropical fruits that we either cannot grow at all or can only grow to a limited extent. They are important to us as coaling stations for our steamships. They furnish a wonderful playground for us that is easily reached. The 

United States, in turn, furnishes the West Indies with the things they need and cannot produce for themselves. Our knowledge of sanitation and of government has been of great assistance in the progress of many of the islands. Much of the capital that the West Indies have needed to develop their natural resources has come from the United States. And this mutual dependence is all the time increasing. 

THE BERMUDAS 

The Bermuda islands do not belong to the West Indies, but are a group of islands about 800 miles north of them. Lying far out in the 

Atlantic, they are in nearly the same latitude as Charleston, South Carolina. They are small coral islands, about 100 in number, some inhabited and some not. The soil is poor, but there are no frosts, and early vegetables are raised to send to northern markets in the spring. In return, grain, flour, rice and livestock are imported from the United States. 

Hamilton, the capital of the Bermudas, is a delightful winter resort because of the warm, even climate. The islands belong to Great 

Britain. They are an important naval station between Canada and the British possessions in the West Indies.

NORTH AMERICA 

1. The West Indies are a group of islands extending in a curve around the Caribbean sea from the mouth of the Gulf of Mexico to South America. They include the Greater and Lesser Antilles, the Bahamas, and other islands. The Greater Antilles are Cuba, Haiti, Jamaica, and Porto Rico. The Lesser An tilles include the Virgin islands, the Leeward islands, the Windward islands, and many smaller islands.

The West Indies lie between the Tropic of Cancer and the Equator. 

2. The climate of the West Indies is warm, with a wet and a dry season. The northeast trade winds bring much rain to the northeastern sides of the islands while the southwestern slopes are drier. 

3. In surface the West Indies are mostly mountainous. 

The Bahamas are low-lying coral islands. 

4. The West Indies are largely agricultural. 

Their products are: sugar, tobacco, coffee, tropical fruits, vegetables, and lumber. 

5. The West Indies have different forms of government. 

Cuba, Haiti and the Dominican Republic have their own government. Porto Rico and some of the Virgin islands are under the United States government. The other islands belong to Great Britain, France, Holland, and Venezuela in South America. 

6. Cuba and Porto Rico have a large white population. 

On the other islands, there are more negroes than white people. 

7. Cuba is the largest island of the West Indies. Suga and tobacco are its chief crops. Havana is the capi tal and chief seaport. 

8. The island of Haiti is made up of two republics, Haiti and the Dominican Republic. Coffee and sugar are the most important products. 

9. Porto Rico is important for its sugar, tobacco, and coffee. San Juan is the capital. 

10. Jamaica belongs to Great Britain. Bananas are the chief export. Kingston, the capital, is a winter resort. 

11. Trinidad is the source of a large amount of asphalt. 

It is also important for its petroleum. 

12. The Bermudas do not belong to the West Indies. 

They lie north of the West Indies and east of South Carolina. They export vegetables, and are a favorite winter resort. Hamilton is the capital.

THE HAWAIIAN ISLANDS 

1. Scene on the island of Hawaii, with the great volcano, Mauna Loa, in the distance. Although the Hawaiian islands are in the Torrid zone, and the climate is so warm that palm trees are common, the top of this high volcano is usually covered with snow. 

2. The inner crater of Kilauea, another volcano on the island of Hawaii. Kilauea is active almost all the time. 

This photograph was taken from an airplane many thousand feet above the volcano. The pit shown in the picture is actually a quarter of a mile deep and nearly two-thirds of a mile across. The smoke comes from hot melted rock in the crater. By looking closely, we can see, to the left of the crater, the road by which scientists come to study the volcano. 5 3. Picking ripe pineapples in the plantation fields near 

Honolulu. These pineapples will be taken to factories for canning. Canned pineapple is an important export of Hawaii. 

4. Loading sugar cane on a Hawaiian plantation. The cane is cut in the fields and dragged to the train on sleds by a tractor made in the United States. Raw sugar will be made from this cane at a Hawaiian factory, and will probably be sent to our country to be refined. 

5. A great transpacific liner arriving in Honolulu harbor, greeted by native Hawaiians in canoes. Liners and freight steamers between Honolulu and the United 

States carry almost all Hawaii's trade.

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS 

1. A coconut grove along the beach of Negros island.

Behind the beach is a lagoon, or enclosed inlet, from which mist is rising. In the distance are lava mountains, of which the Philippines are largely composed. We can see a settlement and boats along the shore. A great deal of Philippine trade and travel is by boat. 

2. Preparing a field for planting rice. Rice grows best in wet soil. The men are working the mud with machines drawn by water buffaloes. The water buffalo, which is fond of mud, is a most useful animal for rice farming. 

3. Rice terraces in the Philippines. The mountain people have very little level land. They make tiny fields on the steep mountain slopes by building walls and carrying earth from the river valleys to fill the spaces behind the walls. Rains keep the soil in the terraced fields wet. 

4. River life in Manila. The rivers are important routes of trade in the Philippines. Small boats like these carry goods, and some of them are the homes of large families. 

In this picture a raft of coconuts is being floated to market. 

5. Bringing copra to a seaport. Copra is the valuable dried meat of the coconut. Pack trains like this are a common means of transportation. Notice the native huts. They are raised on stilts, because of the heavy rains in the Philippines.

DISTANT ISLAND POSSESSIONS OF THE UNITED STATES THE HAWAIIAN ISLANDS 

Nestled in the Pacific ocean, many hundred miles from any mainland, is a little group of islands which are probably the loveliest in the world. And they are as strange as they are lovely. Even their name is odd. The group of islands is named for the largest one among them, Hawaii. They are called the Hawaiian islands. reefs.

HAWAII ISLAND 

Mauna Loa Kilauea 220 20° A group of American missionaries went to the Hawaiian islands shortly after the year 1800 and did much to help the natives and to show them better ways of living. They established schools and taught the people the English language.

In August, 1898, at the request of their own people, the Hawaiian islands became a possession of the United States. They are now known as the territory of Hawaii. 

The Hawaiian islands are almost entirely of volcanic formation, but they have some coral States government in Hawaii. 

The Hawaiian islands are in the Torrid zone. Strong northeast trade winds blow against the eastern slopes of the CLIMATE mountains and cause heavy rainfall in that region. Because the mountains are very high the winds lose much of their moisture in crossing them, and there is hardly any rainfall on the west side. On some of the islands are tourist resorts. These are on the west side, the side protected from heavy winds. Here the climate is warm and fair and there are 153

NORTH AMERICA 

light, cooling breezes from the ocean.

SOIL The rich lava soil of the islands varies from yellow to red-brown. It is fertile, and vegetation is abundant. There are more strange flowers and trees to be seen in Hawaii than in any other land of its size which one might visit. The soil on the west sides of the islands is best suited for farming, but must be irrigated.

PRODUCTS AND OCCUPATIONS 

Near the shores there is a kind of clay in the soil which holds moisture and makes the land suitable for raising sugar cane. Most of the sugar cane is raised on plantations, and the work is done by natives who are paid for their labor by being supplied with houses, water, medicine, and with an amount of money that varies with the price of sugar. 

More than one crop of sugar cane grows from one planting. When the first growth is cut another comes from the same roots. Raw sugar is the chief export crop of the islands. 

Nearly all the sugar is shipped to the United 

States to be refined.

The second product of importance on the islands is pineapples. Acres and acres of pineapples are grown in long straight rows across the gently sloping ground. Thousands of pineapples are canned yearly. Next to agriculture, canning pineapples and the making of raw sugar are the chief industries of the islands.

The farmers raise coffee, rice, figs, nuts, dates, and citrus fruits. They grow a fine quality of sisal and have even succeeded in raising rubber trees. To some extent, they carry on dairying too. Rice is one of the chief foods of the natives and while some of it is grown on the islands, much is imported from Japan.

The area of the islands is somewhat less. than that of our state of New Jersey, and the number of people who live there is somewhat over 300,000. Filipinos and Japanese make up

AREA AND POPULATION 

a large part of the population. There are also 

Americans, Portuguese, Spaniards, and Chinese. Less than a tenth of the people are brown-skinned native Hawaiians.

HONOLULU 

Honolulu, the capital city and chief port of the Hawaiian islands, is on the island of Oahu. 

It has fine wharves at which one may see ships from many lands.

It is a modern city with excellent streets and buildings, electric lights and trolley lines. 

There are several hundred miles of railroads on the islands, including those on the sugar plantations, and there is a good system of highways. 

At Pearl Harbor, on the island of Oahu, the

United States has established a naval base. 

Officers there help to safeguard the NAVAL interests of the United States in the BASE Pacific. In case of war, ships from this base would help protect our western coasts from enemy attack.

COMMERCIAL 

The islands are commercially important because of their position with relation to four of the six continents. They are in paths of Pacific trade from IMPORTANCE 

the countries of North and South America and from those of Asia and 

Australia. Boats from Europe sailing to the far east by way of the Panama canal also pass them. The islands are important coaling, or fueling, stations, because vessels on very long journeys cannot always carry enough coal or oil for the whole trip. The vessels can also get fresh food supplies for their crews and passengers here, and can put up for repairs. The stopping of so many ships, both United States and foreign, brings much trade to the islands. 

THE PHILIPPINE ISLANDS 

In the Pacific ocean, far west of the Hawaiian islands and about 500 miles off the southeast coast of Asia, there is a group of about 

7000 islands, called the Philippines. Some of them are large, but most are very small. In 

1898, at the close of a war with Spain, the 

United States gained possession of these islands for $20,000,000. 

Like the West Indies, the Philippine islands are really the tops of mountains rising out of the sea. Most of them rise to a height of between 2000 and 5000

FORMATION

DISTANT ISLAND POSSESSIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

155 feet above sea level and some of the highest peaks are between 8000 and 10,000 feet high. 

The islands are formed of limestone, coral reefs, and lava. Volcanoes have helped to make some of them quite large. Luzon, the largest northern island, and Mindanao, the largest southern island, are mainly made of volcanic lava.

CLIMATE 

If you will look at the map on page viii, you will see that the latitude of the Philippines is about the same as that of Central 

America. The islands are in the

Torrid zone, and are very, very hot. The high altitude of some mountains provides a few cool spots.

The Philippines are in the path of the northeast trade winds so they have much rainfall on their windward, or eastern, sides. The western slopes are drier, but even here there is plenty of rainfall. Typhoons, or terrific wind and rain storms, often strike the islands. Then the little bamboo houses, which are built on stilts to be out of the mud and floods caused by the heavy rains, are blown down and destroyed.

The natives suffer much from these storms. 

At Manila, the chief city and port, the United States government now has a weather observatory which sends out warnings when a typhoon is on the way. This gives the people a chance to prepare to meet it.

Where there is deep, rich soil and plenty of rainfall in a warm climate we expect to find forests and tangled under- 

AGRICULTURE growth. Years ago when the 

Spaniards went to the Philippine islands the forests were so dense that they could scarcely get through them. A large part of the land is still forest covered, but from year to year more land is being cleared for agriculture, the chief occupation of the people. Farming is carried on by old-time methods and rude tools, but education is causing progress in this as well as in other things. 

Before the white men taught the Filipinos the value of other crops, they grew nothing but rice in their gardens. They raised so much of this food that they had enough for themselves and for export as well. Of late years other crops have been introduced and though much rice is still raised, the rice grown in the Philippines is no longer sufficient for the growing population. Many thousand pounds are now imported yearly from Asia.

Because of the uneven surface of the land, much rice in the Philippines is raised on terraces built like steps on the hillsides. Men, women, and children work in the fields. They are helped by the water buffalo, an animal so lazy that, if he were allowed to do so, he would spend most of his time sleeping. The best thing about him is that he likes mud. What better animal could the Filipinos have to help them cultivate their moist rice fields? Rice, you see, has to do some of its growing under water and the rice fields are surrounded with canals and dikes so that the fields can be flooded part of the time and later the water can be drained off. 

The rice is planted in the spring and harvested late in the autumn. Many farmers have their own rice fields, but much rice is also raised on plantations whose owners pay the native workers with a part of the rice crops. 

The Filipinos grow sugar cane and export sugar. They also raise some coffee. In past years coffee was a more extensive crop, but plant diseases have destroyed much of this industry. Of late, fruits have been introduced and raised extensively. These include strawberries, blackberries, and grapes. Bananas and other fruits which grow wild on the islands are being cultivated by the natives for their own use, and because they can sell them to other countries. Some corn is raised. nuts and their products form a valuable article of commerce. Copra, the dried white meat of the coconut, is crushed to make coconut oil.

The shredded coconut used in puddings and cakes is made from copra.

Coco- In the valley of the Cagayan river, in Luzon, tobacco is the main crop. Both cigars and tobacco are exported. The cigars are made largely by hand, though some machinery is used.

An important export is Manila hemp, a fiber stripped from the leaf stems of a plant called the abaca. These stems are as tall as some trees. MANILA HEMP The fiber is washed and dried, and then is

NORTH AMERICA 

packed in bales to be shipped abroad and made into rope. The natives manufacture some hemp products themselves, either by hand mills or by steam mills. The steam is generated by soft coal mined in the islands.

The Philippine forests contain a variety of trees. Some supply fine cabinet woods. OTHER The bamboo and the rattan are 

RESOURCES plentiful and are used by the na tives for building their homes. 

The bamboo is used to make fine fish poles and for small souvenirs which travelers like to carry home from the islands.

The Philippines have rich, undeveloped mineral resources. Among these are gold, silver, platinum, phosphate and lead. A little of the gold is being mined by primitive methods, but the hot climate is unfavorable for this hard work. It is not likely that many mines will be opened until there is a strong demand for their products. 

Transportation among the many islands of the Philippines depends on tiny boats and 

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 

small steamers, which sail to and from Manila. 

On the islands there

The Bet- are hundreds of miles of railroads. heavy rains made railroads difficult to build. 

Sometimes the road beds were washed out almost as soon as they were laid. ter roads are now slowly replacing the mud paths between sections of the islands. 

Manila, a modern city, has fine pavements. 

The islands have telephone and telegraph communication, and there are cable connections with Asia and the United States. air-mail route between our country and the 

Philippines has recently been established.

An About 12,000,000 people live in the Philippines. The largest number of these are Malays 

POPULATION 

strong, industrious, brownskinned citizens, eager to advance. Next in number are the Negritos, or little negroes, so-called because they are small, unlike the large African negro. Many Chinese and Japanese and immigrants from other countries live in the Philippines, too, as in the 

Hawaiian islands.

The natives of the islands were originally divided into many tribes, widely separated by mountains and sea, and speaking different languages. Soon after gaining possession of the islands, the United States established many schools where the natives might learn to speak English. Many American teachers went to the islands to help in this work. The use of a single language is helping to unite the people in customs and ideas, and so to make them a more progressive nation.

In addition to a public school system, Manila has a university, and there are now many native-born and educated Filipinos who are prepared to teach. Natives from the islands come to our colleges and carry back the ideas and methods they have learned.

Europe and the southeastern countries of 

Asia profit from trade with the Philippines. 

Hemp, copra and tobacco are the TRADE products they buy. In return the islands buy rice and other products from them. 

But by far the largest trade of the Philippines is carried on with the United States. Vessels from Manila bring us sugar, Manila hemp, coconut oil, copra, and tobacco. They go back loaded with cotton cloth, iron and steel, gasoline and kerosene, machinery, automobiles, and flour. This trade is very valuable to the 

United States, because it gives us materials which we need in large quantities and provides buyers for the goods which are made in our factories,

1. Two island possessions of the United States are the 

Hawaiian islands and the Philippine islands. The

Hawaiian islands are in the Pacific ocean, far from any mainland. They are famous for their natural beauty, and for their many varieties of flowers and trees. They have several active volcanoes. The islands are in the Torrid zone, but have a delightful climate on the west side because of ocean breezes. 

2. People who live on the Hawaiian islands are largely 

Filipinos and Japanese. Americans, Chinese, Portuguese, Spaniards and native Hawaiians make up the rest of the population. They raise sugar cane, pineapples, rice, coffee, figs, nuts, dates, and citrus fruits. Besides agriculture, the chief industries are

DISTANT ISLAND POSSESSIONS OF THE UNITED STATES 

157 the canning of pineapples and the making of raw sugar. 

3. The chief city of the Hawaiian islands is Honolulu.

It is modern and has good streets and fine buildings. 

Ships from many countries land at the city's wharves. 

4. The Hawaiian islands are famous as a tourist resort, and serve as a coaling station, and as a naval base to help safeguard the interests of the United States in the Pacific. 

5. The Philippine islands are about 500 miles off the southeast coast of Asia. They came into the possession of the United States in 1898, at the close of a war with Spain, for the payment of $20,000,000. 

6. The Philippine islands are in the Torrid zone in the path of the northeast trade winds, and have heavy rainfall. They are partly of volcanic formation. 

7. Among the products raised by the natives of the 

Philippines are rice, sugar cane, tobacco, fruits, coffee, and hemp. Manila hemp is the most important export product. It is the fiber of the abaca plant, and is used in the making of rope. 

8. The natural wealth of the Philippine islands includes varieties of trees and minerals. The minerals are gold, silver, platinum, phosphate, and lead. These resources have not been extensively used. 

9. The United States government has established schools in the Philippines, where the natives are taught to speak English. The building of roads and railroads are two other ways in which the United States has improved the islands. 

10. The population of the Philippines consists largely of 

Malays and Negritos. The main city is Manila. 

11. The trade of the Philippines is largely with the United 

States. Products exported by the islands to this country are sugar, Manila hemp, coconut oil and copra, and tobacco. In exchange, we send the Philippines cotton goods, iron and steel, gasoline and kerosene, machinery, automobiles, and flour.

THE NORTH AMERICAN CONTINENT AS A WHOLE 

We live on the third largest continent of the world. Only Asia and Africa are larger than 

North America. Lying entirely 

POSITION in the Western hemisphere, the North American continent extends 

SIZE AND 

from within about 7° of the North Pole to within about 7° of the Equator. So, although part of it is in the North Frigid zone and part in the Torrid, most of North America is in the 

North Temperate zone.

North America's highest mountains lie close to the western coast. Her lower ranges are mainly in the east. In the central 

DRAINAGE part of the continent, and along the southern and much of the 

RELIEF AND 

eastern coasts, stretch the plains. 

The Hudson bay drainage area, the Mackenzie basin, the Great lakes drainage area, and the Mississippi basin are the greatest drainage basins of the continent. From the direction in which the great drainage systems flow we can read the story of the varying slopes of the land in different parts of our continent. 

The rivers that flow into Hudson bay show that the land they drain slopes from nearly all directions toward the bay. The Mackenzie and its tributaries in the northwest show a gentle slope to the north. The Great lakes system, which includes the St. Lawrence, shows that here the continent slopes toward the east and northeast.

Possibly the Mississippi drainage basin tells the most interesting story of all the great drainage areas. From the Appalachians on the east, the land slopes slightly downward to the southwest. From the Western highlands the downward slope is toward the southeast. These two slopes meet at the Mississippi. Here the slope of the land is to the south. 

The courses of the North American rivers also show that the two main divides, or watersheds, of the continent are the Appalachians and the Western highlands. 

The countries of North America are Alaska, Canada, Newfoundland and Labrador, the 

United States, Mexico, and the

Central American countries of NORTH 

Guatemala, Honduras, Salvador, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Panama,

AMERICAN COUNTRIES 

and British Honduras. To the northeast of the continent is the immense, high island of Greenland. It is largely within the Arctic circle, and is always covered with ice except along narrow coastal strips. To the southeast are the West Indies. The Bermudas are off the eastern coast.

HOW NATURE AFFECTS CLIMATE 

Nature has made great differences in the climate of different sections of North America. 

How latitude affects cli mate is seen by contrasting the hot Central American jungles with the cold tundras of northern 

Canada. How climate is affected by altitude is shown in the surprising difference between the jungle coastal lowland of Mexico and the delightful climate of the southern part of the 

Mexican plateau.

Winds, too, have a great effect on climate. 

Some of them bring rain, such as those which blow from the Atlantic ocean across the West

Indies. Some dry up the land, like those which come over the western mountains and blow across the highland areas and the great plains. Some warm the land, like those which blow over the warm Japan current. Some make the land cooler, like the local winds which sometimes blow across New England from the cold Labrador current. 

Mountains sometimes shut out cold winds and make the lands behind them warmer. And sometimes they shut out warm winds and make the sheltered lands cooler. 

Ocean currents also have much to do with the climate of North America. We might think that Newfoundland and the Willamette-Puget

Sound valley have the same temperature, since they are in the same latitude. But we know that the Pacific coast valleys are warmed by 159

NORTH AMERICA 

the Japan current, and that Newfoundland is made very cold and dreary by the icy Labrador current. 

As Long island and Lincoln, Nebraska, are in the same latitude we might expect them to have about the same length of growing season, if we did not know that large bodies of water make the climate of nearby lands much milder. Lincoln has a temperature averaging nearly 30° hotter than Long island in summer, about 20° colder in winter, and a growing season over a month shorter.

The seacoasts of the continent have many fine harbors and fishing grounds.

Coastal plains are crop-raising centers. Along the Fall line are many manufacturing cities connected by railroads with the seaports and inland cities.

The Great plains, sloping gently upward to the west, are the great stock-raising section of 

North America. Alfalfa is grown as a forage

The high, rugged 

Western highlands of the United States and Canada contain much hidden mineral wealth, and here are many busy mining regions. 

Here, too, many dry valleys have blossomed under irrigation. On the western side of the mountains are forest-covered mountain ranges of the Appalachian highland have much mining and some manufacturing and grazing. The forested, plateau-like Laurentian highland has lumbering and trapping as its chief industries. 

The fertile, temperate Great lakes region produces fruits.

This map shows the distribution of population in North America. 

In what part of the continent is the densest population? Can you state any reasons for this density? In what parts of the United 

States is the population slight? Why?

The low, rolling Central plains are the grainproducing and stock-fattening regions of the continent. Here we find industries growing out of the products of the region flour mill- 

heavy forests that are being cut carefully to avoid waste, and many fertile, well-watered valleys which produce a wide variety of fruits and vegetables because of the wide range of latitude. 

Mexico's high plateau yields a wealth of silver and gold, and has some manufactures. The flat, coastal lowlands produce sisal and petroleum. Tropical Central

America and the West Indies produce tropical crops. Nature has given North America many of her treasures.

WONDERS OF SCENERY 

Some of the loveliest scenery of the world is found on our continent, and some of the strangest. Nowhere are there more majestic falls than

THE NORTH AMERICAN CONTINENT AS A WHOLE 

161 Niagara falls. Some of the strange, seemingly endless limestone caves and caverns of east-central United States are visited by people from all parts of the world. With its hot springs and gushing geysers, Yellowstone

National park has a wild beauty that has no equal. Crater lake in a dead volcano, and the mile-deep Colorado canyon with its strangely colored walls, each has a claim to rare beauty. 

The snow-topped Canadian Rockies, their lower slopes covered with stately Douglas fir trees, offer some of the finest mountain scenery in the world. And all over the continent, hidden away from the regular routes of travel, are beauty spots that are dearly loved by the people who have chanced upon them.

The greatest population of the continent is near great commercial centers of the eastern, POPULATION AND TRANSPORTATION 

western, and southern coasts, and along the great arteries of trade. The least population is found where conditions of soil, climate, and communication make life difficult.

Transportation and population are closely related. Where people are drawn by right conditions of climate and soil or some special natural resource, there railroads follow, and, where there are means of transportation, settlements spring up. So, on our continent, as on others, there are most railroads through the richest farming lands, and our largest cities have grown up where easy transportation leads to trade and commerce. The poorest transportation is in thinly populated regions, or sections lacking developed resources. 

Today airlines are providing a new and speedy means of carrying passengers and mail between important cities of the United States and between those of other countries of the Americas. 

What a wonderful continent it is this 

North America of ours! Only in the cold, wild stretches of the north do we find much waste land, and even this has excellent promise, because of the variety of its resources. There is plenty of room for growth of population in most of the countries of North America, and there are resources sufficient to bring a comfortable living to one and all.

THE BACKBONE OF A CONTINENT 

2 

(1) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. (2) Pan American Union. 

1. Peaks of the high Andes. No other continent has so great a mountain backbone as South America. The 

Andes mountains extend from the northern end of the continent to its southern tip. They are higher than the 

Western highlands of North America. Even the few passes by which they can be crossed are higher than most of the peaks of the Rocky mountains. There is snow on the high Andes all the year round, even at the Equator. The northeast and southeast trade winds blow over the part of South America which is north of the Tropic of Capricorn. They lose their moisture on the eastern slopes of the Andes, leaving dry deserts on the west coast. South of the Tropic of Capricorn the land is in the belt of the westerlies. They bring rain to the 

Andes' western slopes and leave dry desert lands to the east. 

2. The southern end of the Andes mountains, seen from the Strait of Magellan. At this end of South America the high mountains dip down to the sea, and an arm of the ocean, the Strait of Magellan, runs through their valleys to connect the Atlantic and Pacific oceans. This strait is narrow and rocky, and mist, fogs, and icebergs make passage through it difficult. Before the opening of the Panama canal the strait was used by ships traveling between Europe and the west coast of South America.

NATURAL REGIONS OF SOUTH AMERICA 

3 4 5 (3) Pan American Union. (4) (5) By Ewing Galloway, N. Y. (6) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. (7) Copyright, E. M. Newman from 

Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. 

3. A road in the Brazilian highlands. These highlands are old, worn-down mountains, covered with woodlands, lying near the Atlantic coast. Many difficult problems must be met in building mountain roads like this one. 

4. Among the Guiana highlands. On small farms like this in the foothills of the Guiana highlands in Venezuela the people raise beans, corn, and other crops for their own use. There is plentiful rainfall and the soil is rich and fertile. 

5. A scene in the West Coast desert of Chile. This part of Chile lies in the belt of the moist southeast trade winds. But the Andes mountains rob the winds of their moisture before they reach Chile, and this region has no rain to make plants grow. The riches of the Chilean desert are minerals. Here copper is mined. Nitrate and borax are other mineral products. 

6. On the Pampas of Argentina. On these fertile grasslands large herds of cattle and sheep find rich pasture. 

The flatness of the Pampas makes herding and cropraising easy. This wagon load of wool is on its way to market. 

7. In the rain forests of the Amazon basin. These forests are drenched with the rain of the trade winds and warmed by the equatorial sun. They are crowded with trees, bushes, and plants of every kind, and are so dense that to follow the streams is almost the only means of getting through them.

NATURAL REGIONS OF SOUTH AMERICA 

South America is, in some ways, very like our own North America. The two continents

COMPARISON OF THE AMERICAS 

resemble one another in shape. 

Both are wide in the north and taper to a point in their southern parts. 

Find the chief natural regions of South

America on the map on the opposite page. See how, as in North America, the principal highland region is along the western coast. 

The mountains of this region are the Andes. 

Between the ranges you will find plateaus, deserts, and fertile valleys similar to those of the Western highlands of the United States. 

Like North America, South America has ranges of old worn-down mountains in the east and north. Along the eastern coast are the Brazilian highlands, which remind one of the Appalachians. In the north are the Guiana highlands, which correspond to the Laurentian highland in North America.

Through South America's central part run the lowland areas. These are the Orinoco river basin; the vast Amazon basin, drained by the mighty Amazon river and its tributaries; the smaller Plata drainage area, drained by the Plata river and its tributaries, the Parana and Paraguay rivers; and the Pampas. What are the central lowland regions of North America? 

In some ways North and South America are very different. The coast line of South America is regular and has far fewer harbors than the broken North American coast. Then, too, the continents differ in climate. About two thirds of South America is in the Torrid zone, while North America is largely in the North 

Temperate zone. The southern part of South 

America, lying in the South Temperate zone, has seasons just the opposite of ours. When we are having winter, it is summer there. 

Imagine celebrating Christmas day in the summertime! 

South America lies in three world wind

WINDS 

belts. If you will look at the wind map on page 4, you will notice that the part of the continent north of the Equator is in the path of the northeast trade winds. 

The southeast trade winds sweep the section between the Equator and the Tropic of Capricorn. The section south of the Tropic of 

Capricorn lies in the southern belt of the prevailing westerlies. As we study the different regions we shall see what effects these winds have on the countries over which they blow.

The Brazilian highland ranges lie close to the eastern coast, with only a narrow coastal plain separating them from the sea. 

To the west is the 

Central plateau, which is really a part of this highland PLATEAU region. This level, high pla- 

BRAZILIAN HIGHLANDS AND CENTRAL 

teau is largely grazing lands like the Great plains of North America.

North of the Central plateau and extending almost the width of South America is the vast tropical Amazon basin, crossed

AND ORINOCO 

by the Equator. This is the AMAZON BASIN largest lowland area in South BASIN 

America. The land slopes gently to the east. The many rivers which flow through this region form a great inland waterway, the Amazon river system. It extends more than three thousand miles across the continent. Some of the tributaries are a thousand miles long.

In the Amazon basin are rain forests, hot, wet jungles, so dense that men can penetrate them only with difficulty. Paths have to be cut through the forests, and these are overgrown again within a few weeks by thick vines and swift-growing tree growth. Much of this region is unexplored and is inhabited only by a few small tribes of Indians. 

The Orinoco basin to the north, though smaller in area, is much like the Amazon. It, too, is a tropical jungle region, through which 163

AREA AND THE PAMPAS 

Bordering the Brazilian highlands and Central plateau on the southwest is a lowland area which stretches south

PLATA DRAINAGE almost to the tip of the con tinent and west to to the 

Andes. It includes several regions which differ greatly from each other. 

The northern part of this area is the Plata drainage region, consisting of forests and grasslands. Within it lies the Gran Chaco, a swampy forested region. To the south are the wide grassy plains called the Pampas. 

Most of the rivers of the Plata drainage system are wide and deep, and are navigable far inland. They provide the only easy means of travel to the Gran Chaco and the northern part of the Pampas.

The Andes mountains are a continuation of the Western highlands of North America, and follow the western coast of South

ANDES 

MOUNTAINS America the entire length of the continent in an unbroken chain.

In most places mountains rise abruptly from the shore. The narrow Pacific coastal plain in the north, the West Coast desert, and the fertile Central valley to the south are the only lowlands of this region. Between the mountain ranges are high plateaus, deserts, and some fertile valleys. The largest and bestknown plateau is the plateau of Bolivia.

The Andes are the highest mountains in the 

Western hemisphere and are a great barrier to transportation and to rainfall. Throughout the region north of the Tropic of Capricorn the moisture-bearing winds come from the east. Their moisture falls on the eastern side of the Andes, leaving deserts along the west I SHOULD 

1. The natural regions of South America lie in much the same position as those of North America, but their different latitudes make the two continents quite different in character. 

2. South America is in three wind belts-the northeast and southeast trade wind belts and the southern belt of the prevailing westerlies. 

3. The chief highland regions of South America are the Andes mountains and the Guiana and Brazilian highlands. The chief lowland regions are the ern coast of the continent. The land south of the Tropic of Capricorn is in the southern belt of prevailing westerlies. Here the rain falls on the western slopes of the Andes, and there are deserts and dry grasslands east of these mountain ranges.

On the whole, the coast line of South 

America is very regular. Good harbors are almost entirely lacking on the west 

COAST 

ern coast. A few estuaries and pro- LINE tected bays provide harbors on the eastern and northern coasts, but even here the coast is far less broken than that of North America.

COUNTRIES OF 

Like North America, South America is divided into countries. Find the thirteen countries of South America on the map on page 168, and SOUTH AMERICA learn their names. You will notice that these countries vary greatly in size and shape. Compare huge Brazil with tiny French Guiana. Have you ever seen such an oddly-shaped country as Chile? Can you tell why it is so very narrow? Except for the Guianas, which are colonies of Great

Britain, the Netherlands, and France, all of the countries of South America are republics, and so are self-governing. 

The tropical regions of South America raise many products which our own cooler country needs but cannot raise. We

HOW NORTH AND HELP EACH OTHER SOUTH AMERICA 

also need many products of her temperate countries. 

South America does little manufacturing and therefore needs a large variety of manufactured goods. Many of these she buys from North America. Therefore the two continents are coming to depend more and more upon each other.

Amazon and Orinoco basins, the Plata drainage area, and the Pampas. 

4. Because of their height, the Andes mountains cause many deserts. In the north, where the winds come from the east, there are deserts along the west coast. Farther south, in the belt of prevailing westerlies, there are deserts east of the mountains. 

5. Two great inland waterways of South America are the Amazon and Plata river systems.

7. South America is divided into thirteen countries. 

All but the Guianas are republics.

SOME INDUSTRIES OF BRAZIL AND HER CHIEF SEAPORT 

5 

1. A Brazilian coffee plantation. Brazil supplies more than half of all the coffee which the world uses. This is one of the many estates on which coffee is grown in Brazil. The large, flat enclosures in the picture are the drying yards or floors needed in preparing the coffee. 

The workmen and their families all live on the plantation. 

2. Drying coffee near the city of Sao Paulo. The coffee beans are spread out thinly on level floors to dry in the sun. The men are stirring the beans with wooden rakes so that they will dry evenly on all sides. 

3. Tapping a rubber tree in the Amazon valley. Cuts have been made in the bark of the rubber tree with a sharp knife. A cup is placed just under each cut to catch the latex, or milky juice. The latex of the rubber tree gives us our rubber. 

4. Freshly cut rubber. Latex from the forest is dried layer after layer, on a pole hung over a fire. When a mass about as big as a ham has been formed, it is cut off the pole in lumps such as we see here ready to be shipped away and made into finished rubber. 

5. Rio de Janeiro, the beautiful gateway to Brazil. This view shows us a part of Rio's splendid harbor and the famous boulevard which extends for twenty miles along its shore. See how close the mountains are to the water's edge.

LIFE IN THE AMAZON BASIN 

9 (6) (7) Keystone-Underwood, N. Y. (8) (9) By Ewing Galloway, N. Y. 

6. In the jungle of the Amazon basin. These people poling their flat-bottomed boat slowly along the stream see trees in great variety, covered with climbing plants. 

If they are very still, they may see alligators basking at the water's edge, brilliant birds in the trees, or monkeys swinging from branch to branch. 

7. A mother tapir and her baby. The young tapir is spotted and striped, but it will be like its mother in color when it is older. Notice the mother's bulky form, short ears, and projecting nose forming a sort of trunk. 

The tapir is a shy and rather gentle beast, living in the deep forests and seeking refuge in the streams from danger. 

8. An anteater, one of the strangest of animals. Its hair is dark gray, and its eyes and ears are very small. 

Its long, sticky, string-like tongue is used to catch ants after the anteater's sharp front claws have broken into their ant hills. 

9. A small rest house on a lonely ranch near the mouth of the Amazon. Imagine, if you can, how hot this place must be, just south of the Equator. In northern Brazil, life is little touched by modern methods, and things are still done in a primitive way. These oxen are drawing a load of produce in the two-wheeled cart from a distant part of the ranch to the central ranch buildings. Men and oxen have paused for a rest.

SIZE AND SHAPE  What sort of country is South America's great republic of Brazil? It is one of the largest countries of the world, larger even than our own United States. 

It includes more than half of the entire continent of South America, and spreads out its great length and breadth in such a manner that it touches all but two of the other South American countries. Although Brazil is so large, its seacoast of about five thousand miles is less than half as long as that of the United States. Brazil's coast is much more regular than our northern Atlantic coast line, but it has a number of good harbors.

More than one half of Brazil is made up of lowlands, drained by the great river systems of the Amazon and the Plata. The SURFACE highlands occupy the rest of the country, except for the narrow coastal plain. 

The far northern part of Brazil reaches into the Guiana highlands. 

Brazil has vast stretches of swamps, jungles, grasslands, and waterways practically unknown except to the native Indians. There are people who say that these lands can never be made useful, but the next fifty or one hundred years may tell a different story. More and more, explorers are entering the unknown regions and are learning what can be done with them.

Like our own country, Brazil is a republic. 

Her land is divided into states, and is governed by a native-born Presi- 

GOVERNMENT dent elected every four years, and by a representative assembly much like our Congress. The Portuguese first settled this part of South America, and Portuguese is the official language of Brazil. 

Your first impression of Brazil will be one of beauty if you enter the country by way of its seaport capital, Rio de Janeiro.

JANEIRO Just north of the Tropic of Capri corn your steamer turns towards a

RIO DE 

coast backed by forest-covered mountains. As you draw near, you see a narrow opening that looks very much like the mouth of a river, guarded on either side by towering rocks. 

The great, bare granite cone on one side is called Sugar Loaf mountain. The lofty cliff on the other side is the Hunchback. Your steamer passes between the two and you find yourself in an almost landlocked harbor. 

What a great and beautiful harbor! It is dotted with many lovely little islands. The coast line curves around it in a great crescent. 

The mountainous skyline curves and dips, and mounts up again in jagged peaks. In places the mountains, forested and green, extend down to the water's very edge.

This harbor of Rio de Janeiro, sheltered by the bordering mountains, is a port for vessels from all over the world. There is space enough for great numbers of the largest ships, and the water is so deep that even the hugest ocean liners can easily enter and sail right up to the city's docks. There are few harbors in the world as fine as that of Rio de Janeiro. 

As your ship steams across the harbor, you see the city spread along the crescent coast line. Part of it nestles among the mountain spurs that extend down to the shore, and part climbs up the mountain slopes. Perched high on these slopes you see the summer homes of the city's rich and famous people. Stretched along the harbor and the ocean shore gleam the beaches, the playgrounds of Rio de 

Janeiro. The city's boulevards are fringed by palms. The beautiful government buildings add to the charm of this lovely city that is known in every land as Rio.

Besides being the capital of Brazil, beautiful Rio de Janeiro is also a business and manufacturing center, and a commercial port with the whole world for its market. Its wharves are busy with arriving and departing ships. 

Lining the broad streets are shops selling the country's own goods and those brought from 167

abroad by the great vessels. The smoking chimneys of the factory districts tell us that the city has many industries. 

Industries. Rio has its own textile mills.

Much of the raw material for them comes from the cotton fields and sheep-grazing grasslands of southern Brazil. The grain for her flour mills comes from the upland temperate regions. The sugar plantations to the west and up the coast send their product to her sugar refineries. The forested mountain slopes furnish the timber for her furniture factories and various other purposes. The hides for her shoe factories come from the grazing lands of southern Brazil and from the vast grassy plains of the central highlands. These two regions also send cattle to her slaughter houses and meat-packing plants. The products of these industries are sold in the shops of Rio, are exported to other countries, or are sent inland by rail. 

Commerce. Rio has many natural advantages that aid its commerce. It is one of the few large railroad centers in Brazil. It is in the more temperate southern part of the country, where there are more people and more industries than farther north. Then, too, its unusual position about midway on South 

America's Atlantic coast encourages trade. If you will look on the globe you will notice that 

South America lies much farther to the east than North America. A line drawn from Rio to London is about the same length as a line drawn from Rio to New York. Rio is, therefore, in just as close contact with ports of western Europe as she is with ports of our northern Atlantic coast, and so enjoys extensive trade with both continents.

Rio exports many products. From the rich highland region beyond the city comes coffee, the leading export of Brazil. These coffee lands produce about two thirds of the world's supply, and a large part of this coffee is sent to our own United States. Other products of this highland region, which come to Rio to be shipped, are cotton, hides, meat, raw sugar, bananas, pineapples, oranges, cacao which is made into chocolate and cocoa, and manganese, a mineral used in making steel. From the grazing lands of southern and central Brazil, meat and hides are sent to Rio for export. 

To the towns and the plantations beyond the mountains, and to the smaller nearby ports where ocean steamers do not stop, Rio sends by rail or boat many of the imports which come to her harbor. These include wheat and flour from neighboring Argentina, for Brazil does not grow enough grain to feed her people.

Machinery and iron and steel goods are also imported from the United States to be used in the factories and on the plantations. A large part of Brazil's imports consists of cotton, woolen, and silk textiles. And still other imports are hardware, furniture, crockery, glassware, drugs, oil, lumber, and coal.

Population. Rio de Janeiro has a popula tion of about two million. That is nearly a third of the population of New York City, and about three-fifths of that of Chicago. Rio has as many people as Philadelphia, a third more than Detroit, and almost four times as many as Buffalo. It would rank as a great city in any nation in the world.

BRAZILIAN HIGHLANDS 

The Brazilian highlands occupy much of eastern and southern Brazil. In the east, near the seacoast, is a steep range of mountains running northeast and southwest. On your map, page 

164, find for how much of their length they are bordered by a narrow coastal plain. For the remainder of their length they slope sharply to the sea. Farther inland from the coast mountains are other parallel ranges, not quite so high. Still farther west is the Central plateau, from 1500 to 3000 feet in height.

The São Francisco river rises in the southern part of the highlands, flows north, and then turns east through the mountains. The plateau, too, is cut by river valleys and gorges, the northerly flow of most of the rivers showing that the slope of the plateau is in that direction. In some of these Brazilian rivers are high waterfalls, but so far very little of the available water power has been used.

Climate. The climate of the different sections of the Brazilian highlands is determined by the prevailing winds and the mountain NASA-11

SOUTH AMERICA 

ranges. 

Because the southeast trade winds blow across this region, rainfall is abundant on the eastern slopes of the mountains and the coastal plain, and the climate is both hot and moist. The mountains shut off the rainfall to a great extent from the plateau to the west, and the climate there is hot and fairly dry. Around the São Francisco river droughts are common and irrigation is carried on. The seaport of Recife, or Pernambuco, depends to a great extent upon the products of irrigated land for its exports.

As the southern part of the Brazilian highlands lies in the Temperate zone, the climate there is somewhat cooler. Though the lowland cities are hot and moist, there are many pleasant suburbs on the nearby mountain slopes. Just behind these mountains, on the plateau, the weather is often cool. 

Products. As the lands in the northern section of the Brazilian highlands get little rain they are poor for agriculture and are sparsely settled. In the east the highlands are rich in minerals, and, because they have much rain and the soil is good, they are suited to growing crops. More people live in this part of the highlands than in any other section. 

Toward the west are grasslands which are used as pasture for cattle. In the southern part of the highland region are large forests and in the southeast is the rich red soil which the coffee tree demands if it is to grow and produce much coffee.

Like the coffee lands on the slopes of the 

Mexican and Central American mountains, Brazil's coffee land has a warm but not hot climate, and the rainfall is abundant during the growing season. The rich, well-drained soil of this region, and the dry harvest period between June and September, when the coffee berries may be left outdoors to dry, are conditions which fit southeastern Brazil to be the greatest coffee-raising section in the world. 

In traveling through the coffee country you would probably mistake some of the great plantations for villages. Often there are several thousand people working and living on a single plantation. They have their houses, their streets, and their gardens. On the plantation is a store, a blacksmith shop, a school, and usually a church. If the plantation is big enough there may be a motion picture house. 

The plantation owner has great drying yards, the latest farming machines, and per- 

COUNTRIES OF THE NEW WORLD WHICH LEAD IN EXPORTING COFFEE 

BRAZIL 

100 

COLOMBIA VENEZUELA SALVADOR ALL OTHER COUNTRIES 

200 300 400 500 This graph shows the countries of the New World which lead in exporting coffee. Each unit of the graph scale represents one hundred million pounds of coffee. Does Brazil export more or less coffee than all the rest of the New World? haps even a private railroad for carrying his coffee to market. The owner of a small farm with a small coffee crop cannot afford to buy all these things and without them he cannot compete with the plantation owner, so the wealthy plantation owners rule the coffee industry.

In October, which is early spring south of the Equator, blossoms appear on the coffee trees. Soon these wither, drop off, and are replaced by green coffee berries. The berries ripen slowly and it is May, late in the fall, before they are ready for picking. Even when they are picked there is much work to be done. 

Each berry contains two seeds, or coffee beans. 

The red outside pulp of the berries is removed by soaking, leaving the coffee beans inclosed in two layers of skin. Again they are soaked, then laid out to dry in the sun. During the drying period, which takes from two to three weeks, the berries are turned by stirring them with rakes so that they will dry thoroughly. 

At last they are put through a machine which removes the skins. The beans are then sorted according to size, done up in sacks, and shipped. They must be roasted and ground before they are ready for the coffee pot.

Although coffee is the chief product of the Brazilian highlands, it is by no means the only one. The plantation owners of the coffee lands are beginning to feel that it is dangerous to depend so completely upon a single crop, and are turning many of their acres to the growing of cotton, sugar, and tobacco. Agriculture is becoming more varied in all those Brazilian states which lie in the southern highland region. Manioc, also called cassava, grows there. From it tapioca is made, and also a kind of flour much used by the natives. 

Yerba maté is another product. It is made from the leaves of an evergreen tree and is known as Paraguayan tea. Beans are raised in the central as well as in the southern highland states. Corn is the principal food crop, as in all Brazil. Tobacco is grown throughout the highland region east of the São Francisco river and is among the principal Brazilian exports. Horses and cattle graze in many parts of the highlands. Mules and asses are raised and are used to draw great wagons. In the northern part of the Brazilian highlands are large herds of goats.

Hidden in the mountains of the Brazilian highlands are great mineral resources. Brazilian diamonds, of course, are well known throughout the world, and the diamond mines have been worked for more than two hundred years. There is also gold in these mountains, and iron, and manganese. There are small coal deposits, too, but the coal is of a poor grade. Because the coal cannot be used to smelt iron ore for steel manufacture, the coal and iron mines have been little developed. 

Not much coal nor iron is exported because of the difficulties of transportation and also because other nations have better sources of supply nearer at hand. To make mining a profitable industry in Brazil more people are needed, including both laborers and skilled workmen, and also more railroads to bring mining machinery to the mines and to carry away the ores. 

São Paulo. With the growth of the coffee industry the inland city of São Paulo has come to be prosperous. Perhaps you have noticed that when an industry flourishes there are always one or more cities which grow and prosper with it. São Paulo with a population of 1,000,000 is a city that ranks in size between Cleveland and Los Angeles, but, unlike these two, it has no harbor to aid its growth. It started as a distributing center, selling supplies to the plantation owners, and sending their coffee down to the coastal cities for export. Although this city ships some coffee to 

Rio de Janeiro, the coffee industry has become so vast that São Paulo now has its own port of Santos. And Santos, with 110,000 people, exports more coffee than any other port in the world.

The coffee city of São Paulo is beginning to look much like an American manufacturing city, with its factories and its hordes of workers. The making of shoes is growing very fast in South America, because there are many cattle to supply hides, and São Paulo has many shoe factories. There are cotton mills here, also, and iron and steel works. This city has furniture and glass factories, too. As these industries grow, Brazil will not need to import these products in such large quantities. 

In spite of its busy industries, São Paulo has the charming, leisurely air of most South 

American cities. Its streets are wide and lined with tropical trees. There are beautiful homes, many of which belong to the rich plantation owners who prefer to live in the city. Most of these plantation owners are 

Portuguese or of Portuguese descent, and their homes are modeled after the houses of Portugal. 

Plateau Grasslands. In the western part of the Brazilian highlands, on the great Central plateau, we find a region very much like our Great plains, with vast, rolling, grassy areas. 

Immense herds of cattle graze in this region, and there are many large ranches. During the World War much of the beef that was shipped to the armies in France came from these plateau plains of Brazil. Meat and hides and other cattle products are now exported from Brazil in large quantities.

Still, the plateau region is so poorly developed that you might travel for hundreds of miles without seeing a city or a railroad. Only

SOUTH AMERICA 

enough agriculture is carried on to supply the people living there. Cereals are grown, and some of the land is devoted to cotton, tobacco, and sugar. The soil, well-suited to farming, has attracted Italian and German immigrants, who have settled on the plateau and are introducing new methods of agriculture. Where

As the population of Brazil grows, there will be plenty of use for these thousands of acres that are now all too idle.

As compared with the vast tropical area, the temperate section of Brazil seems very small, yet it is almost as large a region as the state of Texas.

TEMPERATE AREA 

This temperate area includes the southern end of the Brazilian highlands and extends into a grassy lowland drained by the Plata river system. The greater part of the European settlers who have come to Brazil have chosen to live in this section because of its mild climate. Here they carry on mixed farming. There are great cattle and sheep ranches in the grassy lowland plains. 

The southern Brazilian highlands are rich in pine forests, from which very little timber is cut, as the needs of countries that use a great deal of lumber are met by forests nearer home. Rosewood grows a little farther north on the highlands. Much of it is exported from 

Rio de Janeiro. The trees of the highlands are more valuable than the trees of the thick Amazon jungles because they can be transported so much more easily.

THE COASTAL PLAIN 

Here the

As we have seen, east of the Brazilian highlands lies the narrow coastal plain. Although the low parts of this plain have many unhealthful swamps and lagoons, or marshy lakes, the land in many places rises from one to two hundred feet above sea level. possibilities for agriculture are fine, and in many sections the land has been laid out in rich plantations. Lumbering is also carried on. Many of the chief cities of Brazil are located along this coastal plain and are shipping ports for the products of the highlands to the west.

Some of the coastal cities were so afflicted in the past with epidemics of yellow fever, smallpox, and other diseases that for long periods of time no steamers would stop at them. Even Santos and Rio de Janeiro, though not on the coastal plain, used to have dark, narrow, ill-smelling streets, and diseasebreeding open sewers. Fevers carried away large numbers of people each year. All of these cities now owe many thanks to modern science, which has brought them knowledge, sanitation, and safety, and made them into healthful places in which to live. 

Most of the Brazilian negro population, which is quite large, lives on the coastal plain. 

The forefathers of these negroes were brought from Africa by the Portuguese to work as slaves on the plantations. They thrived in the

warm, moist climate, so much like their native Africa.

An important product of the coastal plantations is cacao, from which cocoa and chocolate are made. From the small cacao trees hang pods from six to eight inches long. Each of these pods contains from thirty to forty beans. 

When ripe, the pods are cut from the trees and groups of workers are kept busy shelling the beans. In order to remove the pulp which surrounds the beans and also to improve the flavor of the cacao, the beans are heated and then spread in the sun to dry. When all the bits of pulp have been removed and the beans have become brown and polished in appearance, they are packed in sacks and shipped away. Brazil supplies much of the cacao which the world uses.

THE 

AMAZON 

BASIN 

The seaport of São Salvador, or Bahia, exports cacao in large quantities in addition to its other exports of coffee, tobacco, hides, and sugar. The port of Recife, or Pernambuco, is a shipping center for sugar, cotton, hides, and cacao. Both of these ports export fruit. 

Across most of the northern part of Brazil stretches the Amazon basin. The Amazon river system, with its many tributaries, drains an area two thirds as large as the whole United States. 

Some of these tributaries are so large that in any other country they would be considered mighty rivers, but they are dwarfed beside the giant Amazon. This river is over 

3500 miles long. In some places it is only a mile or so across, but in other places it widens to fifty miles. During flood time, when water pours into the Amazon from its swelling tributaries, it rises halfway up the tree trunks and overflows many miles of land on either side.

The Amazon river rises in the Andes mountains. After crossing practically the entire breadth of South America, it flows into the

Atlantic ocean at the Equator with such force that for more than a hundred miles from its mouth the ocean water is muddy. The country through which the Amazon flows is flat and level so that the river is navigable by small ocean steamers as far as Iquitos, a distance of 2300 miles from the coast. Many of its tributaries are also navigable, but some of them flow through jungle regions where the natives are so savage that it is dangerous for white men to go there. 

Climate. Though not all of the Amazon basin is a jungle, thousands of miles on both sides of the river are covered with dense, tangled forest growth, known as "rain forests." The intense heat and the abundant rainfall account for this jungle growth. Near the

Equator the heat is intense all the year round. 

Here there are two kinds of season, the seasons of extremely heavy rains, which come during our spring and fall, and the seasons of less heavy rains, which come during our summer and winter. These are called the wet and dry seasons. Although the seasons of less heavy rains are known as the dry seasons, we would consider the rainfall very heavy, even when compared with that in the rainiest parts of our country.

There is a reason for all this rainfall. In this torrid region of intense heat the northeast and southeast trade winds meet. Here, you remember, is the belt of calms, or doldrums, where the sun's rays beat down from overhead. In this belt of calms the air, heated by the sun's direct rays, warms, expands, and rises. As the air rises, it cools and drops its moisture, causing constant, heavy rains. At different times of the year, the sun is directly overhead in different parts of the Torrid zone. 

The center of the rainy belt, therefore, shifts with the sun north and south of the Equator, crossing the Equator twice each year. This accounts for the two seasons of extremely heavy rains and the two seasons of less heavy rains in the equatorial Amazon basin. 

Savannas. Though the sections of the Amazon basin which lie farthest from the Equator have heavy rainfall when the rainy belt reaches them, they have little rain at other times. They may be said to have a truly wet season and a truly dry season. These regions are mostly grasslands, with some forest growth and other vegetation. They are called savannas. During the rainy season the grass of the savannas flourishes, but in the dry season it withers and dies. Because of

SOUTH AMERICA 

this, and because of the many insects whose bites cause sickness and death, the savannas are little used for grazing.

The Jungle. The forests of North America are so open that one may walk through them.

But to get through the forests of the Amazon basin one must cut his way. The matted jungle growth comes down to the very banks of the rivers, and a traveler up one of the narrower tributary streams feels as if he were living in an eternal twilight. The jungle growth meets overhead and presses in on all sides. It shuts out the sunlight, and for mile after mile the river seems to flow through a huge darkened cave. The air is not cool here, as it is in our forests, but is hot, damp, and sticky.

The region is unhealthful, too. It breeds fever. Its buzzing mosquitoes carry dreadful diseases to those they bite. The most beautiful plants are often poisonous to the touch. In the each other, so that there is continual decay as well as continual growth.

Yet there is beauty in the jungle. It is found in the swift flight of gloriously colored birds, in the rainbow hues of the flowers, and in the deep, quiet streams banked by forests. 

Many an artist would find inspiration in the wilds of the Amazon basin.
