How do you account for this slight rainfall? jungles there are dangerous animals, and snakes, some poisonous and some so very large and strong that they can easily crush animals of considerable size.

In this climate, always hot and moist, trees and vines and vegetation of all sorts grow wildly in a tangled mass. Plants grow continually and are green all year round. The matted roots and twisting branches choke 

Both river and jungle have a wealth of animal life. There are fish of many kinds in the Amazon and its tributaries. Huge scaly alligators lie along the banks, or, swimming beneath the water, stalk their prey as it comes to the river to drink. Great cat-like jaguars and pumas hunt the peccary, a kind of wild hog, and the tapir, which looks like a small rhinocerous without a horn on its nose. 

Troops of monkeys play in the tree tops or flee screaming from their enemy, the boa constrictor, the largest of South American snakes. Sloths, resembling small bears with coarse greenish-brown fur, climb clumsily from vine to vine and branch to branch, never touching the ground, hanging always head downward.

Giant anteaters thrive on the crawling insect life of the jungle. By day and by night the jungle rings with the many voices of its animal inhabitants. 

Rubber. Native Indians live in the interior of the Amazon lowlands and along the river

banks. Some of them have crude houseboats. 

Others live in huts, which they build high on piles, or stilts, in order to be above the water at flood time. These natives collect the sap from the rubber trees which grow wild in the jungle. They prepare the sap for market, and take it down to the little river ports to which the rubber traders come. From these ports it is shipped down the Amazon. 

The rubber sap is a creamy-white liquid called latex. The season for gathering it is from November to May. To get the sap, the native cuts gashes in the bark of one rubber tree after another, and hangs a cup under each gash. When the cups are full he empties them into a bucket which he carries to a clearing. Here a fire is built. On either side of the fire stands a post with a forked top. In the forked ends of the posts a pole is laid so that it can be turned over and over in the smoke from the fire.

On this pole the rubber gatherer pours some rubber tree sap, turning the pole as he pours. In the heat and smoke from the fire the sap hardens. Then more sap is poured over it and turned in the smoke and heat to harden. By adding more and more sap the native finally has a great mass, called a rubber ham, which he takes from the pole, ready to be carried to market.

At one time Brazil led the world in the production of rubber, but now the countries of southern Asia have taken the lead. There large plantations of rubber trees have been set out, and the native workmen can travel from tree to tree and gather the latex much more. quickly and easily than the Brazilian workmen can go from tree to tree in the matted 

Amazon jungle. Though Brazil still exports enormous quantities of rubber, the amount is not nearly so great as that exported from southern Asia. 

Attempts have been made by white men to establish rubber plantations in the Brazilian jungle and to cultivate the rubber trees, but the heat and the fever and the dangers of the jungle have caused most of these attempts to fail. On the plantations that have succeeded rubber is cultivated, and cacao and other products are grown. If these plantations could be extended and white people could be found who were willing and able to live on them and cultivate rubber systematically, Brazil could increase her revenue from this product by many thousands of dollars. From all over the world there is a steady demand for rubber. 

With the coming of the automobile with its rubber tires, this demand has grown greater year by year, and the automobile tire is only one of the ever-increasing number of products made from rubber.

Most of the successful rubber plantations have been established along the lower Amazon near the Atlantic coast. Belem, or Para, the largest rubber port in South America, is in this region. Though Belem is a beautiful city, the climate is very hot, for the city is in one of the hottest regions of the world.

Near the point where the Rio Negro joins the Amazon river is a jungle city and rubber center, called Manaos. It stands on a little plateau where the air is somewhat better than in the lowlands. Manaos is a modern city and has electric lights, street cars, schools, a library, and a museum. A large part of its 

75,000 inhabitants are native Indians. 

Manaos, although one thousand miles from the sea, is practically a seaport. Great ocean liners from the United States and Europe can come up the mighty Amazon and load the rubber from the city's wharves. In addition to rubber, Manaos sends down the river cacao, dried fish, and Brazil nuts, for which there is a large demand in the United States. 

Other Products. Besides rubber trees, there are many other valuable trees in the jungle of Brazil, but they are so matted by all kinds of growth and are so far from any railroads or ports that the cost of getting them out is often more than they are worth. The cabinet woods and dyewoods are the most valuable. 

From the Amazon jungle are obtained also vanilla, sarsaparilla, and vegetable oils used in margarine, soap, and candles. There is a tree that yields turpentine, and near the coast a palm from which wax is secured.

In considering the Amazon river basin, you might well think back to your study of our

own great river, the Mississippi. The Missis 

COMPARISON Sippi basin has an area only half as large as that of the 

MISSISSIPPI Amazon. But while the Ama- BASINS 

zon area has but two or three million people, most of whom are jungle dwellers, the Mississippi area has almost fifty million inhabitants. Cities, towns, farms, and mines lie along each bank of the Mississippi. 

Along the Amazon is only the jungle, through which man has to fight every step of his way and where he has to endure heat, dampness, and fever. All of these obstacles help to make the real development of the Amazon basin look hopeless. But it is not safe to say that any region is hopeless, once man has decided that it must be developed. 

Interior transportation in Brazil is still terribly lacking. Southern Brazil, which is the

TRANSPORTATION 

most densely populated part of the country, has a number of railroad lines, and a few others run from the ports of Rio de Janeiro and Santos to the lands behind them and also up the coast. A few lines also run from other coastal cities into the interior, but there are not nearly enough to allow the country to develop as it should. Brazil's railroad system amounts to little when compared with the great network of rails stretching for thousands of miles the length and breadth of our own country.

Though there are few railroads, water transportation is very common in Brazil, for the country has at least 40,000 miles of navigable rivers. Motorboats and steamboats ply these waters, and seaplanes use them as landing fields. Water transportation is especially important in the rubber industry, for much of the rubber comes from regions that have never been visited by a train or a truck and probably will not be for many years to come. 

Reaching out from the larger Brazilian cities are a few modern highways, which make truck transportation possible as far as they extend. In the interior, beyond the region of good roads, pack mules and horses and some oxen are used.

The population of Brazil is about forty mil- 

POPULATION 

There is lion people. The native-born population is a very mixed one. There are many descendants of the early 

Portuguese and Spanish settlers. also a very large group, called mestizos, descended from marriages between these settlers and the Indians. To these two groups belong the wealthy and educated people of Brazil, in whose hands rest the government and the control of the industries. Then there are about eight or nine million native Indians in Brazil. 

They live largely in the interior and no census has ever been taken of them because no one could possibly find them all in order to count them. In some cases it would be dangerous to try it, for not all of them are friendly. Another class of native-born Brazilians are the negroes who live along the coast. They are descendants of slaves brought to Brazil many years ago. Still other native-born Brazilians are the descendants of immigrants, who in years past have come from Italy, Germany, Japan, and many other countries. They have opened up many of the agricultural and grazing lands, especially in southern Brazil.

There are possibly two million people in Brazil who are not native-born. These are the recent immigrants. For years the United

States has been considered the land of promise to people of crowded foreign countries, but many now feel that Brazil offers better opportunities. As these people reach 

Brazil they settle on the coastal plain, move into the interior, or remain in the cities where they find work in the factories. 

It is estimated that about three fourths of the people of Brazil can neither read nor write. There are large numbers of native Indians who have never laid eyes on a white man, although they may have heard of these men with white faces and queer clothes who have come up the river. Compare this with the 120,000,000 people in our own United

States, almost all of whom can read and write. 

Do you wonder that Brazil has not grown as we have?

Brazil needs men with families who will occupy her land and plan to make permanent homes there. She needs many agricultural

laborers to develop her soil to the utmost. And she must have trained men and industrial workers to help build up her industries, her cities, her highways, and her railroads.

As these people come, Brazil will expand, and the country will take her rightful place 

TRADE BETWEEN THE UNITED STATES AND BRAZIL 

among the great nations of the world. We hope that she will look to the 

United States for help because these two great nations are fitted by nature to help each other.

EXTENT OF THE TEMPERATE FARMING REGION 

Most of the great temperate farming region of South America lies in the southern part of the area drained by the 

Plata river system. You will remember that the southern part of Brazil is temperate lowland, well suited to agriculture and the raising of cattle and sheep. 

This lowland region extends far to the south and west of the Brazilian boundaries and includes the greater part of Argentina and all of Uruguay. It is this great region of fertile plains which brings wealth to the two countries.

On the map, page 168, find the long wedgeshaped country of Argentina and the small country of Uruguay which just seems to fill out the top of the wedge. Notice that Argentina extends from about 22° to about 56° south latitude. From north to south, then, Argentina is about 34°, or 2350 miles long.

The north of Argentina comes about 3° closer to the Equator than the southern tip of 

Florida, and between the southern tip of 

Argentina and the South Pole there are about 

7° less than between the long northern boundary of the United States and the North Pole.

ARGENTINA 

The Argentine republic has four great natural regions-the plateau of Patagonia, the arid border of the Andes, the 

POLITICAL DIVISIONS NATURAL AND Gran Chaco, and the Pampas. 

Politically, the country is divided into provinces and territories and is governed by a President and a Congress. The seat of government is the great city of Buenos Aires.

sheep-raising section, Tierra del Fuego, an island group separated from the South 

American mainland by the Strait of Magellan.

Tierra del Fuego consists of one large island and many small ones. The small islands are of little importance. Most of them belong to the republic of Chile and a few to Argentina. 

The mountainous western part of the large island also belongs to Chile, but most of the island's plains section is Argentine territory. 

The island mountains are a part of the Andes system, but they are not very high, and winds from the west sweep across them bringing sufficient rainfall to the plains beyond for the growing of grass. Great flocks of sheep are pastured on the plains of Tierra del Fuego, and as time goes on the flocks are increasing in size, and the wool industry is becoming greater and more prosperous.

ARID BORDER 

OF THE ANDES 

North of the plateau of Patagonia, where the Pampas meet the foothills of the Andes, there is a region much like the land east of our Coast ranges. It is called the arid border of the Andes. Here the soil is rich, but rainfall is too scant for farming because the high mountains to the west shut out the moist westerly winds. Yet, thanks to irrigation, here are some of the richest farms of 

Argentina. A few streams from the Andes flow through the region, and their waters are used to irrigate the dry soil. 

In the northern part of this border region, where it is warm, much sugar cane is grown. 

Sugar is refined in Tucuman, a city of more than 100,000. Farther south, around the city of 

Mendoza, is a region of small farms. Irrigation has made this a great grape-growing section and each farm has one or more vineyards. The grapes from these vineyards are used in making wine. In addition to grapes, such fruits as plums, peaches, and apricots are grown. All these fruits are shipped fresh to 

Buenos Aires in refrigerator cars. They arrive by refrigerator ships in the United States and England when the same fruits are out of season in those countries.

The northern part of Argentina contains part of the great region called the Gran Chaco, a huge low plain which was GRAN CHACO once the bed of a great lake. 

The Gran Chaco stretches west from the 

Parana and Paraguay rivers to the Andes mountains. To the north and east it extends beyond the borders of Argentina, and includes western Paraguay and part of Bolivia. To the south it merges into the broad plains of the Pampas.

The northern part of the Gran Chaco is a warm, dense, swampy jungle. Because of the great swamps swarming with snakes and insects, much of this forested section is still unexplored. South of the forests are grassy plains. Although these plains are dotted with many small lakes, the surface of the land is generally very dry.

Cotton. The part of the Chaco which lies in Argentina consists of both forest lands and grassy plains. Although the Chaco has fine alluvial soil, deep and free from stones, farming as yet is of little importance. This is because of the two-season climate. During the rainy season, which comes in summer, the land is too wet for most kinds of agriculture. 

During the dry season, in winter, it is too dry. 

Recent settlers have been raising some cotton on the grassy plains and each year it is becoming an increasingly important product. Cotton, as you may remember, requires a wet growing season and a dry harvesting season. 

Quebracho. The most important product of the Gran Chaco is found in the drier eastern sections of the forests. This product is the wood of the quebracho tree. The word quebracho means ax-breaker, and quebracho lumber is very hard indeed. There are several uses for this wood. Logs of it are much used in Argentina and other South American countries for fence posts and railroad ties. 

European countries, and especially Germany, import large quantities of these logs from Argentina.

Then, too, a great many quebracho logs are chipped up and boiled. The juices form a jelly-like substance, called quebracho extract, which is packed in bags, where it hardens. In this form it is shipped to many parts of the

SOUTH AMERICA 

world to be used in tanning hides to make leather. The extract preserves the hides, and makes them tougher and more waterproof. 

This product of far-away South America was probably used in the manufacture of the shoes you are wearing. Quebracho trees do not grow in the United States, so the leather industries of New England, New York, and other parts of our country have to depend on South America for their supply of this valuable extract.

THE PAMPAS 

The grasslands of the southern part of the Chaco gradually open into the great plains of the Pampas. How flat these plains appear, without a hill or a tree to break the even sweep of land! From

Buenos Aires they spread out like a great fan for hundreds of miles to the north, to the east, to the west, and to the south. They extend from Patagonia on the south to the Gran Chaco on the north, and from the Atlantic ocean on the east to the foothills of the Andes. 

Although seemingly level, the Pampas are like the Great plains of our own country. They lift very gradually to the mountain slopes to the west.

The plains of the Pampas are the most productive land in the temperate farming region of South America, for they are one of the great meat and grain-raising sections of the world. The soil is very rich, as in our Central plains. Because tractor plows and harvesting machines can often be run for miles without coming upon a rock or a tree, the Pampas plains are well-fitted for raising crops on a large scale. The mild climate makes it possible for the cattle to graze out-of-doors during all the year. The rainfall, which varies from ten inches in the west to forty inches in the east, is heavy enough for the growth of good grazing grass throughout the region, and for the raising of many kinds of grains. 

Crop-Growing Area. Where there is enough rainfall, vast quantities of wheat are raised. 

This wheat belt of Argentina curves from north of Rosario to Bahia Blanca. Much flax, from which linseed is obtained, is grown tarther east where rainfall is more plentiful. 

Here, too, is a fine corn belt. As few hogs are raised in Argentina, most of the corn is exported instead of being used to fatten the stock.

In the drier western part of the Pampas and in the sections where cattle are fattened for market, alfalfa is widely raised. 

As you learned in studying the Central and Great 

PRODUCTION OF WHEAT IN THE UNITED STATES, CANADA, AND ARGENTINA 

200 

UNITED STATES CANADA ARGENTINA

Argentina is the only great wheat country in 

South America. Compare the production of wheat in Argentina with that of the United States and 

Canada. Each unit of the graph scale represents two hundred million bushels of wheat. plains of the United States, this crop with its deep-reaching roots grows well in land too dry for grains. In some sections of Argentina, the soil and climate are so well suited to alfalfa that six or more crops can be cut in a season. 

Grazing. Everywhere on the Pampas, beef cattle and sheep are raised on great ranches. 

Dairy cattle are raised in the eastern part of the Pampas, and very fine butter and cheese are exported. As in the United States and 

Australia, the cattle are given the better grasslands. Sheep are able to graze where cattle cannot find enough on which to live. Both thrive on the Pampas, and beef, mutton, hides, and wool are important exports. The hides of the cattle, like the quebracho extract, are greatly needed by our country for the leather industry.

The vast herds of cattle and flocks of sheep raised on the Pampas and other regions of 

Argentina have made the

AND TRADE meat industry the most im- MEAT INDUSTRY 

portant business of the temperate farming region. Great quantities of meat and animal by-products are sent to all parts of the world.

Much of the beef and mutton exported from the temperate grazing area is bought at the ranches by great slaughtering and packing companies. In Argentina most of the slaugh-

tering and packing houses are located along the Plata river, in Patagonia, and at Bahia 

Blanca. In these slaughtering and packing houses, the finest cattle are slaughtered and the meat is cut up. This meat is chilled and is then carefully wrapped for shipping on refrigerator steamers, which keep the meat cool. The chilled beef is ready for sale as soon as it is unloaded. It commands a good price. 

Sheep and less choice cattle are slaughtered and the meat is frozen. Lower temperatures aboard ship keep it frozen on its long journey to market. This meat must be thawed before it can be eaten, and brings less money than chilled beef.

The meat of the poorest grades of cattle is dried. This is called "jerked beef." It is the least valuable of the beef products. It is sold in parts of the world where meat cannot be kept fresh.

In addition to their meat, sheep and cattle furnish many other products. The hides are dried and exported. Some of the bones are used to make buttons, knife handles, and other articles, and the rest of the bones are used for making glue and charcoal. Gelatine is one product of the hoofs of slaughtered animals. 

Fats are used for making butter substitutes, soaps, and tallow for candles. All of these products are exported. So you see that

Argentina's sheep and cattle bring her much wealth. The trade of Argentina is mostly with countries north of the Equator. Think what a long trip products must take to reach northern markets! But shipment by boat is the cheapest form of transportation, and the refrigerator ships keep the perishable products fresh.

Formerly all the best land of Argentina was divided into large estates or ranches, called estancias. Although some of the

ESTANCIAS 

estancias have been divided into small farms, there are still very few farmers in Argentina who own their lands, compared with the number who work on the estancias for a small share of the profits. 

These ranches cover many square miles. 

The landowner usually lives with his family in Buenos Aires, making regular visits to the estancia, but leaving the business affairs in the hands of a manager. Under the manager are bookkeepers, foremen, several hundred tenant farmers, and riders, called gauchos, very much like our western cowboys.

The tenant farmers frequently shift from one estancia to another, so they have no sense of ownership, and take little real interest in the land they work for the owner. They are not interested in the building of schools which their children will not attend if they move away. So schools are very, very few outside the cities. They do not care about the building of good roads in a neighborhood they may soon leave. The landowner, too, has slight interest in such improvements. From his miles and miles of land, he receives enough money with things as they are. His products reach the railroads well enough in carts having great ten-foot wheels to prevent them from sticking in the mud of the poor roads. 

How different this is from our own country, where most of the farming land is in small farms, each worked by the owner. His farm is his permanent home, and he is interested in having good schools nearby for his children. 

He wants good roads, too, so that his products can reach market quickly and in good condition. He pays for these things by paying his taxes, and knows that such improvements will result in increased value for his farm and crops. 

The men who govern Argentina are doing all they can to have the great ranches broken up into small farms. They know that Argentina is now raising about all the sheep and cattle possible unless more alfalfa is grown. 

And they know that to raise more alfalfa the ground must be worked more intensively than it is now. They figure that small farms will encourage the coming of farmers from those countries where intensive farming has long been carried on. They believe that as more and more such farmers settle in Argentina the country will flourish and grow steadily richer.

The ease of laying railroad tracks across the plains has led to the extensive building of NASA-12

SOUTH AMERICA 

railroads in Argentina. No other country in 

South America has nearly

TRANSPORTATION 

so many miles of rails or such fine equipment. From Buenos Aires railroads reach out across the Pampas to all the surrounding lands. Over them, wheat, other cereals, and meat products pour in vast quantities into the capital city to be exported to all the world. These railroads have made it possible for Argentina to export better beef than in the old days when the cattle had to be driven to market. The long forced "drives" were very bad for the stock. Besides the great benefit to the country of getting crops to market promptly and easily, the railroads have also helped greatly in the growth of Buenos Aires and the smaller ports of Bahia Blanca, La Plata, and Rosario. 

One railroad from Buenos Aires crosses the

Pampas, passes through the Gran Chaco, climbs the eastern slopes of the Andes, and goes to the city of La Paz in Bolivia. This road has three connecting lines running to Pacific ports, one to Mollendo, one to Arica, and one to Antofagasta. Still another railroad from Buenos Aires, the Transandine railroad, crosses the Pampas and the Andes and runs to Valparaiso. These are the only railroads across the Andes.

The government is also taking an interest in improving the highways, knowing that good roads are a source of wealth to the country. 

So, although building materials are scarce in Argentina, roads are being extended little by little throughout the country. Besides improving transportation, this provides employment for the nation's people and encourages immigration.

Nor has Argentina been slow to take advantage of the newest and quickest means of transportation, requiring neither rails nor roads. The country already has several air lines, and is rapidly preparing for others. One, from Cordoba to Buenos Aires, carries passengers and mail. Another, between Buenos 

Aires and the Uruguayan city of Montevideo, carries mail, passengers, and light cargo. 

In reading about the various regions of Argentina, we have already learned something of her capital city, Buenos Aires. The name means "good airs," and is well deserved. Buenos Aires is one

BUENOS AIRES 

of the loveliest cities of South America.

In fact, it compares well with the busiest and most beautiful cities of the world. It is sometimes called the New York of South America, as it is the largest city on the whole continent. 

One look at its broad, clean streets, its beautiful buildings, and its many parks will tell you that Buenos Aires is a very wealthy and progressive city. Buenos Aires, with a population of 2,000,000, is about the size of Philadelphia but, although manufacturing in Buenos Aires is growing, she cannot yet compare with Philadelphia in industrial development. 

The fact that Buenos Aires is the great shipping port for the products of the Pampas accounts for much of her wealth and importance. The city is on the broad, deep estuary of the Plata river, only 170 miles from the 

Atlantic ocean. Along her fine waterfront, small river boats from the upper tributaries of the Plata river unload products from far inland. Here, too, come great ocean liners and freighters from every land. From them we see many goods being unloaded: textiles; clothing; furniture; iron, steel, and leather goods; a great deal of machinery of all kinds; automobiles; and coal. All these things are necessary to a great country that has not sufficiently developed resources to do her own manufacturing. 

The goods being loaded on the ships for export are mostly raw products or those requiring simple forms of manufacture. They are meat, butter, cheese, wheat, corn, quebracho logs and extract, hides, wool, linseed, fruits, and wine. The United States imports much of the quebracho extract, hides, wool, and linseed as raw materials for manufacture, and in return supplies Argentina with many manufactured goods. 

On the Parana river, northwest of Buenos

Aires, is Rosario, the second city in size in Argentina, and an important railroad center. Although a river OTHER port, it can receive large ocean steamers. The imports and ex- 

ARGENTINE CITIES

ports of this city are practically the same as those of Buenos Aires, grains forming the largest export. Like Buenos Aires, Rosario's prosperity is shown in her wide, clean streets, lovely parks, and beautiful buildings.

Bahia Blanca, too, is a rapidly growing city. 

Like Buenos Aires, it is a railroad center, and a port for foreign commerce. Cordoba is a large inland city, noted for its beauty as well as for its industries. Located at the western limit of the wheat region, Cordoba has great flour mills, and many other industries are growing up there just as they did in the grainmarket cities of our Central plains. La Plata, nearer the ocean than Buenos Aires, is an important seaport, and is noted especially for its meat exports.

MINERAL RESOURCES OF 

One of the great problems of Argentina is the development of its mineral resources. The 

Argentine Andes are thought to contain many minerals, but ARGENTINA the extent of the supply is not known. Gold and silver mines are worked in various parts of the country, and copper and tin have been found. Some manganese is mined. Coal and iron deposits have been located, but they have not been developed. North American companies have not opened up mines as they have in other South American countries. The government, however, is considering the opening and working of the coal and iron deposits, and these resources may not lie idle much longer.

Argentina, over twenty-three times the size of New York State, has almost two million less population. And nearly one 

POPULATION 

fifth of her people live in the one city of Buenos Aires. For a country with so small a population, her foreign commerce is enormous. But Argentina needs more people to cultivate her lands, to build up her industries, and to work in many ways for the improvement of the country. With a greater population outside of the cities, more products could be raised, and the country would become rich enough to develop her resources fully.

Argentina has one great advantage over some of the other South American countries in that most of her people are white, and most of the immigrants who are entering the country are also white. Many of her people are of Spanish descent: the newcomers are from 

Italy, Spain, France, Russia, Germany, Hungary, and other European countries, with a few from North America. This white population is more industrious and quicker to learn than the South American mixed races. 

The water power of the Plata system can be developed into a great resource. When this is done, and when iron and coal are mined in quantity, one great OF ARGENTINA obstacle to manufacturing in

THE FUTURE 

Argentina will be overcome. At present the country imports its coal; but even under this difficulty manufacturing is growing. Flour and textile mills, shoe factories, and various metal industries are established in the larger cities, and it is likely that manufacturing will increase.

There are few obstacles to the progress of 

Argentina. Good government, white population, and fine climate and soil make it a land of promise for European farmers whose homelands are overcrowded. The fact that manufacturing is just beginning offers fine opportunities for engineers from the United States, England, and Germany. The present population will benefit from every gain in wealth and commerce.

URUGUAY Uruguay, the smallest republic in South America, lies east of the central part of Argentina. It is made up of a number of states, called departments, and is governed by a President and a Congress, much as our United States is governed. The temperate climate of 

Uruguay and its fertile plains help to make it a progressive nation. Uruguay is really a part of the same great plains region as northern Argentina. Although small in size, it is a commercial rival of Argentina.

Uruguay has only one natural region. It is a country of rolling plains with a few low hills. Trees line the banks of the many

SOUTH AMERICA 

streams, but otherwise the plains are almost treeless. The products and industries of 

Uruguay resemble those of the Pampas region of Argentina. Cattle, sheep, and horses are raised in great numbers, and alfalfa is the chief forage crop. Wheat, maize, flax, and oranges and other fruits are also raised. Although a great grazing country, exporting large quantities of meat and animal by-products, Uruguay has few slaughtering and packing houses as compared with those of Argentina. 

Montevideo, the capital of Uruguay, is a large, progressive city. With a population of 

458,784, it is slightly larger than Cincinnati, Ohio. It is a beautiful city, an important railroad center, and the chief seaport of 

Uruguay. Located near the mouth of the

Plata estuary, it is better situated than Buenos Aires. The main exports through the port of 

Montevideo to foreign lands are meat and meat products, wool, hides, and livestock from the Pampas grasslands, and wheat, linseed, flour, and corn from the farms. The imports are about the same as those of Buenos Aires. 

Like Argentina, Uruguay suffers from lack of minerals. Some manganese is mined and some coal has been discovered; but the country's beginning industries still depend on imported coal. The development of water power will help, however, and the country has the same advantages of a progressive and hardworking white population as Argentina.

1. The Argentine republic has four natural regionsthe Gran Chaco, the Pampas, the plateau of Patagonia, and the arid border of the Andes. 

2. Patagonia has a cold, dry plateau where many sheep are raised. People are few. Farther south, in Tierra del Fuego, rainfall is heavier. This island, too, is an increasingly important sheepraising region. 

3. The arid border of the Andes has many small farms. Irrigation makes possible the raising of sugar cane around Tucuman, and grapes and other fruits around Mendoza. Fruits reach northern markets by means of refrigerator cars and ships. 

4. The Gran Chaco has swampy forests and grasslands. Its chief forest product is quebracho. Quebracho logs are used for lumber in Argentina, are exported to European countries, and are used to make quebracho extract, which is sent to tanneries in many parts of the world. 

5. The Pampas are rich, flat, treeless plains. These plains form one of the greatest food-producing regions of the world. Rainfall is slight near the 

Andes, growing heavier toward the eastern coast. 

According to the amount of rainfall, the main products of the different sections are sheep, beef cattle, alfalfa, wheat, corn, and dairy cattle. 

6. The meat industry is the most important business of the great temperate farming region. Great quantities of meat and animal by-products are exported. 

Argentina depends on ocean transportation, which is cheap, to send her goods to market. Refrigerator ships carry fruits and chilled and frozen meats to northern markets in fine condition. 

7. Argentina was formerly a country of great ranches, or estancias. Now there are some small farms run by their owners. The government is trying to break up more of the great ranches into small farms. 

8. Argentina has more and finer railroads than any other country in South America. They bring products to Buenos Aires, and, in smaller amounts, to 

Bahia Blanca, La Plata, and Rosario. The Argen tine government is improving the roads, and air lines are making good progress. The 9. Buenos Aires, the capital of Argentina, is a clean, beautiful city about the same size as Philadelphia.

It does some manufacturing, and is a railroad center and a great seaport of South America. main exports are raw materials or those requiring very simple manufacture. Manufactured goods are the chief imports. 

10. Among the important cities of Argentina are Rosario, noted especially for grain exports; La Plata, a great meat-exporting center; and Cordoba, a flour-milling city. All three have good rail connections. Montevideo, the capital of Uruguay, is a railroad and export center very like Buenos Aires. 

11. Considering her small population, largely white,

Argentina has a great foreign commerce. This will increase if more immigrants come to the country. 

The government is doing everything to encourage the immigration of thrifty white people to Argentina. 

12. Uruguay is a region of rolling plains, where Pampas products are raised and exported. Oranges and other fruits are also exports of this small republic. 

13. The mineral resources of Argentina and Uruguay are little known or worked. When the deposits of coal and iron have been mined and the water power developed, manufacturing promises to be an increasingly important industry.

1. The importance to Argentina and Uruguay of the slaughtering and packing houses. 

2. The making and uses of quebracho extract. 

3. Irrigation in Argentina.

PLATA DRAINAGE AREA 

South America is a continent of mighty rivers. You know that the Amazon river basin in Brazil is the greatest

THE PARAGUAY- river basin in the world. 

PARANA-PLATA 

The Plata drainage basin, to

RIVER SYSTEM 

the south, is the third in size. As the Plata basin extends from about 

15° to about 35° south latitude, it lies partly in the Torrid zone and partly in the South 

Temperate zone. This great river system drains five countries-all of Paraguay and parts of Brazil, Argentina, Uruguay, and Bolivia.

Three rivers make up the main stream of the Plata drainage system. They are the 

Paraguay, the Parana, and the Plata. Find them on your map, page 168. The Paraguay river is a great sluggish stream which rises in the Central plateau and flows south for a distance of about a thousand miles before it pours into the Parana. From the meeting point, the Parana flows south, then southeast for about six hundred miles, at last joining the Plata. Though the Plata is called a river, it is really a great tidewater estuary, more than two hundred miles in length. The whole distance from the headwaters of the Paraguay to the Atlantic ocean is some eighteen hundred miles.

Tributaries. The Paraguay-Parana-Plata river has many mighty tributaries. One of these, the Upper Parana, rises in the Brazilian highlands and drains much of southern Brazil.

This river flows southwest for more than one thousand miles before it becomes the Parana. 

Though it is a larger river than the Paraguay, the Upper Parana is less useful, for it is shallow and unfitted for navigation. The Pilcomayo, a tributary of the Paraguay river, rises in the plateau of Bolivia and flows through the unexplored lands of the Gran Chaco for hundreds of miles. It forms part of the boundary between Argentina and Paraguay. The

Uruguay river, nearly a thousand miles long, rises in the Brazilian highlands, separates 

Brazil and Uruguay from Argentina, and flows into the Plata.

IGUAZU FALLS 

In the Iguazu river, a branch of the Upper 

Parana, are the magnificent, towering Iguazu falls. They lie beween Brazil and Argentina in a part of the country where no white men live. They are surrounded by the great trees of the jungle. 

Wild animals prowl about, and in the air are many swarms of tropical insects. The falls are in the form of a semicircle, 13,000 feetabout two and a half miles-across. This is more than three times the width of Niagara falls. The waters dash over a great cliff and plunge down 210 feet with a mighty roar.

This is a greater drop by fifty feet than that of Niagara. So you see how wonderful the

Iguazu falls really are. But the Iguazu falls, for all their size and beauty, have never been put to any useful work. When we think of the water power that the Niagara falls furnish and the cities that they supply with electricity, think what these Iguazu falls may mean in the future to this part of South America.

POWER 

The Iguazu falls are only one of many possible sources of water power in the northern Plata drainage area. Many of the rivers that drain this great area have WATER falls and rapids whose unused power could be turned to many useful ends. If hydroelectric plants were built throughout the region, the rushing waters could supply electric power enough to run countless factories and to light many cities. The water power of the Plata basin may prove in time to be its greatest natural resource.

Between the Pilcomayo and the Paraguay rivers, in Paraguay, lies a region of grasslands called by the Spaniards

Entre Rios, which means "between the rivers." These grasslands also extend a

1. The Iguazu falls, in the Iguazu river, a branch of the 

Upper Parana. The falls lie on the border between Brazil and Argentina, and are only a few miles from Paraguay. 

They are one-fourth again as high as the Niagara falls and three times as wide-so extensive that only from an airplane can they all be seen at once. This view shows only a part of the falls. Each of these cataracts is about 

100 feet high. In a third cataract nearby, the river plunges straight down for a full 200 feet. Such a fall as this, in the United States, would provide electric current for hundreds of factories and thousands of homes. In the Plata basin, however, there are few factories and few large centers of population to use the current. The falls are therefore undisturbed in the forest, without even a good road by which to reach them. 

2. A ranch scene in the Entre Rios section of Paraguay, between two great tributaries of the Plata. There is much fertile land in the Plata drainage area that can be developed as fast as transportation makes it worth while. 

This ranch belongs to an American. 

3. Cutting yerba maté in Paraguay. Yerba maté is a plant from which South Americans make a beverage much like tea. It is sometimes called Paraguay tea. The leaves of the plant are smoked, dried, and powdered. 

To make the tea, the powder is steeped in hot water. 

Paraguay tea is a favorite drink with the natives.

4. A Paraguay girl with a cap and mantilla of handmade lace. The mantilla, a lace scarf, is a popular article of dress with women of Spanish descent. The women of Paraguay are noted for the beauty and fine workmanship of their laces. 

5. The waterfront of Asuncion, the capital of Paraguay.

River steamers are tied up along the dock. Although 

Asuncion is connected by railroad with Buenos Aires, these steamers are also a very important means of communicating with the outside world. They travel principally between Asuncion and Buenos Aires, carrying agricultural and forest products to be exported by ocean steamers, and bringing back manufactured goods which 

Paraguay does not make. 

6. A river steamer on the Paraguay. Like the steamers which journey up and down the Mississippi, this boat has a wheel at its stern and is built wide and flat, to move safely in shallow waters. There are many shallow places in the rivers of the Plata drainage basin where vessels of deep draft would be utterly useless, and there are many other places where even flat-bottom river boats cannot be used. There people must get about on rafts or in canoes. 

7. A view of Asuncion, looking toward the river. 

Asuncion is a very old city, dating back about 400 years. It was founded by Spanish adventurers as a center from which they explored the surrounding country.

PLATA DRAINAGE AREA 

189 and northern Argentina. They are excellent for pasture. All through this part of the country are cattle ranches, separated by a network of wire fences. In this upper 

Plata basin the raising of cattle is becoming more and more important as an industry. The grasslands of Paraguay, as well as those of Argentina, Uruguay, and Brazil, now support great herds. The Gran Chaco has some open spaces which would make good pasture land, and these will probably be developed more and more and more for cattle raising.

The land between the rivers is also well suited for agriculture. The soil is so fertile and the climate so favorable that cotton, rice, sugar, tobacco, coffee, and indigo grow in abundance.

In the fertile lands of Paraguay, and to some extent in Argentina and Brazil, grows the yerba maté, one of the most important products of the upper basin. This plant, with its bright green leaves, looks somewhat like a holly bush. The leaves are smoked, dried, and powdered, and are used to make a beverage much like tea. 

In all parts of Paraguay, oranges grow without much cultivation. They grow in such quantities that one can buy them very cheaply, a dozen for a cent. Monkeys, as well as people, feed on them; some are fed to livestock; and many are left on the ground to rot.

West of the fertile Entre Rios section is the Gran Chaco. The forests of the Chaco extend from Argentina through western 

Paraguay and into eastern Bolivia. Great stretches of this tropical forest land are unexplored, but the explored sections contain much valuable timber. Here is found the hard quebracho tree, whose wood is used for logs and for quebracho extract, which is so necessary in tanning leather.

Population. With all its great natural resources, the northern part of the Plata drainage area is far behind the southern part in developing what nature has given it. One reason for this is its sparse population. In the Chaco forests of Paraguay are only wild savages, some of whom have never seen the face of a white man. The fertile grasslands are chiefly populated by Indians, mestizos, and a very small number of white people of Spanish descent. The climate is so hot, especially in the summer months of December, January, and February, that few white men can endure it. Most of those who do manage to live there are not progressive.

Many of them cannot even read or write. 

They make little effort to do anything beyond cultivating their gardens and tending their cattle.

Transportation. The northern section of the Plata basin has very poor transportation.

There are almost no good roads in the Gran Chaco, even in dry weather. In wet weather all are impassable. There are very few railroads. Few rivers are navigable by other than small river boats. Some are too shallow even for the smallest river boats, and the channels of others are blocked by rapids. In many places the people have to depend on canoes or rafts to get about. Those living in the fertile grasslands have little contact with the outside world, especially if their homes are far from one of the main streams. 

Asuncion. There is only one important city in the whole northern part of the Plata drainage area. That is Asuncion, the capital of

Paraguay and the chief trading center for the upper basin. It is a very old city with a population of about 140,000. Asuncion is connected with Buenos Aires by rail as well as by the great Paraguay-Parana-Plata river, down which most of the produce of Paraguay is shipped. The city has an interesting public market where the natives sell the products which they bring from their homes, loaded on burros. The beautiful lace made by the natives is one of the things most frequently bought by tourists who visit Asuncion. 

From our study of Argentina, Uruguay, and temperate Brazil, we are already somewhat familiar with the southern

OF THE PLATA DRAINAGE AREA part of the Plata drainage SOUTHERN PART 

area. In this section, conditions are quite different from those farther north. Here there is less

SOUTH AMERICA 

water power and less forest land, but the region has many advantages. The rivers are larger, and more of them are navigable. The soil is rich, and there is more land which is suited for grazing and agriculture. The climate, though warm, is milder, and does not discourage white settlers.

The ease with which the southern section of the Plata basin can be reached by boat brought white settlers from Europe long before any white men penetrated to the northern section. The earliest settlements grew up around the Plata river. From there, settlers moved into the lowlands along the wide navigable Parana river where there was much fertile land for pasture and for crops. As the population kept increasing, more and and more of this fertile land was cultivated for the raising of grains and fruits.

Transportation in the southern part of the Plata basin is far in advance of that in the northern part. Besides the wide navigable rivers, railroads have been built and there are also air lines. In this part of the basin we find the large cities of Buenos Aires, Montevideo, and Rosario.

It is interesting to compare the Plata river system with the Mississippi river system.

The basins of the two rivers are nearly equal in area. Each river has helped greatly in the early cOMPARISON OF 

development and settlement THE PLATA AND 

of the regions through which SYSTEMS it flows. Each has many great

MISSISSIPPI 

tributaries. The basin of each river system includes great sections of fertile land-pastures, farm lands, orchards, and forests. The great rivers of each system are navigable for steamers and barges of considerable size, which handle the products of the regions through which the rivers flow. Both river systems flow to the south. But the southerly flow of the Paraguay-Parana-Plata carries goods away from European and North American markets, and so is a disadvantage. The southerly course of the Mississippi, on the other hand, carries goods toward the Panama canal and the routes of our growing trade with 

South America and the Orient. 

Both the Mississippi and the Plata systems have abundant water power, but that of the Mississippi has been much further developed. 

The Mississippi basin, too, has a far greater population and its people do much more manufacturing. Though the Plata may in time become the Mississippi of South America, it now lags far behind its northern neighbor.

1. The Plata drainage area extends from 15° to 35° south latitude, so that it is partly in the Torrid zone and partly in the South Temperate zone. 

2. The Plata river system is made up of the Paraguay, the Parana, and the Plata rivers and their tributaries. 

3. The most important tributaries are the Upper 

Parana, the Pilcomayo, and the Uruguay rivers. 

4. The Iguazu falls are wider and higher than Niagara falls. Their power has not been put to work. 

5. The northern part of the Plata drainage area has fewer people, cities, and railroads and less cultivated land than the southern part. 

6. The products of the upper part of the basin are quebracho, yerba maté, and oranges. Cattle raising is becoming more and more important as an industry. 

7. Asuncion is the capital of Paraguay and the trading center for the upper basin. It is connected by rail and by river with Buenos Aires. 

8. The lower Plata basin is mostly agricultural land. It is easily reached by boat and has good railroads and airlines. It has several large cities, among them Buenos Aires, Montevideo, and Rosario. 

9. The Plata drainage basin and the Mississippi drainage basin are about equal in size, and both have great areas of pastures, farm lands, orchards, and forests. Both river systems have many tributaries and much water power. Both are great trade routes, and flow in a southerly direction. The 

Mississippi basin, however, is far more developed and has a far greater variety of industries and a much greater population than the Plata basin.

CHILE The republic of Chile is one of the most modern and progressive countries in South 

America. The population of 

POPULATION over four million is largely white, being descended from settlers who in years gone by came from various European countries to make their homes in Chile. Some of these settlers married Chilean Indians. The native Indians of Chile were for the most part finer and more intelligent than those in many other sections of the continent. As a result, Chile has an intelligent and industrious population whose business ability has earned for them the title, the "Yankees of South America." In spite of the fact that the country is generally progressive, there are in Chile large numbers of poorly paid laborers whose living conditions are wretched.

SHAPE 

AND SIZE 

In shape, Chile is quite different from any other country in the world. It is a long, narrow country stretching along the western coast of the continent. It extends from about 18° south latitude to about 56° south latitude. Parts of

Chile are only 100 miles wide and its greatest width is 248 miles, which is less than the width of New York State. Yet Chile is not a small country, for it extends north and south along the Pacific coast for more than 2500 miles. If 

Chile could be placed on North America with its southern tip at the end of Florida, the northern end would reach to the entrance of 

Hudson bay. Because of its long, narrow shape the country has been compared to a sword. 

OF CHILE 

In climate and surface Chile, from north to south, is quite like the western coast of our own continent from south to 

REGIONS north. If you were to take a trip north from the tip of Lower California, you would first pass through hot, arid lands. Then you would travel through the rich central valleys of California, and come to a land of greater rainfall and abundant forests. 

Still farther to the north you would find a region where the mountains come right down to the sea. Here the coast line is very irregular, with many bays, straits, and off-shore islands. This is the fiord coast of the Alaskan panhandle.

The same sort of trip is possible in Chile, though there you would have to travel in the opposite direction. Starting south from Chile's most northern part, you would first make your way through arid, hopeless-looking desert lands. Then, as you reached central Chile, you would enter a rich agricultural valley.

Still traveling south, you would pass through a region of increasing rainfall and abundant forests. Finally, as you approached the extreme southern part of the country, you would find a broken fiord coast. Its maze of channels and islands would remind you very much of the coast of the Alaskan panhandle. The great difference between the Alaskan coast and that of southern Chile is the climate, for the panhandle is washed by the warm Japan current which makes the climate mild, while the Chilean coast is swept by the cold Peruvian current which chills the land.

The eastern part of Chile lies in the young, rugged Andes mountains, the highest mountains of the whole Western hemisphere. Many of the peaks used to be volcanic, and smoke still comes from some of them. This tells us that Chile must be in a region where earthquakes are common. Along the coast of Chile is a range of older, worn-down mountains. 

Between this range and the Andes, running about seven hundred miles through central Chile, is a rich and fertile valley, called the 

Central valley. The coastal range disappears in the southwestern part of Chile. Long ago it sank deep in the ocean waters, and only its former peaks and summits remain above the sea. These form a series of islands, extending along the coast to Cape Horn. Separated by inlets, fiords, and bays, they make one vast archipelago. 192

CHILE 

AND THE ATACAMA DESERT 

From this glimpse of Chile it is evident that in products and industries the country's northern, central, and southern 

NORTHERN REGION regions must differ widely. 

Lying in the northern region between the Coast range and the Andes is the Atacama desert, one of the driest places on the earth. The 

Atacama desert gets its name from the race of Indians who used to live in this section. It is as dusty and bleak and desolate a region as you would care to see. This desert lies on a plateau, from 3600 to 13,000 feet in elevation.

It is rocky, without vegetation, and is covered with sand and deposits of alkaline salts. The surface of the plateau, broken in places by extinct volcanoes, is partly covered by old lava beds. 

The Atacama desert is in the belt of the southeast trade winds, but these winds bring no rain, for they lose their moisture in crossing the Andes mountains. In parts of the desert it is years since a drop of rain has fallen. There is rarely a cloud in the sky. The sun shines mercilessly and the sand burns underfoot. For miles there is no shade at all, and for long stretches there is not a living creature. cept for one river, the mountain streams which tumble down the Andes dry up or sink out of sight before they flow very far into the desert. 

All in all, the Atacama is one of the most desolate deserts in the world.

Ex- 

Nitrate. But, strange to say, it is this hopeless-looking desert that provides Chile with her greatest source of income, or revenue, for the Atacama is the richest desert in the world. 

In the Atacama desert are world-famous and seemingly inexhaustible deposits of the salt called nitrate of soda. Because there is no rain for a year or more at a time, and then very little, the desert deposits of this salt are not dissolved. Because of these nitrates Chile occupies a very important place in the commerce of the world, and in the eyes of other nations. Although the United States is Chile's best customer for this nitrate, great quantities are also bought by Great Britain, Germany, and France.

Nitrate is in great demand in farming regions as a fertilizer. Since almost every country in the world depends to some extent upon agriculture, Chile's nitrate trade is world-wide. 

Nitrate is also used to make explosives. In time of peace explosives made from nitrate are needed to blast rocks in mines and quarries, and in road-building operations. In war time the demand for explosives containing nitrate is even greater. Chile was rushed with orders for her nitrate during the World War and much wealth poured into the country.

The government of Chile puts an export tax on all nitrate which leaves the country. In times of peace the nitrate produces 25% of the government's total revenue, and during the 

World War it produced 50% of the revenue.

This is somewhat different from the way in which our country raises money. The United 

States taxes goods imported from foreign countries but does not tax exports. In fact, our Constitution definitely forbids our national government to tax any exports. This is to guard against taxes that would harm the industries of some states while not affecting others.

The nitrate beds in Chile are some miles from the coast and extend for about 450 miles north and south. They contain different layers of material. The top layer is usually sand, and the second layer rock. The third layer, containing the most nitrate of soda, is a rocky substance called caliche. Holes are dug, and explosive charges are placed beneath this layer. When the caliche is blasted out, it is collected and taken to the refinery factories, called oficinas. There it is crushed by machinery and put into boiling water which dissolves the nitrate and separates it from the other materials. Next the water containing the nitrate is drawn off, the water is evaporated, and the nitrate is left. It looks very much like salt crystals, and in this form is shipped to fertilizer factories, powder factories, and chemical plants, both in Chile and in many other countries.

When the pure nitrate of soda is extracted from the caliche at the refineries, several substances are left. One of these is iodine, which

SOUTH AMERICA 

is useful in treating wounds. This by-product is also an important export of Chile. 

Another product of the Atacama desert is borax, useful as a cleanser and in making glass, cement, and disinfectants. Some of this borax goes to cement factories in central Chile and some is exported, bringing in a considerable revenue.

Nitrate Lands. There are thousands of men who work in this desolate Atacama desert, which has nothing to offer or attract them except its nitrate. The little mining towns are most uninviting with no shade, no creeks, no grass. There is simply the burning sun on the burning sand.

Since this region produces little besides nitrate, everything else must be brought to it. 

By steamer to Antofagasta, and by train from the Central valley of Chile, come the food and clothing and machinery and whatever else the people of this region must have in order to live and work. Water is brought by pipes from the snow-topped Andes, one hundred miles away. The desert is absolutely helpless and its people would die without the products of the outside world. Even the food for the animals must be shipped in. 

Still the people here seem to get along very well.

The seaports are modern and busy. There are no good natural harbors, but ships are protected by long piers and breakwaters. The markets sell fresh fruits and vegetables from central Chile. Iquique is the nitrate port, although the product is also shipped from Antofagasta and other smaller ports. 

Without nitrate, Chile would be poor indeed, and realizing this she has fought fiercely to hold the desert region. About fifty years ago, both Peru and Bolivia went to war with Chile over the possession of this valuable desert, but Chile won and has ever since held her nitrate lands. At the end of the war 

Bolivia was left without a seaport, which is extremely unfortunate for a country that must trade with other countries in order to live. By a treaty with Chile, railroad lines extend from 

Arica and Antofagasta to La Paz, the chief city of Bolivia, and to these Chilean ports Bolivia sends her exports and through them receives her imports. 

Mining. From the desert and from the 

Andes mountains comes Chile's second great export-copper, which is shipped from Coquimbo. Chile is second only to the United

States in the production of copper. Iron is also mined and much iron ore is shipped to the 

United States, although manufactured iron and steel goods are imports of Chile. In Antofagasta is one of the largest silver smelters in

Gold and other minerals are also found in Chile. This country leads the other South 

American countries in the production of minerals, but much of the work is carried on through foreign capital, many of the mines being owned and worked by Japanese and 

American business men.

THE CENTRAL VALLEY 

To the south of the barren northern region is the temperate, fertile, and beautiful Central valley of Chile. In the background the snow- CENTRAL REGION AND 

clad Andes tower thousands of feet in the air, looking in places like massive rocky cities piled against the very sky. One of the peaks rises 23,000 feet. Swift streams flow down the mountains and across the valley. Many lakes form charming spots throughout its length, their shores green with trees, their waters a vivid blue, with mountain tops reflected in their depths. Around the lakes are the summer homes of the wealthy people, abloom with flowers and foliage. This garden spot of Chile includes about one eighth

CHILE of the whole country. It is the most thickly settled region and the most pleasant. 

Thanks to the rain-bringing westerly winds which blow over the valley there is plenty of rain throughout most of its length. In the drier northern part, the mountain streams are used for irrigation. The growing season is long. Mixed farming is carried on and the valley is rich in orchards of oranges, pears, peaches, and apricots and has an abundance of strawberries, cherries, lemons, and grapes which are used in making wine. On its huge plantations are rich fields of wheat, cotton, sugar cane, rice, corn, and tobacco. This Central valley of Chile raises enough agricultural products to feed the people of the whole country and to export some grains and fruits as well.

The Panama canal brings Chile much nearer our eastern seaports, and Chilean fruit reaches them in good condition. It brings high prices because it arrives when native American fruit is out of season. Why is this the case? Honeydew melons are shipped in large numbers. Besides fresh fruits, Chile exports quantities of fruits which have been canned or dried in the sun to preserve them.

The soft-shelled walnuts of Chile are also in great demand in our country. Besides its agricultural products, central Chile has large forest stretches, as well as coal and other rich mineral deposits. 

You would be interested in visiting some of the plantations of this Central valley. Like the coffee plantations in Brazil, these large farms often look much like villages. The house of the owner is usually an extremely attractive one, with many rooms, usually all on one floor, but sometimes on two. Most people are charmed by the Chilean houses, and the way in which they spread over such a large amount of land and surround a central garden. 

The houses of the laborers are far different. 

They are usually small one-story mud huts, with the bare ground forming the floor, and often serving as a bed. 

Transportation. Because of their waterfalls, the many rivers of central Chile are navigable for only short distances. The falls are used to NASA-13 produce electricity which runs factories and street cars, and furnishes electric light. Many trains are run by electricity, since Chile has not enough coal to supply her needs and what she has is very expensive. Chile is well provided with railroads, and the materials used in building them have come largely from the 

United States. The Transandine railroad runs from Valparaiso to Buenos Aires and brings the Atlantic and Pacific coasts in close contact. 

Far above the valley this railroad crosses the mountains into Argentina at Uspallata pass.

The building of the road is regarded as a splendid engineering feat, for tunnels had to be cut out of solid rock. 

Manufacturing. Central Chile is advancing rapidly in manufacturing. There are textile mills, which use the cotton from the irrigated fields and the wool from the grazing lands. 

There are a number of tanneries where leather is made. This leather is sent to great factories where it is made into boots, shoes, and other leather goods. Other manufactures of the valley region are clothing, furniture, paper, cement, and explosives. There is both United 

States and British capital invested in the manufacturing plants of Chile. 

Santiago. The capital of Chile is Santiago, a city of 620,000 people, which is a little larger than Buffalo. It is over 1700 feet above sea level, and about seventy-two miles from the ocean. Santiago is a beautiful city, the imposing public buildings and the attractive private homes being set against a background of snowy mountain peaks.

Valparaiso. The chief seaport of Chile, Val paraiso, is about one third the size of Santiago. 

It is considered a very fascinating city. Its harbor cannot compare with that of Rio or of 

San Francisco. But a harbor is so badly needed in this region that Valparaiso, even though its harbor is poor, has become the largest seaport of South America's west coast. Shelter was once poor; but now great breakwaters protect ships from storms, and vessels need no longer anchor far from shore and load and unload cargo by small boats and lighters. The chief exports of Valparaiso are copper, wool, hides, and grain. Its imports are largely tex-
