CHILE tiles, machinery, coal and iron, oil, hardware, and vehicles.

Valparaiso has two distinct divisions. Part of it lies along the coast and part is built on nineteen hills, from 300 to 1400 feet in height. 

Some of the dwellings perch up on the cliffs midway between the low town and the high. 

Passengers are taken between these two parts of the city in cars or cages, which are somewhat like elevators going up on a slant. They are called ascensors. At night, in entering the harbor, one notices that the lights of Valparaiso start low on the coast and extend up and up until one is left in doubt as to where the city lights leave off and the stars of the sky begin.

Much of the marketing in Valparaiso is done from door to door by means of donkeys. 

The vendors fasten their merchandise, whether it be fish or furniture or cans of milk, to their donkeys and ride about the streets calling their wares. To the tourists these quaint street vendors are among the most in teresting sights of Valparaiso. 

Leaving the Central valley of Chile and traveling south one notices that the rainfall increases and the climate becomes SOUTHERN cooler and the forests more CHILEAN

MAINLAND abundant. The heavy rainfall and cool climate have given southern Chile great forests of the common trees of temperate regions. In the northern end of this region a small part of the timber is cut and used, but most of the woodland is so hard to reach that it is of very little commercial value to the country.

The population of southern Chile is sparse. 

Here live the remnants of the tribe of Araucanian Indians who were once a proud, fierce race, never wholly conquered by the white man. Today they are slowly dying out. There are comparatively few whites in this region. 

Some of the timber land has been cleared and opened for cultivation by Swiss and German immigrants who are introducing modern methods of agriculture brought from their own countries. Much of the agricultural work, however, is still done in a slow, old-fashioned way with oxen.

Cattle raising is one of the industries of the lands just south of the Central valley. This industry and the fact that the bark of many of the trees can be used in tanning hides have led to the establishment of a large number of tanneries in this region. A fine grade of leather is manufactured and exported.

Coal. Coal fields extend down the coast of southern Chile. For the most part they lie under the sea. To enter the mines one must go down from the mainland or from one of the offshore islands in a small elevator running in a shaft, and must travel along dim tunnels straight out under the ocean. These are the most extensive coal deposits in South America, but the coal is of an inferior grade and must be sold at a lower price than the coal which is imported. This lack of good coal handicaps many of the South American countries in their progress, for coal is very important in building up manufacturing and in the running of railroads. 

The "Roaring Forties." All year round the

Pacific ocean off the coast of southern Chile is stormy. Rain falls almost all the time. The sky is constantly gray. The cold westerly winds are often gales, and the sea piles up into massive waves, tossing great ships about and dashing over their decks. This region, between 40° and 50° south latitude, is so wild and raging that it has been called the "roaring forties."

The land is not much better than the sea. 

Along the coast the peaks of an old-time mountain range that long ago sank into the ocean now stick up out of the water in the shape of rocky islands. In the 

In the many inlets and channels that separate the islands one might easily become lost. Desolation island is only one of the many that well deserve such a name. The mainland is a country of timberlands and of rugged, barren mountain peaks, on which the snow line creeps gradually lower as one travels to the south. The evergreen forests are constantly dripping from the almost continual rains. Mists and fogs settle down over land and sea for much of the time. Unlike the Atacama desert, where a cloudy sky is seldom seen, this region has very few sunny

SOUTH AMERICA 

days. It is an angry and wild part of the world, and it takes a stout heart to brave it. 

How very different are the two ends of 

South America! In contrast to the northern part of the continent, where SOUTHERN END the air is warm and still and 

OF THE 

CONTINENT the sea is usually calm, the southern tip 

Cape Hornlooks out over some of the stormiest seas in all the world. At the southernmost point of the continent a rocky mountain rises. The ocean currents are especially strong at this point, and many a ship has been dashed to pieces against the rocks.

The Strait of Magellan. Way back in 1519, long before the days of steamships, even before people were really sure that the earth was round, a stout-hearted Portuguese explorer by the name of Ferdinand Magellan set out with a fleet of sailing vessels to find a westward route to Asia. What a brave man

Magellan must have been to keep on when he struck the wild, gray, misty region near Cape 

Horn! At last he discovered an inner passage from the Atlantic to the Pacific ocean, by means of which his vessels could avoid the dreadful trip around Cape Horn. This passage was named the Strait of Magellan in honor of the brave commander, and it is now used by almost all ships sailing these seas. 

The Strait of Magellan is not easy to navigate, for it is a series of winding channels nearly four hundred miles long. Much of the coast is rocky, the channels are narrow, and mist and fog darken the way. As icebergs are liable to appear in the path of a steamer traveling the channels, ships anchor for the night and continue their voyages in daylight.

Since the opening of the Panama canal, these southern seas are used less and less. 

Ships now steam up the coast from the western ports of South America, through the Panama canal, and on to the ports of the Atlantic ocean. 

Surface and Climate. Knowing that South

America reaches farther south than any other continent, you might think its southern tip would be a barren waste of ice and snow, something like the Arctic wastes of land in the northern hemisphere. Instead, the southern tip of South America is a land of mountains and forested islands. Although the mountains are snow-capped, and glaciers creep down their sides, their lower slopes and the land about their bases are covered with dark evergreens and hardwood trees. Mosses and ferns grow richly and freely so that the region presents a green landscape all year round.

This is but natural when you consider the location of South America's southern tip. The continents of the northern hemisphere extend much farther towards the North Pole than does South America towards the South Pole. 

In fact, the Chilean city of Magallanes, or

Punta Arenas, the southernmost city in the world, is only about 55° south latitude. This means that it is only three degrees nearer to the South Pole than London, England, is to the North Pole. 

Tierra del Fuego. Tierra del Fuego is an archipelago, but the name usually refers to the main island of the group, which is part Chilean territory and part Argentine. It shares the fogs, the mists, and the high winds of southwestern Chile. On the whole the summers are cool. The winters are generally mild with sudden spells of extreme cold. At no time is it ever actually warm, and one wonders how the native Indians, of whom there are three tribes, go about with so little clothing. These Indians are all primitive. One tribe is noted as being treacherous. The Indians of this tribe live for the most part in their canoes, in the center of which they carry their fires of smoldering sods. 

On the beaches of Tierra del Fuego you may see men washing the sand for gold. There are good sheep-raising lands on the islands, and it is estimated that at least 5,000,000 sheep graze here. Farmers also grow such hardy crops as cabbages, potatoes, turnips, and peas. Wild berries and wild grapes grow freely in the interior. Fishing and whaling are two other occupations of the region. 

Magallanes. A large part of the products of southern South America go through the seaport of Magallanes. Although it is a thousand

CHILE miles away from its nearest neighbor of any size, this modern little city has a plaza, electric lights, fine fine public buildings, comfortable homes, and good stores. Its population of 

27,000 is largely made up of Danes, Norwegians, and Swedes.

Magallanes exports wool and hides to Europe and the United States, and imports flour, foods, clothing, and coal. There are sawmills, lumber yards, and ship repair yards in Magallanes. The city also serves as a coaling station for vessels passing through the Strait of Magellan. This out-of-the-way little city has been a most welcome sight to many ships that have been badly buffeted by wind and sea on their way down the eastern or western coast of South America.

COUNTRIES 

Of all the countries of South America the three most important are Argentina, Brazil, and Chile. Because of their initial letters and because they rank ABC first in commercial importance they are spoken of as the ABC countries of 

South America. With their highlands and lowlands, with their forests, their grasslands, and their mineral wealth, and with their vast possibilities for future development, these three great republics have done much to earn for their continent the enticing title of "The 

Land of Promise."

 1. Chile is a modern progressive republic. The popu lation numbers over four million. The people are largely descended from various white races and from native Indians who were for the most part finer and more intelligent than the Indians in many other sections of South America. The people are industrious and ambitious, and have good business ability. 

2. Chile is a long, narrow country extending along the western coast of South America from about 18° south latitude to about 56° south latitude. It has a coast line of more than 2500 miles. 

3. On the eastern side of Chile are the young, high 

Andes mountains. Along the coast is a range of older, worn-down mountains which farther south have sunk in the ocean and formed an immense archipelago. The mainland of Chile has three distinct regions: the Atacama desert in the north; the rich, agricultural valley of central Chile; and a region of increasing rainfall and heavy forests in the southern part. There is also a broken fiord region in the extreme south. 

4. The Atacama desert is Chile's greatest source of revenue. This desert receives scarcely any rain, as the southeast trade winds which blow across it lose their moisture on the eastern slopes of the high Andes. The desert contains vast deposits of nitrate, a very valuable product in which Chile has a world-wide trade. Nitrate is used as a fertilizer, and in the making of explosives. Iodine is a by-product of nitrate, and borax is also found in the Atacama desert. They also contribute a great 

Ideal to Chile's revenue. 

5. The Andes mountains provide Chile with minerals for export. Chile is the second copper country of the world. Much iron ore and silver, as well as a number of other minerals, are shipped out. Nitrate is shipped from Iquique, copper and iron from 

Coquimbo, and silver from Antofagasta. 

6. Agriculture flourishes in the Central valley of Chile.

There is abundant rainfall here, due to the westerly winds, and there are mountain streams which are used for irrigation. This Central valley has a long growing season and supplies the people of Chile with all the agricultural products they need. Manufacturing is advancing in this region. Santiago, the capital of Chile, and Valparaiso, the chief seaport, are in this part of Chile. 

7. Farther south on the Chilean mainland one finds more abundant forests and increasing rainfall. 

Some of the lumber land has been cleared for farm lands. Cattle raising is a leading industry here, and there are a number of tanneries which produce a fine grade of leather. Along the southwestern coast are the largest natural deposits of coal in South America, but they extend under the sea and the coal is of an inferior grade. 

8. The western coast of southern Chile is a region of stormy seas, heavy winds, and almost constant rains. The land is wild and desolate. The coast line is a maze of inlets and channels. This stormy region is called the "roaring forties." 

9. At the southern end of South America is the Strait of Magellan. This inner passage discovered by 

Magellan, between the Atlantic and Pacific coasts, is a series of channels about four hundred miles long. Since the opening of the Panama canal this route has been much less used. 

10. Tierra del Fuego belongs partly to Chile and partly to Argentina. There is much sheep grazing carried on in this southern region. Fishing and whaling are other occupations. Men search for gold along the sandy beaches. There are sawmills at 

Magallanes. This city is a coaling station for steamers and also has ship repair yards. It exports wool and hides to Europe and the United States. 

11. Because Argentina, Brazil, and Chile are the leading countries in commercial importance, they are known as the ABC countries of South America. 1

TWO SEAPORTS OF THE WEST COAST DESERT REGION 

2 (1) (2) By Ewing Galloway, N. Y. So 

1. Lighters unloading ships at Mollendo, Peru. Lighters are big, open boats, like those tied to the dock. They bring ashore coal, food, and supplies of many kinds from the ships which stop here. Mollendo is one of the many 

South American ports that have no natural harbors. The regular coast line and the shallow water close to shore keep large steamships from coming to the docks. steamships must anchor offshore and load and unload by lighters. Many of these harborless ports have built breakwaters which protect parts of their waterfronts against waves and rough weather. The breakwaters at Mollendo give shelter to small boats but large ships cannot come close enough to be protected by them. 

2. The improved harbor at Callao, Peru. Ports like

Mollendo, where ships must use lighters to unload, are very inconvenient. Although few ports on the west coast of South America have any natural shelter for vessels, some have built artificial basins where ships may dock at some distance from shore. Callao has a natural harbor which she has improved greatly. The ship basins and sea walls in the picture not only make the harbor safe against storms but also allow several large ships to dock at one time. Railroad tracks run out along the concrete piers. Callao has fine equipment, and there are few 

South American harbors with better advantages than this port of Peru.

PEOPLE AND INDUSTRIES OF THE WEST COAST DESERT 

(3) By Ewing Galloway, N. Y. (4) Pan American Union. (5) (6) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. 

3. A refinery on Peru's coast. At this refinery gasoline is made from natural gas by a special process. If the gas were not used at the field it would go to waste, as there are no cities near at hand to use enough to pay for laying pipe lines. Peru has other refineries where gasoline and other petroleum products are made from. petroleum. Petroleum is Peru's chief export. 

4. Planting sugar cane in the Peruvian desert. Great crops of cane are produced here, in one of the driest climates on earth, by means of irrigation. The water which makes this rich land productive flows down from the mountains. Notice the way the pieces of cane are planted. When these pieces are covered with moist earth, new plants will spring up from them. The mule is carrying two great bags of seed-cane. 

5. A native Indian family and their home. These Indians are uneducated and their living conditions are primitive. They earn their food by simple farming. Their little one-room house is made of sun-baked mud. The roof of the hut is made of sticks covered with dried mud. 

6. Native Indians in the fields. The women work as hard and long in the fields as their husbands. Notice the primitive wooden spades carved from some sort of hard wood. 

Surely no one could do a great deal of work in a day with such poor tools.

SURFACE AND CLIMATE WEST COAST DESERT 

The Atacama desert, whose nitrate brings so much wealth to Chile, is the southern part of a long narrow desert region which follows the Pacific coast of 

South America for nearly two thousand miles. This West Coast desert, as it is called, extends along the coast of northern Chile and the entire coast of Peru. 

Like the Atacama desert, the whole West

Coast desert lies on a high plateau. The surface is largely rocky, though in places there are wide stretches of shifting sand which the wind is constantly arranging and rearranging into mounds called dunes. At intervals, the surface of the desert is broken by the valleys of streams flowing westward from the snowtopped Andes.

What makes this west coast region a desert? The Andes mountains are the cause.

The southeast trade winds have plenty of moisture when they blow from the Atlantic ocean across the Amazon basin. But as the winds reach the Andes they must rise in order to pass over the mountains. As they do so they grow cool and drop their moisture as rain. Once over the mountains, the winds have no moisture left for the Pacific coast. So, for the greater part of the year, there is hardly a cloud in the sky and little or no rain falls in the hot desert lands. Sometimes in the winter months, between June and October, fogs drift in from the Pacific, bringing light, drizzling rains. When this happens scrubby little plants spring up in the desert, but the rainfall is never very great and these soon wither. For mile on mile, there is neither shade nor water.

PRODUCTS 

One very important product of this desert is petroleum. It is also the leading export of 

Peru. The oil fields are near the coast. In the rest of the desert few things grow without help. But you have studied enough about deserts to know that man has learned how to change into rich fields those barren wastes which are fortunate enough to have rivers.

The Atacama part of the West Coast desert has only one river but the Peruvian part has a number of streams, fed by the snow fields of the Andes. The land along these rivers is rich and fertile, needing only to be watered. Along the river valleys strips of land have been irrigated and divided into farms where sugar cane, cotton, rice, alfalfa, fruits, and vegetables are grown.

A large part of Peru's agricultural land lies in these irrigated oases of the desert. So much sugar and cotton are grown in the irrigated farm lands that these products are two of Peru's leading exports. The cotton, known as 

Peruvian cotton, has a long fiber and is excellent for mixing with wool in weaving cloth. 

Vegetables are sent to supply the markets of many Peruvian cities, and strawberries, oranges, grapes, bananas, alligator pears, and various other kinds of fruit grown on the desert lands are displayed in the city markets. 

Olive trees, fig trees, and date trees thrive in the irrigated districts.

Peru's capital city of Lima is on the Rimac river and owes much of its growth and importance to the rich agricultural region surrounding it. The city is connected by railroad with mining sections in the mountains.

CITIES 

Although Peru has a seacoast that is 1400 miles in length-about three-quarters as long as the Atlantic seacoast of our own countryshe has only one really good natural harbor, that of Callao. Callao is the seaport for Lima, with which city it is connected by rail. 

As the people of Peru need more than one seaport, they have had to make others by using breakwaters. Behind these breakwaters ships can anchor off the coast and send their passengers and freight in smaller boats to the shore. Perhaps the most important of these 201

SOUTH AMERICA 

ports is Mollendo, the seaport of Arequipa, a business and manufacturing center for the southern part of Peru. 

Peru's little seaports are chiefly outlets for the products of inland towns and the agricultural districts. Though a few of them have some remains of an ancient Indian civilization, I SHOULD

The one most interesting fact about this West Coast region is that here still another barren desert of the world has been made to yield products necessary to the life of man.

IN THE HIGH ANDES MOUNTAINS 

1 2 3 (1) (4) (5) Consulate General of Peru, N. Y. (2) Publishers' Photo Service, N. Y. (3) Consulate General of Bolivia, N. Y. 

1. The Plaza of Lima, the capital of Peru. Lima was founded by the Spaniards four centuries ago. Practically every city founded by the Spaniards has a plaza, or public square, which has been made as beautiful as possible. The monument celebrates the winning of 

Peru's independence. The modern electric lights and the paved streets tell us that Lima, though old, is also progressive and up-to-date. 

2. Lake Titicaca, high in the Andes. Lake Titicaca is the highest navigable body of water in the world. This steamship was brought over the mountains in pieces on the backs of Indian porters and put together here. 

3. La Paz, the chief city of Bolivia. Lima is on a flat plateau near the coast, but La Paz is on steep slopes in the very midst of the Andes. Nevertheless it has wellbuilt houses, electric lights, and broad avenues. In the distance we can see peaks of the Andes. 

4. Indian policemen in a Peruvian village. These smiling red men are descendants of the Inca Indians who ruled the country before the Spanish came. Their badges of office are short staffs bound with silver, which hang from their wrists. They have ponchos, or broad capes, around their waists. After sunset these capes will be worn over their shoulders, as the nights are cold in high Peru. 

5. Ruins of Inca buildings. The Incas were a strong people with much skill in building. These ruins were part of their once splendid capital city of Cuzco.

WORK AND TRAVEL IN THE ANDES 

(6) (7) Consulate General of Peru, N. Y. (8) Publishers' Photo Service. N. Y. (9) Ford Motor Co. 

6. A railroad bridge and tunnel in the Peruvian Andes. 

The Andes are higher and steeper than any of our North 

American mountain ranges, and building railroads through them is very difficult. One railroad, from Callao to Oroya, in Peru, has sixty-one bridges and sixty-six tunnels. This train is loaded with bars of copper from the extensive mines of the highlands. It was principally to reach the copper mines that the task of building the railroad was undertaken. 

7. A railroad pass through the Andes, 15,806 feet above sea level. This is higher than the highest mountain in the United States. Although the mountain beyond the pass lies in the Torrid zone, it is so high that glaciers have formed on its slopes. 

8. A pack train of llamas. These strange-looking animals are strong and sure-footed and can carry heavy loads. Before railroads were built, llamas carried almost all heavy loads in the Andes. The llamas also provide valuable wool. 

9. An airplane flying past Mt. Misti, in Peru. Because railroads are expensive and difficult to build, and travel by road is slow, airplanes are much used in the Andes highlands. This airplane carries fifteen passengers. It was made in the United States.

HIGHLANDS OF PERU, BOLIVIA, AND ECUADOR SPANISH CONQUEST OF THE INCA KINGDOM 

High in the Andes mountains in what is now Peru, there was built, long ago, a city called Cuzco. This city was the capital of the great empire of the Incas, a powerful civilized tribe of Indians who had conquered many smaller tribes living in nearby lands. The Incas' chief city was splendidly built, and its great stone buildings were richly ornamented with silver and gold.

Many Spanish explorers came to the New 

World soon after Columbus discovered America. One of these, named Pizarro, heard about the wonderful city of Cuzco and went in search of it. When he and his followers found it they killed the Inca emperor, scattered the members of his tribe or made them slaves, and seized the treasures of the Indian temples. This done, they made the Inca lands into a Spanish colony and built a new capital city which they named the City of the Kings. 

Now it is known as Lima.

From Peru the Spaniards pushed southeast into what is now Bolivia, and north into the present country of Ecuador. The Indians living there were easily subdued, and these lands, too, came under Spanish control. 

Today Peru, Bolivia, and Ecuador belong to neither the Indians nor the Spaniards but are three independent republics. More than half their population, however, is made up of Indians, and many of their white people are descended from the early Spanish settlers. 

In the northern part of the continent near the Caribbean sea the Andes mountains have three distinct ranges. As

SURFACE FEATURES they stretch south these OF THE CENTRAL ANDES COUNTRIES 

ranges come together, until in Ecuador they form a high mountainous plateau edged by parallel ridges. Although crossed by the Equator, this section has many snow-capped peaks. Here, too, are several active volcanoes. 

Through Peru and Bolivia, the Andes have their greatest east-west width. Here there are many ranges, plateaus, and valleys. Some of the ranges are nearly twice as high as the 

Rocky mountains. The largest of the plateaus is in Bolivia, and has an average height of about 12,000 feet above sea level. The surrounding mountains are much higher still. In the whole length of the Andes mountains there are few passes less than 10,000 feet in height. In Peru and Bolivia there are none less than 15,000 feet above sea level. Some of the valleys even are as high as high ranges of the Rocky mountains, and one must travel fully two miles above the sea level to reach them.

SCENERY 

The magnificent scenery of this part of the 

Andes is unsurpassed even in glorious Switzerland. Imagine yourself on a train climbing slowly up these mountains.

The train, from time to time, reaches a crest, from which it zigzags slowly down to meet and climb another sloping wall of rock. You look down into a wide expanse of valley and see little farms, and mountain lakes that glitter far below like pools of silver. Sometimes, and more often as you travel higher, you will see only bare walls of rock, and deep, deep gorges below them. There will be no growing thing, not even a tree, in sight.

In spite of the nearness to the Equator, the climate becomes increasingly colder as one reaches these higher regions. At altitudes above 10,000 feet the air is so thin that many people unaccustomed to such heights are affected by mountain sickness.

CLIMATE 

AND SOIL In the central Andes the mountains shut off the prevailing easterly winds and prevent much rainfall on the plateaus and in the valleys. So there the air is dry as well as thin. 

Such dry thin air warms and cools rapidly, and besides making one sick and dizzy it often burns one by day and then chills one by night. 203

SOUTH AMERICA 

Besides the barren mountain tops there is much barren land even on the plateaus. But in the lovely, sheltered valleys, which are among the highest cultivated valleys in the world, there is fertile soil. Some of this soil has been formed by the deposits of streams; some, as in Ecuador, by lava thrown out by volcanoes. From the days of the Incas, irrigation, though primitive, has been carried on, and today many crops are raised. 

The products raised in this region vary according to the altitude of the farms. Coffee and tobacco are grown at THE FARMS- about 3000 feet above sea AND PRODUCTS level. A little higher, vegetables and fruits are grown. 

THEIR PEOPLE 

Still higher, barley, rye, and much wheat are raised. Just as in the days of the Incas, many of the farms cling to the steep sides of the mountains, the little gardens terraced one above the other.

The coca shrub is also grown in the countries of the central Andes. The shrub grows about six feet in height. The dark heavy leaves are removed and dried. Then the 

Indians chew them, preferring them to tobacco. Cocaine, a drug used to deaden pain, is made from the coca leaves. Cinchona trees, too, grow in large numbers in Bolivia and in Peru. From the bark of these trees comes the medicine called quinine.

The farmers in the central Andes are largely Indians or mestizos. The most primitive of them live in mud huts, usually consisting of one room with a floor of earth and with little or no furniture. The children, the chickens, and the animals alike live by day and sleep by night in these huts. There is no place in such a home to spend a quiet hour with a good story book, but this does not bother the children for they cannot read. Even their parents can neither read nor write. The lack of education has affected the farming methods, which are so primitive and unscientific that the land produces only a small fraction of what it might. For example, Bolivia with its cool, dry plateau might, if the land were properly worked, raise enough wheat to supply all its people. As yet, however, the farmers have not learned how to do this and the wheat crop is small and unimportant. During recent years the governments of the central Andes countries have been doing much to help the farmers by importing fine grades of seeds, and by teaching them better methods of working their soil.

GRAZING 

On the bleak, bare slopes of the mountains where little grows but coarse grass, the Indian shepherds feed their herds of llamas, alpacas, and sheep-all wool-bearing animals. In this region grazing is a most important industry. To the people of the Andes the llamas are the most useful of the domestic animals. Llamas are somewhat like sheep because of their woolly hides, and they are somewhat like camels because of their long necks and their small heads which they hold very high. As they live on less grass and water than sheep, the llama herds attended by their solitary shepherds are often pastured almost as high as the snow line. In addition to providing valuable wool, the llamas are the pack animals of the region. Great trains of them with loads on their backs follow the narrow mountain trails. The alpacas, belonging to the same family as the llamas, are raised solely for their wool, which is stronger and finer than that of the llamas. Wool is an important export of Peru and Bolivia, and alpaca wool makes up two thirds of the wool shipments.

MINING 

The greatest wealth of the central Andes countries is in their mineral deposits. Those of Ecuador, though known to be rich, have been only slightly developed. But in the highlands of Peru and

Bolivia mining is the most important industry. 

Even in these countries, however, the working of the mines depends on American engineers and on money supplied by the United States and European countries. 

Many Indians not engaged in agriculture work in the mines. Let us visit Cerro de 

Pasco, in Peru, a good example of a mining town. There we see the little houses of adobe, or sun-dried clay, with their roofs lined with straw and overlaid with tile. If the day is cold, the natives are well wrapped in their

HIGHLANDS OF PERU, BOLIVIA, AND ECUADOR 

205 ponchos, or blankets, because they have not wood enough to build a fire to keep them warm. Up and down the steep, straggly streets go the natives, carrying great loads or leading their pack animals, the llamas.

Copper is second in importance of Peru's exports. United States companies own smelters and mines at Cerro de Pasco, the copper center, and have increased their production. Silver, lead, tin, and zinc are mined as well as copper. There are many silver deposits throughout the central Andes, the silver center of the region being Potosi in Bolivia. 

Many millions of dollars worth of silver have been mined in the central Andes countries

North America produces three times as much silver as all the rest of the world. South America stands second. This graph shows the countries of the New World which lead in the production of silver. Each unit of the graph scale represents ten million ounces of silver. 50 during the hundreds of years since the time of the Incas.

The Bolivian cities of Oruro and Potosi are centers for the production of tin. This product is of particular importance to us because the United States uses more tin than any other country, and much of our supply is taken from the mines of Bolivia. Gold is so plentiful in many parts of the central Andes that in places rich deposits of it are brought down to the lowlands by the mountain streams. Among the other minerals found in the region are lead; vanadium, used in making strong steel; and bismuth, a mineral used in medicines. Oil is found near Lake Titicaca and in the eastern Andes in Peru.

Rich as the region is in its variety of minerals, there are only scattered deposits of coal. 

These are found mostly in Peru and Ecuador but they are not large and the coal is not of good quality. Coal is one of the chief imports. 

The imported coal is used in the smelting furnaces at the mines, but the necessity of importing it and then carrying it over the mountains makes it very expensive and increases the cost of working the mineral deposits. The lack of fuel affects the home life of the people of the central Andes countries as well as their greatest industry-mining. Coal is so expensive that it cannot be spared for heating. 

As no trees grow in the greater part of the highland region, wood is also very scarce. 

The people, therefore, suffer much discomfort in the cold, dry air of the plateaus. 

In past years mining in the Andes was done with very crude tools. The ore was taken from the earth in small chunks which were sorted by hand. Sometimes women and children helped in this work. Much time and labor, as well as tons of valuable minerals, were wasted by these methods. To get the ore from the mines to points of shipment also presented many difficulties, for it had to be carried over the mountains sometimes by llamas and sometimes on the backs of men.

Today, however, the mines are worked largely by modern machinery though both the machinery and the coal for smelting the ores must be imported. Since neither men nor pack animals can carry coal and machinery quickly, railroads are necessary to serve the mining towns. These have been built at the cost of millions of dollars, hundreds of lives, and years of labor.

RAILROADS 

Probably the most famous of the Andean railroads is that from Callao, the seaport for 

Lima, to Oroya, a mining town of

This railroad is such a AND CITIES 

Peru. marvelous example of engineering skill that it is considered by many people as one of the wonders of the world. Trains going from Callao to Oroya cross sixty-one bridges and pass through sixty-six tunnels. 

Another railroad runs north from Oroya to

Cerro de Pasco. There is as yet no road from 

Oroya to Cuzco, but trains enter Cuzco from 32

SOUTH AMERICA 

the south and connect it with Lake Titicaca, the highest navigable body of water in the world. This lake, which lies partly in Peru and partly in Bolivia, may be crossed by steamer to Guaqui, in Bolivia. Here one may board a train for the city of La Paz. Though this city lies in a valley 1000 feet deep, it is 

12,000 feet above sea level. Journeying to La

Paz by way of Lake Titicaca, one sees the white heads of Mount Sorata and Mount Illimani towering against the sky. These mountains, among the highest in the western hemisphere, reach an altitude of over 21,000 feet. 

Bolivia has no seacoast, so she has made arrangements with her neighbors, Peru and Chile, to be allowed to use their ports. A direct route, about 270 miles in length, has been built from Arica, Chile, to La Paz. This mountain city is also reached by a much longer route from Antofagasta, another 

Chilean port. From Mollendo, southeast of

Callao, on the Peruvian coast, one may reach La Paz by still another route. One travels by rail from Mollendo to Lake Titicaca, crosses the lake by boat, and continues to La Paz by train. The distance from Mollendo to 

La Paz is 525 miles and even by new, fast service the journey takes about forty hours.

The Mollendo, the Arica, and the Antofagasta routes are Bolivia's only outlets to the Pacific.

The railroad from Mollendo passes through Arequipa, a picturesque city which is the metropolis or chief city of southern Peru. 

The atmosphere is very clear at Arequipa. 

Harvard University once had an observatory nearby for the study of heavenly bodies which cannot be seen from the Northern hemisphere.

Arequipa is about 16° south latitude, and many stars can be seen there which are never visible in the United States. On the other hand, the North Star and some other northern stars cannot be seen from Peru. 

The Antofagasta-Bolivia railroad has been extended south to the Argentine coast and much through traffic has developed between La 

Paz and Buenos Aires. As no railroad reaches 

Sucre, the legal capital of Bolivia, the government of the country is carried on in La Paz. 

Lima, in the foothills of the Andes and about five hundred feet above sea level, is the center of white population and of trade and commerce for the central Andes region. While sections of Lima are like bits of old Spain, the city has thriving industries. These include the manufacture of furniture, copper articles, and pottery. The city has also many improvements, such as electric lights and trolley cars. 

Well-educated people from many countries meet in Lima. The Spanish language is the usual speech, though one may hear the Indian dialect in some poorer sections of the city. 

Lima's most important buildings are her cathedral and the old government building, called the National palace. There are many fine residences in the city. The newer buildings are of steel and concrete, instead of the old, plaster-like material so popular in South 

American cities.

The use of modern building materials in Lima illustrates in a small way that the central Andes countries are progressing. Other evidences of this are the extension of railroads throughout Peru and Bolivia, and even more, the establishment of airways. These countries have not yet fully realized the benefits of automobiles because, though motor roads are being built, they are still few in number. But the 

South Americans appreciate, perhaps more than we do, the great aid that the airplane can give them. The journey from Lima, over the 

Andes to Iquitos, once meant several weeks of travel by muleback, canoe, and river boat. 

Now it is a trip of a few hours. The traveler goes from Lima to San Ramon by rail and motor. From there the journey is made by airplane.

The unpleasant climate and the poor means of transportation account largely for the scarcity of population in the central Andes countries. 

POPULATION 

Even La Paz, Bolivia's market center, has only 142,000 people. Potosi, a railroad center, has a population of about 30,000 people. 

Engineers who have gone there to work the valuable mines have been unable to remain long in the thin, cold air. Cuzco, attractive

1. Wild lands of the upper Amazon basin. This rough trail is in the forests of eastern Peru. The palms and ferns have grown to enormous size in the hot, moist climate. These Indians are porters. They walk many miles a day with loads strapped to their backs. 

2. The rubber tree, the most useful plant of the eastern lowlands. The tall, thin plant near the man is a young rubber tree. In the center of the picture is a larger, older tree of the same kind. Rubber trees are scattered through the rain forests. Their juice, or latex, is collected by Indians who harden it into rubber which they sell to traders. This crude rubber is purified and manufactured into many useful articles. 

3. The chief means of transportation in the grasslands. 

Here there are no good roads and the easiest way to get about is by boat. This is one of the many rivers which flow through the eastern plains to join the Amazon or 

Orinoco. Only rough trails over the Andes lead to the

Pacific coast, but one can journey to the cast by boat or canoe. 

4. Jungle natives. The Indians in the interior, where white men seldom come, are still almost savages. They wear little clothing and pierce their ears, through which they thrust heavy ornaments. But though they are only savages, they can guide the white man in their swift canoes where he would not dare to venture alone.

THE COASTAL LOWLANDS OF ECUADOR AND COLOMBIA 

(5) (8) By Ewing Galloway, N. Y. (6) (7) Pan American Union. 5. Cacao growing in Ecuador. The trees are planted quite close together so that their shade will protect the soil at their roots from the hot sun. The pods you see develop from small pink flowers. Six hundred flowers may produce only twenty pods. When ripe the pods are cut from the trees. 

6. Drying cacao beans in Ecuador. Here you see the cacao pods being cut open. Each pod holds from thirty to forty beans. They are placed on racks to dry in the sun. For three days the beans are turned over and over in the sun so that they may dry evenly. Cocoa and chocolate are made from cacao beans. 

7. The tagua palm and fruit. The plant grows in damp places. Its trunk is covered with large, melon-shaped pods full of tagua nuts. The young nut has a liquid not unlike coconut milk. In time this liquid becomes very hard and forms what is called vegetable ivory, a good substitute for real ivory. 

8. Weaving Panama hats. The toquilla plant has furnished the fiber which these women are using to make hats. The fiber becomes brittle in the heat of the sun, so that in making the finer hats the fiber is kept wet. 

Children help with the cheaper hats and sometimes make two in a day. The better hats take much longer to make. 

Most Panama hats are made in Ecuador and Colombia.

EASTERN PLAINS OF BOLIVIA, PERU, ECUADOR, AND COLOMBIA 

In eastern Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, and Colombia are lowland plains larger than the eastern half of our Mississippi basin. These plains are part of the hot, rainy Amazon and 

Orinoco basins and of the northern jungles of the Gran Chaco. They are cut off from the 

Pacific coast by the mountain barrier of the Andes. In surface, climate, life, and products they are so different from the high mountain sections of these Andes countries that they form a distinct region. It is a wild region, parts of which have been little explored and so are little known.

As the eastern plains of these countries are all in the Torrid zone, the climate is hot all through the year. It is not the 

CLIMATE 

kind of climate that makes one want to do much work, nor is it a healthful climate for white people. The plains, lying in the belt of calms, have a wet and a so-called dry season, though there is some rain all through the year. During the wet season, the rainfall is so heavy that some parts of the lowlands are under water, and people have to go about in boats. For this reason, the natives build their huts on stilts to keep them out of the flood waters. The climate here is very different from that of the western slopes of the Andes, where there is little or no rain. 

STREAMS 

Naturally, in a region of such heavy rainfall, streams are many. If you will look on the map on page 168, you will see that many rivers run through the lowland plains of Peru, Bolivia, Ecuador, and Colombia. Most of the streams are tributaries of the 

Amazon river. However, some of the rivers in Colombia flow into the Orinoco river, and some in Bolivia flow into the Paraguay river, a part of the Plata drainage system. 

On the Orinoco lowlands of Colombia there are grasslands, known as the llanos. There are grasslands on the plains of 

Peru and Bolivia, too. But

VEGETATION 

much of the surface of the lowland plains is covered with heavy, tropical forests-dense jungles hard to enter and as yet entirely uncultivated. This is especially true of the 

Ecuador plain. The jungle forests of southeastern Bolivia are in the unexplored and entirely undeveloped part of the Gran Chaco. 

There are few towns and few people in the eastern plains. Most of the inhabitants are pure Indians or mestizos. 

There are very few white

POPULATION 

people. In Peru, the people of the plains live in small towns along the banks of the rivers. 

The only town of any size is Iquitos, a center for the rubber industry. Iquitos is at the head of navigation on the Amazon river, a full two weeks' journey from the Atlantic ocean. In

Colombia, too, the few lowland towns, none of which are very important, lie along the rivers. Most of the settlements on Bolivia's plains are at the foot of the mountains. Eastern Ecuador has no settlements at all except for a few small Indian villages buried in the forests.

In the jungle forests of the Amazon basin and in the Chaco region of Bolivia there are savage Indians. Some of these tribes tattoo their bodies with bright colors and strange designs. Some use the deadly blowgun to shoot arrows tipped with poison. A few tribes arc headhunters who preserve the heads of their enemies as trophies.

TRANSPORTATION 

From the eastern plains only rough trails lead up the slopes of the Andes, and so to the Pacific ocean on the west. Once a road was started from La Paz in Bolivia over the mountains to the eastern plains, but it was never finished. The trails usually follow the streams and most of the travel is on foot. Articles are carried by Indians, or on the backs of llamas or small donkeys called burros. 209

SOUTH AMERICA 

Travel and transportation to the east are mainly by canoe or boat down the broad, long rivers. From the eastern plains of Colombia one can reach the Atlantic by way of the Orinoco river. From the eastern plains of 

Ecuador, Peru, and Bolivia one can sail down the tributaries and the main stream of the Amazon, some two thousand miles, to the port of 

Belem. Or one can reach Buenos Aires from the Bolivian plains by way of the Plata river system. There are also rail connections from 

Bolivia to Buenos Aires. But, at best, these eastern plains of Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, and

Colombia are a long, difficult, and expensive journey from the markets for their products. 

The forests of the eastern plains produce rubber, timber, and cinchona bark. The rub 

PRODUCTS 

ber is gathered by Indians, and most of it is brought to Iquitos to be shipped down the Amazon to Belem. It is the most important product of the region. 

Cinchona bark contains quinine which is a useful medicine for the fevers of the tropics. 

Cattle are raised on the grasslands. During the rains, when the lowlands are flooded, the cattle are taken to the lower slopes of the mountains. On the grasslands of Bolivia, at the foot of the Andes mountains, are raised potatoes, barley, oranges, bananas, sugar cane, coffee, and rice. They are carried on the backs of burros to the mining towns on the plateau. 

The lowland plains of Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, and Colombia have very fertile soil. 

Where the forests are cleared

FUTURE OF THE PLAINS 

away, the rich soil will produce many kinds of tropical products. Where the forests are left, the rubber industry can be developed. The lack of means of transportation now holds these regions back. Some day there may be railroads to the Pacific or it may be that the airplane will help in the development of this region. 

The air service between Lima and Iquitos is a first long step in that direction.

1. The eastern sections of Bolivia, Peru, Ecuador, and Colombia are lowland plains separated from the western sections by the Andes mountains. 

Much of these plains is little known and little developed. 

2. The climate of the eastern plains is hot, and there is a great deal of rain. There are large areas of forests and grasslands. There are many streams in this region. Although most of them are tributaries of the Amazon river, some of the rivers of 

Colombia drain into the Orinoco, and some in 

Bolivia flow into the Plata system. 

3. The population of the eastern plains consists mostly of Indians and mestizos. Some of the Indians are savage. There are few white people. 

4. The travel westward from the plains is over rough mountain trails. Goods are transported on the backs of Indians, or by burros and llamas. Travel and transportation eastward to the Atlantic ocean are mainly on the rivers. 

5. The forest products of the eastern plains are rubber, timber, and cinchona bark from which quinine is obtained. Cattle raising is an important occupation there.

THE COASTAL LOWLANDS OF ECUADOR AND WESTERN COLOMBIA 

In Peru and Chile the Pacific coastal plain of South America is just a narrow strip. In 

Ecuador the coastal plain 

WHERE DESERT broadens out, being in some places a little more than a hundred miles wide. In Colombia the plain grows narrower again. 

MEETS JUNGLE 

The narrow coastal plain of Peru and northern Chile is a naturally dry desert region. 

Just a short distance north of the Ecuador border, however, the plain changes from a desert to a tropical jungle which extends from the Gulf of Guayaquil to Panama. This is because Ecuador and Colombia are both crossed by the Equator and both lie in the belt of equatorial rainfall. 

Like the Amazon basin, Ecuador and Colombia have seasons of heavy rainfall and seasons of lighter rainfall, caused by CLIMATE the center of the belt of calms shifting north and south of the Equator with the shifting of the sun. At different times of year the sun is directly overhead in different parts of the Torrid zone. Along the western coast of the two countries, one rainy season occurs during the three months that the sun's direct rays are striking in the region of the Equator. 

Then, as the rain belt moves farther north toward the Tropic of Cancer, there is a socalled dry season, with lighter rainfall. Later, as the equatorial rain belt approaches the Equator on its southern trip, the coast of these countries has another rainy season. This is followed by a second dry season as the rain belt moves south of the Equator toward the 

Tropic of Capricorn.

Effects of the Climate. The equatorial rains make the land damp and swampy and the air moist. In such a warm, sticky climate people do not feel like working. As food is plentiful, and not much clothing is required, the few inhabitants of this region—mostly Indians-do not need to be very energetic, and so they are in a rather low state of development. 

Because of the heat, the swarms of insects, and the swamps, much of the coast land of Ecuador and Colombia is at present very unhealthful. However, since learning what has been done in the Panama canal zone to im-

Already the wide-awake, progressive government of Ecuador is improving conditions of health in that country. 

The coastal lowland, especially in Ecuador, has splendid alluvial soil. This lowland area is said to be the richest soil 

COASTAL PLAIN belt in all South America. PRODUCTS OF THE 

Cacao, the most important product of the jungle coast, is raised on plantations along the Guayas river, near Guayaquil. The cocoa made from Ecuador's cacao beans is of exceptionally fine quality. Cotton and sugar cane are two other products of increasing importance. Tropical fruits are raised in abundance, and find a nearby market in the towns of the West Coast desert of Chile and Peru. Fruit growing will increase rapidly with the clearing of the jungle and with the coming of more people. The Colombian coastal plain has fewer products. Tagua nuts from this district are exported. 

The tagua nut, called vegetable ivory, is an unusual product of the warm coastal plain of 

Ecuador and Colombia. The nuts grow in 211

SOUTH AMERICA 

long melon-shaped pods on a low jungle palm. 

The pods are opened and the nuts are separated from the fibers in which they are imbedded. The white inner part of the nuts is very hard and is used all over the world for making fine buttons, tiny chessmen, and a variety of other small articles. Its hardness and toughness make it a valuable substitute for ivory in inexpensive products.

In both Ecuador and Colombia are woven the fine light straw hats known as Panamas.

Native women and children, with their swift, skillful fingers, do most of the weaving. The 

Panama hats are made from fibers of the toquilla plant, a species of small palm tree. 

Although this plant grows in many countries of Central and South America, the finest fibers come from the hot, humid forests of Ecuador. 

As the hats made from this straw are light, soft, and durable, they find a good market in. many parts of the world. This is the only manufacturing done in Ecuador and Colombia outside the cities.

Coffee grown on the well-drained lower slopes of the Andes is famous for its delicate flavor. Colombian coffee,

PRODUCTS OF THE raised on these Andean LOWER MOUNTAIN SLOPES 

slopes, brings high prices in markets all over the world. Ecuador, too, is proud of the fine coffee she raises. It is mild in flavor and finds a ready market. Much of the land of western

Ecuador is suited to the growth of coffee, but the small population of the country has not yet taken full advantage of this fact. As the population grows, more and more of these lands will be taken up, and coffee raising will be a steadily increasing industry. Cattle, too, are raised on the lower mountain slopes of Ecuador. They supply meat, hides for shoes, and fats for soap.

Part of Colombia's lowland soil is rich in more ways than one. When the streams of Co- 

OF COLOMBIA 

lombia washed soil down

MINERAL WEALTH from the towering Andes, forming the coastal plain, they also washed down much gold. The Colombian plain is rich with gold nuggets. These do not have to be mined, but are simply "washed out," as was done with the gold found in California's streams. In the southern part of the Colombian west coast, platinum is found. Platinum is a mineral even more valuable than gold. Colombia usually leads the world in the production of this metal, though in some years Russia exports more than 

Colombia. Some coal is mined in Colombia, too, but conditions of climate make mining difficult. This industry promises future development.

BUENAVENTURA 

The chief seaport of the western coastal plain of Colombia is Buenaventura. From this port go coffee, gold, platinum, rubber, rubber, and tagua nuts. The city is likely to grow steadily busier as a port. For a long time it was cut off almost completely from the rest of the country by the sharply rising slopes of the Andes. Finally, a railroad was constructed over the mountains, making connections with the Magdalena valley and the north. The city is also on a transcontinental airplane route which connects it with the Caribbean port of Barranquilla and with many important South American cities.

A branch line extending inland connects it with Bogota, the capital. The planes carry mail, passengers, and light cargo. With these new facilities in transportation, Buenaventura will increase steadily in importance. 

GUAYAQUIL 

Guayaquil, the chief seaport of Ecuador, is the best possible example of a government's interest in sanitation. There was a long time when no vessels would stop at this port, as two terrible diseases were always to be found there. One was called bubonic plague, the other yellow fever. 

Then it was discovered that bubonic plague was spread by fleas, carried from house to house in the fur of rats, and that yellow fever germs were carried by mosquitoes. So Guayaquil was cleaned up. Rats were killed as rapidly as possible, and many old buildings and structures overrun with them were torn down. 

Streets and swamps were drained of standing water. As these breeding places for the yellow fever mosquito were destroyed, the insect pest was destroyed with them. A modern sewage system was built. Streets were widened and

COASTAL LOWLANDS OF ECUADOR AND COLOMBIA 

213 paved. At last the two terrible diseases became rare, and ships passing through the 

Panama canal began to make Guayaquil a regular port of call. 

Guayaquil is now one of the most healthful tropical cities. Through it are shipped the products of Quito, the capital city. Petroleum, from rich oil fields nearby, is another important export of Guayaquil. Guayaquil also has products of its own, for many of the raw materials from the surrounding lowlands and the lower mountain slopes are manufactured in the city and exported from its docks. Its chief manufactures include chocolate, soap, shoes, and candles. More and more white people will be tempted to live in 

Guayaquil, as they have in the city of Panama, now that its health conditions have been improved. What has been done in Guayaquil and Panama is being done in other South American cities and can be done elsewhere in the tropics.

All along the jungle coast of Ecuador and 

Colombia, swamps can be drained and made

THE JUNGLE 

into useful lands. Jungle

THE FUTURE OF growth which has no value can be thinned out, and plants and trees of commercial value can be planted in its place. As soon as these lowlands are made healthful, white people can live in them, and the jungle coastal plain will be turned into a really productive region. 

QUITO A COOL CITY ON THE EQUATOR 

From Guayaquil, in the hot, moist coastal lowlands, let us take a train to Quito, the capital city of Ecuador. This city is northeast of Guayaquil. As it is over a hundred miles nearer the Equator, in fact lying almost on it, we cannot help wondering just how much hotter it will be there than in Guayaquil. Our train soon begins to climb the western slopes of the Andes. We travel very slowly, as the grades are steep and the road very winding. We find it steadily growing cooler instead of warmer. As night approaches, we reach the city of Riobamba.

Here, to our surprise, we are asked to leave the train and pass the night in one of the city's hotels. Passenger trains do not travel at night over the dangerous grades and curves of the road.

The following morning we again board the train and once more we begin the slow steady climb, winding upward along the slopes of the Andes. We are approaching the Equator, but the height to which we are climbing makes the weather grow increasingly cooler and more comfortable. When we finally reach Quito, we find a city with a climate as moderate as our cities in the Temperate zone. Can this be

Quito, which our map shows us is almost on the Equator? Quito is cool because it is over nine thousand feet above sea level, and this high altitude offsets its zero latitude. It has taken us two days to make the trip-a distance of 297 miles! 

For an hour or two every day, Quito has rain. Most of the rest of the day, during the drier season, the weather is fair. People here are energetic and progressive. Most of Quito's streets are paved, and she has fine public buildings and an excellent university. Much manufacturing is carried on here, the chief products being made from Ecuador's own raw materials. Quito makes enough cotton goods to supply all the country's demands, as well as shoes and soap, which are cattle products, and chocolate from the cacao beans. Some of these products are exported through Guayaquil, the port for Quito.

On leaving Quito, we find that we must return to the coast the same way we came, as there is as yet only the one railroad connecting the capital with the coast. We are told, however, that a railroad is being built from the port of Esmeraldas to Quito, and another is under construction from Bahia de Caraquez to the capital. Each of these roads will provide a shorter route to the sea for the city's manufactures. Still another railroad is being planned from Bahia de Caraquez to Guayaquil. 

Where does the money come from to build these railroads? Who paid for the cleaning up of Guayaquil? These

ernment has even called in experts from the United States and Europe to help in its problems of sanitation and business management. What a a fine fine government this small republic of Ecuador has! As the population increases, we can expect to hear more and more of the progress of this Equator country.

1. Lying within the belt of equatorial rain, the coastal plain of Ecuador and western Colombia is a tropical jungle. 

2. The west coast of Colombia and Ecuador has two rainy and two so-called dry seasons. 

3. The coastal plain of Ecuador and western Colombia has very rich soil, but latitude and frequent rains make it hot, swampy, and unhealthful. As a result, the people are not energetic. 

4. On the lowlands and the lower mountain slopes of 

Ecuador are raised cacao, tagua nuts, cotton, sugar cane, tropical fruits, coffee, and cattle. On the 

Colombian plain and lower mountain slopes, coffee and tagua nuts are raised. Petroleum, gold, and platinum are the chief mineral resources. 

5. Buenaventura, the Pacific port of Colombia, is an outlet for the products of the jungle coast. A railroad connects the port with the interior, and a transcontinental airline and a branch line connect it with important South American cities. 

6. The government of Ecuador has already done much to make the chief seaport, Guayaquil, more healthful. Breeding places of disease carriers were destroyed, a modern sewage system was built, and streets were widened and paved. Guayaquil's chief manufactures are shoes, soap, chocolate, and candles. Petroleum is a leading export. 

7. Quito, situated almost on the Equator, is a splendid example of the effect of altitude. It has a temperate climate in spite of its latitude. The people are energetic and progressive. Considerable manufacture of local raw materials is done here. Guayaquil, the port of Quito, is connected with the capital city by a railroad. 

8. The progressive government of Ecuador, with headquarters at Quito, is planning more railroads, and has sought advice on sanitation and managing the business affairs of government from experts of the United States and Europe.

PACIFIC COUNTRIES SOME INTERESTING COMPARISONS A LONG COAST WITH FEW HARBORS 

From sultry Panama, 7° north of the Equator, to wind-swept Tierra del Fuego, 56° south latitude, stretches the long Pacific coast of South America. 

Throughout almost its entire length of 4500 miles the coast line is regular. Only along the dreary southern part of the continent are there any very great irregularities in the shore line.

Because of the evenness of the coast, the 

Pacific seaports of South America are few, and those having good harbors are even fewer. 

Valparaiso, with almost no natural harbor, Callao, Guayaquil, and Buenaventura are the only important ports. Ships anchoring at these cities are protected from storms and waves by great walls called breakwaters, built out from shore into the sea. These walls provide docking space for the ships. But

This coast is quite unlike the Atlantic coast of South America, where a number of deep estuaries and bays provide some of the best natural harbors in the world. It is also unlike the Pacific coast of North America. Except for the Gulf of California, it is true that there are no great irregularities along the southern part of North America's Pacific coast. there are a number of fine harbors and on some of them there are several seaports. 

There is San Francisco bay, around which lie a whole group of ports. Portland, Oregon, although on a river, can be reached by great ocean liners. In the still waters behind Vancouver island are the busy ports of Vancouver, Victoria, Seattle, and Tacoma. North of the 

50° parallel the North American Pacific coast is more irregular. The ragged coast line of 

Alaska has numerous natural harbors, and several seaports. Nature has been kinder to 

North America than to South America in the matter of natural west coast harbors. 

It is interesting to note the many ways in which the Pacific regions of South America are like those along the Pacific coast of North 

America. In Ecuador and

Colombia there are hot THE PACIFIC 

coastal jungles, very similar REGIONS OF to the narrow strip of jungle SOUTH AMERICA coast in Central America and

NORTH AND 

Mexico. The products of these regions are much the same. Both raise coffee and tropical fruits, and in addition the South American jungle produces cacao. 

Parts of the West Coast desert of Peru and northern Chile may be compared to the 

Sonoran desert of Mexico and the Mohave desert and Death valley in the United States, except that North America has no section like 

Chile's Atacama desert with its rich nitrate deposits. In the southern part of the Peruvian desert we find river oases and irrigated lands in a desert region not unlike the Imperial valley of California, where irrigation has turned a desert into fertile farming lands. Like the Imperial valley, this section produces cotton, fruits, and grains.

The Central valley of Chile is like the western coastal valleys of the United States. Here some irrigation is necessary, as in the San 

Bernardino and Great valleys of California.

The products of these two Californian valleys are practically the same as those of the northern and central parts of the Central valley of 

Chile-fruits, vegetables, and grains.

In climate and products, the southern part of Chile's Central valley resembles the Willamette-Puget sound valley of North America. In both these regions apples grow abundantly and such fruits as strawberries, raspberries, and loganberries are raised in quantities. The forested area of Chile, just south of the Central valley, is like the forested western slopes of British Columbia. 

There is one interesting thing to remember about the Pacific products of South America.

The crops vary with altitude quite as much as with latitude. Crops raised not far from the 215

SOUTH AMERICA 

coast on the mountain slopes of tropic South America vary, as one ascends, from those of a hot climate to the hardy grains and vegetables of more temperate latitudes. 

Even the fiord coast regions of North and South America have some similarities. Both have broken shore lines with many bays, straits, and offshore islands. The great difference between these regions, however, lies in their climates. While the fiord coast of southern South America is chilled by the icy Peruvian current sweeping up from the Antarctic, and is lashed by the storms of the blustering "roaring forties," the Alaskan fiord coast of

North America is warmed by the Japan current and so has a much warmer climate. 

Though the South American Pacific regions are in some ways very like the western regions of our own continent, they are very different in others. The southern continent is a land of greater extremes. In South America, dense, moist jungles give way to the hottest and most barren of deserts. The desert, a little farther south, becomes a garden spot as the result of glacial streams and irrigation. 

This sunny region leads to a forested area. 

Then comes the cold, windy, rain-washed region of southern Chile. Throughout the length of our own Pacific coast, the changes from one region to another are less abrupt, the temperatures milder, and the climates in general better suited to human life. 

Though not so broad as the Rockies, the Andes are the highest mountains of the West- 

TRADE BARRIER 

ern hemisphere. Their high 

THE ANDES-A est peaks are thousands of feet taller than the tallest of the Rockies, and nearly four times as high as the highest mountain in the Appalachian system. Extending north and south, they are the only ranges on the two American continents with no breaks in an east and west direction well fitted for the building of railway lines. 

The Appalachians have water gaps where rivers have made deep valleys, and along these the railroads wind. One of the passes, the 

Mohawk pass, is only 450 feet above sea level at its highest point. Even the Rockies have a few low passes which the rivers have cut. 

Through these, and through a few of the higher passes, railroads have been built. 

How different are the Andes! Except in the lonely southern part of the continent, where rail communication is not of great importance, the passes are 10,000 to 15,000 feet high-as high as the highest peaks in the United 

States! Yet one can cross the Andes by railroad. From Buenos Aires the Transandine line crosses directly to Valparaiso. The railroad from Buenos Aires to La Paz, with connecting lines to the Pacific coast cities of 

Antofagasta, Arica, and Mollendo, makes a more roundabout trip across the mountains. 

The building of these roads was an enormously difficult and expensive task and the cost of operating them is very great indeed. 

So we can never expect cheap railroad transportation from central South America to the west coast ports.

The Andes have a strange effect on South America's Pacific countries. Though they rob the Pacific countries of TREASURE HOUSE revenue by shutting off east THE ANDES A 

and west commerce, they add to the wealth of these countries by giving them mineral resources. Like the 

Western highlands of our own continent, they are rich with stored minerals-gold, silver, platinum, copper, tin, and iron. The extent of the mineral wealth of the Andes will not be known until the continent is more thickly settled and the Pacific countries of South America take an active interest in locating the minerals and working mines. Up to the present, the opening and working of mines has been done largely with United States money.

Through nearly half the length of the Andes, the crests of the mountains form a political boundary between the countries of 

Argentina and Chile. A POLITICAL ONE 

Though the mountains

America is almost unbroken. It has few natural harbors. Except in the north, the Pacific coast of

North America is largely regular, but it has several fine bays and harbors.

America. Ecuador and western Colombia have a hot jungle coast similar to the jungle coast in 

Central America and Mexico. 

3. Parts of the West Coast desert of Peru and Chile are similar to the Sonoran and Mohave deserts and to Death valley in North America. The Chilean part of the desert has rich nitrate beds which are not found in any North American region. In southern Peru we find irrigated lands like those of 

Imperial valley.

1. Tell how the Andes are a trade barrier. 

5. The fiord coast of southern South America is washed by the cold Peruvian current. It is very different in climate from that of the Alaskan panhandle, which is warmed by the Japan current. 

6. The Appalachians in the eastern part of North 

America are cut by rivers along whose valleys many railways wind. The Rocky mountains have a few low passes and also some quite high passes through which railways have been built. The Andes are much higher than either of these ranges. The passes through them are between 10,000 and 15,000 feet high, so that rail communication is not yet extensively developed. 

7. The Rockies and the Andes are both rich in minerals. In South America most of the mines now in operation have been opened and worked with the aid of outside capital.

Argentina and Chile. In Ecuador and Colombia they form a barrier, separating the western coasts of these countries from their eastern plains. 

EXERCISE 

2. How do the Andes help South America? How do they hinder her progress?

Pacific coast which are similar to three regions of the North American Pacific coast. How are they similar?

The northern shores of both Venezuela and Colombia lie along the Caribbean sea. The 

Guianas are east of these countries and are bordered, not by the Caribbean, but by the 

Atlantic ocean. All these northern lands are so much alike in climate and general conditions that they really form one region. 

The Guianas are the only countries in South America which are not independent republics. 

British Guiana belongs to Eng- 

THE GUIANAS 

land, Dutch Guiana to the 

Netherlands, and French Guiana to France.

Commercially, they are not important to the other nations of the world because most of the products they raise are either used at home or are sent to the mother countries. 

Most of the goods they need, too, are bought from the nations that own them.

Climate. The Guianas lie between 1° and 8° north of the Equator and so are in the Torrid zone. Their climate is very hot and moist and is unhealthful for white people. All three of the Guianas, therefore, have small populations. 

Because the northeast trade winds, blowing over the Atlantic ocean, bring some relief from the heat, most of the people live along the coast.

The trade winds also bring a good deal of rain. Reaching the Guiana highlands, the winds rise, become cooler, and drop still more moisture. This increases the already heavy rainfall of the Guianas. Guiana is an Indian name meaning well-watered country, and the Guianas are certainly that. They have two seasons-a rainy season, and a less rainy season. During the season of heaviest rains, the low lands are often flooded. This makes drainage by canals or pumps necessary. 

Surface. Along the Atlantic coast of the 

Guianas, there are coastal plains. The soil of these plains is very rich from the deposits 

Back of the washed down by the rivers. coastal plains are low, densely forested hills. 

The forests merge into broad grasslands or savannas. Still farther back from the ocean are the Guiana highlands. The great amount of rain in these hot lands is responsible for the thick forests and the plant life that grows riotously over everything. Even the lakes and streams have abundant plant life, for spreading over their surfaces are giant water lilies with lily pads six feet or more across. 

There are many rivers flowing into the Atlantic ocean through the Guianas. Though all of them are small, a few are navigable by river steamers and most of them by light boats and canoes. These rivers form the only practical means of reaching the interior from the coast. 

In the center of British Guiana, in one of the rivers, are the beautiful Kaieteur falls, which are about five times as high as our Niagara falls.

Products. All of the crops that are produced in the Guianas are grown on the coastal plains and along the rivers. The most important crop is sugar, which is raised on plantations along the Atlantic coast. Rice is the second crop in importance. Two rice crops are grown each year, one in the spring and one in the fall. Coffee and cacao are exported. 

Coconuts, maize, yams, and bananas and other fruits are among the foods which are grown for the use of the Guiana people. There are also some cattle raised on the grasslands.

In the forests along the river valleys and on the slopes of the hills, there are rubber trees. 

The forests are rich in timber, too, including such fine cabinetwoods as crabwood and hard cedar. There are also balata trees, from which is gathered the gum that is used to insulate, or cover, electric wires. 

There is some gold in the mountains. 

Placer mining of gold is carried on in British Guiana. Each year this country also exports thousands of diamonds from its diamond mines. In British and Dutch Guiana, bauxite, or aluminum ore, is found along the rivers, and some is exported. The forests and the moun- 218

1. A Bush negro of Dutch Guiana. This savage has come into Paramaribo to trade. There are about ten thousand of these negroes in the country. They are descended from slaves who long ago escaped into the Dutch Guiana jungles. They make their living by trading articles which they carve out of wood for food, knives, and other things they need. 

2. Kaieteur falls, in British Guiana. There are many cataracts in the rivers that run through the Guianas, but these are the most wonderful of them all. This river pours over the steep cliff and falls in an almost unbroken cascade to the bottom of the deep valley more than eight hundred feet below. The waterfall is five times as high as the falls of Niagara, but Niagara is more than ten times as wide. This is only one of the many waterfalls of the continent which may some day supply power for great industries. 

3. A donkey train in Venezuela. The pack train is wending its way down the mountains. The donkeys have been loaded with coffee and cacao at the mountain town of Caracas and they are carrying their loads down to La Guaira to be shipped from there. 

4. A Venezuelan oil well. This well, drilled thirty feet under water in Lake Maracaibo, is a strange sight. It is connected by pipes with a tank on shore, and produces an excellent quality of oil. Venezuela is becoming one of the important oil-exporting countries of the world.

5. An irrigated farm in Colombia. We see in this picture a number of farm workmen cultivating tobacco. 

They are working by hand, with very simple tools. The yoke of oxen in the background draws the plows and wagons of the farm. Labor is cheap in almost all of 

South America, and very little expensive farm machinery has been introduced except on the large banana and coffee plantations. 

6. An old Spanish church in Cartagena. A number of 

South American cities were founded and built by the Spaniards long before the English settled in North America. Cartagena is the oldest of them all. It has many very old buildings. Like most Spanish buildings in tropical lands, this church has thick walls to keep out the heat. 

The interior is cool even on the hottest days. 

7. A steamer at a landing on the Magdalena river. The

Magdalena has always been a main artery of trade from the interior of Colombia to the sea. Only steamers with small draft, like this, can navigate its upper waters, but for 600 miles from Barranquilla it is open to good-sized ships.
