British Guiana. British Guiana is the most important of the Guiana countries, yet of its 250 miles of coastal plain only a small part is cultivated. Although it is about the size of 

Great Britain, British Guiana has only 308,000 inhabitants. Of these, 57,000 live in Georgetown, the capital. There are comparatively few white people. The many East Indians and negroes are lazy and backward, using primitive methods in raising their crops. Both of these races are able to stand the hot climate of the lowlands because it is similar to that of their home lands in the East Indies and Africa.

In the forests are wild, native Indians much like the tribes of the Amazon basin. 

Dutch Guiana. Dutch Guiana is not so large as British Guiana, but it has about the same length of coast line. Its population is about 

143,000. There are a few white people, the rest being largely East Indians and negroes.

In the interior are the Bush negroes, who are descendants of runaway slaves. Once a year they come to Paramaribo, the capital of Dutch

Guiana, to exchange articles they have carved out of wood for clothing, food, knives, and other things they need. 

French Guiana. Like the other two colonies,

French Guiana is little developed and has very few white people. In fact, French Guiana has by far the smallest population of any of the 

Caribbean countries. Of the 47,000 inhabitants, 14,000 live in the capital, Cayenne.

France uses French Guiana as a prison colony to which she sends her worst criminals. this hot, unhealthful land the convicts are put to work mining gold.

In 

West of the Guianas lies Venezuela, the first part of South America to be seen by a

VENEZUELA 

white man. Christopher Columbus visited its shores on his third voyage to the New World. Some Spaniards later sailed along the shores of Lake Maracaibo and saw villages of Indian huts built above the water on piles. Because the villages reminded them of the island city of Venice, Italy, they named the land Venezuela, which means "Little Venice."

NASA-15 

Except for a small lowland area lying around Lake Maracaibo, the western part of 

Venezuela consists of high ranges of the 

Andes, separated by plateaus and valleys. A spur of the Andes also runs along most of the coast, separating the interior of the country from the sea. As this mountain spur is 9000 feet in height and rises steeply from the Caribbean, it presents a real barrier to travel and transportation. The interior of Venezuela is made up mainly of the low, flat plains of the 

Orinoco river basin. South and east of the 

Orinoco basin is a section of plateaus belonging to the Guiana highlands. Unlike the broad, level Central plateau of the Brazilian highlands, these plateaus of the Guiana highlands are small and are cut by steep, jungle-filled valleys.

There is great timber and mineral wealth in the highlands and mountains of Venezuela. There are deposits of gold, copper, iron, and some coal. However, these sections of the country are so difficult to reach that their resources have been little developed. In the 

Guiana highlands, development is obstructed by heat, tropical forests, and a lack of navigable rivers. Lack of transportation also prevents the development of the mineral and forest wealth of the Andes. 

Climate. As Venezuela lies between 1° and 

12° north latitude, it is entirely in the Torrid zone. It is, therefore, very hot in the lowlands and in the river valleys. But on the mountain slopes and plateaus of the Andes the temperature is cooler. For this reason the mountain region is the most densely populated section of the country. 

Like the Guianas, Venezuela is in the belt of the northeast trade winds that bring so much rain from the sea. The winds, rising over the coast mountains and the Guiana highlands, drop their moisture as heavy rains. 

Coming down to the heated grasslands of the 

Orinoco river basin the winds are warmed, and less rain falls. But as the trade winds go up over the cool slopes of the Andes mountains beyond the basin the rainfall is heavy again.

Cities. La Guaira, the chief seaport of

SOUTH AMERICA 

Venezuela, lies at the base of a mountain. It is called the gateway to Venezuela, as most of the steamers coming to Venezuela from the

United States and Europe dock there. La 

Guaira is connected by rail with Caracas, the capital of Venezuela, and serves as the port for that city.

Besides being the capital, Caracas is the center of trade for the interior of the country. 

It is situated about 4000 feet above sea level in a beautiful valley set in the midst of mountains. The railroad which leads up to it follows one of the most beautiful scenic routes of the world. The actual distance between La 

Guaira and Caracas is eight miles. But the railroad between the two cities winds for twenty-three miles through tunnels and over bridges. As the train climbs higher, one glimpses every now and then the blue Caribbean far below, or the lofty mountain peaks towering high above. There is a fine motor road, too, between Caracas and the coast, and the views from it are unsurpassed. 

Because the climate of Caracas is cool and agreeable, many white people live there. The soil of the valley is fertile, and all about are coffee and cacao groves, sugar plantations, and lemon and orange trees. The sugar is raised for local use, but the coffee and cacao are exported in large quantities. Cacao from 

Caracas is used in Europe for making a fine quality of chocolate.

The Orinoco Valley. Venezuela has numer ous rivers and streams with 6000 miles of navigable waterway. The Orinoco river, the third largest river in South America, rises in the Guiana highlands, and, after making an almost complete semicircle towards the north and east through the center of the country, finally flows into the Atlantic ocean. At its mouth it divides into several smaller streams, forming a broad delta. It is navigable for over 700 miles, and large steamers go up to the river port of Ciudad Bolivar, 230 miles from the coast.

In spite of the fact that the lower Orinoco and its tributaries are navigable for so many miles, the river system is not a busy route of travel and transportation. This is due to the fact that the valley through which the river flows has great stretches of grasslands, called llanos, which are flooded during the rainy season and very dry at other times. There is little to attract people to this warm lowland region and not many white people live here, the few inhabitants being mostly Indians and negroes.

The only industry that could be carried on extensively in the Orinoco valley is cattle raising, and the hot, dry season withers the grass so that even that occupation is difficult. 

Another difficulty preventing the development of the cattle industry in the Orinoco valley is the problem of getting good labor. The Indians and negroes, who might do the work because they are accustomed to the climate, are lazy by nature and because of the heat. 

The mestizos are not anxious to work either. 

So, although cattle and goats are raised in other parts of the country and their hides are an important export of Venezuela, the industry has not developed to any great extent in the Orinoco basin.

Asphalt. Opposite the delta of the Orinoco river is the British island of Trinidad. Here there is a great lake of asphalt, a substance used in paving streets. Asphalt is also found in Venezuela on the shores of Lake Maracaibo, and many thousand tons are exported to the United States each year.

There are rich petroleum wells, too, around Lake Maracaibo. The crude oil is piped from the wells to the seaport of Mara-

caibo. Most of it is shipped to the nearby 

Dutch island of Curacao to be refined. Venezuela is second to the United States among petroleum-producing countries, and petroleum is its leading export.

The Future of Venezuela. Much of Vene zuela is still undeveloped. Transportation into the interior needs to be improved so that her forest and mineral wealth may be of benefit to her. She has a fortunate position in relation to the trade route through the Panama canal, and as more foreign capital is invested to develop her natural resources she will probably progress rapidly in years to come. 

Colombia, also in the belt of the northeast trade winds, has 600 miles of coast along the shore of the Caribbean sea. This

COLOMBIA 

is a country of plains and mountains. We have studied its western coastal lowlands and its eastern plains that lie in the 

Amazon and Orinoco basins. 

The grasslands of the Orinoco basin lie between the Guiana highlands and the Andes mountains. Although well fitted for cattle raising, they are but little used. This is partly due to the fact that there are only native Indians in the upper part of the Orinoco valley. But it is due more to the lack of transportation. At present the Orinoco river is not navigable for large steamers as far as the Colombian border because of sand bars and rapids. If the river could be opened to transportation as far as the grasslands of 

Colombia, cattle raising would probably be developed as an industry. Some cattle are raised in other parts of the country, and cattle and hides are important exports. 

Magdalena Valley. The Andes mountains in 

Colombia are divided into three ranges. Between the eastern and central ranges, extending north and south, is the Magdalena valley. 

Through it runs the Magdalena river, nearly 

1000 miles in length, a great highway for transportation and travel. The river has many tributaries, of which the largest is the 

Cauca, which also runs between ranges of the 

Andes, joining the Magdalena 200 miles from its mouth. The Magdalena is navigable for good sized boats for 600 miles. Small boats can go up its course 300 miles farther.

The plateau regions in the Andes are reached by river boats going up the Magdalena river. 

Crops. Colombia lies in the Torrid zone, but, while the climate in the lowlands is very hot, the highlands are temperate. This variety of temperature gives Colombia a variety of crops. Tropical fruits, especially bananas, are grown along the hot Caribbean coast and in the river valleys. Here, too, there are plantations of coffee, rice, sugar cane, cotton, and cacao. In the temperate highlands, wheat, corn, barley, maize, peaches, apples, and pears are grown. Much coffee is exported. 

Minerals. In the highlands of Colombia are valuable deposits of gold, silver, platinum, emeralds, copper, and coal. Owing to scarcity of labor and insufficient means of transportation little has so far been done with most of these resources, although platinum and emeralds are exported in valuable quantities. 

Platinum is found mixed with other metals in the stream beds. It is more valuable than gold. Emeralds are mined on the plateaus, and are sent to New York and London to be cut and polished. Petroleum is another most valuable export. The oil fields are on the 

Magdalena river. The oil is piped to Barranquilla and is shipped from there. 

Cities. Bogota, the capital of Colombia, is the distributing center for the products of the interior. It is over 8000 feet above sea level, on a plateau among the mountains. The city has such a wonderful climate, like continual spring, that white people can live there comfortably. The population is largely of Spanish descent. Beautiful flowers-roses, lilies, and violets-grow in profusion. The fields of the country about are planted with the crops of a temperate climate. 

To reach Bogota one goes first to Barranquilla, the river port of the Magdalena valley, ten miles from the ocean. Regular steamships start from Barranquilla and go up the river to Girardot. From this point a railroad runs to the capital city. This trip takes from seven to ten days. If one wishes to travel more quickly, one may go from Barranquilla to

SOUTH AMERICA 

Girardot in two days by seaplane, and then by train up the mountain to Bogota. 

The Muzo emerald mines, which are owned by the government, are near Bogota. Emeralds and other products of the interior go from Bogota down the railroad and the river to 

Barranquilla and on to the ocean port, Puerto

Colombia, where they are shipped to the 

United States and Europe. Goods cannot be exported directly from Barranquilla because the Magdalena river has sand bars at its mouth which prevent ocean steamers from going up to the river city. A passage is now being dredged through them to the sea.

Sixty miles east of the mouth of the Magdalena river is the seaport of Santa Marta.

An American company has huge banana plantations all along the coast near Santa Marta. 

The bananas, when ready to ship, are sent on trolleys or flat cars to the seaport for shipment to the United States and to Europe. 

West of the Magdalena river on the Caribbean sea is Cartagena, the oldest Spanish city in South America. It was built in 1533 and still has many romantic reminders of its early Spanish origin. The same old forts are still there that protected it when it was a stronghold on the Spanish Main, as the Caribbean was then called. The great wall that the 

Spaniards built around the city also remains. 

Even the houses, with their balconies and iron-barred windows, are of Spanish style, be-

2. British Guiana belongs to England, Dutch Guiana to the Netherlands, and French Guiana to France. 

3. On account of the climate, which is very hot with heavy rains, the population of the Guianas is sparse. Trade is mostly with the mother countries.

OUR INTEREST IN THE CARIBBEAN COUNTRIES 

The Caribbean countries are nearer to our land than any other of the South American countries. Steamers from 

New York reach Colombia and Venezuela in a week's time. We need their coffee, cacao, tropical fruits, petroleum, and metals, and they want our textiles, manufactured goods, machinery, wheat, corn, flour, and other foodstuffs. With the opening of the Panama canal, these Caribbean countries were brought in closer touch with our western ports. Until recent years, Europe had the bulk of the trade with most of the South American countries, but the United States is now becoming a strong rival for the Caribbean trade as well as for that of other parts of the continent.

Some American capital is already invested in the large banana plantations and in the oil industry of the Caribbean countries. Railroads and highways are needed in these lands, and the skill of our engineers, as well as our capital, can help to make transportation there a less difficult problem. With easier ways of getting their produce to the ports of shipment, there will be a greater development of their natural resources. The United States will then profit by increased trade with South America's northern countries.

SOUTH AMERICAN SIGHTS AND PEOPLE 

1. The Christ of the Andes. This statue stands in a mountain pass between Chile and Argentina. It was erected by the people of both countries as a monument to their agreement never to go to war with each other. 

Though the people of the South American countries are of very nearly the same national descent, the continent has been torn by many wars. This promise of peace between two of the leading South American nations is a long step in the right direction. 

2. Scene in the Amazon basin, a region hard to conquer. 

Floods, dense jungles, terrific heat, poisonous insects and plants, and dangerous wild animals have prevented large numbers of white people from settling in the Amazon basin. It is one of the least known regions of the world. 

3. A copper mine in the Andes. South America has enormous mineral wealth. Some of it is being used, as we see in this picture. Most of it, however, remains undeveloped because of lack of transportation, capital, and workmen. 

4. Branding a horse in Argentina. The gaucho, or cowboy, is stamping with a hot iron the owner's mark on a riding pony. The gauchos look after immense herds of cattle on the rich grazing lands of the Pampas. 

5. Sifting meal after grinding. This Indian woman makes her own flour. Most of the native people of 

South America are backward and uneducated. They have little money for trade, and must do almost all things for themselves.

PROBLEMS AND ACHIEVEMENTS OF SOUTH AMERICA

6. Rio de Janeiro, seen from a nearby mountain. The east coast of South America has several good harbors.

That of Rio is one of the best in the world. At some of these harbors, large cities have grown up to take care of shipping the continent's raw materials and receiving its imports. There are, however, no great manufacturing cities like those of the United States. 

7. A lighter loaded with hides at a west-coast seaport. 

The west coast of South America has few good natural harbors. In many places cargoes must be taken to and from the ships in lighters like this one. Such handling is slow and costly and interferes with trade. 

8. A railway trestle in the Andes mountains. The Andes make traffic between the eastern and western countries of South America very difficult, and prevent efficient transportation in many parts of the highland. The cost of building and running railroads through the Andes is very great. 

9. Brazilian mule teams hauling coffee to the seacoast.

Coffee is one of the South American products which are bought in large quantities by the United States. The money which Brazil receives for this coffee will be spent for American automobiles, machinery, shoes, textiles, and other manufactured goods which Brazil needs. 

10. Headquarters of the Pan American Union, at Washington, D. C. This society of North and South American republics was established to promote friendship and service among its members.

THE SOUTH AMERICAN CONTINENT AS A WHOLE 

225 South America are used to their fullest extent.

A LAND WITH 

Although these regions are

TOO FEW PEOPLE the most productive areas of 

South America, they have too few people to work the land intensively. 

The population has also tended to divide itself into two classes, the wealthy, educated group, mostly white, and the uneducated laboring class, largely a mixed race descended from the white, the Indian, and the negro races. Lacking energy, these people are fairly well satisfied with life as it is, so long as there is enough to eat and a shelter from the rains. 

There are rich mineral deposits in all the highland regions of the continent. Some are 

UNDEVELOPED RESOURCES 

worked a little, but many lie untouched. American engineers, supplied with money from the United States, are using modern methods and machinery to work a few of the deposits. Here and there the wealthy classes of South America are beginning to realize that it would be greatly to their advantage if they were to work these rich mines themselves, so they are sending their sons to North American colleges and universities to study to become mining engineers. 

With all her great mineral wealth, South

America lacks one most important resourcecoal. The few deposits to be found on the continent produce coal of a poor grade. The lack of good coal is one factor which has hindered the mining of other minerals, for coal is needed to smelt the ores. The growth of manufacturing, too, has been hindered by the shortage of coal.

Even though little coal is mined in South 

America, manufacturing has a great future there. The many mighty waterfalls can be used to generate electricity to drive machinery in countless factories. There is an almost unlimited supply of raw material. It is possible that South America may some day become one of the great manufacturing sections of the world.

But the minerals and water power are not South America's only undeveloped resources. 

In all South American countries, vast stretches of land lie idle or partly cultivated, waiting for men to turn them into rich farms. With more intensive farming, grain lands like those of Argentina and Uruguay could be made to produce several times their present yield. 

Also, great pasture lands, in such regions as the Central plateau and the Orinoco llanos, are waiting for herds.

Probably the least developed resource of this great continent is the most important one of all-the working people. South America has a large population of Indians, many of whom are uncivilized dwellers of the jungle. 

The Indians, mestizos, and negroes receive low wages because at present they are capable of only unskilled labor. Schools are needed that will teach them about other sections of their own land, better ways of living, more intelligent ways of working, and modern methods of farming and coöperative marketing. So far, only the wealthy land-owning classes have the advantages of education. 

Most of the great masses can neither read nor write. When the farmers are able to buy land of their own which they can work with the pride of ownership, they will have better standards of living.

Skilled labor is scarce all over South America, and it will take time to develop a trained working class. By educating her children and taking in only the best of the people who are knocking at her doors for admission, South America can in time realize her great possibilities.

The regions of South America likely to be developed first are those with the best governments and the largest white populations. 

Strong governments will do much to improve transportation, sanitation, education, manufacturing, and progress in mining. A white population is more energetic, more easily educated, and more anxious to get ahead than a population of mixed blood. The governments of several of the South American countries are already taking steps to give better education to their people. These countries are showing real improvement in farming, manufacturing, and commerce. Central Chile, with its water power and natural wealth; Argentina and

Uruguay, with their splendid farm lands and

SOUTH AMERICA 

white populations; Brazil, with its rich coffee sections and its fine grazing lands; Ecuador, with its cacao lands; and Venezuela, with its rich natural resources, should be the regions to develop the most rapidly. 

A few South American cities now rank or are beginning to rank with the important cities of the world. Rio de

GROWING CITIES 

Janeiro ships the products of the rich surrounding land from her wonderful port. Recife is the great sugar port, and 

Belem the great rubber port, of Brazil. São

Salvador exports great quantities of cacao. 

São Paulo, through her port of Santos, ships more coffee than any other place in all the world.

Argentina's capital, Buenos Aires, is growing rapidly because of its fine harbor and the railroads that bring Pampas food products to the city for export. Bahia Blanca and Rosario are also railroad centers and ports for foreign commerce. 

Montevideo, as the chief outlet for Uruguayan products, has a promising future. On the west coast, Valparaiso and Callao, the leading ports of Chile and Peru, are rapidly growing cities. In Ecuador, Guayaquil's fine harbor, now that the city is healthful, will continue to bring her riches. The fact that South 

America's greatest cities are seaports shows that much of her population lives on or near the coast. 

A few inland cities, too-Quito, Bogota, and Santiago, for instance-are developing and are showing great promise for the future.

Most of the great cities of the continent, whether on the coast or inland, have grown because nearby are raw materials of one kind or another. Better transportation will do much for every one of them. Manufacturing, as it progresses with the training of skilled labor, will increase the development of these already important cities.

TRANSPORTATION 

Inland transportation in South America is very difficult. Only on the level Pampas and the plains of Uruguay can railroads be laid at all easily. 

In most of the other sections of South America where there are railroads at all, they run inland from the coast for only short distances. The matted vines and trees which grow with such amazing rapidity, the swamps, and the unhealthful climate are only a few of the problems that prevent railroad building through the jungle. The Andes, too, form an almost unconquerable barrier to east and west transportation. The engineering problems of building the few roads that do cross the mountains were tremendous, and the cost of running trains over their rails makes travel and the shipment of goods over the Andes very expensive.

Because of the mountains and dense jungles, road building, too, is very difficult in many parts of South America. Even on the open plains, where the land is level, the shortage of stone and other road-building materials hinders the work and adds to the expense. 

The Amazon river will undoubtedly always be one of the main South American trade routes, especially if its floods are controlled and settlements and fueling stations are established along its banks. Other rivers of the continent will increase in importance as the lands through which they flow are more intensively cultivated. 

Seaplanes, using the rivers as landing fields, can pass quickly over prairies and even mountains, linking all parts of the continent. Air lines are increasing and may some day solve many of South America's problems.

In spite of her many products and numerous seaports, South America is still at some disadvantage in trading with other nations, be- FOREIGN TRADEcause of her location in DISADVANTAGES AND ADVANTAGES 

and south of the Torrid zone. Most of her foreign markets are far distant from her seaports and it costs both time and money to carry products to them. 

However, she has much that is needed by the countries of the northern continents-products which they cannot raise for themselves. In addition to her foreign trade she is developing coastwise trade between her own countries. 

This will increase as they manufacture more of their raw materials into finished articles. 

North and South America have come to de-

THE SOUTH AMERICAN CONTINENT AS A WHOLE 

227 pend more and more on each other's trade. 

Ships between the two continents need not cross wide oceans, and have many profitable stopping places on the way back and forth. 

The Panama canal has also made trade very easy between the west coast of South America and the east coasts of both continents. The tropical climate of most of South America gives it products which North America needs: coffee and rubber, sugar, bananas, Brazil nuts, tagua nuts, and quebracho extract. We also buy petroleum, nitrates, copper, gold, platinum, diamonds, manganese, and hides. Our great industries supply South America with automobiles, machinery, textiles, and other manufactures. We also send her coal.

Europe also has a great trade with South 

America. European seaports are as near Rio de Janeiro and Buenos Aires as American seaports are, and Brazil and Argentina have more trade with Europe than with the United States. Crowded Europe needs foods and has manufactured goods to sell. These facts make 

South America truly a land of promise. With many markets waiting for her products, she is able to sell all she can raise and transport. 

Her commercial growth, therefore, is sure. 

THE PAN AMERICAN 

To encourage more friendly relations between the Americas, especially in commerce, the Pan American Union has been formed, with headquarters in 

Washington. Pan means all, and UNION the Union lives up to its name in working toward a closer All-American relationship. Through the efforts of this Union, we are learning more and more about South America, her problems of government, health, transportation, and agriculture. In the same way, South America is becoming better acquainted with North America, and is learning from her the advantages of a well-educated working class, better methods of sanitation, and the importance of manufacturing in a country rich with raw products. So, as time goes on, stronger and more friendly ties are being made between the two great continents of the Western hemisphere.

2. Using a map of the world, estimate the approximate distance in miles from Rio de Janeiro to New

Panama canal.

EQUATOR 

Greenwich 45° 30° 15° 60° UTH 

MER I CA 

30. 15° This is a picture of the globe, drawn to show North America, South America, the North Pole, and the lands around the Pole. Find the line labeled "Equator". This line runs around the earth midway between the North and South Poles. 

The lines around the earth parallel to the Equator are called parallels of latitude. Notice that the Equator is the 0° parallel and that the other parallels are numbered north and south from it. Only a few of the parallels are shown on this map.

There are other lines running around the earth. These cross the Equator at equal distances apart and pass through the North and South Poles. They are called meridians of longitude. Notice that they are farthest apart at the 

Equator and that they draw nearer together as they approach the Poles. Find the meridian labeled 0°. Through what town does it pass? Notice that the meridians are numbered east and west from the 0° meridian. 228

PARALLELS AND MERIDIANS 

We have all read about heroic rescues at sea. From the wide stretches of the ocean comes a radio call for help, "S.O.S.", and at once ships start to the aid of some sinking vessel that may be hundreds of miles away. How do the rescuers know where to look? How can they find a sinking ship in all of the thousands of miles of ocean? Let us find out.

Notice on the globe that there is a line running around the earth midway between the poles. It is labeled "Equator." Both north and south of the Equator there are other, parallel lines around the earth. These are called parallels. The distance between one parallel and the next is 1°, or one-ninetieth of the distance between the Equator and the North or the South Pole. If all the parallels were shown on a globe, they would be so close together that there would not be room enough to show the oceans, the continents, the countries, and the cities clearly. That is why most globes or maps show only every fifth, tenth, or fifteenth parallel.

Unlike the parallels shown on the globe, the parallels of the earth are not really drawn on the earth's surface. They are just imaginary lines. As in the case of the globe, however, the distance between one parallel and the next is one-ninetieth of the distance between the Equator and either pole, but the earth is so big that they are not close together at all. They are 1°, or about 70 miles, apart.

Study your globe, or the picture of it on page 228. Find the line north of the Equator marked 30°. All places on this parallel are 

30° north of the Equator. Follow this line until you find New Orleans. How many degrees north of the Equator is New Orleans? 

About how many miles is that? South of the

Equator are other lines, numbered in the same way. How far south of the Equator is a place on the parallel marked 30°? Distance north of the Equator, measured in degrees, is called north latitude; distance south of the Equator is called south latitude.

Parallels not only help in measuring distances from the Equator, but they also help us in measuring north and south distances anywhere on the earth's surface. On a map of the 

United States, notice that the long, straightline northern boundary is on the 49° parallel, and that the main southern boundary is about the 30° parallel. How many degrees is it from north to south across our country between these two points? About how many miles?

In order to locate a place exactly we must know more than its latitude. If we know that 

New Orleans is 30° north latitude we know that it is somewhere on an imaginary line running around the earth 30° north of the Equator, but we do not know where on that line it is. Meridians tell us this.

Look at your globe or its picture again. Notice the lines running north and south. These meridians, as they are called, are imaginary lines around the earth. Each one crosses the

Equator and passes through both the North and South Poles. There are 360 of these meridians, 1° apart, though most maps and globes show only some of them. 

We have seen that latitude is measured from the Equator north and south. In much the same way, distance east and west called longitude is measured from a certain meridian, called the prime meridian. The prime meridian passes through the town of Greenwich in England. It is labeled o°. Find it on the globe. Any point east of the prime meridian is in east longitude, and any point west of the prime meridian is in west longitude. There are 180° of east longitude and 180° of west longitude. New Orleans is on the 90° meridian west of the prime meridian. We say that its longitude is 90° west. All places on this meridian have a longitude of 90° west.

At the Equator the distance between the meridians is the same as the distance between the parallels, but the distance between merid- 229

PARALLELS AND MERIDIANS 

ians grows constantly less and less as the meridians go north and south, until they finally meet at the poles. Because the distance between meridians varies as we go north or south, we do not commonly use meridians to measure distance in miles, as we do the parallels.

Although many different places may have a latitude of 30° north, and many may have a longitude of 90° west, notice on the globe that only one place can be both 30° north and 90° west. What is the name of that place? What is the latitude and longitude of your town?

Has any other town the same latitude and longitude? If you know the latitude and longitude of any place, how can you find it on the map? 

Let us go back to our first question. How can the rescuers find a sinking ship that has radioed for help? Every ship's captain must keep constant track of the latitude and longitude of his vessel in order to steer the ship accurately from port to port. This is done by means of simple instruments. When danger threatens, and the ship's radio calls for help, this latitude and longitude are broadcast and the rescuers know exactly where to go. 

Suppose an S.O.S. message says, "Latitude 

35° north, longitude 75° west." All who receive the message know that the sinking ship is 35° north of the Equator, and that it is 75° west of the prime meridian which passes through Greenwich. Find this location on a globe or a map. Near what famous cape is it?

This is one of the most dangerous places off our eastern coast.

You will find it fun to locate on a map the position of ships at sea and of airships on long flights whenever you know their latitude and longitude as given in newspapers or over the radio.

THE IROQUOIS SERIES OF GEOGRAPHIES AND WORK BOOKS 

By ABRAMS-BODLEY-THURSTON 

A new and complete series of Geographies and Work Books presenting the subject of Geography from the modern point of view. Emphasis is placed on the natural regions and their effects on the lives and activities of the people who live in them. Political units, too, are fully recognized and judiciously treated. 

The text stories are full and rich in content and simple in style. At the end of each unit are: a summary of the facts which the pupil should remember, topics for discussion, question and map exercises, and projects. Many graphs and tables supply important statistics. Complete pronouncing indexes, cross-referenced for ease of use, make the finding of any topic an easy matter. 

A wealth of illustrations and varied maps enliven and supplement the texts. The pictures were chosen for their geographic values and their artistic qualities. They are grouped at the beginnings of the units and at strategic points in the text to provide interest-arousing preliminary views of the chapter contents. The maps show the locations of all places mentioned in the text, and are especially clear and easy to interpret. 

HOME GEOGRAPHY. A book for beginners in Geography which introduces the child to the study of 

Geography through the region he knows best-his own surroundings. A text and work book combined.

PEOPLES OF OTHER LANDS. A basal text, providing studies of type regions of the earth and of the reactions of their peoples to their environments. 

A WORK BOOK TO ACCOMPANY PEOPLES OF OTHER LANDS. A work book, built to key with 

PEOPLES OF OTHER LANDS, and providing in full for map work and other pupil activities.

NORTH AMERICA AND SOUTH AMERICA. An exceptionally readable basal text which gives the pupil distinct, clear cut impressions of the natural regions and countries of the continents of the 

Western hemisphere.

A WORK BOOK ON NORTH AMERICA AND SOUTH AMERICA. A companion book to NORTH 

AMERICA AND SOUTH AMERICA, providing varied, well-balanced material for pupil activities.

THE OLD WORLD CONTINENTS. A basal text on Europe, Africa, Asia, and Australia, fixing essen tial points by sharp, clear contrasts between regions and between countries.

A WORK BOOK ON THE OLD WORLD CONTINENTS. A companion book to THE OLD WORLD 

CONTINENTS, providing modern drills and tests and map exercises for all pupil activities. 

NORTH AMERICA. A basal text, presenting a well-rounded treatment of the regions and countries of our own continent of North America.

SOUTH AMERICA AND THE OLD WORLD CONTINENTS. A basal text covering all continents ex cept North America. Rich in teaching aids.

A WORK BOOK ON SOUTH AMERICA AND THE OLD WORLD CONTINENTS. A companion book to SOUTH AMERICA AND THE OLD WORLD CONTINENTS, providing drills, tests, map exer cises, and other features for pupil activities.

WORLD GEOGRAPHY. An advanced basal text grouping geographic facts in new and significant ways to show relationships. Treats the basic activities of mankind, the great facts of commerce and industry, and the factors which make for the strength of nations. A fitting capstone for any Geography course.

A WORK BOOK ON WORLD GEOGRAPHY. A companion book to WORLD GEOGRAPHY. Particu larly rich in varied material-modern tests, drills, map and graph exercises for pupil activities.

A COMPLETE HISTORY SERIES FOR THE GRADES 

Southworth and Southworth

An exceptional new series of basal texts in history for the grades. Noted for simple, complete presentation of the subject, many splendid illustrations and maps, and varied and effective teaching helps. 

THE STORY OF LONG AGO. Greek and Norse myths, the story of Early Man, and Old World his tory to the fall of Rome.

THE STORY OF THE MIDDLE AGES. Old World history from the fall of Rome through the Period of Exploration.

AMERICA'S OLD WORLD BACKGROUND. Greek and Norse myths, the story of Early Man, and the history of the Old World from the Dawn of Civilization through the Period of Exploration.

THE THIRTEEN AMERICAN COLONIES. The American Indians before the coming of the white man, the Period of Exploration, the Colonial Period, and the Intercolonial Wars.

EARLY DAYS IN AMERICA. The Period of Exploration, the Colonial Period, the Intercolonial Wars, the Struggle for Independence, and the adoption of the Constitution.

AMERICAN HISTORY, COMPLETE EDITION-Illustrated. The complete history of our country from the beginning of the Period of Exploration to the present day.

A WORK BOOK IN AMERICAN HISTORY. Built to key with AMERICAN HISTORY, COMPLETE 

EDITION, but usable with any complete standard text. Contains a wide variety of modern tests and other pupil activities.

NEW YORK IROQUOIS PUBLISHING COMPANY, INC.
